# Fake Engagement and the Law: How Federal, State and European Union Rules Apply to the Twenty Most Common Topics in 273,771 Engagement-Pod Posts

Daniel Hall · October 21, 2026

Draft of November 5, 2026. Not submitted to or accepted by Harvard University, and no Dissertation Acceptance Certificate has been signed.

© 2026 Daniel Hall. This dissertation is licensed under the [Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International License (CC BY 4.0)](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/). You may share and adapt it for any purpose if you credit the author, link to the license and say whether you made changes.

Suggested citation: Hall, Daniel. 2026. *Fake Engagement and the Law: How Federal, State and European Union Rules Apply to the Twenty Most Common Topics in 273,771 Engagement-Pod Posts.* Draft dissertation. <https://github.com/DreamITSoftware/SPOTAPOD/tree/main/dissertation>

## Abstract

Engagement pods produce fake social media indicators. The likes, comments and shares that a pod produces come from an agreement among its members, or from a tool that prompts them, and not from readers who chose on their own to engage, so they say little about how a post was received. The Federal Trade Commission's rule on fake indicators of social media influence, 16 CFR 465.1(h), gives the term a legal definition. Whether that definition reaches each kind of pod engagement is an open question, and this dissertation maps what the law says about it.

This dissertation is primary research on largely uncharted ground. In the sources I opened, I found one peer-reviewed study of engagement pods: O'Meara (2019) interviewed 16 Instagram influencers, and her study does not address law or LinkedIn. My own earlier book on pods (Hall, Adam and Berg, 2025) is a trade book that is not peer reviewed and applies no statute or rule to a corpus of posts. I found no earlier study that analyzes a large corpus of pod posts, none that sets the rules in force against such a corpus topic by topic, and none that connects pods to mental health. A search that finds nothing does not show that nothing exists, and I did not do a systematic review of the literature, so a reader who knows of earlier work that I missed should treat this paragraph as needing correction.

The research is primary because the de-duplication rule, the counts, the topic model and the reading of the rules are an original analysis of the corpus, and because I collected one of the four sources, the 65 export files, myself and have not published it. I ran no interviews or surveys, and the corpus analysis is not an experiment. My earlier book reports two field demonstrations that I ran in pods, which Section 2.9 describes and this dissertation does not analyze. The mental-health sections review published research. I, Daniel Hall, am the sole author of this dissertation and the only researcher named in it. I used an AI assistant to help retrieve sources, run the counts and draft text, as Section 1.5 describes, and I am responsible for the result.

Federal, state and EU law reach a pod-boosted post through what the post says, sells or discloses, and rarely through the boost itself. I reached this finding by pairing a corpus of engagement-pod posts with a topic-by-topic reading of the rules. The corpus holds 397,192 records from four sources, which reduce to 273,771 distinct posts. A topic model places 64,970 of them in named topics, and the twenty largest topics hold 54,870 posts (84.5% of the placed posts). For each topic I asked which laws a regulator, a competitor or a court would examine first, which actor each law reaches (the author, the pod-tool operator, the engaging member or the platform), what the rules mean for readers and for young people in particular, and which questions no court or agency has answered.

The topic chapters cover the rules on endorsements, fake reviews and fake indicators of influence, pricing and fees, earnings claims, securities offers and touting, health claims, children's privacy and child influencers, forced-labor imports and customs fraud, competition, and EU consumer, platform and AI law. Only two instruments are aimed at the engagement itself: 16 CFR 465.8, which took effect on 21 October 2024, and a platform's own contract. In the sources I opened I found no complaint or decision that applies section 465.8 to pod engagement, so I list 25 questions that remain open. The engaging member is the party for whom I found the least authority.

The data show that pods rarely announce themselves: the word "pod" appears in 43 of the 54,870 posts in the twenty topics (0.08%). The words that bear on a rule are present in a minority of posts in every topic, and only links and hashtags reach a majority anywhere. I have no data on how any young person reads an engagement count, so the chapters label the link between pods and coming generations as inference. Each chapter also has a section on mental health that draws on published research I opened; none of that research studies engagement pods, and I label each link to a topic as inference.

The study has limits. The posts are those that people submitted to pod tools, the corpus file used for the counts holds no dates, although every record in the 65 export files carries a creation date, and many primary legal texts reached me as summaries. Each claim carries an evidence tier. This is research and not legal advice, and no chapter says that any author, tool operator or member broke any law.

**Keywords:** engagement pods; fake engagement; FTC Act section 5; 16 CFR Part 465; endorsements; consumer protection; platform regulation; children and young people

## Chapter 1. Introduction

### 1.1 The question

This dissertation asks which laws apply to the twenty most common topics in a large collection of engagement-pod posts, and what those laws mean for the people who read the posts now and for the generations who will grow up on the platforms that carry them. It is primary research on ground where, in the sources I opened, little earlier work exists (Section 1.4).

An engagement pod is a group of people who agree to like, comment on or share one another's posts. Many pods run through software that tells each member which posts to engage with and when. The collection I analyze holds 397,192 records from four sources, which reduce to 273,771 distinct posts (Chapter 2). A topic model places 64,970 of those posts in a topic with confidence, and the twenty largest topics hold 54,870 of them (84.5%). The posts concern business, technology, money, health, travel and family. Most look like ordinary professional posts. What they have in common is that their authors listed them in a pod tool so that other members would engage with them.

The likes, comments and shares that a pod produces are fake social media indicators in the plain sense of the term. They exist because members agreed to give them in return, or because a tool told members to give them, and not because each reader independently chose to engage, so a count that a pod produced does not measure how readers received the post. The law gives the term a narrower meaning. The Federal Trade Commission's rule on fake indicators of social media influence defines fake indicators in 16 CFR 465.1(h) as those generated by bots, those from "purported individual accounts not associated with a real individual," those from hijacked accounts, and those that "otherwise do not reflect a real individual's or entity's activities, opinions, findings, or experiences." Whether a like that a real member gives at a tool's prompt meets that last clause is the first open question in Chapter 3, and I found no decision on it.

I ask four questions.

1. For each topic, which laws would a regulator, a competitor or a court examine first if the posts in that topic were boosted by a pod?
2. For each law, which actor does it reach: the author of the post, the operator of the pod tool, the member who engages, or the platform that hosts the post?
3. What do the rules mean in practice for people who rely on these posts when they decide what to buy, whom to hire, whom to trust and where to put their money, and what do they mean for young people in particular?
4. Which of these legal questions has no answer yet because no court or agency has addressed it?

### 1.2 Why the topic of a post matters to the law

The topic of a post largely decides which rules apply to it, so the chapters are organized by topic and each chapter selects the laws that fit.

A post about a spreadsheet template and a post about a stock tip can be boosted by the same tool, and the law treats them differently. Securities law applies to the stock tip. Health-claim rules apply to a post about a sleep supplement. Children's privacy law applies to a post that features a child. The Federal Trade Commission's rule on fake indicators of social media influence applies to all of them (Chapter 3), and its consequences differ with what the post asks the reader to believe or buy. Boosting does not change what a post says. It changes how many people see the post and how credible it looks. Legislatures and agencies have written specific rules for claims about money, health, children and product safety, so those are the topics where a boosted post is most likely to meet a specific rule. That is my reading of the legal landscape, and each topic chapter tests it against the text of the rules.

### 1.3 Why I write about future generations

Each topic chapter closes its legal analysis with a section on what the rules mean for people and for coming generations, because the people exposed to platform signals for the longest time are young.

Chapter 3 gives the sourced facts. The U.S. Surgeon General's advisory on social media and youth mental health reports that up to 95% of young people aged 13 to 17 use a social media platform. The argument that connects those facts to engagement pods is mine, and I label it INFERENCE wherever it appears. It has two parts. The first is that visible counts teach young people what credibility and success look like, so a manufactured count teaches the wrong lesson at the age when the lesson forms. The second is that legal answers given early become the baseline for every later cohort: the first court to decide whether a prompted like "reflects" a real person's opinion will set the answer that children now in school will live under as adults.

I have no data on how teenagers read engagement counts, and the posts I analyze say nothing about who read them. Where a chapter can anchor the argument in a published study or an official report, it does. Where it cannot, it says so.

### 1.4 What the dissertation adds

The dissertation adds a map from topic to law, tied to a defined corpus of pod posts. For each of twenty topics it states which rules apply, who each rule reaches, and what remains untested. Every legal claim marked VERIFIED rests on a primary text that I opened, and every claim I could not check at the source carries a lower tier (Section 2.4). The last chapters compare the topics and list the questions that no court or agency has yet answered.

The subject is largely uncharted. In the sources I opened, I found one peer-reviewed study of engagement pods. O'Meara (2019) interviewed 16 Instagram influencers and analyzed the interviews with grounded theory, and her title describes pods as practices of resistance to platform labor. Her study rests on interviews, concerns Instagram and does not address law or LinkedIn.

Beyond that study, I found no earlier work that analyzes a large corpus of pod posts, none that sets the rules in force against such a corpus topic by topic, no complaint or decision that applies 16 CFR § 465.8 to pod engagement (Chapter 3 and Section 25.4), and no study that connects pods to mental health. I also saw a 2022 paper in the *Journal of Marketing Management* on mutual engagement among food influencers. I met it only in search results and did not open it, so I do not describe it (References, Section I). A search that finds nothing does not show that nothing exists, and I did not do a systematic review of the literature. A reader who knows of earlier work that I missed should treat these statements as needing correction.

My own book, *Top 100 List of Lies on Social Media* (2025), also describes pods. It is a trade book that is not peer reviewed, it gives a participant's account and two field demonstrations, and it applies no statute or rule to a corpus of posts. Section 2.9 says how I use it. Because I wrote it, it does not corroborate anything in this dissertation.

The work is primary research. The de-duplication rule, the counts, the topic model, the merging of components into topics and the reading of the rules topic by topic are an original analysis of the corpus. I collected the 65 export files myself, by navigating directly to the site that served the data and capturing what it returned on a local server, and the files are unpublished (Section 2.1). The legal findings rest on the texts of statutes, rules and court records that I opened, which are primary legal sources (Section 2.4).

The work is primary only in those respects. The podawaa source is a published dataset, so my analysis of it is a new analysis of existing data, and my public repository, the SPOTAPOD Analysis Repository (Hall, 2026), already analyzes the first three sources in aggregate and, since 22 October 2026, profiles the 65 export files in aggregate (Section 2.1). It does not hold this dissertation's de-duplication rule, topic model or legal analysis. I ran no interviews or surveys for this dissertation. My earlier book reports two field demonstrations that I ran in pods, and Section 2.9 describes them, but they are not part of the analysis. The mental-health sections review published research and are secondary.

I, Daniel Hall, am the sole author of this dissertation and the only researcher named in it. It names no co-author, supervisor or institution. Section 1.5 describes the AI assistant that I used, and I am responsible for the result.

The contribution is the pairing of a large pod corpus with a topic-by-topic legal analysis, offered so that others can test it.

### 1.5 My position, my disclosures and the limits of what I claim

I have worked with engagement pods since 2019. I coauthored *Top 100 List of Lies on Social Media*, I wrote the teen guide *DIGITAL BOUGHTulism: POONAMI*, and I am the CEO of Dream IT Software, which publishes the SPOTAPOD Analysis Repository (Hall, 2026; Section 2.1). I therefore have an interest in how this subject is described. The evidence tiers and the rule that VERIFIED legal claims rest on an opened primary text are my safeguards against that interest, and a reader should still weigh my position.

The book states that I am a member of several pods, and it reports two demonstrations that I ran in pods (Section 2.9). I am therefore a participant in the activity that this dissertation studies and not only an observer, and a reader should weigh that as well.

The case correspondence in Chapter 10 adds two disclosures. In March 2024 I told LinkedIn that I had created a second account to expose pods and find vulnerabilities and that I use it for LEMPOD, a pod tool, so I have used a pod tool myself. I also reported two flaws to LinkedIn, in 2023 and 2024, and I did not take up LinkedIn's suggestion of its bug bounty program for the second (STATED; the emails are supplied by me and I cannot authenticate them).

I used Claude, an AI assistant made by Anthropic, to help retrieve and read sources, run the counts and draft text. I chose the questions and the claims, and I am responsible for the result.

Three limits apply to every chapter.

- This is research and not legal advice. A person facing a legal decision should consult a licensed attorney in the relevant jurisdiction.
- The data hold post text and engagement fields. They do not hold anyone's intent, knowledge or identity, and several rules turn on knowledge ("knew or should have known" in 16 CFR § 465.8, for example). No chapter says that any author, tool operator or member broke any law.
- I name no author or account. Where a post is quoted, the quotation is of text that appears in the data, and the speaker stays anonymous.

### 1.6 How the dissertation is organized

Chapter 2 describes the data and the method. Chapter 3 sets out the legal framework that applies to every topic. Chapters 4 to 23 take the twenty topics in order of size, from technology and innovation (5,201 posts) to crypto and blockchain (1,180 posts). Each topic chapter has six parts: the topic in the data; the laws that apply; how the rules reach pod-boosted posts; what this means for people and for coming generations; how this topic bears on mental health; and what the data cannot show. Chapter 24 compares the topics and shows which laws recur. Chapters 1 to 3, 24 and 25 each include a section on mental health. Chapter 25 gives the conclusion, the limitations and the ethics of the study. The References list every source I opened, with a link to each.

### 1.7 Mental health in this dissertation

Each chapter has a section on mental health, and each rests on published research that I opened, because the posts say nothing about how anyone felt.

The question in each section is how the subject of the chapter, or the law that governs it, bears on the mental health of the people who write, read or are affected by posts on that subject. I answer it with four rules. I report an association as an association: the American Psychological Association's 2023 advisory on social media says that "findings suggesting causal associations are rare." I do not say that engagement pods cause any mental-health outcome, because none of the sources I opened studies pods and the posts carry no information about their readers. I label each link between a source and a topic as INFERENCE. Where I found no source on a topic, the section says so and names the nearest indirect evidence.

I am not a clinician. Nothing in these sections is a diagnosis or clinical advice. The figures come from organizations such as the American Psychological Association, the U.S. Surgeon General, the World Health Organization and the Federal Trade Commission, and several of them say that the evidence is still developing. Chapter 2 describes how I handled that evidence, and Chapter 25 states what remains open.

## Chapter 2. Data and method

The analysis rests on 273,771 distinct posts drawn from four sources that together hold 397,192 records. Each record is a post that someone listed on an engagement-pod or boost tool.

### 2.1 The four sources

Table 2.1 lists the four sources, the number of records in each and what each contains.

**Table 2.1.** The four sources of posts, with the number of records in each and what each contains

| Source | Records | What it contains |
| --- | --- | --- |
| podawaa | 213,491 | Post text, like and view counts, and an author identifier. The provenance notes in the SPOTAPOD Analysis Repository (Hall, 2026) record a checksum match between this file and Harvard Dataverse record doi:10.7910/DVN/WD9AUR, titled LinkedIn posts using fake socials, which I did not open (Section 2.1 and References, Section I). |
| HyperClapper | 49,369 | Post text, like and comment flags, creation time and author profile fields, from 702 distinct authors. It has no Dataverse citation. |
| LinkBoost-2025 | 77,969 | Post titles and profile links. By the construction of the dataset, each record is a logged pod action. It has no Dataverse citation. |
| 65 export files | 56,363 | Post text, a link, a state, an engagement type, a timing setting and a count of engaged users. Records were created between 11 October 2024 and 16 October 2026, and the files were exported on 16 October 2026. These files are not published. Section 2.10 describes them. |

My public repository is the SPOTAPOD Analysis Repository (Hall, 2026), at [github.com/DreamITSoftware/SPOTAPOD](https://github.com/DreamITSoftware/SPOTAPOD). Dream IT Software publishes it, with the code under the MIT licence and the documents under CC BY 4.0. I cite the repository as it stood at commit 2c8d3f8 on 22 October 2026, and it can change after that date. Its citation file states that it is independent of the project at github.com/PolymathWizard/SPOTAPOD, which has the same name, and this dissertation does not cite that project. The citation file also lists a Harvard Dataverse record, Replication Data for: SPOTAPOD Analysis Repository (doi:10.7910/DVN/NT6YM2). I did not open that record and make no claim about what it holds.

The repository analyzes the first three sources in aggregate. Since 22 October 2026 it also holds an aggregate profile of the 65 export files, the content scans run on them, and a manifest of their file hashes. It holds no raw file from any source (its test fixtures are fabricated records), and the 65 export files stay in a private workspace because each record carries the name of its poster and a link to the post (Section 2.5).

The repository does not hold the de-duplication rule in Section 2.2, the topic model in Section 2.3 or the counts that depend on them, including the 273,771 distinct posts and the topic shares in later chapters. I ran those steps in my private workspace and have not published the code for them. The repository sorts records into nine categories by keyword, which is a different method from the topic model, and its profile scripts count records as each file holds them without applying the rule in Section 2.2. A figure from the repository and a figure from this dissertation for the same source can therefore differ. No result in this dissertation names an account, an author or a post.

I collected the 65 export files myself. I navigated directly to the site that served the data, linkboost.co (Section 2.8), and captured the data it returned on a local server.

### 2.2 Counting distinct posts

A post counts once, however many times it appears. I drop any record whose text is shorter than 40 characters. I treat two records as the same post when their first 200 characters are identical after lowercasing the text and collapsing every run of non-word characters into a single space. I applied the rule in a fixed order: podawaa, then HyperClapper, then LinkBoost-2025, then the 65 exports. The result is 273,771 distinct posts. The same post can be listed many times by one tool or by several, so the 397,192 records overstate how many different posts the tools handled.

### 2.3 The topic model and the twenty topics

I grouped the posts by subject with a standard text-mining method. Each post was converted to weighted word and two-word-phrase features (TF-IDF, with 60,000 features, common words above a 15% document frequency removed, and phrases that appear in fewer than ten posts removed). I then fitted non-negative matrix factorization with 80 components on a random sample of 70,000 posts, using a fixed random seed, and scored all 273,771 posts against the result.

A post is placed in a component only when that component holds at least 30% of its weight. Placement is therefore conservative: 147,147 posts met the threshold and 126,624 did not. I then merged the 80 components by hand into 33 named topics. Seventeen components were mostly written in French, Spanish, German, Italian, Portuguese or Dutch, and seven described how a post was written (hashtag lists, contractions, closing questions) or were too generic to name a subject. I set both groups aside. A second filter on word counts set aside a further 6,355 posts that were in another language but sat inside English components, and 442 posts were in non-Latin scripts. The remaining 64,970 posts, 23.7% of the distinct posts, sit in named topics.

Table 2.2 accounts for all 273,771 distinct posts.

**Table 2.2.** Distinct posts by whether the topic model placed them in a named topic

| Posts accounted for | Count |
| --- | --- |
| Placed in a named topic | 64,970 |
| Not placed confidently | 126,624 |
| Other language, by component | 61,536 |
| Other language, by post | 6,355 |
| Non-Latin script | 442 |
| Format-driven or generic | 13,844 |
| **All distinct posts** | **273,771** |

### 2.4 Evidence tiers

Every factual statement in the chapters that follow carries one of five tiers, and the tier is named whenever a statement could be read more strongly than the evidence allows.

- **VERIFIED:** computed by me from the files, or read by me in the text of a statute, rule, court record or agency page.
- **CORROBORATED:** supported by a second, independent source.
- **STATED:** an account given by the author of a claim or by a party to it, not independently checked.
- **UNCORROBORATED:** reported, but with no supporting source I could find.
- **INFERENCE:** my own conclusion from the evidence.

For legal statements, VERIFIED means I opened the primary text. Where I could reach only a summary or a news report of a legal development, the text says so.

### 2.5 Privacy and ethics

The analysis is aggregate-only. No chapter identifies an account, an author, a company or an individual post, and no quotation is long enough to identify its source. The posts were written for public audiences, but their authors did not agree to be studied, and the study does not need their identities. The legal chapters apply rules to categories of speech. They make no finding that any author violated any law.

### 2.6 Limits

Four limits bound every later chapter. First, the data are posts that people submitted to pod and boost tools. They are not a random sample of LinkedIn, and they say nothing about posts that were never submitted. Second, the records show what was submitted and, in the 65 exports, how a tool handled it. They do not show who engaged, who paid, what a post earned or what any viewer did next. Third, the topic model places fewer than a quarter of the posts, so the topic shares describe the placed posts and not the whole corpus. Fourth, 22 of the 65 export files hold exactly 5,000 records, which looks like an export limit, so the number of records per month is not a trend (Section 2.10).

The legal analysis has its own limit. I am not a lawyer, and this dissertation is not legal advice. Whether a rule applies to a particular post depends on facts that the data do not contain, such as who wrote the post, what they were paid and what they knew.

### 2.7 How I handle mental-health evidence

The data cannot measure mental health, so the mental-health sections rest on outside sources and follow a stricter evidence rule than the counts do.

The corpus holds post text and tool records. It holds nothing about readers, and the fields I know about, such as creation time, engagement type and engaged-user count, describe what the tool did and not how anyone felt (Chapter 10). A count of words such as "burnout," "loneliness" or "anxiety" shows that the word occurs. It does not show that the author or any reader had the condition, and the sections report such counts as counts of words.

For outside evidence I applied four tests. I opened the source myself. I record its kind: an advisory, a survey, a systematic review, a single study or a press account of one. I report its design limits where it states them. And I do not combine sources of different design into one figure. A source that I read only as an abstract, or through a university or news summary, is marked STATED in the text and in the References.

The study by Sherman and colleagues (2016) shows why the tests matter. In a study of 34 adolescents, 32 of whom were in the brain-imaging analysis, photos shown with many likes drew more activity in reward-related regions than photos shown with few likes. The study did not measure mental health, and I cite it only for what it measured.

One source of a different kind is my own book, *Top 100 List of Lies on Social Media*. It asserts that the pursuit of vanity metrics through pods harms the mental health of creators and of society, and it cites no study. I record those passages as my opinion (STATED) and do not use them as evidence for any mental-health claim in this dissertation (Section 2.9).

### 2.8 The terms of the site that served the data

The 65 export files came from the LinkBoost site, linkboost.co. The file called LinkBoost-2025 in Section 2.1 shares the product's name, and this section concerns only the 65 export files. I read the site's terms of service and privacy policy through the fetch tool, which returns a summary of a page and not its full text, so I quote only phrases that the tool returned and every statement about the terms carries the tier STATED. The terms are issued by Linkboost, Inc., state that they were last updated on 01/01/2024, are governed by Delaware law and send disputes to binding arbitration on an individual basis.

One clause addresses the kind of capture that Section 2.1 describes. Section 6.1 prohibits "Engaging in data scraping or data harvesting outside of approved functionalities of the Service." The summaries that I received did not say which functionalities the terms approve, and they did not mention bots, scripts, crawlers, application programming interfaces or browser access. Section 6.2 lets the company investigate suspected violations and suspend or terminate access without notice.

A second clause bears on publication. Section 8 defines confidential information as "any non-public information disclosed to you by Linkboost or made available through the Service" and says "You shall not disclose such information to any third party without Linkboost's prior written consent." I do not know whether the posts in the 65 files count as non-public information under that clause. This study publishes aggregate counts and names no account, author or post (Section 2.5), and I do not decide whether that satisfies the clause.

In the summaries that I received, the terms say nothing about research, benchmarking or publishing results, about LinkedIn's own terms, or about engagement pods and fake engagement. Section 5.1 grants a limited, revocable license for personal or internal business use. The privacy policy, which states that it was last updated on 01/01/2022, does not mention LinkedIn data, session tokens, browser-extension data, or post and engagement data.

This section reports what the terms say and does not decide whether my capture complied with them. That question turns on what the approved functionalities are and on the terms of my access (the book states that I am a member of LinkBoost, Section 2.9), and this dissertation does not settle it. I am not a lawyer, and this is not legal advice (Section 2.6).

### 2.9 My earlier book and how this dissertation uses it

This dissertation follows a book that I wrote with Mic Adam and Shari Berg, *Top 100 List of Lies on Social Media* (2025; References, Section J). It has nine chapters on engagement pods. Chapters 7 and 8 are signed by Berg and Chapter 9 by Adam, and Chapters 1 to 6 are unsigned and mostly in my voice. The book is a trade book and is not peer reviewed. It mentions terms-of-service violations, lawsuits and jail time in general terms, and I found no citation in it of a statute, a rule, a court decision or a study. It reports none of the corpus counts in this dissertation.

The book is also my own account as a participant. It states that I am in several online pods, among them LEMPOD, ALCAPOD, HyperClapper, LinkBoost, PowerIn and Podawaa (spelled PODOWAA at that point in the book), for three reasons: to learn how pods operate and report on them, to expose people who game the system, and to post satire in them (p. 49). The book does not say how the 65 export files were collected (Section 2.1).

Because I wrote the book, it cannot corroborate a claim in this dissertation. The tier CORROBORATED needs a second source that is independent of the author (Section 2.4), and a book by the same author is not one. I use the book for what a participant says that he saw and did, and for what it claims about how pods are built, sold and run. Both are STATED, and I mark each inference of mine as INFERENCE.

The book reports two demonstrations that I ran in pods. In the first, a post that said in its own text that it was fake news, with a false statement about the number of church fires, was released into pods, and the book reports that it drew more than 200 reactions from members who "had no idea they were spreading fake news" (pp. 49 to 50). In the second, called PODAPALOOZA, the book says that I ran an experiment over twenty or more posts to show that the author of a post can supply the comments that other members' automated accounts then post, without those members knowing it was happening (pp. 77 to 82). The book describes the method and the settings and does not report the data from either demonstration. I have not analyzed them for this dissertation, I cannot check the figures, and I treat both as STATED. They are field demonstrations and not controlled experiments: the book mentions no comparison group, no sampling plan, no consent procedure and no ethics review.

The book also asserts that the pursuit of vanity metrics through pods harms mental health, and it explains the claim with four brain chemicals: dopamine, oxytocin, serotonin and endorphins (pp. 44, 52 and 60 to 63). It cites no study of pod users or of mental health. I treat those passages as my opinion and not as evidence, and the mental-health sections of this dissertation rest on the published research that Section 2.7 describes.

Section 10.2 uses the book's statements about automated settings, timing, members' knowledge, the sale of pod engagement and the platforms. Sections 1.5 and 25.3 use its account of my membership of pods and of the demonstrations.

### 2.10 What the 65 export files hold

Section 2.1 gives the number of records in the 65 export files. This section describes how the files are built, because later chapters rely on them. The collection holds 83 files, and 18 of them are byte-for-byte copies of another file, so 65 files are distinct. Each file is one response from the site's server and holds a list of records. The file names carry a time stamp, written year, day, month, and all 65 stamps fall between 12:24 and 13:44 on 16 October 2026. The time zone is not stated.

The 65 distinct files hold 184,737 records between them, which reduce to 56,363 distinct records when I match the record's own identifier. Of these, 32,842 appear in more than one file. I kept one copy of each record, and where the copies differ I kept the one with the highest count of engaged users. Eight records have a different state in different files, so the state counts in this dissertation depend on that rule. Under it, 54,377 records (96.5%) are finished, 1,631 are deleted, 263 are stopped and 92 are running.

Twenty-two of the 65 files hold exactly 5,000 records, and no file holds more. I infer that an export returns at most 5,000 records, although the site does not say so (INFERENCE). The records in those 22 files span a median of 35 days from the earliest to the latest, and the records in the other 43 files span a median of 735 days. Older records are therefore probably missing from the 22 files. The 56,363 records are the ones that the exports returned, and the site may hold more.

Every one of the 56,363 records carries a creation time, which I read as stored and as UTC. The earliest is 11 October 2024 and the latest is 16 October 2026. Of the records, 438 (0.78%) were created before 21 October 2024, the date on which 16 CFR 465.8 took effect by my count (Chapter 3), and 55,925 (99.22%) were created on or after that date. Table 2.4 counts the records by the quarter of creation.

**Table 2.4.** Distinct records in the 65 export files by the quarter in which they were created (UTC), with the share of all 56,363 records

| Quarter | Records | Share of records |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 2024 Q4 (from 11 October) | 3,809 | 6.8% |
| 2025 Q1 | 4,667 | 8.3% |
| 2025 Q2 | 5,754 | 10.2% |
| 2025 Q3 | 6,220 | 11.0% |
| 2025 Q4 | 5,363 | 9.5% |
| 2026 Q1 | 5,599 | 9.9% |
| 2026 Q2 | 6,809 | 12.1% |
| 2026 Q3 | 13,313 | 23.6% |
| 2026 Q4 (to 16 October) | 4,829 | 8.6% |
| Total | 56,363 | 100.0% |

The quarters are not a trend. The limit of 5,000 records removes the older records from the 22 files that reach it, and September 2026 alone holds 7,285 records (12.9%). The count in a quarter says how many of the returned records were created then, and it says nothing about how many records the site held.

The creation time and the post time can differ. Each record's link contains the identifier of the post that the record targets, and the upper bits of that identifier appear to hold a publish time in milliseconds (INFERENCE; LinkedIn has not confirmed the layout). On that reading, a record was created a median of 0.39 hours after the post that it targets was published. Of the 56,363 records, 64.0% were created within 1 hour of the post's publish time, 89.8% within 24 hours and 2.2% more than 7 days after, and none was created before its post. The decoded post dates run from 14 April 2019 to 16 October 2026, and 534 records target a post that decodes to a time before 21 October 2024. For most records, then, the creation date is close to the date of the post, and the 2.2% created more than a week after their post show that the two can differ.

A split by date is a count and gives no finding that anyone broke a rule. Section 465.8 reaches the selling, distributing and buying of a fake indicator, and it turns on what the person knew (Chapter 3). The records show no purchase, no payment and no knowledge. I therefore report the split and draw no conclusion from it about any member, tool operator or author.

The script that produced these counts, `analysis/export65_profile.py`, is in the SPOTAPOD Analysis Repository (Hall, 2026), together with a manifest of the file hashes in `checksums/EXPORT65_MANIFEST.csv`. The script prints counts only, and the files themselves are not published (Section 2.1). The repository page docs/research/export-65-profile.md holds the script's output, and docs/provenance.md records the source and the method of collection as I stated them, which the repository cannot check (STATED).

## Chapter 3. The legal framework common to every topic

Most of the legal exposure around a boosted post comes from a short list of rules that apply whatever the post is about. This chapter sets those rules out once, so that each topic chapter can add only what is specific to its subject.

### 3.1 Section 5 of the FTC Act

The Federal Trade Commission Act makes unlawful "unfair or deceptive acts or practices in or affecting commerce" (15 U.S.C. § 45(a)(1)). Two standards sit under that sentence.

The deception standard comes from the Commission's Policy Statement on Deception, dated 14 October 1983. It has three elements: "a representation, omission or practice that is likely to mislead the consumer"; judged "from the perspective of a consumer acting reasonably in the circumstances"; and the representation, omission or practice "must be a 'material' one." The statement presumes that express claims are material, and it judges a message by its "net impression" on the general public. A post does not have to contain a false sentence to deceive. The overall impression is what counts.

The unfairness standard is in 15 U.S.C. § 45(n). A practice is unfair when it causes or is likely to cause substantial injury that is "not reasonably avoidable by consumers themselves and not outweighed by countervailing benefits." A viewer cannot avoid a popularity count that looks real and is not, because the viewer has no way to tell the difference. That is why I treat unfairness as a live theory for fake engagement, and I mark it as my reading (INFERENCE).

The remedy matters as much as the standard. Under 15 U.S.C. § 45(m)(1)(A) the Commission can seek civil penalties for violations of a trade regulation rule when the violator acted with "actual knowledge or knowledge fairly implied on the basis of objective circumstances." The amount in 16 CFR § 1.98 is $53,088 per violation, as adjusted effective 17 January 2025 (90 FR 5581). The figure is adjusted each year, so a reader who needs the current number should check the regulation. A rule is what turns a deception theory into a penalty theory, which is why the next section matters.

### 3.2 The Commission's rule on reviews, testimonials and fake indicators

The Commission announced its final rule on fake reviews and testimonials on 14 August 2024 and published it at 89 FR 68077 on 22 August 2024. It took effect 60 days after publication, which by my count is 21 October 2024. It is codified at 16 CFR Part 465 under the Commission's rulemaking authority in 15 U.S.C. § 57a.

Table 3.1 lists the sections of Part 465.

**Table 3.1.** Sections of 16 CFR Part 465 and their subjects

| Section | Subject |
| --- | --- |
| 465.1 | Definitions |
| 465.2 | Fake or false consumer reviews, consumer testimonials, or celebrity testimonials |
| 465.3 | Reserved |
| 465.4 | Buying positive or negative consumer reviews |
| 465.5 | Insider consumer reviews and consumer testimonials |
| 465.6 | Company-controlled review websites or entities |
| 465.7 | Review suppression |
| 465.8 | Misuse of fake indicators of social media influence |
| 465.9 | Severability |

Section 465.8 is the provision that fits engagement pods most directly. It declares it "an unfair or deceptive act or practice and a violation of this part for anyone" to do either of two things. Paragraph (a) covers selling or distributing, and paragraph (b) covers buying or procuring, "fake indicators of social media influence" that the person "knew or should have known to be fake." The indicators must be ones that individuals or businesses could use to misrepresent their influence or importance materially, for a commercial purpose.

The definitions do much of the work. Section 465.1(j) defines indicators of social media influence as "any metrics used by the public to make assessments of an individual's or entity's social media influence," with followers, likes, views, shares and comments as examples. Section 465.1(h) defines a fake indicator as one generated by bots or by "purported individual accounts not associated with a real individual." The definition also reaches hijacked accounts, and it ends with a clause that matters a great deal for pods: metrics that "otherwise do not reflect a real individual's or entity's activities, opinions, findings, or experiences."

That last clause leaves three questions open, and I know of no decision that answers any of them. The first is whether a like clicked by a real person, because a pod tool prompted that person to click, "reflects" that person's activity or opinion. The second is who "sells or distributes" the indicator: the tool operator, the member who agrees to engage, or both. The third is what counts as a commercial purpose when a post promotes the author's own business, a job search or a personal brand. I read the clause as broad enough to reach engagement that no one chose freely, but that is a legal interpretation of untested text (INFERENCE).

Two neighboring sections matter in some chapters. Section 465.2 bars a business from writing, creating or selling fake or false reviews or testimonials (paragraph (a) has no knowledge qualifier), from buying or disseminating them when it knew or should have known (paragraph (b)), and from procuring reviews from insiders for posting on third-party sites (paragraph (c)). Section 465.5 requires officers and managers to disclose their material relationship to the business when they write a review. For testimonials, it covers anyone in the workforce, including employees and agents, when the business knew or should have known of the relationship.

The data show the scale of the activity the rule describes, and nothing more. In the 65 export files, 54,377 of 56,363 records (96.5%) reached the state "finishedEngagement," and the median post drew 278 engaged users (VERIFIED). The files do not show whether those users were real people, bots or hijacked accounts, so they cannot show whether any indicator was "fake" in the rule's sense.

### 3.3 The Endorsement Guides

The Guides Concerning the Use of Endorsements and Testimonials in Advertising, 16 CFR Part 255, were last revised at 88 FR 48102 on 26 July 2023. They are administrative interpretations of Section 5 and, in the Guides' own words, "provide the basis for voluntary compliance with the law." Conduct inconsistent with them "may result in corrective action by the Commission under section 5" (16 CFR § 255.0). The Guides are therefore not a statute, and the Commission enforces them through Section 5.

Section 255.0 defines an endorsement as any advertising, marketing or promotional message that consumers are likely to believe "reflects the opinions, beliefs, findings, or experiences of a party other than the sponsoring advertiser." Section 255.5 requires disclosure of a material connection between an endorser and an advertiser when the connection is "not reasonably expected by the audience," and it applies where "a significant minority of the audience" would not understand or expect it. Free or discounted products count as connections, and the Guides tell advertisers to have procedures to monitor what endorsers post.

Reciprocity is the connection that matters here. A pod member who comments on a post is often promised comments on their own posts in return. Whether that exchange is a "material connection" is unsettled, and whether a bare like is an endorsement at all is doubtful. A comment that praises a product, written because the pod required a comment, is closer to the line: it appears to state the member's opinion while the member is, in effect, being paid in engagement (INFERENCE).

### 3.4 The Lanham Act and private suits

The Commission is not the only enforcer. Section 43(a)(1)(B) of the Lanham Act, 15 U.S.C. § 1125(a)(1)(B), creates a civil action for false or misleading commercial advertising and promotion, and it lets "any person who believes that he or she is or is likely to be damaged" sue. The Supreme Court narrowed that language in *Lexmark International, Inc. v. Static Control Components, Inc.*, 572 U.S. 118 (decided 25 March 2014). A plaintiff must allege an injury to a commercial interest in reputation or sales, and must show that the injury was proximately caused by the deception, which for false advertising means that the deception caused consumers to withhold trade from the plaintiff. Competitors can sue under this test. Consumers generally cannot.

Consumers have a different route in state law. New York's General Business Law § 349(h), for example, lets a person injured by a deceptive practice bring an action (VERIFIED in my earlier research for this project). I do not survey the other states, which have comparable statutes of varying scope.

### 3.5 Contract and computer-access law

Platform terms are contracts, and courts enforce them. In *hiQ Labs v. LinkedIn*, the district court found in a ruling of 4 November 2022 that hiQ had breached LinkedIn's User Agreement through scraping and the creation of fake accounts, subject to hiQ's equitable defenses. On 6 December 2022 the parties filed a stipulated consent judgment that, as summarized by the law firm Proskauer, entered a $500,000 judgment against hiQ, permanently barred scraping that violates the User Agreement and the use of fake accounts, and required deletion of the member data and code. The stipulation's conclusions were agreed by the parties, so they carry no precedential weight.

LinkedIn has also sued sellers of fake engagement. In *LinkedIn Corp. v. TopSocial24 et al.*, No. 5:23-cv-00110 (N.D. Cal., filed 10 January 2023), it pleaded breach of contract, fraud and deceit, and intentional interference with contract. A trade report of 4 January 2024 says a final judgment of $43,086 in damages and a ban on selling fake followers, likes and comments was ordered on 23 October 2023, and does not say whether any money was collected (STATED). The defendants sold fake engagement through fake accounts. They were not pod operators, and I do not treat the case as a precedent for pods.

The Computer Fraud and Abuse Act is the criminal and civil statute for unauthorized access. Section 1030(a)(2)(C) reaches whoever obtains "information from any protected computer" after accessing it without authorization or by exceeding authorized access. The statute defines "exceeds authorized access" as accessing a computer with authorization and using that access "to obtain or alter information in the computer that the accesser is not entitled so to obtain or alter" (§ 1030(e)(6)). A first offense under the basic provision carries up to one year in prison, rising to five years with aggravating factors such as commercial advantage or information worth more than $5,000, and a person harmed can sue for damages if the conduct meets one of the statutory factors, within two years (§ 1030(c)(2), (g)).

*Van Buren v. United States* (decided 3 June 2021, 6–3, opinion by Justice Barrett) held that a person exceeds authorized access when they reach areas of a computer that are off limits to them, and not when they misuse information they were entitled to see. The Court called this a "gates-up-or-down inquiry" and, in footnote 8, left open whether the gates must be technological or can also be set by contract or policy. In *hiQ*, the Ninth Circuit held in April 2022 that scraping public LinkedIn profiles even after access was revoked raised "at least serious questions" about whether it violates the Act (as summarized by Proskauer).

These cases bear on pods in two ways. A tool that uses a member's own session with the member's consent is operating inside a gate the member opened, and the dispute there is about contract. Someone who captured another member's session cookie and used it to log in would be passing a gate that was never opened to them, which is the paradigm of unauthorized access (INFERENCE). The LEMPOD exposure I reported was a vulnerability, not an exploitation, and nothing in the data shows that anyone exploited it. I could not open LinkedIn's User Agreement, because the site blocks my fetch tool, so I do not quote its wording on automation. What I cite is the court's finding in *hiQ*.

### 3.6 Who is exposed

Table 3.2 sets out the actors and the rules most likely to reach each.

**Table 3.2.** Actors in the framework, what they do, and the rules most likely to reach them

| Actor | What they do | Rules most likely to reach them |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Author who submits a post | Lists a post for boosting and publishes it | 16 CFR 465.8(b) if the engagement is fake and the purpose commercial; Section 5; Endorsement Guides; platform contract |
| Pod tool operator | Runs the exchange and, in some tools, holds member access | 16 CFR 465.8(a); platform contract; computer-access law; breach-notification and data-protection law where credentials leak |
| Engaging member | Likes or comments because the tool prompts it | Platform contract; Endorsement Guides where a comment praises a product |
| Competitor | Loses sales to a boosted rival | Lanham Act § 43(a)(1)(B) |
| Viewer or buyer | Relies on the count | State consumer-protection statutes; Section 5 is enforced by the Commission, not by the viewer |

Nothing in this table says that any particular actor broke any rule. It says which rule would be examined first.

### 3.7 Why the stakes reach across generations

The rules above were written to protect consumers, and the consumers who will live longest with their effects are young. The U.S. Surgeon General's advisory *Social Media and Youth Mental Health* reports that up to 95% of young people aged 13 to 17 use a social media platform, that nearly two thirds of teenagers use it every day, and that about a third use it "almost constantly." It concludes: "We cannot conclude that social media is sufficiently safe for children and adolescents." The Committee on the Rights of the Child adopted General Comment No. 25 on children's rights in the digital environment, published 2 March 2021; I could not retrieve its text in this research, so I cite its existence and date only.

My argument connects those facts to pods in three steps, and I label the connection as inference. Visible counts are among the signals that young people use to judge who is credible, successful or worth following. If the counts are manufactured, the signal teaches the wrong lesson at the age when people form their ideas about what success looks like. And a legal answer to the three open questions in section 3.2 will be set by the first cases, then applied to every cohort that follows. I have no data on how teenagers read engagement counts, and the 273,771 posts say nothing about their audience. The claim rests on the Surgeon General's usage figures and on my reasoning, and each topic chapter returns to it with evidence specific to its subject.

### 3.8 What the common framework means for mental health

The rules in this chapter protect people from false signals, and the mental-health harm best documented for people who are misled is the harm that follows fraud.

I found no study of how fake indicators of social proof affect mental health. The nearest evidence concerns fraud in general. Button, Lewis and Tapley (2014, *Security Journal*) interviewed 30 fraud victims face to face and nearly 800 by telephone. Their abstract lists psychological effects among the impacts and says that impact varied widely, from little or none to "devastating impacts." The Identity Theft Resource Center's 2025 Consumer Impact Report, supported by Experian, found that 14.4% of the victims it contacted who answered its survey said they had seriously considered self-harm. About 4% of those contacted answered, so the figure describes the people who chose to respond, and the report concerns identity crime and not engagement fraud.

The scale of loss on social media is large. The FTC reported on 27 April 2026 that people lost $2.1 billion in 2025 to scams that began on social media, eight times the 2020 figure, and that nearly 30% of the people who reported losing money to a scam said it began there. Investment scams were $1.1 billion of the total.

The American Psychological Association's 2023 advisory points to a protective channel. It advises adolescents to limit social media use for social comparison, and it recommends social media literacy, including learning how images and content can be manipulated. I read that as support for the view that understanding how signals are produced protects a reader (INFERENCE). The provisions I read in this chapter do not mention mental health.

## Chapter 4. Technology and innovation: substantiation, AI washing and securities disclosure

### 4.1 The topic in the data

Technology and innovation is the largest of the twenty topics, with 5,201 posts, which is 8.0% of the 64,970 placed posts. The podawaa file supplies 3,866 of them (74.3%), the 65 exports 897 (17.2%), HyperClapper 417 (8.0%) and LinkBoost-2025 21 (0.4%).

Keyword counts show what kind of statements these posts make. Each count is of posts that contain the term, and one post can appear in several counts. A total of 988 posts (19.0%) mention AI or artificial intelligence. A total of 423 (8.1%) use words such as "revolutionary," "breakthrough," "game-changing," "cutting-edge" or "state of the art." Eighty (1.5%) describe something as AI-powered, AI-driven or AI-enabled, and 29 (0.6%) state a multiple such as "10x." A percentage figure appears in 164 posts (3.2%), a currency amount in 104 (2.0%) and a mention of a patent in 53 (1.0%). Only 7 posts use the word "guarantee." A link appears in 1,238 posts (23.8%), and 2,398 posts (46.1%) carry five or more hashtags. These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and the keyword lists are mine, so each count measures a word and not a claim.

Most posts in this topic describe or promote technology in general terms. A minority contain a figure or a statement about what a product does, and the legal attention below concentrates on that minority.

### 4.2 The laws that apply

Four bodies of law matter most for technology claims: the Commission's substantiation doctrine, the securities rules on advisers and issuers, criminal fraud statutes, and, for European audiences, the transparency article of the EU AI Act. The Commission's rule on fake indicators (Chapter 3) applies on top of all of them.

**Advertising substantiation under Section 5.** The Commission's policy statement on substantiation, appended to its decision in *Thompson Medical Co.*, 104 F.T.C. 648 (1984), says that "advertisers and ad agencies have a reasonable basis for advertising claims before they are disseminated." It adds that "a firm's failure to possess and rely upon a reasonable basis for objective claims constitutes an unfair and deceptive act or practice." How much support counts as reasonable depends on the type of claim, the product, the consequences of a false claim, the benefit of a truthful claim, the cost of developing the support, and the amount of support that experts in the field consider reasonable. Where an advertisement states a level of support, such as "tests prove," the firm is expected to have at least that level. A statement that a tool is "10x faster" is an objective claim that can be tested. A statement that a product is "revolutionary" is closer to opinion. The Commission's 1983 Policy Statement on Deception says that the Commission generally will not pursue "obviously exaggerated or puffing representations" (footnote 42) and that opinions are not pursued when consumers understand their source and limits (footnote 40). It also says that "some exaggerated claims, however, may be taken seriously by consumers and are actionable," and that an opinion is actionable if it is not honestly held or if the recipient reasonably reads it as an implied statement of fact (footnote 41). The line between puffery and a claim that needs support therefore turns on how consumers read the statement, which is a question of fact.

The Commission applied this doctrine to AI claims on 25 September 2024, when it announced five cases under the name Operation AI Comply. In *DoNotPay*, the Commission alleged that a service marketed as "the world's first robot lawyer" had not tested whether its chatbot matched a human lawyer's output and employed no attorneys. The proposed settlement required a $193,000 payment, notice to subscribers about the service's limits, and a bar on unsupported claims that the service can substitute for professional services. Three other cases involved income schemes that advertised AI-powered tools (Chapter 14), and the fifth involved a review-writing tool (Chapter 12). The Chair at the time said: "The FTC's enforcement actions make clear that there is no AI exemption from the laws on the books." The press release reports the cases at the stage of a proposed settlement or a temporary court order, and I did not check how the litigated cases ended.

**Securities disclosure by advisers.** On 18 March 2024 the Securities and Exchange Commission announced settled actions against two investment advisers for what its Chair called "AI washing." Delphia (USA) Inc. had said in its filings, in a press release and on its website that it used AI and machine learning that incorporated client data, including a claim that its AI could "predict which companies and trends are about to make it big." The Commission found that Delphia lacked those capabilities. Global Predictions Inc. had called itself the "first regulated AI financial advisor" and had claimed "\[e\]xpert AI-driven forecasts" on its website and social media. Both were charged under the Investment Advisers Act and its Marketing Rule, which bars a registered adviser from disseminating advertisements that contain untrue statements of material fact. The civil penalties were $225,000 and $175,000, a total of $400,000. Each firm was censured and ordered to cease and desist, and neither admitted or denied the findings. The Chair said: "Investment advisers should not mislead the public by saying they are using an AI model when they are not."

**Securities disclosure by issuers.** On 14 January 2025 the Commission issued a cease-and-desist order against Presto Automation Inc., a restaurant-technology company that was listed on Nasdaq until September 2024. The order found that from November 2021 to May 2023 the AI speech recognition in all deployed units of its Presto Voice product was owned and operated by a third party, and that Presto did not disclose this. It found that Presto falsely claimed its own version of the product "eliminated the need for human order-taking" when the vast majority of drive-thru orders needed human intervention, and that Presto misleadingly reported its rate of orders completed without human intervention. The Commission found violations of Section 17(a)(2) of the Securities Act of 1933, Section 13(a) of the Securities Exchange Act of 1934, and Rules 13a-11 and 13a-15(a). It imposed no civil penalty and cited the company's cooperation and remedial efforts. Presto neither admitted nor denied the findings.

**Criminal fraud.** On 9 April 2025 the U.S. Attorney's Office for the Southern District of New York announced charges against the former chief executive of Nate, Inc.: one count of securities fraud and one count of wire fraud, each carrying a maximum of 20 years in prison. The release alleges that he told investors and the public that the company's app used proprietary AI to complete online purchases without human intervention, while hundreds of contractors in a call center in the Philippines completed the purchases by hand, and that the company raised more than $40 million from investors. The release calls the charges "merely accusations" and says the defendant is presumed innocent. I found no reported outcome. The wire fraud statute, 18 U.S.C. § 1343, covers anyone who has "devised or intend\[s\] to devise any scheme or artifice to defraud" and who transmits writings or signals by wire in interstate commerce "for the purpose of executing" it. The maximum is 20 years, or 30 years and a fine of up to $1,000,000 when the offense affects a financial institution or involves a declared disaster or emergency.

**The EU AI Act.** Article 50 of Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 sets transparency duties. Providers must design AI systems that interact directly with people so that the people are told they are dealing with an AI system, unless that is obvious to a person who is "reasonably well-informed, observant and circumspect." Providers of systems that generate synthetic audio, image, video or text must mark the output in a machine-readable format as artificially generated. Deployers must disclose deep fakes, and they must disclose AI-generated text published to inform the public on matters of public interest, unless the text has been through human review or editorial control and someone holds editorial responsibility. A law-firm alert dated August 2026 reports that these duties became generally applicable and enforceable on 2 August 2026, that providers of generative systems already on the market have until 2 December 2026 to meet the marking duty under Regulation (EU) 2026/1744, and that breaches carry fines of up to €15 million or 3% of worldwide annual turnover, whichever is higher (Article 99(4)). I read the Article's text on an unofficial mirror and took the dates and the fine from the alert, so these points are CORROBORATED and not VERIFIED against the Official Journal.

### 4.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

The rules reach each actor by a different route, and boosting changes none of the substantive tests.

The author is the party who must have a reasonable basis for a claim. A pod does not change what the claim says, so the substantiation question is the same for a boosted post and an unboosted one. A pod can change how far the claim travels and how credible it looks, and both bear on materiality and on the consequences-of-a-false-claim factor in the substantiation policy (INFERENCE). Authors that are public companies or registered advisers also face the securities rules. The Delphia and Global Predictions orders concerned statements on websites and social media, so a post is an ordinary place for a statement that the securities rules reach. If the author also bought or procured engagement that the author knew or should have known was fake, 16 CFR § 465.8(b) applies as a second and separate problem, because the post could be both an unsupported claim and a manufactured sign of popularity.

The tool operator is within § 465.8(a) if the operator sells or distributes fake indicators. The operator's position under the AI Act depends on facts that the data do not show. If a pod tool generates comments with AI, it may be a system that generates synthetic text and so fall under Article 50(2) for users in the EU, but I hold no evidence that any tool in these four sources has such a feature, so I treat this as a question and not a finding. The Commission's Rytr case, discussed in Chapter 12, was brought against the supplier of a tool, although the Commission set that order aside in December 2025.

An engaging member has little exposure under these rules in the ordinary case. A member who writes a comment that praises a technology product because the pod expects a comment comes closer to the line described in Section 3.3, where an endorsement is paid for in reciprocal engagement. The platform is not the speaker under any of the rules in this chapter, and Chapter 10 covers its separate position.

Table 4.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on technology and innovation, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 4.1.** Rules that reach posts on technology and innovation, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy or penalty stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| FTC Act § 5 and the substantiation policy | Authors of objective claims; in *Rytr*, the supplier of the tool (order set aside in December 2025; Chapter 12) | Operation AI Comply, 25 Sept 2024 | $193,000 payment in *DoNotPay*; court orders halting schemes and appointing receivers |
| Advisers Act and Marketing Rule | Registered investment advisers | Delphia and Global Predictions, 18 Mar 2024 | $225,000 and $175,000; censure; cease and desist |
| Securities Act § 17(a)(2); Exchange Act § 13(a) | Issuers | Presto Automation, 14 Jan 2025 | Cease and desist; no civil penalty |
| Securities fraud and 18 U.S.C. § 1343 | Anyone who devises a scheme to defraud | Charges against Nate's former CEO, 9 Apr 2025 | Up to 20 years per count; allegations only |
| EU AI Act, Article 50 | Providers and deployers of covered systems | None found | Up to €15 million or 3% of turnover (CORROBORATED) |

### 4.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

Young people meet technology claims in the same feeds as everyone else, and they are among the heaviest users of the tools those claims describe. The Pew Research Center surveyed 1,458 U.S. teens aged 13 to 17 between 25 September and 9 October 2025, with a margin of error of 3.3 percentage points. It found that 64% of teens use AI chatbots and that about three in ten use them daily. A total of 57% had used a chatbot to search for information, 54% for help with schoolwork and 12% for emotional support or advice. Parents underestimated the use: 51% of parents said their teen uses chatbots, 13 points below the teens' own figure. Among teens who expect negative effects on society, 34% cited overreliance or loss of critical thinking and 13% cited misinformation or difficulty telling what is real.

A teenager who asks a chatbot for information relies on a system whose abilities were described by someone with a stake in the description. The enforcement cases show that the gap between a capability claim and the system can be wide. The Presto order found that, for a version marketed as eliminating human order-taking, the vast majority of orders needed a person, and the Nate indictment alleges an automation rate of effectively zero. Both are individual cases chosen by regulators because they were deceptive, and they say nothing about the typical post in this topic. What they establish is what the law treats as a violation: a statement about what a technology does without human help must be true, and the person making it must be able to support it. My inference is that this standard protects the ability of buyers to judge what tools can do, and that for a generation that uses these tools for schoolwork and, in 12% of cases, for emotional advice, that judgment is part of how they learn to rely on a tool and how far (INFERENCE).

The law also protects young people as investors, though less directly. Delphia and Global Predictions were advisers whose clients relied on claims about AI forecasting. Young savers who use advisers or investment apps meet the same kind of claim, and the Marketing Rule applies to the adviser regardless of the client's age (INFERENCE from the rule's text as described in the press release; I did not open the rule itself).

The one rule I checked that speaks to the interaction itself is Article 50(1) of the AI Act, which requires that a person be told they are dealing with an AI system unless that is obvious to a reasonably well-informed, observant and circumspect person. The sentence I read does not mention age, so whether a teenager would find the fact obvious is left to regulators and courts. I did not survey U.S. state laws on chatbot disclosure.

The baseline is young. The enforcement standard for AI claims was first applied in 2024 and 2025, and the questions that matter for pods have no answer yet: whether a boosted claim is judged differently from an unboosted one, and whether the operator of a boosting tool bears responsibility for the claims it carries. The first decisions will set the baseline for the cohorts now in school.

### 4.5 How this topic bears on mental health

The mental-health evidence that fits this topic concerns work and teenagers' use of AI, and none of it tests a technology claim.

AI appears in 988 of the 5,201 posts (19.0%), so the topic is a place where people read about tools that may change their work. The American Psychological Association's 2025 Work in America survey, run by the Harris Poll with 2,017 employed U.S. adults between 26 March and 4 April 2025, found that 54% said job insecurity had significantly affected their stress at work. Workers whose employers had been affected by policy changes reported emotional exhaustion more often (27% to 30%) than workers whose employers had not (18%). The page I read gives no figure on worry about AI in particular, so I claim none.

For teenagers, Section 4.4 reports that 12% used chatbots for emotional support or advice. The association's June 2025 health advisory on AI and adolescent well-being says that "research on its impacts is still developing" and that "AI is not all 'good' or 'bad.'" It adds that adolescents may struggle to tell simulated empathy from real understanding.

I found no study of whether an overstated capability claim harms mental health. The link I propose is narrow (INFERENCE). A person who believes that a tool will replace their work, or that a tool can give them emotional support, will make choices about work or relationships on that belief, and the substantiation rules in Section 4.2 bear on whether the belief is accurate.

### 4.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show that any claim in these 5,201 posts is false. They hold text and engagement fields. I tested no product and checked no claim against a product. The 988 posts that mention AI include general commentary, and the 80 posts that call something AI-powered are the group where a capability claim is most explicit, but I did not judge any of them true or false.

The data cannot show who wrote the posts or in what capacity. A post could come from a public company, a registered adviser, a founder or an individual with an opinion, and the securities rules apply only to the first two. I cannot identify that subset without naming authors, which I do not do.

The data cannot show whether engagement on any of these posts was fake in the sense of § 465.8, or whether a boost changed what anyone believed. The enforcement examples were chosen because each reached an order or a charge, and I have no denominator, so they show what the rules reach and not how often. I did not check developments after the dates of the sources I opened, except where I say so.

## Chapter 5. Events, community and networking: ticket pricing, "free" offers and scarcity claims

### 5.1 The topic in the data

Events, community and networking is the second-largest topic, with 4,444 posts, which is 6.8% of the 64,970 placed posts. The podawaa file supplies 3,190 of them (71.8%), the 65 exports 766 (17.2%), HyperClapper 468 (10.5%) and LinkBoost-2025 20 (under 0.5%).

The posts mostly invite people to an event or describe one. A total of 814 posts (18.3%) mention a conference, summit, expo or forum, and 644 (14.5%) mention a speaker, keynote or panel. The word "networking" appears in 521 (11.7%), "join us" or "join me" in 496 (11.2%), and "register" or "registration" in 476 (10.7%). A venue or in-person attendance is mentioned in 243 (5.5%), tickets in 223 (5.0%), a webinar in 162 (3.6%) and a sponsor in 151 (3.4%). A prompt to reserve a place ("RSVP," "save," "reserve," "book" or "secure your seat or spot") appears in 189 posts (4.3%).

Three kinds of wording matter for the law. The word "free" appears in 285 posts (6.4%), and 238 (5.4%) pair it with an event word such as register, webinar, event or workshop. Scarcity or deadline wording ("limited seats," "spots left," "sold out," "last chance" or "early bird") appears in 119 posts (2.7%). Prices are rare: only 15 posts (0.3%) pair a mention of tickets with a currency amount, which is 6.7% of the 223 posts that mention tickets. A link appears in 1,437 posts (32.3%). These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts a word and not a claim.

Most posts in this topic invite and describe, and few state a price. That limits where the pricing rules in this chapter can reach.

### 5.2 The laws that apply

Four rule sets matter for event posts beyond those in Chapter 3: the Commission's rule on fees, the statute on ticket-buying software, the guide on "free" offers, and the rules on false scarcity.

**The FTC rule on unfair or deceptive fees (16 CFR Part 464).** The Commission published the rule at 90 Fed. Reg. 2066 on 10 January 2025 (FR Doc. 2024-30293), and it took effect on 12 May 2025. It covers live-event tickets and short-term lodging. The Commission's staff FAQ says it applies to any business that offers, displays or advertises these, "including third-party platforms, resellers, and travel agents," and that it covers business-to-business transactions. Section 464.2(a) makes it "an unfair and deceptive practice and a violation of this part" "for any business to offer, display, or advertise any price of a covered good or service" "without clearly and conspicuously disclosing the total price." Section 464.2(b) requires the total price to be disclosed "more prominently than any other pricing information," with an allowance for the final amount of payment. Section 464.2(c) requires a business to disclose, before the consumer agrees to pay, the nature, purpose and amount of any fee excluded from the total price and the final amount of payment. A law-firm alert quotes the rule as defining total price as "the maximum total of all fees or charges a consumer must pay" and lists the exclusions as government charges, shipping charges and charges for optional goods or services. The FAQ gives an example of a misrepresentation: advertising tickets at a price when none are available. It says violators could be ordered to comply, refund consumers and pay civil penalties, and it states no amount. Section 3.1 gives the general per-violation figure for violations of a trade regulation rule. I read the rule text of § 464.2 on a mirror of the Code of Federal Regulations and the rest from the FAQ and the alert, and I did not open the definition of "live-event ticket" in § 464.1. For that reason I do not say whether a conference registration is a covered ticket.

**The BOTS Act (15 U.S.C. § 45c).** The Act makes it unlawful to "circumvent a security measure, access control system, or other technological control or measure" that a ticket issuer uses to enforce posted purchasing limits or order rules, and to sell or offer a ticket obtained that way if the seller took part in the circumvention or had the ability to control it, or knew or should have known how the ticket was obtained. A violation "shall be treated as a violation of a rule defining an unfair or a deceptive act or practice," the Commission enforces it, and a state attorney general may sue on behalf of residents. The leading example is the complaint the Commission and seven states filed on 17 September 2025 against Live Nation and Ticketmaster, which cites Section 5(a) and the BOTS Act. As summarized by a law firm, it alleges that displayed prices left out mandatory fees that raised the checkout cost by 24% to 44% and totaled $6.4 billion from 2019 to 2024; that Ticketmaster misstated the maximum number of tickets a buyer could purchase; and that five brokers controlled 6,345 accounts and held 246,407 tickets to 2,594 events. These are allegations, the summary reports no ruling, and the conduct alleged came before Part 464 took effect. I did not open the complaint, so the figures are STATED.

**The Guide on "free" (16 CFR Part 251).** Section 251.1 says that offers of "free" goods "must be made with extreme care so as to avoid any possibility that consumers will be misled or deceived," that a purchaser "has a right to believe that the merchant will not directly and immediately recover" the cost of the free item through other means, such as marking up the item that must be bought, and that the conditions should be "set forth clearly and conspicuously at the outset of the offer." The section sits in Subchapter B of Title 16 under the title "The guide." I did not open a statement of its legal force, so I treat it as the Commission's statement of how it reads Section 5. In the European Union, point 20 of Annex I to the Unfair Commercial Practices Directive (2005/29/EC) lists "describing a product as 'gratis', 'free', 'without charge' or similar" when the consumer has to pay anything beyond the unavoidable cost of responding and of collecting or paying for delivery.

**False scarcity and urgency.** A statement that seats are nearly gone is an objective claim, so the deception and substantiation standards in Chapters 3 and 4 apply to it. In the European Union, point 7 of Annex I lists "falsely stating that a product will only be available for a very limited time, or that it will only be available on particular terms for a very limited time, in order to elicit an immediate decision and deprive consumers of sufficient opportunity or time to make an informed choice." The Annex is headed "commercial practices which are in all circumstances considered unfair," so I read it as listing these practices without a case-by-case assessment of effect (INFERENCE from the heading; I did not open Article 5 of the Directive).
