The text I read is the 2005 text as hosted by the UK National Archives, and I did not check later amendments.

**Endorsements by speakers, sponsors and attendees.** Section 3.3 sets out the rule on material connections. A speaker who is paid or given free access and praises the event, a sponsor, and an attendee whose praise was prompted by a pod all raise the question in 16 CFR § 255.5. An audience probably expects a speaker to promote the speaker's own session, so disclosure may not be needed for that (INFERENCE). Part 465 applies to fake testimonials, such as praise for an event by someone who did not attend.

### 5.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

The duty in each rule attaches to what a post says or shows, and boosting adds an effect on how credible that content looks.

The author may be an organizer, a speaker, a sponsor or an attendee. For an organizer, Part 464 attaches to a price. The FAQ says that a business that includes pricing in its advertising must tell people upfront the total price, so a post that shows no price does not raise the question by itself, and 208 of the 223 ticket posts show no currency amount. The question then moves to the page the post links to, and I did not open any linked page. Free-offer and scarcity statements are a separate matter because they can appear without a price. A statement of "only 12 seats left" is the organizer's claim, and the organizer needs support for it. A pod can raise how many people see that statement and how many likes appear beside it, and a scarcity claim next to a high like count may read as proof of demand (INFERENCE). If the organizer bought or procured the engagement and knew or should have known it was fake, § 465.8(b) applies in addition.

The tool operator is within § 465.8(a) if the operator sells or distributes fake indicators. The BOTS Act does not reach pods, because it concerns circumventing ticket purchase controls, and nothing in the posts or in the tool records I hold involves buying tickets. I describe the Act because the Ticketmaster complaint is the leading enforcement example for ticket rules.

An engaging member who comments "great event" because the pod expects a comment has the exposure described in Section 3.3. Whether a social platform that displays an organizer's price is a business that "displays" it under § 464.2 is a question the text I read does not settle, and I treat it as open (INFERENCE).

Table 5.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on events, community and networking, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 5.1.** Rules that reach posts on events, community and networking, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 16 CFR Part 464 | Any business that offers, displays or advertises live-event tickets or short-term lodging | None found under Part 464 itself | Compliance orders, refunds and civil penalties, with no amount in the FAQ |
| BOTS Act, 15 U.S.C. § 45c | Anyone who circumvents ticket purchase controls or sells tickets so obtained | FTC and seven states against Live Nation and Ticketmaster, 17 Sept 2025 | Penalties under the FTC Act; allegations only |
| 16 CFR Part 251 | Advertisers of "free" offers | None found | Not stated |
| Annex I, points 7 and 20, Directive 2005/29/EC | Traders in the EU | None searched | Set by Member State law; not checked |

### 5.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

Event and networking posts invite people into rooms, and the U.S. Surgeon General's 2023 advisory on loneliness and isolation treats the time young people spend in rooms with each other as a public-health matter. The advisory says that time spent in person with friends by people aged 15 to 24 "has reduced by nearly 70% over almost two decades," from roughly 150 minutes a day in 2003 to 40 minutes a day in 2020. It says that "approximately half of U.S. adults report experiencing loneliness, with some of the highest rates among young adults." On technology it says the evidence is complex and that "we have reason to be concerned about the impact of some kinds of technology use," including technology that "displaces in-person engagement, monopolizes our attention, reduces the quality of our interactions." The advisory does not mention engagement pods, and I do not claim that it addresses them.

The link to this chapter's rules is one of cost and time. A young person choosing which in-person events to spend limited money and time on relies on popularity signals, scarcity messages and prices. A hidden fee takes money. A false scarcity claim compresses time, and the Directive's own text names that harm: it deprives consumers of "sufficient opportunity or time to make an informed choice." As an illustration, the 24% to 44% increase that the Ticketmaster complaint alleges would add $12 to $22 to a displayed price of $50. That arithmetic is mine and shows scale, and the complaint's figures are allegations (INFERENCE).

Whether prompted, reciprocal engagement adds to in-person connection or substitutes for it is unknown. A pod gives its members the experience of an audience, and the advisory's concern about technology that lowers the quality of interactions makes the question worth asking. I have no evidence that bears on the answer, and I list it as an open research question.

The baseline is young here as well. Part 464 took effect in May 2025, so people now buying their first tickets do so under the first federal rule that requires a total price for live events. How the rule is applied to promotion on social platforms will be set by the first cases.

### 5.5 How this topic bears on mental health

Events bear on mental health through two channels in the sources I opened: loneliness, which in-person gatherings can reduce, and fear of missing out, which scarcity wording can use.

The topic invites people to rooms. The word "networking" appears in 521 of 4,444 posts (11.7%), and a venue or in-person attendance in 243 (5.5%). The Surgeon General's 2023 advisory says that "approximately half of U.S. adults report experiencing loneliness," that the odds of developing depression are more than double among people who often feel lonely, and that time spent in person with friends by people aged 15 to 24 fell from roughly 150 minutes a day in 2003 to 40 minutes in 2020. A real event that brings people together is the kind of in-person time that the advisory describes as scarce.

The second channel is scarcity. Przybylski and colleagues (2013, *Computers in Human Behavior*) define fear of missing out as "a pervasive apprehension that others might be having rewarding experiences from which one is absent." Across three studies of about 3,179 people, it was associated with lower mood (b = .20) and lower life satisfaction (b = .17). The studies are correlational and rely on self-report. Scarcity or deadline wording appears in 119 posts (2.7%), and "free" paired with an event word in 238 (5.4%). I found no study that links such wording to fear of missing out, so the connection is INFERENCE, and the rules in Section 5.2 address misleading offers and do not address mental health.

### 5.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show that any event post is false or deceptive. I checked no event page, price or seat count. Only 15 posts pair tickets with a price, so the posts themselves rarely give the information that Part 464 concerns.

The data cannot show who wrote a post or in what role. An organizer, a speaker, a sponsor and an attendee can write similar sentences, and the rules reach them differently. Scarcity wording appears in 119 posts, but the data show the wording and not whether any seats were in fact limited.

The data cannot show whether boosting an event post sold a single ticket, because I hold no sales data. I did not open the definition of a live-event ticket in § 464.1, so I make no claim that conference registrations are covered. The Ticketmaster figures are allegations from a law-firm summary, and I did not check developments after the complaint was filed.

## Chapter 6. Leadership, teams and trust: insider endorsements, executive disclosure and employee account privacy

### 6.1 The topic in the data

Leadership, teams and trust is the third-largest topic, with 4,019 posts, which is 6.2% of the 64,970 placed posts. The podawaa file supplies 1,647 of them (41.0%), the 65 exports 1,354 (33.7%), HyperClapper 910 (22.6%) and LinkBoost-2025 108 (2.7%). It is the topic where the smaller sources contribute most: 108 is the largest number of posts LinkBoost-2025 supplies to any of the twenty topics, and 910 is the second largest for HyperClapper.

The posts give advice about leading people. The word "leader" or "leadership" appears in 3,386 posts (84.2%) and "team" in 1,389 (34.6%). "Trust" appears in 597 (14.9%), "CEO" or "executive" in 412 (10.3%), "manager" or "management" in 373 (9.3%), "employee," "staff" or "workforce" in 249 (6.2%), "culture" in 245 (6.1%) and "feedback" in 119 (3.0%). Employment events are less common: hiring or recruiting appears in 123 posts (3.1%), layoffs, firing, resigning, quitting or burnout in 93 (2.3%), a union or labor in 34 (0.8%), and pay, salary, wages or compensation in 4. A link appears in 806 posts (20.1%), five or more hashtags in 963 (24.0%), and an invitation to comment or follow in 78 (1.9%). These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts a word and not a claim.

Most posts in this topic are opinion and advice from the author's own position. Few state a fact about a product, a company's results or an employment term. That decides which rules can reach them.

### 6.2 The laws that apply

Four rule sets matter here: the insider provisions of the fake-reviews rule, the Endorsement Guides as they apply to employees, Regulation FD as applied to an officer's social media, and state laws on employer access to employees' personal accounts.

**Insider reviews and testimonials (16 CFR § 465.5).** Section 465.5(a) makes it an unfair or deceptive practice for an officer or manager of a business to write or create a consumer review about the business or its products without disclosing, clearly and conspicuously, the officer's or manager's material relationship to the business. Section 465.5(b)(1) applies to testimonials. When a business disseminates a consumer testimonial from one of its officers, managers, employees or agents, the relationship must be disclosed if the business knew or should have known of it. Section 465.5(b)(2) excludes generalized solicitations to purchasers to post testimonials and the mere hosting of reviews. Section 465.5(c) addresses solicitation inside the business: an officer or manager who solicits reviews from immediate relatives or from employees or agents violates the rule when the officer or manager encourages non-disclosure, fails to instruct disclosure, or knows that a review appeared without disclosure and does not take remedial steps. The definitions in § 465.1 narrow the reach. "Officers include owners, executives, and managing members of a business," and a manager is "an employee of a business who supervises other employees or agents." A consumer review is an evaluation of a product, service or business "published to a website or platform dedicated in whole or in part to receiving and displaying such evaluations," which a general social network is probably not (INFERENCE). A consumer testimonial is "an advertising or promotional message" that consumers are likely to believe reflects the opinions or experiences of a consumer "who has purchased, used, or otherwise had experience with a product, service, or business." All of this is VERIFIED against the text on the Cornell Legal Information Institute site.

**The Endorsement Guides and employees.** Section 3.3 describes 16 CFR § 255.5, which asks for disclosure of a connection between an endorser and an advertiser that the audience would not reasonably expect, and which tells advertisers to have procedures to monitor what their endorsers post. An employee who praises the employer's product has such a connection. The Guides are interpretations enforced through Section 5, so their force is that of the Commission's reading of the statute.

**Regulation FD and an officer's personal posts (17 CFR § 243.100).** Regulation FD applies "whenever an issuer, or any person acting on its behalf, discloses any material nonpublic information regarding that issuer or its securities" to certain people: broker-dealers, investment advisers and institutional investment managers, investment companies, and holders of the issuer's securities where it is "reasonably foreseeable that the person will purchase or sell the issuer's securities on the basis of the information." The issuer must then make public disclosure simultaneously if the disclosure was intentional and promptly if it was not. The rule excludes disclosures to people who owe a duty of trust or confidence to the issuer or who expressly agree to keep the information confidential. On 2 April 2013 the Securities and Exchange Commission issued a report of investigation (Release No. 34-69279) about Netflix and its chief executive. On 3 July 2012 he had announced on his personal Facebook page that monthly viewing had passed one billion hours for the first time, with no press release, no Form 8-K and no post on the company's channels. The page had more than 200,000 subscribers, including analysts, shareholders, reporters and bloggers. The Commission said that social media communications need the same Regulation FD analysis as other channels, that social media can be a recognized channel of distribution only if the company has first alerted investors to the channels it will use, and that disclosure of material nonpublic information on an individual officer's personal social media site is unlikely to satisfy the rule without that notice. It decided "not to pursue an enforcement action in this matter." The report is an interpretation and not a rule, and I opened the text of § 243.100 but not the rest of Regulation FD.

**State laws on employer access to personal accounts.** The National Conference of State Legislatures reports that "twenty-seven states have enacted laws that apply to employers" on the privacy of employees' social media accounts, although its table totals 26 states and Guam. The summary was updated on 8 August 2022, and legislators began introducing these bills in 2012. A 2015 article by the Society for Human Resource Management listed the earliest laws, from 2012, in California, Illinois, Maryland and Michigan. It reported that the laws typically bar an employer from requiring or requesting usernames or passwords. Some also bar requiring an employee to log in to an account in the employer's presence, adding the employer as a contact or changing privacy settings, making account access a condition of employment, and disciplining an employee who refuses. It reported exceptions for workplace investigations in several states. I read neither the statutes nor any penalty provisions, and the 2015 list has since changed, so the details are CORROBORATED at best and the count is as of 2022.

### 6.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

The rules reach a small part of the topic through the content of the posts and a larger part through the way engagement is produced.

The author is usually an executive or manager. The posts are mostly opinion, so the testimonial provisions apply only to the posts that praise a product as a user would, and Regulation FD applies only to an officer of a public company who discloses material nonpublic information. The data do not show who the authors are, so I cannot say how many fall in either group. The engagement is a separate question. If an author bought or procured engagement that the author knew or should have known was fake, § 465.8(b) applies to the extent the purpose was commercial, and for executive thought leadership that question is open (Section 3.2). A boosted post by a public-company officer is also a wider distribution of whatever the post says, and the Netflix report shows that the Commission looks at the audience a personal page reaches (INFERENCE).

Employer-directed engagement raises three questions. First, if a company asks its employees to like and share an executive's posts, § 465.5(c) reaches the solicitation only when the posts are consumer reviews, which a general social post probably is not. Second, if a pod tool needs an employee's account session and the employer tells the employee to install or hand over access, the request is the kind that the state account-access laws describe, in the states that have them (INFERENCE; I read no statute). An employee who joins voluntarily is outside those laws as I understand them. Third, the like of an employee who was told to give it comes from a real person but may not reflect that person's opinion, and whether it is a "fake indicator" under § 465.1(h) is the open question in Section 3.2.

The tool operator is within § 465.8(a) if the operator sells or distributes fake indicators, and the operator's handling of member credentials is a data-security matter I take up in Chapter 8. The platform is not the speaker.

Table 6.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on leadership, teams and trust, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 6.1.** Rules that reach posts on leadership, teams and trust, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 16 CFR § 465.5 | Officers and managers; businesses that disseminate insider testimonials | None found | Not stated in the text I read |
| 16 CFR § 255.5 | Advertisers and endorsers, including employees | None searched | Corrective action under Section 5 |
| Regulation FD, 17 CFR § 243.100 | Issuers and persons acting on their behalf | Netflix and its chief executive, report of 2 Apr 2013 | No enforcement action taken |
| State account-access laws | Employers in the states that have enacted them | None searched | Not stated in the sources |

### 6.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

Leadership posts are the place where many young people first see what leading looks like, and the people those posts are meant to describe report low engagement in their own work. Gallup's 2025 global workplace release reports that employee engagement was 21% in 2024, down from 23% in 2023, and that manager engagement fell from 30% to 27%. Engagement among managers under 35 fell by five percentage points, though the release does not state the levels. Gallup estimates the cost at US$438 billion in lost productivity in 2024. The figures are global and say nothing about LinkedIn or pods.

My inference is that published examples of leadership shape what a young manager treats as normal, and that when reciprocal engagement and not the audience's response chooses which examples travel, the sample that a young manager sees is not the sample readers would have chosen (INFERENCE). I know of no study that measures this, and the Gallup release does not address it.

The account-access laws bear on young workers more directly. A first-time employee has the least bargaining power over a request to hand over access to a personal account. In the states that have laws on this, the request is limited. NCSL counts 27 such states as of 2022, and I did not check whether the remaining states or federal law offer an equivalent protection. A young worker who joins an employer-run engagement program therefore has a statutory shield in some places and none that I verified in others.

The Regulation FD report touches a different group: young investors who follow executives on social media. The Commission's statement that social media can be a recognized channel only if investors have been told in advance protects the person who reads a chief executive's page and does not know that it is the company's official channel (INFERENCE from the report's reasoning).

### 6.5 How this topic bears on mental health

Leadership posts bear on mental health through the workplace, and the Surgeon General's workplace framework is the clearest official statement of how.

The topic is about leading people. The word "leader" or "leadership" appears in 3,386 of 4,019 posts (84.2%), "team" in 1,389 (34.6%), and layoffs, firing, resigning, quitting or burnout in 93 (2.3%). The 2022 Surgeon General's Framework for Workplace Mental Health and Well-Being lists five essentials: protection from harm, connection and community, work-life harmony, mattering at work and opportunity for growth. It says that "Long work hours have also been shown to raise workers' risk for exhaustion, anxiety, and depression," and that "Knowing you matter has been shown to lower stress, while feeling like you do not can raise the risk for depression." Advice about recognition, feedback and trust concerns factors that the framework names.

Employer-directed engagement raises a narrower question, which is whether an employee asked to promote an executive's posts is under pressure. I found no source on that. The nearest is the association's 2023 Work in America survey of 2,515 U.S. workers, in which 51% said they knew their employer monitors them with technology. Monitored workers more often rated their mental health fair or poor (32% against 24%), said they typically felt tense or stressed during the workday (56% against about 40%) and said they intended to look for a new job within 12 months (42% against 23%). The report shows correlations, and I do not claim that monitoring caused the differences. A request to engage with an employer's posts is not monitoring, so this evidence bears on the setting and not on the request (INFERENCE).

### 6.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show who wrote any post or what role the author held, so they cannot show how many posts come from officers or managers, from public-company officers, or from employees speaking about their employer. They cannot show that any post breaks any rule. The posts hold almost no pay or employment terms (4 posts mention pay), so employment-terms law has little textual footing in this topic.

The data cannot show whether any employer directed its employees to engage with a post, or whether any pod tool took an employee's account access at an employer's request. The state counts are from a 2022 summary and a 2015 article, I read no state statute, and the Gallup figures are global and from a press release. The Netflix report concerns one post from 2012 and a Commission decision not to act, so it shows how the Commission read the rule and not how often it enforces it.

## Chapter 7. Apps, software and no-code tools: subscriptions, app reviews, children's data and state privacy law

### 7.1 The topic in the data

Apps, software and no-code tools is the fourth-largest topic, with 3,903 posts, which is 6.0% of the 64,970 placed posts. The podawaa file supplies 2,674 of them (68.5%), HyperClapper 643 (16.5%), the 65 exports 579 (14.8%) and LinkBoost-2025 7 (0.2%).

The posts describe tools and what they do. The word "app" appears in 1,908 posts (48.9%), and productivity, automation or workflow in 872 (22.3%). A total of 539 posts (13.8%) mention no-code or low-code tools, and 89 (2.3%) name a platform such as Zapier, Airtable, Notion or Make. AI appears in 741 posts (19.0%). A launch, release, update or new feature is mentioned in 440 (11.3%), software or SaaS in 397 (10.2%), and downloading or installing in 322 (8.3%). A browser extension or plug-in is mentioned in 24 (0.6%).

Three groups of words matter for the law. The word "free" appears in 314 posts (8.0%), and 58 (1.5%) pair it with a trial, plan, tier, freemium or "forever." Terms of payment are rare: subscription, pricing or a plan appears in 159 posts (4.1%), a monthly price in 12 (0.3%), "no credit card" in 5 and "cancel anytime" in 4. Reviews are mentioned in 210 posts (5.4%), but only 17 (0.4%) ask the reader to leave a review or rating. Children and privacy are also rare: privacy, data protection, GDPR or CCPA appears in 86 posts (2.2%), and kids, children, teens, students or school in 59 (1.5%). A link appears in 1,267 posts (32.5%), and 115 (2.9%) pair a link with a download or install prompt. These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts a word and not a claim.

The posts promote tools but seldom state the terms on which the tool is sold, so the subscription rules mostly attach to the sign-up page behind the post.

### 7.2 The laws that apply

Four rule sets matter beyond Chapter 3: the statute on online subscriptions, the fake-reviews rule as applied to app reviews, the children's privacy rule, and California's privacy statute. A fifth concern, the security of the credentials that software holds, is taken up in Chapter 8.

**The Restore Online Shoppers' Confidence Act (15 U.S.C. § 8403).** The Act makes it unlawful "for any person to charge or attempt to charge any consumer for any goods or services sold in a transaction effected on the Internet through a negative option feature" unless the person does three things. The person must provide "text that clearly and conspicuously discloses all material terms of the transaction before obtaining the consumer's billing information." The person must obtain "express informed consent before charging" the consumer's account. And the person must provide "simple mechanisms for a consumer to stop recurring charges." I opened the text of § 8403 and not the enforcement section that follows it. On 25 September 2025 the Commission announced a settlement with Amazon over Prime. According to a law-firm summary, the settlement totals $2.5 billion, made up of a $1 billion civil penalty and $1.5 billion in consumer refunds. The Commission alleged that Amazon used dark patterns to enroll consumers and made cancellation difficult. It cited Section 4 of the Act and Section 5 of the FTC Act. The summary lists injunctive terms that include clear disclosure of material terms before charging, a plain option to decline membership, and a simpler cancellation process. It reports that the complaint had been amended in September 2023 to add three individual executives and that Amazon and two of them agreed to the settlement. It does not say that Amazon admitted wrongdoing. I did not open the Commission's release, so these details are STATED.

The Commission's 2024 amendments to its Negative Option Rule, known as the click-to-cancel rule, have been vacated. On 8 July 2025 the Eighth Circuit issued a per curiam opinion in *Custom Communications, Inc. v. FTC*, No. 24-3137, and vacated the rule in its entirety, days before the rule's 14 July 2025 compliance date. According to a law-firm alert, the court held that the Commission had skipped the preliminary regulatory analysis required for rules with an estimated annual economic effect of at least $100 million, and it did not reach the substantive challenges. The alert lists what remains: the Act, Section 5, and state auto-renewal laws such as those of California and New York. A second alert reports that on 30 January 2026 the Commission announced that it had sent a draft advance notice of proposed rulemaking on negative option plans to the Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs, with no proposed rule yet. I did not check developments after that alert.

**The fake-reviews rule and app reviews (16 CFR Part 465).** Section 465.1(d) defines a consumer review as a consumer's or purported consumer's evaluation of a product, service or business that is published "to a website or platform dedicated in whole or in part to receiving and displaying such evaluations." An app store is such a platform, although the text does not name app stores (INFERENCE). Section 465.2 bars writing, creating or selling fake reviews, and bars buying or disseminating them when the business knew or should have known they were fake. Section 465.4 covers buying reviews, and Chapter 3 lists the other sections. A review posted on an app store by an account that is not a real user, or by a person paid for a particular sentiment, is the situation the rule was written for.

**The children's privacy rule (16 CFR Part 312).** The amended COPPA Rule was published on 22 April 2025 and took effect on 23 June 2025. Full compliance was due on 22 April 2026, so it now applies to all operators. According to a law-firm alert, the amendments require separate parental consent before an operator discloses a child's personal information to a third party (§ 312.5(a)(2)), and the Commission said that disclosures for money, advertising or the development or training of AI are never integral to a service. Operators may keep a child's personal information only as long as "reasonably necessary" for its purposes (§ 312.10), and indefinite retention is prohibited. Operators must publish a written retention policy and keep a written security program for children's data. The definition of personal information now includes biometric identifiers and genetic data. In the text of § 312.2, a child is "an individual under the age of 13." An operator is a person who runs a website or online service for commercial purposes and collects or maintains personal information from its users. The Commission weighs factors such as subject matter, visual content, animated characters and child-oriented activities to decide whether a service is directed to children. A mixed-audience service is not treated as directed to children for any visitor not identified as under 13. The sources I read state no penalty amount.

**California's privacy statute (the CCPA).** The California Privacy Protection Agency's inflation adjustment, effective 1 January 2025, sets administrative fines of up to $2,663 per violation and up to $7,988 for each intentional violation and for violations involving the personal information of consumers the violator actually knows are under 16. It sets statutory damages in the private action for data breaches at $107 to $799 per consumer per incident, or actual damages if greater. The same page puts one of the coverage thresholds, annual gross revenue, at $26,625,000. I did not read the other thresholds, so a small software operator may fall outside the statute. I found no enforcement example in the sources I opened.

### 7.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

The rules reach the product that a post advertises more often than the post itself, and boosting affects how many people arrive at the product.

The author is usually a developer, a marketer or a user describing a tool. A claim about what the tool does needs support under the standard in Chapter 4, and "free" claims fall under the Guide described in Chapter 5. The subscription statute governs the transaction, not the post. Because the posts state a monthly price in 12 cases and a cancellation term in 4, the terms that the statute concerns appear on the sign-up page, which I did not open. A pod can raise the number of people who click through to that page, and the Amazon allegations show that the sign-up and cancellation steps are where the Commission looks (INFERENCE). Only 17 posts ask readers to leave a review, so the review rule reaches this topic through the apps the posts promote and not through the posts.

The tool operator is a software operator. A pod tool is a piece of software that holds member information, and the privacy and breach rules in this chapter and in Chapter 8 apply to it according to their coverage thresholds. Children's privacy is unlikely to apply to a tool built for professionals on a professional network, because the Rule applies to services directed to children or with actual knowledge of child users (INFERENCE; I did not open the statute). The engaging member and the platform have no exposure under these rules in the ordinary case.

Table 7.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on apps, software and no-code tools, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 7.1.** Rules that reach posts on apps, software and no-code tools, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy or penalty stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| ROSCA, 15 U.S.C. § 8403 | Any person who charges for an online sale through a negative option feature | Amazon Prime settlement, 25 Sept 2025 | $1 billion civil penalty and $1.5 billion in refunds (STATED) |
| 2024 click-to-cancel amendments to the Negative Option Rule | Vacated in full on 8 July 2025 | *Custom Communications v. FTC* | Not applicable |
| 16 CFR §§ 465.1, 465.2, 465.4 | Businesses that write, buy, sell or disseminate fake reviews | None found for app reviews | Civil penalties for rule violations (Section 3.1) |
| COPPA Rule, 16 CFR Part 312 | Operators of services directed to children, or with knowledge of child users | None searched | Not stated in the sources |
| CCPA | Businesses that meet a coverage threshold | None found | Up to $2,663 per violation; up to $7,988 for intentional violations and minors under 16 |

### 7.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

The rules in this chapter protect a person's money and a person's data, and for children the data rules look furthest ahead. A child's personal information collected today can be kept for decades unless the law limits it. The amended COPPA Rule limits retention to what is "reasonably necessary," bars indefinite retention, and requires separate consent before a child's data is disclosed for advertising or for developing AI. California sets a higher fine ceiling when the violator knows the consumer is under 16, $7,988 against $2,663, and I read that as a legislative judgment that minors need more protection (INFERENCE). Both rules bear on the cohort that is now young, because these records follow them into adulthood.

The topic's own posts rarely concern children. Only 59 of 3,903 mention kids, children, teens, students or school, so the children's rules matter less for what the posts say than for the products they advertise.

Subscription practices affect young people differently. A person with little experience of recurring charges is more exposed to a hidden renewal, though I have no data on the age of the people in the Amazon matter, and the Commission's allegation is that it involved "millions of consumers" (INFERENCE as to age). The absence of a national click-to-cancel rule means that, for now, protection rests on the Act, Section 5 and state auto-renewal laws, so what a young subscriber can do about a hard-to-cancel app depends in part on the state where the subscriber lives (INFERENCE from the alert's list of California and New York).

For reviews, the effect on young people is indirect. App-store ratings are one signal a young person uses to choose an app, and the review rule protects the honesty of that signal. I found no study that measures how young people use ratings, and the data hold no ratings.

### 7.5 How this topic bears on mental health

The mental-health evidence that fits apps concerns design features and time online, and it shows small average effects that may be larger for some young people.

The topic promotes tools. The word "app" appears in 1,908 of 3,903 posts (48.9%), and kids, children, teens, students or school in 59 (1.5%). The American Psychological Association's 2023 advisory names the like button, recommended content, unrestricted time and endless scrolling as features to tailor to adolescents' developmental abilities, and it says that social media is "not inherently beneficial or harmful to young people." The Surgeon General's advisory page says that children and adolescents who spend more than 3 hours a day on social media "face double the risk of mental health problems including experiencing symptoms of depression and anxiety," and that the evidence leaves the Surgeon General unable to conclude that social media is "sufficiently safe for children and adolescents."

Orben and Przybylski (2019, *Nature Human Behaviour*) analyzed three datasets with 355,358 participants and found that digital technology use explained at most 0.4% of the variation in adolescent well-being. They concluded that the effects were too small to warrant policy change. The two sources measure different things, and an average can hide a larger effect on some young people, a point the association's advisory makes when it says that effects depend on adolescents' own characteristics.

I found no source that links subscription terms, sign-up practices or app-review rules to mental health. Section 7.2 governs those practices as consumer deception and as children's data protection, and I claim no mental-health effect for them.

### 7.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show whether any app named in a post uses a subscription, how its sign-up and cancellation steps work, or whether it collects children's data. I opened no sign-up page and no privacy policy. The posts state few terms of payment, so I cannot say whether any promoted product meets the statute's disclosure and consent conditions.

The data cannot show whether any review of any app was fake, because the data hold no reviews. The Amazon figures come from a law-firm summary, and I did not open the Commission's release or the complaint. The status of the click-to-cancel effort is as of the January 2026 alert. I read only one threshold of the California statute, and I found no enforcement example for it.

## Chapter 8. Sales, leads and email outreach: commercial messages, contact data and the credentials tools hold

### 8.1 The topic in the data

Sales, leads and email outreach is the fifth-largest topic, with 3,673 posts, which is 5.7% of the 64,970 placed posts. The podawaa file supplies 2,167 of them (59.0%), the 65 exports 913 (24.9%), HyperClapper 553 (15.1%) and LinkBoost-2025 40 (1.1%).

The posts concern how to reach people. The word "email" appears in 2,011 posts (54.8%), "lead" or "leads" in 1,315 (35.8%), and "sales" or "selling" in 1,013 (27.6%). Cold email, outreach or prospecting appears in 616 (16.8%), and scraping, automation tools, Sales Navigator or lead generation in 403 (11.0%). Measures of performance such as ROI, conversion, reply rate, open rate or response rate appear in 344 (9.4%), a percentage figure in 425 (11.6%), a currency amount in 170 (4.6%), and revenue or pipeline in 239 (6.5%). Booking a call, meeting or demo appears in 110 (3.0%), and a phone, text message, SMS, cold call or phone number in 142 (3.9%). Contact lists or databases appear in 94 (2.6%) and buying leads or lists in 22 (0.6%). Unsubscribe, opt-out or consent appears in 42 (1.1%), and GDPR, CCPA, privacy or compliance in 77 (2.1%).

One count points to private channels. A call to "DM me," "inbox me" or "message me" appears in 120 posts in this topic (3.3%). Across all 273,771 distinct posts the same wording appears in 3,262 (1.19%). A link appears in 775 posts (21.1%), and the word "guarantee" in 23 (0.6%). These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts a word and not a claim.

This topic is about the mechanics of reaching people, so much of its legal exposure concerns how contact data is collected and used and how messages are sent, and less concerns what a post says.

### 8.2 The laws that apply

Five bodies of law matter here: the federal statute on commercial email, the federal statute on calls and texts, European data protection law on marketing and contact data, the case law on scraping and platform contracts, and the statutes on breaches of credentials.

**The CAN-SPAM Act.** The Commission's compliance guide lists eight requirements for commercial email. A sender must not use false or misleading header information or deceptive subject lines. The sender must identify the message as an advertisement, include a valid physical postal address, and tell recipients how to opt out. The guide adds that subscribers and members can opt out too, that opt-outs must be honored "within 10 business days," and that a company must "monitor what others are doing on your behalf." It states that "each separate email in violation of the CAN-SPAM Act is subject to penalties of up to $53,088," a figure that is adjusted for inflation. It states that "the law makes no exception for business-to-business email." It also says that "both the company whose product is promoted in the message and the company that actually sends the message" may be held legally responsible, and that hiring another company to send does not transfer the responsibility. I opened the guide and not the statute, so these points are VERIFIED as the Commission's statement and not against 15 U.S.C. § 7701 and the sections that follow. I did not determine whether a LinkedIn direct message is an "electronic mail message" under the Act.

**The Telephone Consumer Protection Act (47 U.S.C. § 227).** Section 227(b)(1)(A) prohibits calls "using any automatic telephone dialing system or an artificial or prerecorded voice" to telephone numbers assigned to cellular service, with exceptions. Section 227(a)(1) defines an automatic telephone dialing system as "equipment which has the capacity" "to store or produce telephone numbers to be called, using a random or sequential number generator" and "to dial such numbers." Under § 227(b)(3), a person may bring an action "to receive $500 in damages for each such violation," and a court that finds a willful or knowing violation may increase the award to not more than three times that amount. The text I read speaks of calls, and I did not open the agency interpretation that treats text messages as calls or any later court decision on the definition.

**European data protection law and contact data.** Article 21(2) of the GDPR gives a person the right to object at any time to processing of personal data for direct marketing, and Article 21(3) provides that after an objection "the personal data shall no longer be processed for such purposes." The leading enforcement example is a decision of the French authority, the CNIL, adopted on 5 December 2024 and published on 19 December 2024 (SAN-2024-020). It fined KASPR €240,000. KASPR sells a paid Chrome extension that lets customers obtain the professional contact details of people whose LinkedIn profiles they visit, and its database holds about 160 million contacts. The CNIL found a breach of Article 6 because contact details were collected even where users had limited their visibility to first- and second-degree connections. It found a breach of Article 5(1)(e) because data was kept for five years from each update. It found breaches of Articles 12 and 14 because people were told only from 2022, about four years after the extension launched, and by an email in English. It found a breach of Article 15 because the company answered an access request with a generic reference to "publicly accessible sources." It ordered KASPR to stop collecting the data of people who had limited their visibility, to delete data collected that way, to stop the automatic renewal of retention periods, to inform people in a language they understand, and to answer access requests in full. The compliance deadline was 18 June 2025. The page states no payment for non-compliance. The European Data Protection Board's news item on the same decision carries a different figure in its headline (€200,000), which I did not open.

**Scraping and platform contracts.** Section 3.5 describes *hiQ Labs v. LinkedIn* and the Computer Fraud and Abuse Act. A second decision points the other way on contract. On 23 January 2024 the court in *Meta Platforms v. Bright Data* (N.D. Cal., No. 3:23-cv-00077-EMC, Judge Edward Chen) granted summary judgment to Bright Data on Meta's breach-of-contract claim. According to a law-firm summary, the court read Meta's terms as governing "your use" of the services and concluded that Bright Data "did not 'use' Facebook and Instagram" when it scraped public data while logged off. Meta's claim for tortious interference remained, and I did not check what followed. The two decisions answer different questions from the CNIL decision. The American cases concern whether a platform's contract or the access statute bars the collecting, and the CNIL decision concerns whether the resulting use of personal data has a legal basis, notice and limits.

**Breaches of credentials.** All 50 states, the District of Columbia, Guam, Puerto Rico and the Virgin Islands have laws that require private businesses, and in most states government bodies, to notify individuals of breaches of personally identifiable information, according to the National Conference of State Legislatures (summary updated 17 January 2022). The page gives examples such as a name combined with a Social Security number, a driver's license number or an account number, and it does not mention usernames or passwords, so it does not tell me whether a session cookie counts. Under the GDPR, Articles 33 and 34 require a personal-data breach to be notified to the supervisory authority and, in specified cases, to the people affected. I opened those Articles in earlier work for this project and did not reopen them for this chapter.

### 8.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

The rules reach the sending, the collecting and the storing, and a pod bears on all three through the offers it makes more visible.

The author of most posts in this topic is describing sales practice, and the posts are not the outreach. An author who also sends commercial email is the sender under the Commission's guide, and a company that promotes a product through someone else's emails shares the responsibility. A boosted post that offers a service and invites a direct message moves the conversation to a channel outside the public feed. The 3.3% rate of that call in this topic against 1.19% in the full corpus suggests that sales posts lead people into private messages more often than other posts do (INFERENCE), and I have not determined which of the statutes in this chapter, if any, applies to a message sent on LinkedIn.

The tool operator is the actor most exposed. A tool that collects contact data from profiles faces the reasoning in the CNIL decision. A tool that automates activity inside a member's logged-in session faces the contract questions in *hiQ*, where the court found that fake accounts and scraping breached the User Agreement. A tool that stores session credentials faces the breach statutes. In one pod tool that I reported to LinkedIn in 2024, members' LinkedIn session cookies were stored in clear text, and LinkedIn replicated the issue (STATED; my own report). Nothing in the data shows that anyone used those cookies, so the breach statutes would apply only if an unauthorized acquisition occurred, and I do not claim that it did.

An engaging member has no exposure under these rules in the ordinary case. The platform is the plaintiff in the cases in Section 3.5.

Table 8.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on sales, leads and email outreach, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 8.1.** Rules that reach posts on sales, leads and email outreach, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy or penalty stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| CAN-SPAM Act | The sender and the company whose product is promoted | None searched | Up to $53,088 per email |
| TCPA, 47 U.S.C. § 227 | Callers using an automatic dialing system or prerecorded voice | None searched | $500 per violation, up to three times for willful violations |
| GDPR Articles 6, 14, 15, 21 | Controllers of personal data | CNIL against KASPR, 5 Dec 2024 | €240,000 and orders to stop, delete and inform |
| Contract and access law | Scrapers and operators of automation | *hiQ*; *Meta v. Bright Data*; *LinkedIn v. TopSocial24* | $500,000 consent judgment in *hiQ*; summary judgment for the scraper on contract in *Bright Data* |
| State breach laws; GDPR Articles 33 and 34 | Businesses that hold personal information | None searched | Not stated in the sources |

### 8.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

Sales outreach is a path by which strangers reach young people, and the Commission's April 2026 data on social media scams show how much money moves through that path. Reported losses to scams that started on social media reached $2.1 billion in 2025, about eight times the $261 million of 2020, and the Commission notes that most scams go unreported. Investment scams account for $1.1 billion, more than half of the total. The share of loss reports that concerned a scam starting on social media is highest in the youngest group: 40% for people aged 18 to 29, against 32% for those aged 30 to 59, 23% for those aged 70 to 79 and 14% for those aged 80 and over. Those are shares of loss reports in each age group and not the share of each group that lost money. In the category of job or business opportunities, one in three loss reports began on social media. The Commission's page does not break out LinkedIn, and I do not claim that any of these losses began on a pod-boosted post.

My inference is that the combination matters. A young adult looking for a first job or a first business opportunity sees posts in this topic that ask for a direct message, and the move to a private channel removes the public context in which others can see and question an offer (INFERENCE). The rules in this chapter govern the later steps. CAN-SPAM does not exempt business-to-business email, so a young professional's work address has the same protection as anyone's. The GDPR gives a person the right to object to marketing use of data collected without their knowledge.

Contact data creates a second long-term exposure. The CNIL found that KASPR kept data for five years from each update and informed people about four years after its extension launched. A person who starts a career today may enter databases like that without knowing it, and the right of access and the right to object are the tools the law gives them. The practical value of those rights depends on a person knowing the data exists, and the CNIL's finding on late notice shows why the notice rules matter.

The baseline is still being set. The decisions in *hiQ* and *Bright Data* have not settled whether a platform's contract can reach logged-off scraping. The first appellate answer will decide what data about young professionals can be collected at scale from public profiles in the United States.

### 8.5 How this topic bears on mental health

The mental-health evidence that fits outreach concerns people whose personal information is misused, because victims of identity crime report distress.

The topic is about reaching people. The word "email" appears in 2,011 of 3,673 posts (54.8%), cold email, outreach or prospecting in 616 (16.8%), and scraping, automation tools, Sales Navigator or lead generation in 403 (11.0%). Section 8.2 describes the rules on commercial messages, contact data and the credentials that tools hold, and those rules aim at the misuse of personal information (INFERENCE). The Identity Theft Resource Center's 2025 Consumer Impact Report, supported by Experian and published on 28 October 2025, surveyed people who had asked the Center for help between August 2024 and July 2025. Of the victims who answered, 14.4% said they had seriously considered self-harm, which the report describes as more than two percentage points above the 2024 figure. About 4% of the 4,122 people contacted answered, so the figure describes respondents and not all victims. The survey concerns identity crime. It does not concern cold outreach or scraping, and I do not claim that those practices cause identity crime.

On the sender's side I found no source on how outbound sales targets affect mental health, and I opened none on how recipients experience unsolicited messages. The 120 posts that ask readers to "DM me," "inbox me" or "message me" (3.3%) move contact into private channels, where the data show nothing about what follows.

### 8.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show that any post in this topic is itself outreach, or that any author sent a message that fell under any of these statutes. The posts describe practice. They cannot show which tools collect contact data or store credentials, whether any recipient consented, or whether any message was sent to a cell phone with an automatic dialer.

The CNIL decision is one authority's decision about one company, and it does not show how other authorities would treat pod tools. I did not open the CAN-SPAM statute, the agency interpretation of the TCPA or later cases on its dialer definition. I did not determine whether a LinkedIn message is an email under the Act. The Commission's data cover scams reported to it and do not mention LinkedIn, so they cannot show that pods played any role in any loss. The cookie issue I reported is STATED, and the data do not show that anyone exploited it.

## Chapter 9. Money, prices and value: price comparisons, all-in pricing and algorithmic pricing

### 9.1 The topic in the data

Money, prices and value is the sixth-largest topic, with 3,267 posts, which is 5.0% of the 64,970 placed posts. The podawaa file supplies 1,590 of them (48.7%), the 65 exports 1,106 (33.9%), HyperClapper 525 (16.1%) and LinkBoost-2025 46 (1.4%). The posts are long: the median is 683 characters.

The word "value" or "worth" appears in 2,549 posts (78.0%), but it is common in business writing and does not signal a price claim. More specific counts show the content. Cost, spend or save appears in 731 posts (22.4%), and invest, crypto, stock or share terms in 763 (23.4%). Profit, margin, revenue or earn appears in 484 (14.8%), a percentage figure in 399 (12.2%), and a currency amount in 284 (8.7%). Words from personal finance (budget, debt, credit, loan, mortgage, retire, savings) appear in 341 (10.4%). Price or pricing appears in 299 (9.2%), and discount, sale, deal, coupon or promo in 289 (8.8%). Make-money language ("make money," "passive income," "side hustle," "financial freedom," "get rich," "easy money" or "wealth") appears in 198 (6.1%), fees, hidden costs, surcharges or charges in 149 (4.6%), and inflation, tariffs, recession or interest rates in 92 (2.8%). A guarantee, risk-free offer, money-back promise or refund appears in 59 (1.8%). A direct price comparison ("cheaper," "lowest price," "best price," "save N," "N% cheaper") appears in 44 (1.3%), and urgency wording such as "buy now," "limited offer" or "ends today" in 7 (0.2%). A link appears in 521 posts (15.9%). These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts a word and not a claim.

The topic is mostly commentary on money and value, and offers that state a price are a small share. The pricing rules below attach to that share.

### 9.2 The laws that apply

Three rule sets matter beyond Chapter 3: the Commission's guides on price comparisons, state laws that require advertised prices to include mandatory fees, and antitrust law as applied to pricing software.

**The Guides Against Deceptive Pricing (16 CFR Part 233).** Section 233.1(a) says that a former price used in a comparison must be "the actual, bona fide price at which the article was offered to the public on a regular basis" for "a reasonably substantial period of time." If the former price is "not bona fide but fictitious," the advertised reduction is a false bargain, and the section gives the example of an inflated price set so that a large reduction can be advertised when the figure is really the seller's regular price. Section 233.1(e) says that an advertisement that merely states "Sale" must not rest on a reduction that is "so insignificant as to be meaningless." It also treats as misleading a claim that a price has been "reduced to $9.99" when the former price was $10. The text ends with the annotation "\[Guide I\]" and sits in Part 233 among the Commission's guides, so I treat it as the Commission's statement of how it reads Section 5 and not as a rule with its own penalties. I opened the text of § 233.1 and not the other sections of the Part.

**State all-in pricing laws.** California's SB 478 took effect on 1 July 2024 and has since been amended by SB 1524. The Attorney General's page quotes the statute, Civil Code § 1770(a)(29), as barring "advertising, displaying, or offering a price for a good or service that does not include all mandatory fees or charges." It describes the law as a price-transparency law and not a price cap. It covers most goods and services sold or leased for personal use, including event tickets, short-term rentals, hotels and food delivery platforms, and it excludes purchases for commercial use. The advertised price may leave out government taxes and fees and reasonable shipping charges, and handling charges must be included. Optional fees and fees triggered by later conduct need not be included. Restaurants, bars and grocery stores are largely exempt, and a delivery platform's advertised delivery price must be the full price. The page does not describe penalties or private remedies. The federal fee rule in Chapter 5 reaches only live-event tickets and short-term lodging, so for other goods a state statute such as this one is the closer rule where it exists (INFERENCE; I did not survey other states).

**Antitrust law and pricing software.** The Department of Justice sued RealPage, alleging that its revenue-management software used nonpublic, competitively sensitive information shared by landlords to set rents, in violation of Sections 1 and 2 of the Sherman Act. On 24 November 2025 the Antitrust Division announced a proposed settlement. According to a law-firm alert dated 2 December 2025, RealPage may not use active lease data to train its algorithms and may use only historical data at least 12 months old. Its pricing models may not analyze geographies narrower than a state, it may not make identical pricing recommendations to different owners in the same geographic market, and it may not solicit nonpublic data through market surveys to recommend pricing. A court-appointed monitor oversees compliance, the term is seven years with a possible end after four, and the settlement includes no financial penalty and no admission of wrongdoing. The alert says that RealPage and its landlord clients also face class actions and separate suits by the attorneys general of Maryland, New Jersey, Kentucky, Washington, Arizona and the District of Columbia. It says the settlement requires court approval, and I did not check whether the court approved it. I did not open the Sherman Act or the complaint.

### 9.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

The pricing rules reach the seller who states a price, and boosting changes how many people see that statement and how trustworthy it looks.

The author may be a seller, an adviser or a commentator. A seller who posts a comparison such as "was $X, now $Y" must be able to show that $X was a bona fide former price under § 233.1. A seller who posts a savings claim such as "save 30%" needs support under the standard in Chapter 4, and 399 posts here contain a percentage. A seller in California who posts a price must include mandatory fees. Only 44 posts make a direct price comparison and 7 use urgency wording, so these rules reach a small share of the topic. A commentator who describes prices in general states no price of their own and falls outside all three rule sets (INFERENCE). When a post with a price claim is also boosted, the pod raises the visibility of the claim, and a count of likes can suggest that many people found the offer worthwhile (INFERENCE). If the author bought or procured engagement that the author knew or should have known was fake, § 465.8(b) applies as a separate problem.

The tool operator is a seller too. A pod tool that is sold by subscription faces the rules in Chapter 7 on how its own price and renewal terms are shown. The pricing-software cases concern a different kind of tool, one that sets prices for others, and they do not apply to engagement tools. The engaging member and the platform have no exposure under these rules in the ordinary case.

Table 9.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on money, prices and value, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 9.1.** Rules that reach posts on money, prices and value, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy or penalty stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 16 CFR § 233.1 | Sellers who advertise former-price comparisons or "sale" prices | None searched | Not stated; a guide |
| California Civil Code § 1770(a)(29) | Sellers of goods and services for personal use in California | None searched | Not described on the Attorney General's page |
| Sherman Act §§ 1 and 2, as applied to pricing software | Software vendors and the landlords who share data with them | *United States v. RealPage*, proposed settlement 24 Nov 2025 | No payment; data limits; monitor for 7 years (STATED) |

### 9.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

Money rules decide how much of a young person's income goes to the first decade of independent life, and housing is the largest single item. The Joint Center for Housing Studies at Harvard reported in June 2026 that 22.7 million renter households, 49%, were cost-burdened in 2024, a record, and that average asking rents for professionally managed apartments in the first quarter of 2026 were 29% higher than in 2020. These figures come from a summary by the National Low Income Housing Coalition. The summary says that many young adults cannot afford to rent on their own and live with roommates or family, but it gives no numbers for young adults. The RealPage case concerns whether pricing software helped coordinate rents of this kind. The proposed settlement limits the software's use of nonpublic data and carries no payment, and the question of how the law applies to such software is not finished, since class actions and state suits continue. My inference is that the rules on how prices are set and displayed bear most heavily on people with the least income and the fewest alternatives, which describes many people starting out (INFERENCE).

All-in pricing matters in the same way. A buyer with a fixed budget who sees a low advertised price and then meets mandatory fees at checkout has already invested time in the purchase. California's statute and the federal rule in Chapter 5 move the total to the start of the transaction. The protection is partial, because the federal rule covers two industries and the state law covers one state.

Make-money language is the part of this topic that meets young adults most directly. It appears in 198 posts here (6.1%). The Commission's April 2026 data in Chapter 8 report $1.1 billion in reported losses to investment scams that started on social media in 2025, and they report that the share of loss reports involving scams that started on social media was 40% for people aged 18 to 29. I do not claim that any of those losses began on a post in this topic. The link is one of subject: the posts promise income, and the Commission's categories include investment scams and job or business-opportunity scams (INFERENCE).

The baseline is uneven. A young person in a state with an all-in pricing statute is protected at the point of display, and a young person elsewhere relies on Section 5 and the Commission's guide.

### 9.5 How this topic bears on mental health

The best-documented link between money and mental health is the link between debt and distress, and the sources I opened do not say which causes which.

The topic is commentary on money. Personal-finance words (budget, debt, credit, loan, mortgage, retire, savings) appear in 341 of 3,267 posts (10.4%), and make-money language ("make money," "passive income," "side hustle," "financial freedom") in 198 (6.1%). Richardson, Elliott and Roberts (2013, *Clinical Psychology Review*) reviewed studies covering nearly 34,000 participants. According to a University of Southampton release on the study, people in unsecured debt were more than three times as likely to have a mental health problem as people who were not in debt, and under 9% of people without mental health problems were in debt, against more than a quarter of people with one (STATED; I read the release and the repository record, not the paper). The lead author said that "it is hard to say which causes which at this stage."

The pricing rules in this chapter bear on that evidence indirectly (INFERENCE). A deceptive price comparison or a hidden fee costs a buyer money, and for a household near its limit the cost can become debt. I found no study of the mental-health effect of deceptive pricing as such. Fees, hidden costs or surcharges appear in 149 posts (4.6%), and a direct price comparison in 44 (1.3%).

### 9.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show that any price claim in these posts is false. I checked no former price, discount or fee. Most posts in this topic comment on money in general terms, so a count of posts is a count of subject matter and not of claims that the pricing rules reach.

The data cannot show whether any seller in the posts is in California or covered by any statute. I did not open any part of Part 233 beyond § 233.1, the California statute itself, or the RealPage complaint, and I did not check whether the court approved the settlement. The housing figures come from a summary of the Harvard report and not from the report. The scam figures are from the Commission's data in Chapter 8 and say nothing about LinkedIn or pods.

## Chapter 10. LinkedIn, content and social media: fake indicators of influence, platform immunity and platform contracts

### 10.1 The topic in the data

LinkedIn, content and social media is the seventh-largest topic, with 3,266 posts, which is 5.0% of the 64,970 placed posts. The podawaa file supplies 2,255 of them (69.0%), the 65 exports 497 (15.2%), HyperClapper 485 (14.8%) and LinkBoost-2025 29 (0.9%). The median post is 399 characters.

This is the one topic whose posts discuss the thing the pods exist to produce. The word "LinkedIn" appears in 1,743 posts (53.4%). Likes, comments, shares or reposts appear in 1,061 (32.5%), and engagement, reach or impressions in 396 (12.1%). Followers or connections appear in 231 (7.1%), viral or views in 173 (5.3%), and the word "algorithm" in 132 (4.0%). Posting tips or content strategy appear in 199 posts (6.1%), personal brand, thought leader or influencer in 154 (4.7%), and a newsletter, subscribe or follow-me call in 196 (6.0%). Another platform is named (TikTok, Instagram, YouTube, Twitter, Facebook or Threads) in 316 (9.7%), and sponsored, paid-partnership, #ad or ad words appear in 171 (5.2%). A link appears in 592 posts (18.1%), five or more hashtags in 701 (21.5%), and a call to comment or follow in 134 (4.1%). An AI term appears in 255 (7.8%), and words about AI-written content (ChatGPT, AI-generated or GPT) in 82 (2.5%).

Few posts talk about the practices that Section 465.8 addresses. Words for pods, automation, bots or auto-likes appear in 47 posts (1.4%), and language about fake followers or buying followers in 35 (1.1%). These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts words and not claims, so a post that warns against buying followers is counted together with a post that offers them.

The podawaa file records a like count and a view count for each post. In this topic 742 of the 2,255 podawaa posts (32.9%) show zero views. Of the 1,513 with a nonzero view count, 52 (3.4%) show more likes than views. Across all 184,687 distinct podawaa posts the figures are 42.0% and 2.1%. A view count of zero beside a nonzero like count looks like a value that was not recorded (INFERENCE), so I do not use either field as evidence that any indicator is fake.

The 65 export files record how each boost was run in two fields, and Table 10.1 gives the counts. The field engagementType has four values across the 56,363 records: normal in 42,021 (74.6%), human in 8,938 (15.9%), viral in 4,263 (7.6%) and steady in 1,141 (2.0%). The field engageTiming holds a value such as 20, 120 or 300to900, and it is empty in 14,184 records (25.2%). Every record of type normal has a timing value. Of the 14,342 records of the other three types, 14,184 (98.9%) have none. The most common value is 20, in 20,379 records (36.2%), followed by 120 in 6,190 (11.0%) and 300to900 in 3,803 (6.7%). Five values carry the suffix a, such as 300a, and I show them as stored. These counts are VERIFIED against the exports.

The files do not say what the values mean, which unit the numbers use, or whether the type human refers to a person acting. The pattern fits a pacing setting that applies only to normal engagement (INFERENCE). It does not show who or what performed any engagement, and a record in the state finishedEngagement shows that the tool finished its job, not that a person read the post.

### 10.2 The laws that apply

Four rule sets matter beyond Chapter 3: the Commission's rule on fake indicators of social media influence, the federal immunity for online platforms, the European Union's platform and consumer-practices rules, and the platform's own contract.

**The rule on fake indicators of social media influence (16 CFR § 465.8).** Section 465.8 makes it "an unfair or deceptive act or practice and a violation of this part for anyone" to do either of two things. Paragraph (a) covers selling or distributing fake indicators of social media influence that the seller "knew or should have known to be fake" and that "can be used by individuals or businesses to materially misrepresent their influence or importance for a commercial purpose." Paragraph (b) covers purchasing or procuring fake indicators, with the same knowledge standard, that "materially misrepresent their influence or importance for a commercial purpose." The section contains no exception for individuals, so it reaches a person who buys followers as it reaches a company that sells them. I opened the text of §§ 465.8 and 465.1 on the Cornell Legal Information Institute site (VERIFIED). The rule took effect on 21 October 2024, according to the Commission's warning-letter template described below.

Two definitions in § 465.1 carry the section. Paragraph (j) defines indicators of social media influence as "any metrics used by the public to make assessments" of an individual's or entity's influence, and lists followers, friends, connections, subscribers, views, plays, likes, saves, shares, reposts and comments. Paragraph (h) defines fake indicators as those "generated by bots," those from "purported individual accounts not associated with a real individual," those from accounts created with a real individual's personal information without consent, those from hijacked accounts, and those that "otherwise do not reflect a real individual's or entity's activities, opinions, findings, or experiences."

Three questions are open for pod engagement. The first concerns the last clause of paragraph (h). A pod member is usually a real individual with a real account who likes a post because a tool or a message asked. Whether that like "reflects" the member's "activities, opinions, findings, or experiences" depends on how the clause is read. If a like is an activity of the member, the clause is not met. If the clause asks whether the like reflects the member's opinion of the post, a like given without reading the post may not. The Commission's own examples do not settle it. Its press release of 14 August 2024 describes "followers or views generated by a bot or hijacked account," and its warning-letter template of 22 December 2025 gives the same examples under the heading "Misuse of Fake Social Media Indicators." Neither mentions prompted engagement by real people, and I found no Commission statement, order or court decision on it. A search that finds nothing does not show that nothing exists.

The second question is the meaning of "purchase or procure." A member who pays a subscription to a tool that supplies engagement fits the ordinary sense of purchase (INFERENCE). A member who receives engagement in a free exchange for giving it is closer to the word "procure," and my notes on § 465.1 record no definition of either word. I did not search the Commission's guidance for one.

The third question is "for a commercial purpose." Section 465.1 as I read it does not define the phrase. A post that seeks clients or sells a course has a commercial purpose on its face, and a post about a family event does not. In this topic 154 posts (4.7%) use personal-brand, thought-leader or influencer words and 171 (5.2%) use sponsor or ad words. These counts do not show the purpose of any post (INFERENCE).

Enforcement so far consists of letters. Civil penalties for knowing violations of a rule come from 15 U.S.C. § 45(m)(1)(A), as set out in Chapter 3. The Commission's press release says that the rule allows it to seek "civil penalties against knowing violators" and cites *AMG Capital Management v. FTC*; I did not open that decision. The warning-letter template, dated 22 December 2025, lists bullets for §§ 465.2, 465.4, 465.5, 465.6, 465.7 and 465.8, says a court "could impose civil penalties of up to $53,088 per violation," and asks the recipient to email the Commission within five business days to identify the person responsible and describe planned compliance steps (VERIFIED; I opened the template). A law-firm article dated 6 April 2026 reports that the letters went to nearly a dozen companies and calls them the Commission's first public use of the rule as an enforcement tool. It mentions no complaint or settlement and does not say whether the letters cited § 465.8 (STATED). I searched for a complaint or order under § 465.8 and found none.

**Section 230 (47 U.S.C. § 230) and *FTC v. LeadClick Media*.** Section 230(c)(1) says that no provider or user of an interactive computer service "shall be treated as the publisher or speaker" of information "provided by another information content provider." Section 230(c)(2) protects "any action voluntarily taken in good faith to restrict access to or availability of material" that the provider or user considers "obscene, lewd, lascivious, filthy, excessively violent, harassing, or otherwise objectionable." Section 230(f)(3) defines an information content provider as a person "responsible, in whole or in part, for the creation or development of information." Section 230(e)(1) says the section does not impair enforcement of the listed obscenity and child-exploitation provisions "or any other Federal criminal statute," and § 230(e)(3) preserves state law "consistent with this section" and bars liability under inconsistent state law (VERIFIED; I opened the statute on Cornell). The text does not mention federal civil enforcement, and I found no decision on whether Section 230 limits Commission action over indicators of influence.

The one appellate decision I reviewed that applies Section 230 in a Commission case is *FTC v. LeadClick Media*, decided by the Second Circuit on 23 September 2016. I read a commentary by Eric Goldman and not the opinion (STATED). LeadClick managed an affiliate network for a weight-loss seller, and affiliates promoted the products through fake news sites with fabricated articles and fake consumer testimonials. According to the commentary, LeadClick knew of the practice and approved of it, sometimes supplied content for the fake pages, and bought advertisements on legitimate news sites to promote them. The court held that LeadClick was not entitled to immunity because it "participated in the development of the deceptive content," that its role "far exceeded that of neutral assistance," and that a defendant is liable under Section 5 when, knowing of the deception, it "directly participates in a deceptive scheme" or "has the authority to control the deceptive content at issue." The commentary adds that the court called it doubtful that LeadClick was an interactive computer service but did not decide that point, and it criticizes the court for leaving "development" and "neutral assistance" undefined. The decision shows that immunity depends on what the defendant did with the content. It does not say how a court would treat a tool that organizes likes.

**European Union: the Digital Services Act and the Unfair Commercial Practices Directive.** The Commission's page on designated platforms lists LinkedIn Ireland Unlimited Company, with the entry "25.04.2023: designation" as a very large online platform and logged-in active users of 45.2, which I read as millions (INFERENCE; I did not open the DSA article on designation). Article 34(1) of the Digital Services Act lists four categories of systemic risk: the dissemination of illegal content; negative effects on the exercise of fundamental rights; negative effects on civic discourse, electoral processes and public security; and negative effects in relation to gender-based violence, the protection of public health and minors, and serious negative consequences for physical and mental well-being. Article 34(2) says the assessment must take account of the design of recommender systems, content moderation systems, terms and conditions and their enforcement, advertising systems and data practices, and must examine "intentional manipulation of their service, including by inauthentic use or automated exploitation of the service." Article 35(1) requires "reasonable, proportionate and effective mitigation measures, tailored to the specific systemic risks." Its list includes adapting the design, features or functioning of the service, adapting terms and conditions and their enforcement, testing and adapting recommender systems, and "targeted measures to protect the rights of the child, including age verification and parental control tools." I read Articles 34 and 35 on an unofficial mirror of the Regulation and not in the Official Journal. I did not open Article 52 on penalties, and I could not open LinkedIn's published risk assessments, because the files redirect to a host that I was unable to fetch. These Articles bind the platform and not authors or tool operators. My inference is that pod activity is one form of "inauthentic use or automated exploitation," and that a platform in LinkedIn's position has to consider it in its assessment. The Articles do not mention pods or engagement metrics (INFERENCE).

The Commission's page on the Unfair Commercial Practices Directive lists Directive (EU) 2019/2161 as an amendment that applied from 28 May 2022 (VERIFIED). A web page on influencer marketing and the Commission's own information-request message on an Italian draft law about fake reviews describe two points added to Annex I. Point 23b prohibits stating that reviews of a product are submitted by consumers who have actually used or purchased it without reasonable and proportionate steps to check.
