Point 23c prohibits "submitting, or commissioning another legal or natural person to submit, false consumer reviews or endorsements," and "misrepresenting consumer reviews or social media endorsements, in order to promote products." The exact wording of point 23c comes from the web page (STATED), and the Commission's message corroborates the substance of both points (CORROBORATED). I could not read the consolidated text of the Directive, because the page returned no text. The web page says nothing about fake followers or likes. A law firm's article advises businesses to treat submitting "likes" as within the ban, which is advice and not law. As in Chapter 5, I read the Annex heading as listing practices considered unfair in all circumstances (INFERENCE from the heading). Whether a bought or prompted like is a "social media endorsement" is open (INFERENCE). The Directive protects consumers against traders' practices, and I did not check how it applies to content addressed to business readers, who make up much of LinkedIn's audience. As far as I know, the Directive on misleading and comparative advertising covers advertising between businesses, and I did not open it.

**The platform contract.** In Chapter 3 I reported *LinkedIn Corp. v. TopSocial24* (N.D. Cal., filed 10 January 2023, claims of breach of contract, fraud and deceit, and intentional interference) and the hiQ result (a breach of the User Agreement found on 4 November 2022, and a consent judgment of $500,000 with a permanent injunction on 6 December 2022). A trade report records a final judgment of $43,086 and a ban in the TopSocial24 case (STATED; I did not open the docket). I could not read LinkedIn's User Agreement, because the site disallows automated readers, so I cannot quote its terms. Social Media Today reported on 20 August 2025 that LinkedIn's help documentation says that if LinkedIn detects "excessive comment creation or use of an automation tool, we may limit the visibility of those comments." The article also says LinkedIn told its author that it reduces the reach of engagement-pod activity and automated engagement when it detects them, that fake engagement violates its terms, and that it is aware of pod services and is exploring that area (STATED). The contract is the rule that applies most directly to pods today, because enforcing it needs no showing that an indicator is "fake" under § 465.1(h) and no showing of a commercial purpose (INFERENCE; I could not read the contract).

**What happened to one pod tool after LinkedIn was told.** On the night of 26 March 2024 I tried to report a flaw in LEMPOD, a pod tool, to LinkedIn and received errors. LinkedIn's first reply is dated 27 March and carries support case number 240327-019836. I do not know how the case was opened, and I asked in the thread whether my post of that day had triggered it; the thread I have shows no answer. I use the case emails as a source (STATED; they come from one side of the exchange and carry no transport headers, so I cannot authenticate them). They show that on 27 March LinkedIn wrote that it had escalated the matter to its security and engineering teams, and that on 8 April, when the case was marked closed, its case manager wrote that LinkedIn's engineering and trust teams "were able to replicate the issue" and that "actions were put in place to mitigate the potential risk." In its reply of 27 March the case manager also wrote that LinkedIn is committed to keeping members' data safe and that the use of add-ons, extensions or tooling that scrape, modify the appearance of, or automate activity on LinkedIn's website is "prohibited under LinkedIn's User Agreement" and that members who use such tools risk suspension, permanent restriction and disruption in the availability of the tools. The reply calls LEMPOD a third-party tool with a potential vulnerability (STATED). The thread also records that I told LinkedIn that I had created a second account to expose pods and find vulnerabilities, that I use that account for LEMPOD, and that I had said so in an earlier case, 231026-020492, a number that by LinkedIn's numbering pattern points to 26 October 2023 (INFERENCE; I do not have that thread). It records that I asked LinkedIn for credit for the finding; that on 29 March LinkedIn recommended its bug bounty program for future reports; and that I questioned whether a flaw in a third party's tool fits that program and did not take it up. On 4 April I wrote that I was closing the ticket because LEMPOD appeared to have fixed the flaw. In the same message I wrote that creators who use LEMPOD had been hacked in the past; I cite no record of that, and this dissertation does not rely on it (UNCORROBORATED). The thread does not describe in text what the flaw exposed. It refers to a video that I made, which this dissertation does not use, and the video's file name refers to li\_at, the name my book gives to a LinkedIn session cookie (p. 48). I do not reproduce the technical details, because they concern a third party's system. I then asked what happened to the tool. Table 10.2 gives what I found, with the type of each source.

**Table 10.2.** LEMPOD: dated events, what each source says, and the type of each source

| Date | What the source says | Source | Type of source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 27 Mar 2024 | A tracker lists a LEMPOD extension release with permission changes (version 7.2.1 to 8.0.88). I cannot link it to my report. | chrome-stats | Third-party tracker |
| 7 May 2024, 20 Feb 2025 and 15 May 2025 | Further releases with permission changes (to 8.5.1 and to 9.2.20), and a last update on 15 May 2025 (version 9.5.12). | chrome-stats | Third-party tracker |
| 29 Jul 2025 | The extension was removed from the Chrome Web Store, with 10,000 users. The tracker does not say who removed it. | chrome-stats | Third-party tracker |
| 22 Aug 2025 | The lempod.com home page, by its metadata date, says "other lempoders can automatically like and comment on your posts," offers AI-generated comments and cites "80,000+ users." | lempod.com | The vendor's own page |
| 6 Nov 2025 | LinkedIn's vice president of product management says the goal is to make pods "entirely ineffective" and that LinkedIn will "crack down on any third party tools, like a browser extension or a plug-in." The article names no tool and describes no completed action. | Social Media Today | Trade press |
| May to Jul 2026 | One competitor calls LEMPOD "effectively inactive," one says it is gone from Chrome and was "banned and removed," and one says it "can still generate early-post engagement signals." None cites a LinkedIn action against LEMPOD. | Loopvolt, HyperClapper, Commentify | Sellers of rival tools |

The tracker's dates show that the extension kept receiving releases for more than a year after LinkedIn's reply of 8 April 2024 and stayed listed until 29 July 2025, more than fifteen months after it. The vendor's page described automatic engagement in August 2025 (STATED; the page is the vendor's own description). I found no source that reports a LinkedIn action against LEMPOD or its users. That does not show that none occurred, and it does not show that LinkedIn allowed engagement from the tool to count. I have no data on whether accounts that used the tool were restricted, on who caused the removal from the Chrome Web Store, or on whether the engagement reached any post. LinkedIn's statement of 6 November 2025 concerns pods in general and names no tool. The three competitors have a commercial interest in how LEMPOD appears, and they disagree on whether it works. One of them, HyperClapper, shares its name with a source file in Chapter 2, and I do not know whether the company and the file are connected.

The tracker lists a LEMPOD release on 27 March 2024, the date of LinkedIn's first reply in the case thread, and my message of 4 April says that I believed LEMPOD had fixed the flaw (STATED). I cannot link the release to my report, because the tracker gives no reason for it and the thread does not say what changed.

**A second case, about LinkedIn's own service.** The other thread I have is support case 230721-025520, from 21 to 28 July 2023 (STATED; the same limits apply). It does not mention LEMPOD or any pod tool. It concerns view counts. I reported that feed responses from LinkedIn's servers to a logged-in member carried the view counts of posts written by other creators, although the post page did not show those counts to that member. The thread records permission from one creator for my first test and says nothing about whether the creators of the other posts I cited had given permission. LinkedIn's case manager answered on 21 July, the day of the first report, and passed the matter to LinkedIn's product and engineering teams. My messages say that the view-count field was empty in the responses I checked on the evening of 21 July, that I saw it again in other kinds of feed responses on 24 July, and that I saw it in home-feed responses on 26 July. On 27 July the case manager wrote that LinkedIn had "thoroughly investigated and resolved the issue," and the thread ends with a closing message on 28 July.

**What the two threads show.** In both, LinkedIn answered on the day the case was opened, escalated the report and said that it had acted: it said that it had resolved the view-count issue, and that it had put actions in place to mitigate the potential risk in the LEMPOD report. In March 2024 it also wrote that automation tools are prohibited and that members who use them risk suspension or permanent restriction. The threads therefore document notice to LinkedIn and a response to it. They do not show whether LinkedIn blocks, discounts or counts engagement that comes from pod tools, and I found no source that does. The view-count thread concerns a field in LinkedIn's own data, and the podawaa file in Chapter 2 records a view count for each post. I do not know how that file's authors obtained their counts, and nothing I have connects the 2023 issue to any file in the corpus.

The vendor's page is the first source in this chapter in which a pod tool describes its own engagement as automatic. If the page is accurate, the first open question in this section, which assumes a real member who likes a post because a tool asked, does not describe tools of this kind. The question then becomes whether a like that software places through a member's account is "generated by bots" (INFERENCE).

The LinkBoost site, which served the 65 export files (Section 2.8), presents itself as a pod product. Through the fetch tool, its home page returned the phrases "Join pods of creators in your niche" and "Likes and comments from pod members arrive while the algorithm is still deciding," and its plan list included "AI comment generation" and "Auto-connect with pod members." The page's metadata keywords include "Linkedin Automation tool," which the page body, as returned, does not repeat. I cannot tell from these phrases whether a member's engagement on this site is prompted, automatic or both. The site's navigation also included a link labeled "Buy likes." I did not open the page behind that link, so I do not know what it offers, and the label alone does not show that any engagement is sold or that 16 CFR 465.8(a) reaches it. These statements are STATED, because I saw the page only through a summary.

**What my earlier book reports about automatic engagement, members' knowledge and the sale of pod engagement.**

The book gives a participant's account of how automated pod tools work (STATED; Section 2.9). It says that a member can set the frequency of engagement "at like 20-30 seconds" (p. 50), that reactions and comments from one pod can come in "about 10-20 seconds apart," and that a post can be deployed into 30 to 50 pods (pp. 79 to 80). It says that tools offer automatic commenting with a minimum number of words per comment, automatic reactions, and a list of comments written by the post's author from which the tool picks at random, and that some tools let a member turn reactions and comments on or off for each pod (pp. 51 to 52 and 80). It describes one post whose two hundred comments were all comments that the author had supplied in the HyperClapper tool, so that a member in the author's pod would not know what the member's own comment said without looking (pp. 51 to 52). These are the book's statements, and I did not test any tool. The values 20, 40 and 60 in the timing field of the exports (Table 10.1) are of the same order as the book's seconds, but the book does not name that field, so it does not settle what unit the field uses (INFERENCE).

The book also says that a member in an automated pod has no idea what the account is doing: "you have no idea that you are reacting and commenting on that post if your POD settings allow it" (p. 50). If that is accurate for a member, whether the member "knew or should have known" under 16 CFR § 465.8 would turn on what the member knew about the tool's settings, which the data do not show. The statement is the book's description of members in general and is not evidence about any member (INFERENCE).

The book names four roles (pp. 68 to 74): a recruiter who is rarely in a pod and enlists others, an operator of many fake accounts who deletes and re-creates them, a marketer who sells pod engagement as part of a service, and a client who buys without knowing how the engagement is produced. Its labels for them are accusations of deception, so this dissertation uses the roles and not the labels and makes no finding about any person. The marketer role matters to § 465.8(a), which reaches a person who sells or distributes fake indicators. A chapter signed by Berg quotes a company chief executive who says that a marketer he hired used automated pods to build his LinkedIn presence and that he later asked to be removed from them (pp. 94 to 97). The same chapter says, citing "industry insiders" without naming them, that an estimated nine in ten LinkedIn marketing services use pods for their clients, "often without full transparency" (p. 97). The book gives no other source for the figure, and I treat it as UNCORROBORATED.

On the platforms, the book says that pods violate the terms of service of most platforms and that a "blind eye" is turned to the activity "by all parties involved including the platforms" (p. 44). The same chief executive says that LinkedIn "could easily crack down on this practice but chooses not to" (p. 99). Both statements are opinions in the book. They do not show what LinkedIn knew or did, and I treat them as UNCORROBORATED. LinkedIn's own statements that I found are in the chronology above.

A chapter signed by Berg reports an interview with a specialist in cyber intelligence, who says that some political and state-linked groups use pods to make fake profiles look real and that such profiles are used for fraud, including fake job applications and attacks on executives (pp. 85 to 91). The chapter gives no case that I could check, so I treat the statements as UNCORROBORATED. They touch Article 34 of the Digital Services Act, which lists "negative effects on civic discourse, electoral processes and public security" among the systemic risks that a very large platform must assess, and I draw no conclusion about that Article from them.

### 10.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

The fake-indicators rule and the contract apply to this topic more directly than to any other, because the posts concern reach and the boosting acts on reach itself.

The author who posts and receives pod engagement faces § 465.8(b) only if the author purchased or procured indicators that the author knew or should have known to be fake, and only if they materially misrepresented influence for a commercial purpose. Each element is open for pod engagement, as 10.2 explains. In this topic 154 posts (4.7%) use personal-brand, thought-leader or influencer language, which suggests that influence has commercial value to some authors (INFERENCE). The author also faces the contract, and LinkedIn's help documentation describes visibility limits for comments made through automation tools. In the European Union, an author who is a trader and who commissions false endorsements faces point 23c, and whether a prompted like is an endorsement is open.

The tool operator faces paragraph (a), which is easier to meet than paragraph (b) because it requires only that the indicators "can be used" to misrepresent influence and does not require that they were. An operator that sells access to a service that produces likes and comments is within paragraph (a) if what it supplies is "fake" under § 465.1(h), which is the first open question. If a tool produces engagement from members' accounts at times that the tool sets, without the member reading the post, the operator's position is weaker than if each member acts on each post (INFERENCE). The 65 exports contain fields named engageTiming and engagementType, and I do not know what they mean in practice (Table 10.1 gives the counts). If an operator writes comment text for members, *LeadClick* suggests that immunity would be at risk, although whether the operator is a provider of an interactive computer service is itself unclear (INFERENCE). The operator also faces the contract claims that LinkedIn has brought against sellers of fake engagement.

The engaging member who likes or comments at a tool's prompt neither buys nor sells in the usual sense. The member could be said to supply an indicator, which is paragraph (a), or to procure one in exchange, which is paragraph (b). I found no authority for either reading (INFERENCE). The knowledge standard asks whether the member knew or should have known that the indicator was fake, and a member who clicks without reading the post may know that the like says nothing about it (INFERENCE). Point 23c is addressed to traders, so a member who is a consumer is likely outside it (INFERENCE; I did not open the Directive's definition of trader).

The platform is protected by Section 230(c)(1) from treatment as the publisher of its members' posts. The like counts it displays are built from members' actions, so I expect a court to treat a count as information provided by others, but I found no decision on the point (INFERENCE). Section 230(c)(2) covers good-faith actions to restrict material, which would include limiting the visibility of automated comments if a court agreed that such comments are "otherwise objectionable" (INFERENCE; I found no case). No United States statute that I opened requires LinkedIn to detect pods. In the European Union, LinkedIn's designation as a very large online platform brings Articles 34 and 35 to it.

Table 10.3 sets out the rules that reach posts on LinkedIn, content and social media, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 10.3.** Rules that reach posts on LinkedIn, content and social media, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy or penalty stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 16 CFR § 465.8 | Anyone who sells, distributes, buys or procures fake indicators of social media influence, with a knowledge standard | Warning-letter template, 22 Dec 2025; no complaint found | Civil penalties up to $53,088 per violation, as stated in the template |
| 47 U.S.C. § 230(c)(1), (c)(2), (f)(3) | Providers and users of interactive computer services; no protection for a party responsible in part for developing the content | *FTC v. LeadClick Media* (2d Cir. 2016) | Immunity denied and Section 5 liability found (STATED) |
| Digital Services Act, Arts 34 and 35 | Very large online platforms; LinkedIn designated 25 Apr 2023 | None searched | Not stated; Article 52 not opened |
| UCPD Annex I, points 23b and 23c | Traders, in practices directed at consumers in the EU | None searched | Not stated in the sources I opened |
| LinkedIn User Agreement (contract) | Members and others who use the service | *LinkedIn v. TopSocial24*; hiQ | $43,086 and a ban (STATED); $500,000 and an injunction (Chapter 3) |

### 10.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

Fake indicators matter for young people because they use counts to decide whom to trust, and the evidence I opened shows that counts can change judgments in some conditions and not in others.

The Surgeon General's advisory page on social media and youth mental health states that up to 95% of young people aged 13 to 17 report using social media, that nearly two thirds do so daily and that about a third use it "almost constantly," and it says "We cannot conclude that social media is sufficiently safe for children and adolescents." It reports that adolescents who spend more than three hours a day on social media "face double the risk of mental health problems," including symptoms of depression and anxiety, and that 46% of adolescents aged 13 to 17 said social media makes them feel worse about their body image (VERIFIED against the page). The page I opened does not mention like counts or social comparison, so it does not link metrics to those outcomes.

The experimental evidence on like counts is mixed. Hilverda, Kuttschreuter and Giebels (2018, *Frontiers in Communication*) ran two experiments in which participants viewed a fictitious Facebook page about organic food. In Study 1, 124 adults who passed the manipulation checks (of 241 recruited, mean age 48) saw either fewer than 20 likes or more than 200, and the number of likes had no effect. In Study 2, 88 psychology students (of 145, mean age 21) saw either more than 300 likes on positive statements and fewer than 20 on negative ones, or the reverse. Anxiety was lower when positive statements carried the likes, at 1.61 against 2.04 (p = 0.01), and willingness to pay for an organic apple over a regular one was 25 cents against 13 cents (p = 0.02). The authors report that only 52% of Study 1 participants identified the number of likes correctly and that about half of the Study 2 participants failed the check. The study concerns food, Facebook, adults and students. It does not concern teenagers, LinkedIn or pods (VERIFIED against the article page). It shows that in one design like counts moved reactions and in the other they did not, and that many participants do not notice the counts.

My inference is that a count of likes works as a shortcut for how much attention a post deserves, for readers who notice it, and that a person who learns what to post and whom to trust from counts that were partly prompted learns from a distorted sample (INFERENCE). The sources I opened do not measure that effect for teenagers or for pod-boosted posts. The same reasoning applies to young adults entering work, who build a professional presence on the platform and compete for attention with profiles whose counts may include prompted engagement. Followers or connections appear in 231 posts in this topic (7.1%) and personal-brand language in 154 (4.7%). I have no figure on how many young people on the platform use pods (INFERENCE).

None of the rules in 10.2 was written for minors, with one exception. Section 465.8 as I read it has no age provision, and the parts of Section 230 I opened have none. The Digital Services Act names minors in Article 34(1) and Article 35(1)(j), which makes it the one rule in this chapter that speaks to young people, and it acts through the platform's risk assessment and not through any duty owed by a poster. A young person in the European Union lives under a platform-risk regime that names minors, and a young person in the United States lives under a contract and a rule with no age provision (INFERENCE from texts that I read in part).

### 10.5 How this topic bears on mental health

This is the topic where engagement counts are the subject, and the evidence on counts and mental health is mixed and does not cover manufactured counts.

Likes, comments, shares or reposts appear in 1,061 of 3,266 posts (32.5%), and personal brand, thought leader or influencer in 154 (4.7%). Sherman and colleagues (2016, *Psychological Science*) showed 34 adolescents photos with like counts that the researchers had assigned. Participants were more likely to like photos that appeared popular, and photos with many likes drew more activity in reward-related regions. The study did not measure mental health. Section 10.4 reports that the experiments on like counts by Hilverda, Kuttschreuter and Giebels gave mixed results. The American Psychological Association's 2023 advisory says that research links appearance-based social comparison, and attention to one's own photos and feedback, with poorer body image, disordered eating and depressive symptoms, particularly among girls, and it advises adolescents to limit social media use for comparison. The analysis by Orben and Przybylski (Section 7.5) found a small average association between technology use and adolescent well-being.

These studies concern adolescents and general social media use. None concerns professional networks or manufactured counts. The link I propose is INFERENCE: a count that signals approval the post did not earn gives a reader a false comparison point. I found no study of whether a false comparison point has a different effect from a true one.

My earlier book asserts that pods drive a pursuit of vanity metrics that harms mental health, and it explains the claim with four brain chemicals (Section 2.9). It cites no study and reports no measurement of mental health, and I treat it as my opinion and not as evidence for this section.

### 10.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show that any like, comment or view on any post in this topic was prompted, bought or automated. The podawaa file gives totals for each post and nothing about who engaged or why, and a view count of zero on 32.9% of this topic's podawaa posts suggests that the field is often not recorded. I did not use per-post counts from the other three files in this chapter.

The data cannot show that any author or tool operator is covered by § 465.8, because the section turns on knowledge, on whether an indicator is "fake" under § 465.1(h) and on commercial purpose, and none of these appears in a post. I found no enforcement under § 465.8 beyond a warning-letter template, no court decision on prompted engagement by real people, and no decision on Section 230 and like counts. A search that finds nothing does not show that nothing exists.

My reading of the law has gaps. I read *LeadClick* through a commentary, the Digital Services Act on a mirror, and the wording of point 23c on a secondary page. I did not read LinkedIn's User Agreement, its risk assessments under the Digital Services Act, or Articles 33 and 52 of that Regulation. The evidence on young people comes from an advisory about social media use in general and a study of Facebook posts about food, and neither tests pod boosting.

## Chapter 11. Founders, startups and small business: private offerings, general solicitation and investor fraud

### 11.1 The topic in the data

Founders, startups and small business is the eighth-largest topic, with 3,217 posts, which is 5.0% of the 64,970 placed posts. The podawaa file supplies 2,271 of them (70.6%), HyperClapper 501 (15.6%), the 65 exports 430 (13.4%) and LinkBoost-2025 15 (0.5%). The median post is 326 characters.

The vocabulary is about starting and funding companies. The word "startup" appears in 1,463 posts (45.5%), "founder" or "co-founder" in 849 (26.4%), "entrepreneur" in 566 (17.6%) and "small business" in 57 (1.8%). Fundraising words (raising capital, a seed round or Series A to C, angel or venture capital, or "VC") appear in 357 posts (11.1%), and "investor" in 338 (10.5%). Equity, SAFE, cap table, convertible note or valuation appear in 123 (3.8%). Launch, waitlist, beta, pre-order or early-access words appear in 166 (5.2%), and lessons, failure or mistake in 176 (5.5%). A currency amount appears in 224 posts (7.0%), a thousands-or-millions figure such as "$2M" or "six figures" in 154 (4.8%), revenue, ARR or MRR in 85 (2.6%), and a percentage figure in 158 (4.9%). Loans, grants or SBA appear in 37 (1.2%), hiring words in 55 (1.7%), and legal, compliance, contract or intellectual-property words in 88 (2.7%). Franchise, business-opportunity, turnkey, work-from-home, side-hustle or passive-income words appear in only 18 (0.6%). A link appears in 709 posts (22.0%), five or more hashtags in 953 (29.6%), an AI term in 263 (8.2%), and "DM me" in 23 (0.7%).

The counts closest to a securities offering are small. Language of currently raising or looking for investors appears in 14 posts (0.4%) and 40 (1.2%) under two different patterns, and announcements of a completed raise (for example "we raised" or "closed our seed round") appear in 38 (1.2%). Crowdfunding appears in 8 posts (0.2%), and one post uses any of a set of exemption terms (accredited, Regulation D, Regulation CF or A, Form D, Rule 506, SEC filing or securities law). Return or multiple language ("ROI," "10x," "100x") appears in 27 posts (0.8%), and a guarantee or risk-free promise in 11 (0.3%). Students, teens, Gen Z, college or "under 30" appear in 79 posts (2.5%). These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts words and not claims.

### 11.2 The laws that apply

Three rule sets matter beyond Chapter 3: the registration requirement of the Securities Act and the exemptions that founders use to avoid it, the antifraud provisions that apply to anything said to an investor, and the remedies that the Act gives to people who bought.

**Registration and its exemptions.** Section 5(a) of the Securities Act makes it unlawful, "unless a registration statement is in effect as to a security," to use interstate commerce or the mails to sell it, and § 5(c) makes it unlawful "to offer to sell or offer to buy" a security "unless a registration statement has been filed" (VERIFIED; I opened 15 U.S.C. § 77e on Cornell). Founders who raise money without registering rely on an exemption. The one most often used is Rule 506 of Regulation D, which has two forms. The SEC's page on Rule 506(b) says the offering requires "no general solicitation or advertising to market the securities," allows sales to no more than 35 non-accredited investors in any 90-calendar-day period if each has enough financial and business knowledge to evaluate the investment, requires disclosure documents for those non-accredited buyers, has no cap on the amount raised, and requires a notice on Form D within 15 days after the first sale. The page says general solicitation "is incompatible with the private placement exemption." The SEC's page on Rule 506(c) says it "permits issuers to broadly solicit and generally advertise an offering" if "all purchasers in the offering are accredited investors" and the issuer takes "reasonable steps to verify purchasers' accredited investor status." Both pages say the offerings are subject to "bad actor" disqualification and that federal law preempts state registration, but states "still have authority to require notice filings and collect state fees" (VERIFIED against the pages).

The line between the two forms is the meaning of general solicitation. A Harvard Law School forum article says that neither the JOBS Act nor the SEC's rules define the term, so guidance comes from the examples in Rule 502(c), staff no-action letters and staff interpretations. It reports that the staff look at the breadth of a communication, including how many people received it, their relationship to the issuer, their financial sophistication and the form of the materials. It adds that communicating with a prospective investor with whom the issuer has a pre-existing substantive relationship is not general solicitation, that the quality of the relationship is the critical factor, and that self-certification alone is insufficient (STATED). A Dechert alert of September 2015 says that an offer made on an unrestricted, publicly available website is general solicitation (STATED). I did not open Rule 502(c) or the staff interpretations, and neither article discusses social media posts.

Two accommodations exist. The Harvard forum article says Rule 148, effective 15 March 2021, treats communications at a qualifying demo day as outside general solicitation if conditions are met, including a qualifying sponsor and no reference to a specific offering, and that Rule 241 allows a generic solicitation of interest before an exemption is chosen, with no money accepted (STATED). A law-firm alert dated 20 March 2025 reports that the staff of the Division of Corporation Finance issued a no-action letter on 12 March 2025 saying an issuer may treat a minimum investment of at least $200,000 for a natural person or $1 million for an entity as a reasonable verification step under Rule 506(c), with written representations from the purchaser that it is accredited and has not financed the investment through a third party, and with no actual knowledge to the contrary (STATED).

Regulation Crowdfunding has its own limit on advertising. Section 227.204(a)(1) says an issuer "may not, directly or indirectly, advertise the terms of an offering made in reliance on section 4(a)(6)," with exceptions for a notice that "directs investors to the intermediary's platform." Such a notice may contain "no more than" a statement that the issuer is offering under § 4(a)(6), the intermediary's name and a link, the terms of the offering, and limited factual information about the issuer (name, address, phone, website, a representative's email and a brief business description). The rule says "issuer includes persons acting on behalf of the issuer." Paragraph (e) defines the terms of the offering as the amount, nature and price of the securities, the closing date, the planned use of proceeds and the issuer's progress toward its funding target (VERIFIED; I opened § 227.204 on Cornell). I did not open § 227.205, which the page links to.

**Antifraud provisions.** Section 17(a) of the Securities Act applies to anyone in the offer or sale of a security using interstate commerce or the mails. Paragraph (a)(1) bars "any device, scheme, or artifice to defraud," paragraph (a)(2) bars obtaining "money or property by means of any untrue statement of a material fact" or an omission that makes the statements made misleading, and paragraph (a)(3) bars any "transaction, practice, or course of business" that "operates or would operate as a fraud or deceit upon the purchaser." Section 17(b) bars publicizing a security for "a consideration received or to be received, directly or indirectly, from an issuer, underwriter, or dealer" without "fully disclosing" it (VERIFIED; I opened § 77q on Cornell, which contains no penalty provisions). Section 17(b) is the subject of Chapter 13.

The Commission's use of § 17(a)(2) against a start-up founder is recent. In Litigation Release No. 26619 of 26 August 2026, the SEC reports a settled action in the Southern District of Florida against an artificial intelligence start-up and its founder and former chief executive. The release alleges that from December 2019 through December 2024 the defendants raised more than $5.3 million from more than 4,000 investors through Regulation Crowdfunding and Regulation A offerings while misrepresenting the company's financial prospects. It alleges revenue projections that rose as high as $250 million by 2024 and "lacked any reasonable basis," valuations that rose above $200 million in 2022 and "were based on" the founder's "subjective estimations," claims of as many as 25 "partnerships" that were not enforceable, and a customer waitlist when "no actual customer waitlist existed." It alleges that the marketplace was in a testing phase until 2022 and never commercially viable. The release charges that the defendants acted negligently in violation of § 17(a)(2). The defendants consented to judgments without admitting or denying the allegations, subject to court approval. The judgments would permanently enjoin them from violating § 17(a)(2) and require the founder to pay $50,000 in disgorgement, $9,184.53 in prejudgment interest and a $50,000 civil penalty (VERIFIED against the release; I did not check whether the court approved the judgments). The release does not mention LinkedIn or engagement pods.

**Remedies for buyers.** Section 12(a)(1) makes a person who "offers or sells a security in violation of" § 5 liable to the purchaser, who may sue to recover "the consideration paid for such security with interest thereon," less income received, upon tender of the security, or damages if the purchaser no longer owns it. Section 12(a)(2) makes a seller liable to the purchaser if the offer or sale was made by a prospectus or oral communication that includes an untrue statement of a material fact or omits one, unless the seller proves that it "did not know" and "in the exercise of reasonable care could not have known" of the untruth (VERIFIED; I opened 15 U.S.C. § 77l on Cornell). I did not open § 77l(b) or the case law on which communications count as a "prospectus" under § 12(a)(2).

The accredited-investor definition decides who may buy under Rule 506(c). A law-firm alert on the SEC staff's December 2023 report says the current thresholds are a net worth above $1,000,000 excluding the primary residence, or income above $200,000 in each of the two most recent years ($300,000 jointly with a spouse) with a reasonable expectation of the same in the current year. It reports the staff's estimate that 1,510,000 households (1.8%) qualified in 1983 and 24,300,000 (18.5%) in 2022, and that 12.5% would qualify on net worth under the current definition, against 8.8% if retirement assets were excluded. The alert says the report took no position on proposed changes and gives no demographic breakdown (STATED).

### 11.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

The securities rules reach a founder who offers or sells securities, and boosting changes the breadth of the audience that sees an offer.

The author is the issuer or someone acting for the issuer. A founder who posts that the company is raising, with the amount or the terms, may be making an offer. If the post reaches the open feed, the staff's breadth factors point toward general solicitation. A Rule 506(b) offering that includes such a post would not meet that rule's condition of no general solicitation, and a Rule 506(c) offering needs verified accredited purchasers (INFERENCE from the sources above, none of which addresses a social media post). A Regulation Crowdfunding issuer faces § 227.204 directly, because a post that states the terms outside the platform is advertising of terms unless it fits the notice format (INFERENCE from the text). Only 14 posts (0.4%) use currently-raising language and 40 (1.2%) use looking-for-investors language, and only one post mentions an exemption or an accredited investor, so the topic gives little sign that authors address these rules (INFERENCE). A post that makes claims about revenue, customers or valuation to people who might invest meets § 17(a), and the Commission's August 2026 release shows how those claims are charged. Revenue, ARR or MRR appears in 85 posts (2.6%), and I cannot tell which of them speak to investors.

Pod boosting adds to the breadth of the audience that sees a post, because it raises the number of people who see it (INFERENCE). It does not change who the investors are or what the post says. If the author bought or procured likes that the author knew or should have known to be fake, § 465.8(b) from Chapter 10 applies as a separate problem, and an investor who relied on a count of likes as evidence of interest in the company might point to the count in a claim (INFERENCE; I found no case on the point).

The tool operator is not an issuer and has no exposure under these rules in the ordinary case. The engaging member is a possible purchaser, and a member who comments in praise of an offering in return for the founder's engagement on the member's own posts raises a question under § 17(b), which asks about consideration received "directly or indirectly" from an issuer. Whether reciprocal engagement is consideration is open, and I found no authority (INFERENCE; Chapter 13 returns to it). The platform has no securities-law duty that I opened, and the only securities rule it meets in this topic is as the venue for communications (INFERENCE).

Table 11.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on founders, startups and small business, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 11.1.** Rules that reach posts on founders, startups and small business, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy or penalty stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Securities Act § 5, 15 U.S.C. § 77e | Any person who offers or sells a security without registration or an exemption | None searched | Purchaser's rescission or damages under § 12(a)(1) (VERIFIED) |
| Rule 506(b) and 506(c) | Issuers raising money under Regulation D | None searched | Not stated on the SEC pages |
| Regulation Crowdfunding, 17 CFR § 227.204 | Issuers and persons acting for them | None searched | Not stated in the text I opened |
| Securities Act § 17(a) | Anyone offering or selling a security | SEC Litigation Release No. 26619 (26 Aug 2026) | Injunction, $50,000 disgorgement, $9,184.53 interest, $50,000 penalty, subject to court approval |
| Securities Act § 12(a)(2) | A seller who communicates a material untruth or omission | None searched | Rescission or damages to the purchaser |
| Securities Act § 17(b) | A person who publicizes a security for consideration from an issuer, underwriter or dealer | See Chapter 13 | Not stated on the page |

### 11.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

The founder rules decide who may put money into new companies and what protection they have, and the starting position is uneven by wealth.

Private offerings that allow general solicitation are open only to accredited investors, and the SEC staff estimate that 18.5% of U.S. households qualified in 2022 (STATED). The alert I read gives no breakdown by age. My inference is that households headed by young adults, who have had less time to build net worth, are less likely to qualify, so that an early-stage investment that is publicly advertised under Rule 506(c) is open to few of them (INFERENCE). Regulation Crowdfunding and Regulation A reach people who are not accredited, as the Commission's August 2026 release shows with its count of more than 4,000 investors in one company. People who are not accredited have, by the definition's thresholds, less net worth and income than those who are, so on average they have less capacity to absorb a loss (INFERENCE). The release states that the defendants raised more than $5.3 million and proposes a founder payment of $50,000 in disgorgement, $9,184.53 in interest and a $50,000 civil penalty. It does not say how much investors lost.

Business survival is the second anchor. The Bureau of Labor Statistics reports from its Business Employment Dynamics program that of private-sector establishments born in March 2013, 79.6% were operating a year later, 50.6% after five years and 34.7% in March 2023 (VERIFIED against the BLS page). These are establishments and not firms, and the page does not say what share of them were venture-backed or founder-led. Posts about success and posts about lessons or failure are of similar size in this topic: success-story, bootstrapping or scaling language appears in 168 posts (5.2%) and lessons, failure or mistake in 176 (5.5%). My inference is that a young person reading these posts sees a sample selected by who chooses to post and who gets boosted, and a pod raises the visibility of the posts its members choose to promote (INFERENCE). The sources do not measure that effect.

The rules say little about young founders. None of the provisions I opened distinguishes by age, and students, teens, Gen Z, college or "under 30" appear in 79 posts (2.5%) with no indication of who the authors are. A person who raises money from friends and family at age 20 under a Rule 506(b) offering meets the same registration and antifraud rules as an established company (INFERENCE; I opened nothing on minors as issuers). People who bought in a start-up that failed have the remedies in § 12 and the Commission's antifraud authority, and those remedies depend on proving a violation. In the August 2026 release the offerings ran from December 2019 to December 2024 and the action was filed twenty months after the last of them. The release does not say when the Commission began its investigation.

### 11.5 How this topic bears on mental health

Founders report higher rates of several conditions than comparison participants in the study I opened, and the study cannot say whether the work or earlier traits explain the difference.

The topic is about starting and funding companies. The word "startup" appears in 1,463 of 3,217 posts (45.5%), and lessons, failure or mistake in 176 (5.5%). Freeman, Staudenmaier, Zisser and Andresen (2019, *Small Business Economics*) surveyed 242 entrepreneurs and 93 comparison participants by self-report. Entrepreneurs reported more depression (30%), ADHD (29%), substance use (12%) and bipolar disorder (11%) than the comparison participants, and 72% said that mental-health differences had affected them directly or indirectly. I read the abstract record, which gives no comparison percentages. The sample is small and self-reported, and the design cannot show that starting a business causes any condition.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics reports that 34.7% of private-sector establishments born in March 2013 were still operating in March 2023, which means that most did not reach ten years. I found no study that connects that rate to founders' mental health.

Raising money is a second route. The rules on private offerings in Section 11.2 protect investors from fraud, and Section 3.8 cites evidence that the effects of fraud on victims range from little to devastating. Language of currently raising money appears in 14 posts (0.4%) and 40 (1.2%) under two patterns, so the text says little about offerings.

### 11.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show that any post in this topic is an offer or sale of a security. Each count is of words, a post that says "we raised" may describe a past round, and a post that says "looking for investors" may be a joke or a general remark. I cannot tell whether an author is an issuer or whether the audience includes anyone with whom the author has a pre-existing substantive relationship, which is the factor the staff treat as central. The data cannot show that any author used an exemption or ignored one, and one post mentioning an exemption says nothing about the others.

My reading of the securities rules has gaps. I read Rules 506(b) and 506(c) on SEC pages and did not open the text of Rule 506, Rule 502(c), Rule 148, Rule 241, Section 4(a)(2) or Regulation A. The accommodations and the staff's factors come from law-firm and forum articles. The accredited-investor figures come from an alert on the staff report and not from the report. The August 2026 release is a settled action with allegations that the defendants neither admitted nor denied, and I did not check whether the court approved it. The survival figures are for establishments and not for companies that raise money. I did not open the Business Opportunity Rule or the Franchise Rule, which Chapter 14 discusses with earnings claims.

## Chapter 12. AI agents and AI tools: performance claims, tool suppliers and the protection of young users

### 12.1 The topic in the data

AI agents and AI tools is the ninth-largest topic, with 2,984 posts, which is 4.6% of the 64,970 placed posts. It differs from the topics before it in where its posts come from. The 65 exports supply 1,658 of them (55.6%), HyperClapper 1,174 (39.3%), podawaa 136 (4.6%) and LinkBoost-2025 16 (0.5%), so 94.9% come from HyperClapper and the 65 exports. Podawaa supplies 67.5% of all 273,771 distinct posts in the corpus and only 4.6% of this topic. The posts are long: the median is 1,163 characters and the 90th percentile 2,288.

The word "AI" appears in 2,338 posts (78.4%). The phrase "AI agent" or "agentic" appears in 1,498 (50.2%), and the bare word "agent" in 2,268 (76.0%), which includes agents of other kinds. Named models or vendors appear as follows: ChatGPT, OpenAI or GPT in 516 posts (17.3%), Claude or Anthropic in 464 (15.5%), and Gemini, Copilot, Llama, Perplexity, DeepSeek or Mistral in 329 (11.0%). Technical words are common. Workflow appears in 910 posts (30.5%), automation in 610 (20.4%), MCP, RAG, API or vector in 602 (20.2%), prompt in 586 (19.6%), autonomous in 496 (16.6%), LLM or large language model in 361 (12.1%), chatbot in 171 (5.7%) and hallucination in 99 (3.3%). Enterprise, B2B, SaaS or customer words appear in 1,187 (39.8%), and cost, ROI or pricing words in 463 (15.5%).

Claims and caution both appear. A percentage figure appears in 497 posts (16.7%) and a currency amount in 325 (10.9%). Productivity claims of the form "10x," "save N hours" or "N% faster" appear in 255 (8.5%), and a guarantee in 25 (0.8%). Risk, safety, governance, ethics, bias, guardrail or trust words appear in 1,209 (40.5%), privacy, security or data-protection words in 595 (19.9%), and regulation, compliance, law, legal or liability words in 456 (15.3%). Only 19 posts (0.6%) name the EU AI Act, 16 (0.5%) name the FTC, SEC, NIST or FDA, and one uses the word "washing." Language about AI writing posts or comments, auto-commenting or ghostwriting appears in 48 (1.6%), and 43 of those are in HyperClapper. Words about AI-generated content (AI-generated, deepfake, synthetic) appear in 40 (1.3%), and fake or AI reviews in 2 (0.1%).

Few posts touch the groups most affected by the rules in this chapter. Replace-jobs language appears in 18 posts (0.6%), minors, children, teens or age verification in 14 (0.5%), students, teens, kids or school in 23 (0.8%), and companion, therapy, mental-health or loneliness words in 17 (0.6%). Make-money-with-AI language appears in 14 (0.5%), and a DM-me call in 85 (2.8%). A link appears in 753 posts (25.2%) and five or more hashtags in 687 (23.0%). These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts words and not claims. The "risk" and "trust" words are common in business writing, so the 40.5% figure overstates how many posts discuss AI risk.

### 12.2 The laws that apply

Four sets of rules matter beyond Chapters 3 and 4: the Commission's treatment of AI tool suppliers, its action against a seller of AI agents, its inquiry into chatbots used as companions, and the European and state rules that address manipulation and young users.

**The Commission and the supplier of an AI tool: *Rytr*.** In Chapter 4 I described the Commission's 2024 action against Rytr, whose AI writing service let subscribers generate false and deceptive online reviews, and I said it showed that the Commission had pursued the supplier of a tool as well as its users. That statement needs a correction. On 22 December 2025 the Commission voted 2-0 to reopen and set aside the final order. Its press release says that "the facts alleged in the complaint fail to support allegations that Rytr violated Section 5 of the FTC Act" and that the order "unduly burdens innovation in the nascent AI industry," and it ties the decision to the Administration's AI Executive Order and America's AI Action Plan. The 2024 order had barred Rytr from offering "any AI-enabled service generating consumer or customer reviews or testimonials." The release also says the Commission will still pursue actors who use AI to break the law or misrepresent what their generative AI can do, and that Rytr's case was not that kind of case (VERIFIED against the release). The 2024 vote was 3-2 with Commissioners Holyoak and Ferguson dissenting, according to the release I opened in Chapter 4, and the Commission's September 2025 release on chatbots names Andrew N. Ferguson as Chairman. I no longer treat *Rytr* as authority for supplier liability. The review rule itself is separate. The Commission's press release of 14 August 2024 says the rule bars "reviews and testimonials that misrepresent that they are by someone who does not exist, such as AI-generated fake reviews," and its subtitle says the rule will "deter AI-generated fake reviews" (VERIFIED against the release). My reading of the rule's text in Chapter 3 found no mention of AI, so the reach to AI-generated reviews rests on the "someone who does not exist" language.

**A seller of AI agents: *Air AI*.** On 25 August 2025 the Commission voted 3-0 to sue Air AI in the District of Arizona. The release says the company sold business coaching and support bundled with an "Air AI Access Card" and licenses to resell its services, advertised a flagship "conversational AI" feature that it claimed could replace human customer service representatives, and told buyers they could earn back tens of thousands of dollars in days or months and that some could make millions. It says buyers were promised refunds if they did not earn two or three times their investment, that the defendants "rarely honor their guarantee," and that consumers lose "as much as $250,000." The release names the Telemarketing Sales Rule and the Business Opportunity Rule, which Chapter 14 discusses, and does not mention Section 5 (VERIFIED against the release). A trade article dated 24 March 2026 reports a proposed settlement that bans the company and its owners from marketing business opportunities, enters an $18 million monetary judgment that "will be largely suspended," requires the operators to pay $50,000 for consumer relief, and is subject to court approval (STATED; I did not open the order, and the article does not say whether the defendants admitted the allegations). The case is one in which the AI product was the subject of the claims and the earnings promise was the harm.

**Chatbots used as companions.** On 11 September 2025 the Commission voted 3-0 to issue orders under Section 6(b) of the FTC Act to seven companies that operate consumer-facing AI chatbots. The release says the inquiry asks how the companies measure, test and monitor possible negative effects on children and teens, how they limit use and inform parents, how they monetize engagement and handle personal information from conversations, and whether they comply with the COPPA Rule. It says that 6(b) studies are "wide-ranging studies that do not have a specific law enforcement purpose" (VERIFIED against the release). I found no finding, penalty or enforcement action attached to the inquiry.

**European Union and state rules on manipulation and young users.** Article 5(1)(a) of the AI Act prohibits AI systems that use "purposefully manipulative or deceptive techniques," and Article 5(1)(b) prohibits systems that exploit vulnerabilities "due to their age, disability or a specific social or economic situation," each with a requirement of significant harm. The page I read on the Commission's AI Act service desk does not state when Article 5 applies, states no penalty content, and says that two additional prohibitions apply from 2 December 2026, which I did not open (VERIFIED against the page for the wording only). Article 50 is discussed in Chapter 4.

California's SB 243, signed on 13 October 2025 and effective 1 January 2026, regulates operators of companion chatbot platforms. A law-firm article says it requires a clear and conspicuous notice that a chatbot is artificially generated if a reasonable person could be misled, a protocol that keeps the chatbot from producing content about suicidal ideation or self-harm and refers at-risk users to crisis services, and, for users the operator knows to be minors, a notice that they are talking to AI, break reminders "at least every three hours," and reasonable steps to prevent sexually explicit material. Operators must report to the Office of Suicide Prevention from 1 July 2027. Any person injured by a violation may sue for the greater of actual damages or "$1,000 per violation" and attorney's fees (STATED; I did not open the statute). The article lists exclusions for customer service bots, video game characters with limited dialogue and voice assistants without sustained relationships.

Colorado shows how unsettled state AI law is. A law-firm article says that on 14 May 2026 the Governor signed SB 26-189, which repeals and replaces the 2024 Colorado AI Act, postpones the effective date to 1 January 2027, replaces the duty of reasonable care and the impact assessments with notice and documentation duties, gives no private right of action, and allows a 60-day cure period until 1 January 2030 (STATED). A second law-firm article reports that a social media and AI company sued over the 2024 Act in April 2026, that the Department of Justice intervened, and that on 27 April 2026 the District of Colorado approved a joint request to suspend enforcement (STATED). I did not open either court filing, and Colorado's law concerns automated systems used in consequential decisions about people, which is a different subject from the posts here.

### 12.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

The rules reach a poster who sells an AI product and states what it does, and the supplier of an AI tool only if it misrepresents the tool or builds on a fake-review function.

The author is often a vendor or an adviser. A vendor who posts that an agent saves "10 hours a week" or cuts costs by "40%" makes an objective claim that needs the support described in Chapter 4, and 255 posts here (8.5%) use that form. A post that promises earnings from reselling or running AI services is the pattern in *Air AI*, but only 14 posts (0.5%) use make-money language and I cannot tell whether any of them offers a business opportunity within the Rule (INFERENCE). Most posts discuss tools and workflows, and a commentator who describes a technology without selling anything has no exposure under these rules (INFERENCE).

The tool operator is a different party. A pod tool that generates comments with AI would bring two questions. One is § 465.8(a), because a comment written by an AI system and posted under a member's name may be an indicator that does not reflect "a real individual's ... opinions" within § 465.1(h), the clause that Chapter 10 identified as open (INFERENCE). The other is the Rytr question, which no longer has an order behind it. I hold no evidence that any tool in these four sources generates comments with AI, as I said in Chapter 4, and 48 posts (1.6%) that discuss AI writing posts or comments are a fact about vocabulary and not about any tool. Under Article 50(2), a provider of a system that generates synthetic text must mark its output in a machine-readable format for users in the EU, as described in Chapter 4.

The engaging member who posts an AI-drafted comment faces the question that Chapter 10 left open about prompted engagement, with the added point that the words may not be the member's own (INFERENCE). A comment on a general post is not a "consumer review" under § 465.1(d) as I read it in Chapter 6, so § 465.2 is unlikely to apply (INFERENCE).

The platform's position is the one in Chapter 10. Article 50 binds providers and deployers of AI systems and not a platform that hosts posts, and I opened no provision that makes LinkedIn answerable for AI-written comments (INFERENCE). SB 243 applies to operators of companion chatbot platforms, which LinkedIn is not, as far as I know (INFERENCE).

Table 12.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on AI agents and AI tools, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 12.1.** Rules that reach posts on AI agents and AI tools, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy or penalty stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| FTC Act § 5 (Chapters 3 and 4) | Sellers of AI tools who make objective claims | Operation AI Comply (Chapter 4) | See Chapter 4 |
| FTC action on a tool supplier | Supplier of an AI review-writing service | *Rytr*, order set aside 22 Dec 2025 | None; the order was vacated (VERIFIED) |
| Telemarketing Sales Rule; Business Opportunity Rule | Sellers of AI-related business opportunities | *Air AI* (D. Ariz., filed 25 Aug 2025) | Proposed: business-opportunity ban; $18 million judgment, largely suspended; $50,000 (STATED) |
| FTC Act § 6(b) inquiry | Seven chatbot operators | Orders of 11 Sep 2025 | None found; the release describes a study with no specific law enforcement purpose (VERIFIED) |
| 16 CFR Part 465 (AI-generated fake reviews) | Businesses that write, buy or disseminate fake reviews | Warning-letter template (Chapter 10) | Up to $53,088 per violation (Chapter 10) |
| EU AI Act, Article 5(1)(a), (b) | Providers and deployers of manipulative or vulnerability-exploiting systems | None searched | Not stated on the page |
| California SB 243 | Operators of companion chatbot platforms | None searched | $1,000 per violation or actual damages (STATED) |
| Colorado SB 26-189 | Developers and deployers of automated decision systems | None; enforcement stayed | Fines with a cure period to 2030 (STATED) |

### 12.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

The evidence I opened points to people entering the workforce as the group most affected so far by AI agents, and to children and teenagers as heavy users of AI chatbots, and the rules I opened address the second group more directly than the first.

A working paper from Stanford's Digital Economy Lab, updated in August 2026, uses ADP payroll records covering 3.5 to 5 million employees a month from January 2021 through June 2026. It reports that employment of workers aged 22 to 25 in AI-exposed occupations stands 19% below where it would be had it kept pace with less-exposed peers, and that experienced workers show no comparable gap. Declines are concentrated in occupations where AI use primarily substitutes for human tasks, and the divergence operates mainly through reduced hiring and not through separations. Adding the college share of workers halves the estimate for the most exposed quintile, from -0.18 to -0.09. The authors call the results "early, descriptive indicators ... rather than causal estimates," and say that the paper does not estimate a causal impact of AI (VERIFIED against the paper). The evidence bears on the careers of people now leaving school, and it does not show that agents caused the decline. In this topic, workflow words appear in 30.5% of posts and automation in 20.4%, while replace-jobs language appears in 0.6% (VERIFIED), so the posts discuss tools in enterprise terms and say little about who loses work (INFERENCE).

For children and teenagers the evidence is about use. The Pew Research Center reported in February 2026, from a survey of 1,458 teens aged 13 to 17 fielded from 25 September to 9 October 2025, that 64% use chatbots, about three in ten daily, 57% for information and 54% for schoolwork, and 12% for emotional support (VERIFIED against the report in Chapter 4). The Commission's inquiry, California's SB 243 and Article 5(1)(b) of the AI Act each address a part of this use: the inquiry studies it, SB 243 sets operating duties and a private remedy for known minors in one state, and Article 5(1)(b) prohibits systems that exploit vulnerabilities due to age where significant harm results. None binds the vendors who post in this topic as authors, and only 14 posts (0.5%) mention minors, children, teens or age verification (INFERENCE). My inference is that the protections for young users attach to a product category, companion chatbots, that this topic's posts rarely discuss.

The change in the *Rytr* order affects the information environment in which young people form judgments. The Commission's stated position is that it will pursue those who use AI to break the law or misrepresent their AI, and that it will not condemn a tool because it could be misused. Whether a supplier of a review-writing or comment-writing tool faces any rule other than the review rule's "someone who does not exist" language is unsettled (INFERENCE). The rules for children also differ by place. A teenager in California has SB 243, a teenager in the European Union has Article 5, and a teenager in a state with no statute has the Commission's inquiry and its general authority (INFERENCE from the sources opened; I did not survey other states).

### 12.5 How this topic bears on mental health

Professional bodies say that research on AI and young people's mental health is still developing, and the early findings concern companionship and trust.

AI appears in 2,338 of 2,984 posts (78.4%) in this topic, and companion, therapy, mental-health or loneliness words in 17 (0.6%). Section 4.4 reports that 12% of teens used chatbots for emotional support or advice. Common Sense Media reported on 16 July 2025 that 72% of teens aged 13 to 17 had used AI companions at least once, that about one in three found conversations with an AI companion as satisfying as, or more satisfying than, conversations with real-life friends, and that about one in three users had felt uncomfortable with something a companion said or did (STATED; I read the organization's press release, which gives no sample size). The American Psychological Association's June 2025 advisory says that adolescents are less likely than adults to question information from a bot, that strong attachments to AI characters "may contribute to struggles with learning social skills," and that this research is early. It recommends reminders that a user is talking to a bot and routes to human support for serious difficulties.

The claims in this topic's posts concern what an AI tool can do. If a teenager relies on a tool for emotional support because a post said it could provide it, the accuracy of the claim matters to that reliance (INFERENCE). The data show 17 posts that name this use and 14 that name minors or age verification, so the topic's text barely touches it.

### 12.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show that any AI claim in these posts is false, that any post offers a business opportunity within the Rule, or that any pod tool generates comments with AI. The counts are of words. A post that says an agent saves ten hours may be accurate and may rest on a study that I did not see. The data cannot show who the posters are, whether they sell AI products, or whether any minor reads or posts in this topic.

My reading of the law has gaps. I read the *Rytr* and *Air AI* matters through the Commission's press releases and a trade article and did not open the orders or complaints. I read SB 243 and the Colorado developments through law-firm articles and not the statutes or court papers. I read Article 5 of the AI Act on the Commission's service desk page and not in the Official Journal. I did not open the Business Opportunity Rule or the Telemarketing Sales Rule. The Stanford paper is a working paper that its authors describe as descriptive, and the Pew figures are for teens in the United States in late 2025. The 2024 *Rytr* vote count comes from the Chapter 4 source and not from a new opening of the 2024 release.

## Chapter 13. Investing, markets and finance: touting, antifraud rules and the young retail investor

### 13.1 The topic in the data

Investing, markets and finance is the tenth-largest topic, with 2,853 posts, which is 4.4% of the 64,970 placed posts. The podawaa file supplies 1,344 of them (47.1%), the 65 exports 1,069 (37.5%), HyperClapper 411 (14.4%) and LinkBoost-2025 29 (1.0%). The median post is 587 characters and the 90th percentile 1,485.

The word "invest" in some form appears in 1,043 posts (36.6%). Stock, equity or market words appear in 587 (20.6%), fund, ETF, portfolio, hedge-fund, private-equity or venture words in 345 (12.1%), and real estate, property, rent, mortgage or REIT words in 365 (12.8%). Buy, sell, price-target, undervalued, bullish or bearish language appears in 342 (12.0%), return, yield, dividend or ROI in 205 (7.2%), and interest-rate, Fed, inflation, recession or tariff words in 237 (8.3%). Get-rich or wealth language (millionaire, billionaire, financial freedom, passive income or wealth) appears in 199 (7.0%), and a percentage figure in 500 (17.5%). Trading-signal, trading-course, forex or day-trading words appear in 164 (5.7%), ticker, stock-pick, penny-stock, IPO or meme-stock words in 55 (1.9%), and cashtags such as a dollar sign before a ticker in 21 (0.7%). Crypto, Bitcoin or NFT words appear in 113 (4.0%), financial adviser, wealth manager, fiduciary or broker words in 139 (4.9%), and retirement, 401(k), IRA or pension words in 56 (2.0%). Fundraising, accredited, private-placement, syndication, crowdfunding, SPV or LP words appear in 58 (2.0%), and an invitation to invest with the author or a call for investors in 15 (0.5%). A link appears in 695 posts (24.4%), five or more hashtags in 990 (34.7%), a DM-me call in 24 (0.8%), and a guarantee or risk-free promise in 19 (0.7%).

Warnings and disclosures are far less common than recommendations. A phrase such as "not financial advice," "do your own research," "for educational purposes," "disclaimer" or "past performance" appears in 35 posts (1.2%). A statement that the author owns, is long or is short a position appears in 11 (0.4%), and sponsored, paid-partnership, affiliate or "promoted" words in 16 (0.6%). Words that name a regulator or securities law (SEC, FINRA, CFTC, "securities law") appear in 79 (2.8%), and scam, fraud, Ponzi, pump-and-dump or "beware" in 45 (1.6%). Students, teens, Gen Z, millennials, young investors, college or first-time words appear in 75 (2.6%). These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts words and not claims. A post that warns against a Ponzi scheme is counted with a post that runs one.

### 13.2 The laws that apply

Three rule sets matter beyond Chapters 3 and 11: the general antifraud rule, the rules on promoting securities for payment, and the rules for investment advisers and broker-dealers who communicate with the public.

**The antifraud rule (17 CFR § 240.10b-5).** Rule 10b-5 applies to any "person" who acts directly or indirectly through interstate commerce, the mails or a national securities exchange facility, and it applies "in connection with the purchase or sale of any security." It makes it unlawful to use any "device, scheme, or artifice to defraud" (paragraph (a)), to make an "untrue statement of a material fact" or omit a material fact needed to keep statements from being "misleading" (paragraph (b)), or to engage in any "act, practice, or course of business" that operates or would operate as a fraud or deceit on any person (paragraph (c)) (VERIFIED; I opened the rule on Cornell). Section 17(a) of the Securities Act, which Chapter 11 set out, uses similar words for the offer or sale of a security.

**Promoting securities for payment: § 17(b) and *Kardashian*.** Section 17(b), also set out in Chapter 11, bars publicizing a security for "a consideration received or to be received, directly or indirectly, from an issuer, underwriter, or dealer" without "fully disclosing" it. The text requires disclosure of the payment and its amount. It does not address whether the publicity is accurate. In SEC press release 2022-183 of 3 October 2022, the Commission announced that it had charged a celebrity with touting a crypto asset security, EMAX tokens, on social media without disclosing a $250,000 payment for an Instagram post that linked to the issuer's website. The release says the order found a violation of "the anti-touting provision," which I read as § 17(b), although the release does not give the section number (INFERENCE). Without admitting or denying the findings, the celebrity agreed to pay $1.26 million in total, covering about $260,000 in disgorgement (the promotional payment), prejudgment interest and a $1,000,000 penalty, to stop promoting crypto asset securities for three years, and to cooperate with the continuing investigation. The Chair said that celebrities must disclose "when and how much they are paid to promote investing in securities," and the Director of Enforcement said that "Investors are entitled to know whether the publicity of a security is unbiased" (VERIFIED against the release).

A second example involves ordinary traders. A National Law Review article dated 6 January 2023 reports that the SEC charged eight men with a scheme that produced about $100 million in fraudulent profits, in which they used Twitter and Discord to present themselves as seasoned stock traders and promoted stocks to their followers. It says seven promoted the stocks directly and the eighth hosted a podcast that presented the others as experts, and it quotes the SEC as describing a "steady diet of misinformation." The article reports the charges and no outcome, and it passes on the SEC's warnings not to make investment decisions based on social media testimonials and to check registration on Investor.gov (STATED; I did not open the SEC's release or complaint).

**Advisers and broker-dealers.** The Advisers Act Marketing Rule, 17 CFR § 275.206(4)-1, governs advertising by registered investment advisers, including testimonials, endorsements and third-party ratings. A law-firm summary of the SEC Division of Examinations' risk alert of 16 December 2025 reports that the most common problem was missing required disclosures when a testimonial or endorsement was disseminated, that disclosures were sometimes hyperlinked or set in smaller or lighter type, and that other findings included undisclosed compensation terms, undisclosed conflicts and missing written agreements with paid promoters. It says the alert names social media as a channel where noncompliant advertisements appeared and gives an example of promoters, including social media influencers, who were paid for client referrals while the advisers omitted material information about the payments (STATED; I did not open the alert or the rule). For broker-dealers, FINRA Rule 2210 governs communications with the public. FINRA Regulatory Notice 26-14, published on 9 July 2026, requests comment on a proposal to modernize retail communications rules, with comments due on 11 September 2026. According to a copy of the notice hosted by a law firm, the proposal would drop the distinction between static and interactive social media content, says that the risks of finfluencers "persist regardless of whether the content is static or interactive," would have firms consider the qualifications of anyone paid for or endorsing content including finfluencers, and would apply one supervisory standard to AI-generated communications (VERIFIED against that copy; the proposal would need SEC approval and has not taken effect as far as I know). I did not open Rule 2210 itself.

### 13.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

The antifraud rules reach a poster whose statement is false or whose scheme is a fraud, and the touting rules reach a poster who is paid by an issuer, so the question for each post is who is speaking and who is paying.

The author who comments on markets without payment and without a false statement is outside § 17(b) and outside Rule 10b-5 (INFERENCE from the texts, which require a consideration from an issuer, underwriter or dealer in the first case and a misstatement or scheme in connection with a purchase or sale in the second). An author who posts a stock pick while being paid by the issuer must disclose it under § 17(b), and only 16 posts here (0.6%) use sponsored, affiliate or promoted words and 11 (0.4%) disclose a position. A pick that rests on a false statement of fact meets Rule 10b-5(b). A post by a registered adviser that praises the adviser or a service meets the Marketing Rule if the praise is a testimonial or endorsement, and a post by a registered representative meets FINRA's rules through the firm (INFERENCE; I did not open either rule). I cannot tell from the data which authors are registered. Words that point to advisers or brokers appear in 139 posts (4.9%).

Pod boosting adds a question that no source I opened answers. If an author buys or procures likes that the author knew or should have known to be fake, § 465.8(b) from Chapter 10 applies. Beyond that, a bought or prompted crowd of likes and comments under a stock promotion might be part of a "device, scheme, or artifice" under Rule 10b-5(a) or an "act, practice, or course of business" under paragraph (c), if the promotion is connected with a purchase or sale and the engagement was meant to make the promotion look popular (INFERENCE; I found no case that treats engagement as part of a scheme). The same question arises for the engaging member. A member who comments in praise of a stock or an adviser in return for engagement on the member's own posts does not receive money from an issuer, and whether engagement is "consideration received ... directly or indirectly" under § 17(b) is open. The text of § 17(b) names an issuer, underwriter or dealer as the source of the consideration, so a reciprocal exchange between two individual members would not fit unless one of them is such a person (INFERENCE). Under the Marketing Rule, an adviser who gives non-cash benefits to an endorser raises the compensation question that the risk alert describes for gift cards and referral payments (INFERENCE; I did not open the rule's definition of compensation).

The tool operator is not an issuer, adviser or broker in the ordinary case and has no exposure under these rules (INFERENCE). The platform is not a securities professional, and the rules I opened place no duty on it.

Table 13.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on investing, markets and finance, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 13.1.** Rules that reach posts on investing, markets and finance, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy or penalty stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 17 CFR § 240.10b-5 | Any person, in connection with a purchase or sale of a security | SEC charges against eight social media promoters (reported 6 Jan 2023) | Not stated; charges only (STATED) |
| Securities Act § 17(b) | A person who publicizes a security for consideration from an issuer, underwriter or dealer | SEC Press Release 2022-183 (3 Oct 2022) | $1.26 million total, three-year ban on promoting crypto asset securities (VERIFIED) |
| Securities Act § 17(a) | Anyone offering or selling a security | See Chapter 11 | See Chapter 11 |
| Advisers Act Rule 206(4)-1 | Registered investment advisers and their promoters | Division of Examinations risk alert, 16 Dec 2025 | Not stated; a risk alert (STATED) |
| FINRA Rule 2210 | Broker-dealers and their associated persons | Regulatory Notice 26-14 (proposal, 9 Jul 2026) | Not stated; a proposal (VERIFIED against a copy) |

### 13.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

Investment posts reach young people who are building their first portfolios, and the evidence I opened shows that many of them take their information from social media.
