The FINRA Investor Education Foundation's investor survey, reported in a release in late 2025 on the sixth wave of its National Financial Capability Study, sampled 2,861 U.S. investors with non-retirement accounts. It reports that 29% of investors rely on social media for investment information, that 26% use recommendations from influencers, and that 61% of investors under 35 and 57% of investors with under two years of experience use them. Of investors under 35, 43% trade options, against 10% of those aged 55 and over, 22% buy on margin, against 4%, 29% have bought meme stocks or other viral investments, and 62% feel they need to take big risks to meet their goals. The survey found that 37% worry about losing money to investment fraud and that 89% do not believe they have been targeted by an investment scam, and respondents answered an average of 5.3 of 11 investing quiz questions correctly, with margin and short-selling questions missed most often (STATED; I read the release and not the report). An earlier FINRA Foundation report, "Gen Z and Investing" (May 2023, from an online survey in November and December 2022), reports that 48% of U.S. Gen Z investors learn about investing mainly through social media, that 37% rate influencers or pundits as a major factor in their decision to invest, and that 50% of Gen Z investors say they have made an investment driven by fear of missing out (VERIFIED against the report). Its sample covered four countries, with U.S. respondents just over half.

The Commission's data from Chapter 8 point the same way. They report $1.1 billion in reported losses to investment scams that started on social media in 2025, and they report that 40% of loss reports by people aged 18 to 29 involved a scam that started on social media (VERIFIED in Chapter 8). I do not claim that any of these losses began with a post in this topic.

The rules protect these investors in a limited way. Section 17(b) requires disclosure of payment from an issuer, underwriter or dealer and does not require that a recommendation be sound or that the engagement behind it be real. Rule 10b-5 reaches false statements and schemes but needs proof of a connection to a purchase or sale. The *Kardashian* remedy, a payment of $1.26 million and a three-year promotion ban, was imposed on one promoter for one undisclosed payment of $250,000 (VERIFIED against the release). My inference is that a young investor who relies on a count of likes or on an influencer's pick is protected by disclosure rules and not by any rule that tests whether the popularity is real, and that the harm of an early loss compounds over a long horizon because there are more years in which the lost capital would have grown (INFERENCE; no source I opened measures this).

The topic's own posts give little help. Only 35 carry a disclaimer of the "not financial advice" kind, and 75 mention young people, students or first-time investors (VERIFIED). I cannot tell whether any reader acted on any post.

### 13.5 How this topic bears on mental health

Investing connects to mental health through losses and through trading that resembles gambling, and the studies I opened concern narrow groups.

Trading-signal, trading-course, forex or day-trading words appear in 164 of 2,853 posts (5.7%), and get-rich or wealth language in 199 (7.0%). Mills and Nower (2019, *Addictive Behaviors*) surveyed 876 adults who gambled at least monthly. More than half had traded cryptocurrency in the past year, and gamblers who traded both cryptocurrency and high-risk stocks reported more problem gambling and more symptoms of depression and anxiety than gamblers who traded only one of the two (STATED; I read the Rutgers press release, not the paper). A one-time survey cannot show that trading causes harm. The debt evidence in Section 9.5 is the related finding for losses that become debt.

Fraud is a second route. The FBI's 2025 Internet Crime Report, released on 6 April 2026, reports nearly $21 billion in losses from cyber-enabled crime, and the FBI's press release says that investment fraud accounted for nearly 49% of scam-related losses. The FINRA Foundation's Gen Z study, cited in this chapter, is the evidence on younger investors. I found no source on the mental-health effect of ordinary investment losses on young investors, and none on the effect of touting. The antifraud rules in Section 13.2 reach false statements and undisclosed promotion, and I do not claim that they change that effect.

### 13.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show that any post in this topic is a stock promotion, that any author was paid, or that any statement is false. A count of "buy" or "bullish" words is a count of words, and a post that says it owns a position may be accurate or not. I cannot tell whether an author is registered as an adviser or broker, whether any engagement under a post was bought or prompted, or whether any reader invested.

My reading of the law has gaps. I opened Rule 10b-5 and the SEC's Kardashian release. I read the eight-promoter case, the Marketing Rule risk alert and the FINRA survey through articles and a release and not the underlying filings, alerts or reports. I opened a copy of FINRA Regulatory Notice 26-14 and not Rule 2210, and I did not open Rule 206(4)-1, the Investment Advisers Act, Section 9 of the Exchange Act or the case law on whether commentary is protected speech. The question of whether reciprocal engagement is consideration under § 17(b), or engagement is part of a scheme under Rule 10b-5, is one I could not answer from any source.

## Chapter 14. Motivation, mindset and growth: earnings claims, the Business Opportunity Rule and the young person's first business

### 14.1 The topic in the data

Motivation, mindset and growth is the eleventh-largest topic, with 2,420 posts, which is 3.7% of the 64,970 placed posts. The podawaa file supplies 1,579 of them (65.2%), the 65 exports 536 (22.1%), HyperClapper 262 (10.8%) and LinkBoost-2025 43 (1.8%). The median post is 327 characters and the 90th percentile 1,002, so these posts are shorter than those in the investing topic of Chapter 13, where the median was 587.

Mindset or motivation language (mindset, motivation, inspire, discipline, habit, grind, hustle, "never give up," "believe in yourself") appears in 1,616 posts (66.8%), and success, winner or achievement words in 717 (29.6%). An advice-style word such as stop, start, don't, never or always appears in 742 (30.7%). Words about failure, lessons, mistakes, rejection or setbacks appear in 257 (10.6%), career, job, promotion, interview or résumé words in 204 (8.4%), and wellness words (mental health, burnout, anxiety, stress, depression, therapy, self-care, resilience, well-being) in 243 (10.0%). Burnout, mental health, anxiety or depression words alone appear in 41 (1.7%). A first-person statement such as "I was," "I started," "I quit" or "my story" appears in 173 (7.1%). Words about students, teens, Gen Z, young people, children, parents or graduates appear in 58 (2.4%). A link appears in 221 posts (9.1%), five or more hashtags in 811 (33.5%), and a call to comment or follow in 71 (2.9%).

Commercial language is a minority. Coach, mentor, course, masterclass, webinar, workshop, bootcamp, mastermind, program or "join my" words appear in 302 posts (12.5%), and proven, system, blueprint, formula, secret, hack, step-by-step or framework words in 157 (6.5%). A call to action such as "book a call," "link in bio," "DM me," "enroll," "sign up," "register" or "join" appears in 51 (2.1%), and a coaching or course word in the same post as a call to action in 12 (0.5%). Urgency language (limited spots, last chance, "don't miss") appears in 10 posts (0.4%), and a guarantee, risk-free or money-back promise in 10 (0.4%).

Income claims are rarer still. Income or earnings language (six-figure, seven-figure, passive income, financial freedom, income, earn, quit my job, laptop lifestyle) appears in 24 posts (1.0%), a dollar figure of any kind in 25 (1.0%), and a dollar figure followed by k, m, million or billion in 15 (0.6%). An income, revenue, profit or per-month word in the same post as a dollar figure appears in 5 (0.2%). A multiplier or growth claim (10x, doubled, tripled, a percentage growth) appears in 22 (0.9%), and testimonial, case-study, "before and after" or "my clients" words in 17 (0.7%). Words for the kinds of business at the center of the FTC cases in Section 14.2 (side hustle, ecommerce, dropshipping, Amazon FBA, print on demand, or "AI" followed closely by income, money or business) appear in 7 posts (0.3%). The word AI appears in 43 (1.8%). These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts words and not claims. A post that warns against a get-rich promise is counted with a post that makes one.

The main result of this section is negative. Most of this topic is general encouragement, and the language that the earnings-claim rules address, a specific level of income attached to an offer, appears in a handful of posts.

### 14.2 The laws that apply

Four sets of rules matter here: the Business Opportunity Rule, Section 5 of the FTC Act as applied to earnings claims, the Endorsement Guides on testimonials, and the EU's unfair commercial practices directive. A fifth item, the Supreme Court's decision in *AMG Capital*, explains how the FTC now obtains money from sellers.

**The Business Opportunity Rule (16 CFR Part 437).** The Rule defines a "business opportunity" in § 437.1(c) as an arrangement with three elements. The seller solicits a prospective purchaser to enter a new business. The purchaser makes a "required payment," meaning consideration the purchaser must pay the seller or an affiliate, by contract or by practical necessity, to obtain or start the business, with an exception for reasonable inventory purchases at bona fide wholesale prices. And the seller represents, expressly or by implication, that the seller or designated persons will provide locations for equipment or displays, provide outlets, accounts or customers (including Internet ones) for the purchaser's goods or services, or buy back what the purchaser makes. An "earnings claim" under § 437.1(f) is any oral, written or visual representation that conveys, expressly or by implication, a specific level or range of actual or potential sales, or gross or net income or profits, and it includes statements from which a purchaser can reasonably infer a minimum income level (VERIFIED against the Cornell text of § 437.1, which the fetch tool returned as a summary and not verbatim).

Section 437.4 then governs earnings claims by a seller of a business opportunity. According to the same kind of summary of the Cornell text, paragraph (a) allows a claim to a prospective purchaser only if the seller has a reasonable basis for it when it is made, holds written material that substantiates it, makes that material available to the purchaser and the FTC on request, and gives the purchaser a single written statement under the title "EARNINGS CLAIM STATEMENT REQUIRED BY LAW." That statement must give the seller's name, the date, the claim, the beginning and ending dates of the period in which the earnings were achieved, the number and percentage of purchasers before the ending date who reached at least the stated level, any characteristics of those purchasers that differ materially from the prospective purchaser's, and a statement that substantiation is available on request. Paragraph (b) applies the substantiation requirement to claims in general media and adds that the dates and the number and percentage of purchasers who reached the stated level must appear immediately alongside the claim. Paragraph (c) bars industry financial or performance information unless the seller has written substantiation that it reflects, or does not exceed, the typical experience of purchasers of the opportunity offered. Paragraph (d) requires written notice of any material change before the purchaser signs or pays (VERIFIED in substance against that summary). The Rule's section list also includes § 437.2 on furnishing documents, § 437.3 on the disclosure document, § 437.6 on other prohibited practices, § 437.8 on a franchise exemption and § 437.9 on preemption, and § 437.9(b) states that the FTC does not intend to preempt state or local business-opportunity sales-practices laws except where they conflict, and that a law is not in conflict if it gives purchasers equal or greater protection (VERIFIED against the Cornell page for § 437.9). I opened none of the state laws and did not open the Franchise Rule, 16 CFR Part 436.

**Section 5 of the FTC Act, applied to earnings claims.** Chapter 3 set out Section 5. The complaint in the Ascend Ecom case shows how the FTC pleads earnings claims under it. Its first three counts rest on Section 5 alone: false or unsubstantiated earnings claims, a deceptive "risk free" offer with a buyback guarantee, and an unfair practice of threats and intimidation to suppress reviews. Counts four to eight plead violations of the Business Opportunity Rule, citing § 437.6(d) on misrepresenting income or profits, §§ 437.2 and 437.3(a) on the disclosure document, § 437.4(a) on unsubstantiated claims or a missing earnings claim statement, § 437.4(b) on general-media claims without the required dates and percentages, and § 437.4(c) on industry earnings information. Count nine pleads the Consumer Review Fairness Act, 15 U.S.C. § 45b(c), over form contracts that restrict reviews. The complaint quotes claims such as "five figures" or more per month in "passive income," that consumers could "passively make $100K a month," and that "You can get a fully automated TikTok shop to make $10,000+ per month," and a buyback promise, "If you don't make 100% ROI on your upfront you don't pay" (VERIFIED against the complaint, as summarized by the fetch tool). The structure of the counts matters for this chapter, because only the Rule counts depend on the three-element definition above. Counts one to three do not. They apply to any seller who makes a false or unsubstantiated claim.

**The Endorsement Guides, 16 CFR Part 255.** Section 255.1 says that endorsements "must reflect the honest opinions, findings, beliefs, or experience of the endorser" (paragraph (a)), that an advertiser "may be liable for a deceptive endorsement even when the endorser is not liable" (paragraph (d)), and that endorsers "may be liable for statements made in the course of their endorsements" (paragraph (e)). If an advertisement says the endorser uses the product, the endorser must have been "a bona fide user of it at the time the endorsement was given" (paragraph (c)) (VERIFIED against the Cornell text of § 255.1, as summarized; the page did not state the legal status of the Guides, and I do not rely on one). Section 255.2 supplies the rule on typical results. An endorsement about a key attribute may be read as saying that the endorser's experience "is representative of what consumers will generally achieve." A disclaimer such as "You should not expect to have similar results" does not cure that, according to Example (e)(1)(ii), unless the advertiser has substantiation that new users typically get similar results, and "Results not typical" is insufficient. The advertiser should "clearly and conspicuously disclose the generally expected" result, and a disclosure that covers only the users who stuck with a program, and not those who joined, is inadequate (VERIFIED against the Cornell text of § 255.2, as summarized, with quotations limited to words the summary placed in quotation marks). Section 255.6 reads in full: "Endorsements in advertisements addressed to children may be of special concern because of the character of the audience," and "Practices that would not ordinarily be questioned in advertisements addressed to adults might be questioned in such cases" (VERIFIED against the Cornell text). Part 465, which Chapter 10 set out, covers fake testimonials. Section 465.1(f) defines a consumer testimonial as a promotional message that consumers are likely to believe reflects the opinions or experiences of a consumer who purchased or used the product, and § 465.2 bars fake or false ones (VERIFIED in Chapter 10).

**The 2021 notice of penalty offenses.** On 26 October 2021 the FTC announced that it had sent a Notice of Penalty Offenses about money-making opportunities to more than 1,100 businesses. The release lists the claims it treats as deceptive: representing that participants will make a profit, representing that profits shown are typical, falsely telling consumers they do not need experience, falsely telling them that they must act immediately, and using testimonials to mislead about the rewards. It states a civil penalty of up to $43,792 per violation at that date, and it says that receiving the notice does not suggest that a company has engaged in deceptive or unfair conduct (VERIFIED against the release, as summarized by the fetch tool). I did not open the notice itself.

**Remedies and *AMG Capital*.** In *AMG Capital Management v. FTC*, decided on 22 April 2021, a unanimous Supreme Court held that § 13(b) of the FTC Act does not authorize the FTC to seek, or a court to award, equitable monetary relief such as restitution or disgorgement, and the opinion says: "We conclude that it does not." The Court noted that § 13(b) remains available for injunctions and that § 19 provides a route to consumer redress with its own conditions (VERIFIED against the Cornell text of the opinion, as summarized). The Ascend complaint seeks relief under §§ 13(b) and 19 together with the Business Opportunity Rule and the Consumer Review Fairness Act (VERIFIED). My inference is that the Rule counts matter for money because a rule violation opens the § 19 route, but I did not open § 19 (INFERENCE).

**The EU.** The Unfair Commercial Practices Directive 2005/29/EC prohibits unfair practices in Article 5(1) and treats a practice as unfair under Article 5(2) if it is contrary to professional diligence and materially distorts or is likely to materially distort the economic behavior of the average consumer. Article 6(1) makes a practice misleading if it deceives or is likely to deceive the average consumer about, among other things, "the main characteristics of the product," which the text says includes "the results to be expected from its use," and Article 7(1) covers omission of material information. Annex I lists practices that are unfair in all circumstances, among them falsely stating that a product will be available only for a very limited time (point 7), describing a product as "free" when the consumer pays more than the unavoidable cost (point 20) and operating or promoting a pyramid promotional scheme (point 14) (VERIFIED against the Directive as adopted in 2005, which I read on the UK legislation site, so amendments are not reflected). I did not measure recruitment language in this topic, and I did not open the Directive's later amendments or any member state's law.

### 14.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

The rules reach a post only when it is part of a sale, so the first question for each post is whether the author is selling something and whether the post states what a buyer can earn.

An author who posts encouragement and sells nothing is outside every rule in Section 14.2. The Business Opportunity Rule applies to a seller of a business opportunity, the earnings-claim counts in the Ascend complaint apply to claims made to sell, and the Endorsement Guides apply to advertisements (INFERENCE from the texts I opened). That description fits the large majority of this topic if the word counts above are a guide, because 1,616 posts (66.8%) use mindset language and 24 (1.0%) use income language.

An author who sells a coaching program or course and states an income level in a post meets Section 5 and its substantiation requirement for any such claim. Whether the Business Opportunity Rule also applies depends on the three elements of § 437.1(c). The third is the one that separates a business opportunity from a course. A seller who promises only instruction, and does not promise to provide locations, outlets, accounts or customers or to buy back what the purchaser makes, falls outside the definition on its face (INFERENCE from the definition). The Ascend order nonetheless bars the defendants from selling "business coaching" products as well as business opportunities, so the Commission treats the two together when it fashions relief, whatever the definition requires for the Rule counts (VERIFIED against the FTC release of 23 June 2025). Where the Rule does apply, a post on a public feed that states an earnings level is a general-media claim under § 437.4(b), and the seller would need the dates and the number and percentage of purchasers who reached the level to appear immediately alongside it (INFERENCE as applied to a LinkedIn post). I found five posts (0.2%) that pair an income word with a dollar figure, and the data cannot show whether any is an offer.

The Operation AI Comply cases show what the FTC has pleaded when the elements are all present. On 25 September 2024 the Commission announced a set of actions under that heading, with the Chair saying that "there is no AI exemption from the laws on the books." Three of the actions concerned income schemes. Ascend Ecom, in the Central District of California, was alleged to have defrauded consumers of at least $25 million by promising passive income from "cutting edge" AI-powered tools, five-figure monthly income by the second year, and a guaranteed buyback, and to have pressured customers to change or remove negative reviews. Ecommerce Empire Builders, in the Eastern District of Pennsylvania, advertised an "AI-powered Ecommerce Empire" and, in social media ads, income of $10,000 a month that the FTC said it had no evidence for. Its training programs cost almost $2,000 and its "done for you" storefronts tens of thousands of dollars, and the release says the owner used as much as $35,000 of storefront buyers' money for personal gain. FBA Machine, formerly Passive Scaling, in the District of New Jersey, was alleged to have taken more than $15.9 million with promises of a "7-figure business," testimonials of over $100,000 in monthly profit, a "risk-free" venture and a refund guarantee. In each case the Commission voted 5-0 to file, and a federal court temporarily halted the scheme and appointed a receiver (VERIFIED against the FTC release of 25 September 2024, which did not name the statutes; I took those from the Ascend complaint).

The later outcomes are on record. A stipulated order in the Empire Builders case was entered on 8 May 2025. It permanently bans the company and its owner from selling business opportunities, bars unsubstantiated earnings claims and review-restricting contract terms, and enters a judgment of $9,786,124 that is partly suspended because the defendants could not pay in full, with assets such as rental properties and luxury watches turned over for refunds (VERIFIED against the FTC release; the Commission voted 3-0). On 23 June 2025 the FTC announced a proposed order in the Ascend case with a permanent ban on selling any business opportunity or business coaching, a $25 million judgment partly suspended, and a 3-0 vote (VERIFIED against the release). On 30 July 2025 it announced a proposed order against FBA Machine and its owner with a similar ban, a $15.7 million judgment partly suspended, the contents of several financial accounts to be turned over, and the owner's spouse named as a relief defendant who received proceeds and was not directly involved, by a 3-0 vote (VERIFIED against the release). In all three the full judgment becomes payable if a defendant is found to have misrepresented finances or assets. I did not confirm that the Ascend and FBA Machine orders were entered.

In March 2026 the Commission described the criminal side in a statement to Congress. It says the FTC sued 8 Figure Dream Lifestyle in 2019 over deceptive earnings claims and that three of its ringleaders were later criminally prosecuted and sentenced in 2024 and 2025 for conspiracy to commit wire fraud, that it sued IM Mastery Academy over a scheme the statement says has taken in over a billion dollars from consumers worldwide, and that it sued Air AI in August 2025, a case Chapter 12 covers (STATED; the statement is by an FTC associate director to the Joint Economic Committee on 25 March 2026, and I did not open court records).

The pattern those cases describe is a combination of an income figure, an AI or storefront product, a guarantee, and review suppression. In this topic, words for that kind of business appear in 7 posts (0.3%), and a guarantee or risk-free promise in 10 (0.4%). I cannot tell whether any of those posts is from a seller like the defendants.

Pod boosting adds three questions. The first is the one in Chapter 10. If an author who sells a program buys or procures likes that the author knew or should have known to be fake, § 465.8(b) applies, and the engagement serves as proof of popularity for the offer. The second concerns comments. A comment under a seller's post that says a program "changed my life" is likely to be read as a testimonial by a person who used it. If the commenter did not use the program, the comment is a candidate for § 465.2 if it is a consumer testimonial in the § 465.1(f) sense, and a member who writes it as an endorser faces § 255.1(a), (c) and (e), whether or not the seller asked for it (INFERENCE; I found no case that applies these rules to a comment prompted by a pod). Only 17 posts here (0.7%) use testimonial or results words, and the comment threads are not in the data. The third concerns typicality. A popular post that features one customer's result invites the question that § 255.2 answers for advertisements, which is what consumers generally achieve, and likes and comments do not answer it (INFERENCE). A purely personal story with no sale, which describes most of the 173 first-person posts, is not an advertisement on the face of the texts (INFERENCE).

The tool operator does not sell a business opportunity in the ordinary case and has no exposure under Part 437 (INFERENCE). Chapter 12 covered the one FTC action against a tool supplier, which the Commission set aside. The platform's position is the one in Chapter 10.

Table 14.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on motivation, mindset and growth, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 14.1.** Rules that reach posts on motivation, mindset and growth, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy or penalty stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 16 CFR § 437.4 (earnings claims) | Sellers of business opportunities | Ascend Ecom complaint, counts 6 to 8; FTC release 23 Jun 2025 | Permanent ban on selling business opportunities or coaching; $25 million judgment, partly suspended (VERIFIED) |
| FTC Act § 5(a) | Any seller making a false or unsubstantiated earnings claim | Ascend Ecom complaint, counts 1 to 3 | Same order as above; Empire Builders order of 8 May 2025: $9,786,124, partly suspended (VERIFIED) |
| 16 CFR § 255.1 and § 255.2 | Advertisers and endorsers; claims of typical results | None opened | Not stated in the text I read |
| Consumer Review Fairness Act, 15 U.S.C. § 45b(c) | Form contracts that restrict reviews | Ascend Ecom complaint, count 9 | Bar on such terms in the proposed order (VERIFIED) |
| Notice of Penalty Offenses on money-making opportunities | The 1,100 or more recipients | FTC release, 26 Oct 2021 | Up to $43,792 per violation at that date (VERIFIED) |
