| Directive 2005/29/EC, Art 6(1)(b), Annex I | Traders dealing with EU consumers | None opened | Not stated in the text I read |

### 14.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

Young adults report the highest share of scam losses that began on social media, and one in three reported losses to job or business-opportunity scams began there, according to the FTC data I opened.

The FTC's data spotlight of April 2026 reports that losses to fraud reported with social media as the contact method reached $2.1 billion in 2025, about eight times the $261 million of 2020. It says that one in three people who reported losing money to a job or business opportunity scam in 2025 said the scam started on social media, and that among people who reported a loss, the share whose scam started on social media was 40% for ages 18 to 29, 32% for ages 30 to 59, 29% for ages 60 to 69, 23% for ages 70 to 79 and 14% for those 80 and over. The page does not say whether 2025 is a calendar or fiscal year, the figures exclude reports that gave no contact method, and the FTC did not collect reports during the 2025 government shutdown (VERIFIED against the page). The page does not break out LinkedIn. I do not claim that any loss began with a post in this topic.

Interest among young adults in starting a business is high, according to one commercial survey. An Intuit survey of 1,000 people aged 18 to 35, fielded through Pollfish in November 2024, reports that 66% of Gen Z and millennial respondents combined have started or plan to start a side hustle, that over 44% rely on platforms such as Instagram, TikTok and LinkedIn as their primary marketing tools, and that 32% credit social media as the key driver of business success (STATED; a company's blog report of its own survey, not peer reviewed, and the 66% combines Gen Z with millennials and combines people who have started with people who plan to start). Chapter 11 supplied the baseline that a first business faces. The Bureau of Labor Statistics reports that of private-sector establishments born in March 2013, 79.6% were operating a year later, 50.6% after five years and 34.7% after ten (VERIFIED in Chapter 11, and the figures are for establishments and not for online stores).

Two features of the law bear on those readers. The first is that the law regulates the claim and the sale. It does not regulate the general message. The earnings-claim rules and the Guides address a specific level of income attached to an offer, and they test it against what purchasers generally achieve. The Business Opportunity Rule goes furthest, because paragraph (a) of § 437.4 requires the seller to state how many purchasers reached the level and over what period. A general statement that discipline and the right attitude produce success is not an earnings claim under § 437.1(f), because it conveys no "specific level or range" of income (INFERENCE from the definition). My inference is that a young reader receives legal protection at the point of an offer and receives none from the repeated general message that precedes it, and that the repeated message may shape what the reader expects an offer to deliver, a possibility no source I opened tests (INFERENCE).

The second feature is that the judgments are partly suspended, and the releases do not say how much consumers will receive. The three judgments I found, $25 million, $9,786,124 and $15.7 million, are each partly suspended because the defendants could not pay in full, and each release says assets are to be turned over for consumer redress without stating an amount (VERIFIED against the releases). The permanent bans stop the named sellers from selling business opportunities, and the *AMG Capital* decision means that the FTC reaches money through the Rule and § 19 and not through § 13(b) alone. For a generation that will come to these offers after the three cases, the practical protection is the deterrent effect of the bans and the 2021 notice, and I opened nothing that measures that effect (INFERENCE).

The topic's own content on wellbeing is a small part. Wellness words appear in 243 posts (10.0%) and burnout, mental-health, anxiety or depression words in 41 (1.7%). Chapter 10 cited the Surgeon General's advisory on social media and youth mental health, and I found no source that measures the effect of motivational posts on young people's decisions or wellbeing. Section 255.6 recognizes that advertising addressed to children may call for closer scrutiny, and only 58 posts (2.4%) mention students, teens, young people or children, so I cannot say that any post is addressed to minors.

### 14.5 How this topic bears on mental health

Motivational posts bear on mental health through two findings I opened, one on positive self-statements and one on work stress, and both suggest that the effect depends on who reads.

Mindset or motivation language appears in 1,616 of 2,420 posts (66.8%), and burnout, mental-health, anxiety or depression words in 41 (1.7%). Wood, Perunovic and Lee (2009, *Psychological Science*) found that people with low self-esteem who repeated the statement "I am a lovable person" felt worse afterward than a low-self-esteem group who did not, while people with high self-esteem felt slightly better. Low-self-esteem participants who were allowed to list negative thoughts as well had better moods than those told to focus only on positive thoughts (STATED; I read a summary, and the page gives no sample size). The authors concluded that positive self-statements "backfire for the very people who need them the most." A post that tells all readers to believe in themselves addresses both groups at once.

The World Health Organization defines burn-out in ICD-11 as "a syndrome conceptualized as resulting from chronic workplace stress that has not been successfully managed," with dimensions of exhaustion, cynicism or distance from one's job, and reduced professional efficacy. It says that burn-out is an occupational phenomenon and "is not classified as a medical condition." The Surgeon General's framework says that long work hours raise the risk of "exhaustion, anxiety, and depression." To the extent that posts in this topic praise relentless effort, they praise a condition the framework lists as a risk (INFERENCE; I counted words and not praise).

When a post attaches an income figure to an offer, the earnings-claim rules in Section 14.2 apply, and income language appears in 24 posts (1.0%). I found no study of people who paid for a program and did not earn what was claimed. The debt evidence in Section 9.5 is the nearest.

### 14.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show that any post in this topic is an earnings claim made to a prospective purchaser, that its author sells a business opportunity or a course, or that any statement is false. The posts seldom carry a price, a program name or a statement of how many buyers reached a result, which are the facts the rules turn on. A count of income words is a count of words. I cannot tell whether any engagement under a post was bought or prompted, whether any commenter used the program that the comment praises, or whether any reader paid anyone.

My reading of the law has gaps. For several primary texts, including §§ 437.1, 437.4, 437.9, 255.1, 255.2 and the *AMG Capital* opinion, the tool I used returned summaries and not the verbatim text, so I paraphrase them and quote only words it placed in quotation marks. I opened the Ascend Ecom complaint, and not the Empire Builders or FBA Machine complaints or any of the three orders, so I rely on the FTC's releases for their terms. I did not open §§ 437.2, 437.3 or 437.5 to 437.8 (I cite § 437.6(d) as the Ascend complaint does), the Franchise Rule, any state business-opportunity law, the text of Sections 5, 13(b) or 19 of the FTC Act, the Consumer Review Fairness Act, the 2021 notice itself, the court records of the 8 Figure Dream Lifestyle prosecutions, the amendments to the EU Directive, or any national law in the EU. Chapter 20 returns to earnings claims in the sale of courses, and Chapter 16 takes up § 255.5 and comments between members. Two questions remain open after this chapter. One is whether a comment prompted by a pod under a seller's offer is a consumer testimonial under § 465.1(f). The other is whether a seller of coaching alone, with no promise of locations, outlets, accounts or buyback, can be a seller of a business opportunity under § 437.1(c).

## Chapter 15. Strategy and planning: public signals, forward-looking statements and trade secrets

### 15.1 The topic in the data

Strategy and planning is the twelfth-largest topic, with 2,106 posts, which is 3.2% of the 64,970 placed posts. The podawaa file supplies 1,166 of them (55.4%), the 65 exports 626 (29.7%), HyperClapper 286 (13.6%) and LinkBoost-2025 28 (1.3%). The median post is 541 characters and the 90th percentile 1,408.

Words such as strategy, planning, roadmap, goal, OKR, KPI, objective, vision or mission appear in 2,083 posts (98.9%). That figure describes how the topic was built, because the model grouped posts by these words, and it is not a finding about the posts. The more informative counts are for what the posts discuss. Competitor, rival, market-share, benchmark, competitive-advantage or moat words appear in 131 (6.2%), and price, pricing, discount or margin words in 66 (3.1%). A phrase about changing a price (a price increase, hike or cut, "raise prices," a surcharge) appears in 3 (0.1%). Capacity, supply, demand, production or inventory words appear in 81 (3.8%). Forecast, projection, outlook or prediction words, a year from 2026 to 2029, or a phrase such as "next year" or "by 2030" appear in 112 (5.3%), and merger, acquisition, partnership, joint-venture, alliance or collaboration words in 110 (5.2%). Words about data, analytics, dashboards, AI, machine learning or automation appear in 381 (18.1%), consult, advisory or advisor words or the name of a large consulting firm in 178 (8.5%), and leadership, CEO, board, executive or C-suite words in 416 (19.8%). Startup, founder, scaling, venture or fundraising words appear in 202 (9.6%).

Words that bear on the legal questions below are less common. A word for price fixing, a price war, collusion, antitrust, a cartel, monopoly, competition law, the FTC, the DOJ or the Sherman Act appears in none of the 2,106 posts. A broader set of regulation words (regulation, compliance, GDPR, the AI Act, legislation, SEC) appears in 47 (2.2%). Investor, earnings, shareholder, quarterly, guidance, IPO or exchange-name words appear in 92 (4.4%), and "announce" words, "we are pleased" or similar phrases, or "our new" or "our latest" in 51 (2.4%). A phrase that signals a projection ("forward-looking," "we expect," "we believe," "will grow," "expected to," "projected") appears in 17 (0.8%), and a disclaimer phrase ("disclaimer," "not financial advice," "views are my own," "forward-looking") in 6 (0.3%). A word for confidential, proprietary, trade secret, NDA, non-compete, patent, IP or intellectual property appears in 12 (0.6%).

Workforce words are more common. Job, workforce, hiring, layoff, employee or talent words appear in 236 posts (11.2%), layoff, restructuring, headcount or return-to-office words in 16 (0.8%), and entry-level, early-career, new-graduate, intern or junior words in 11 (0.5%). Words about students, teens, Gen Z, kids, children, graduates, early-career workers or interns appear in 34 (1.6%). The word AI appears in 216 posts (10.3%), and 30 posts (1.4%) pair AI or automation with a job, workforce, worker, employee, talent or hiring word. The share of posts with the word AI differs by source: 48 of 1,166 in the podawaa file (4.1%), 60 of 286 in HyperClapper (21.0%), 4 of 28 in LinkBoost-2025 (14.3%) and 104 of 626 in the 65 exports (16.6%). I do not know the dates of the podawaa posts, so I draw no conclusion about time. A link appears in 494 posts (23.5%), five or more hashtags in 660 (31.3%), a DM-me call in 40 (1.9%), and a call to action such as "link in bio," "book a call," a free guide or a newsletter in 110 (5.2%). These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts words and not claims.

### 15.2 The laws that apply

Four sets of rules reach strategy talk: the rules on competitor signaling and information exchange, the securities rules on forward-looking statements and selective disclosure, the trade-secret statute, and, for the workforce posts, the federal plant-closing notice law.

**Competitor signaling: Sherman Act § 1 and Section 5.** Section 1 of the Sherman Act, 15 U.S.C. § 1, declares "illegal" every "contract, combination in the form of trust or otherwise, or conspiracy, in restraint of trade or commerce among the several States, or with foreign nations." A person who makes such a contract or joins such a combination or conspiracy commits a felony, punishable by a fine of up to $100,000,000 for a corporation and $1,000,000 for any other person, or imprisonment of up to 10 years, or both (VERIFIED against the Justia copy of the 2024 Code). The text requires a contract, combination or conspiracy, so a company's unilateral statement of its own plans is not covered unless it forms part of an agreement (INFERENCE from the text; I did not open the case law).

The FTC has reached public signals through Section 5. In a proposed consent order with U-Haul International and its parent, described in a Federal Register analysis of 21 June 2010 issued to aid public comment, the Commission charged an "invitation to collude," meaning an attempt to get competitors to raise one-way truck rental prices. The analysis says that the company's chief executive told regional managers in 2006 to raise rates and then tell a competitor that the company would lower its rates unless the competitor followed, that a regional manager called the competitor in late 2006 and October 2007, that in November 2007 the chief executive ordered a nationwide increase, and that in a February 2008 earnings call he described the company as the industry's "price leader." It says the company "acted with the specific intent to facilitate collusion." The complaint did not allege an agreement. The analysis explains that an unaccepted invitation can still cause harm, that the Commission does not need to prove market power, a defined market or substantial competitive harm, and that "the public statements made by the CEO of U-Haul could have encouraged competitors to raise rates." It adds that "it is possible that less egregious conduct may result in Section 5 liability." The proposed order would bar inviting a competitor to divide markets, allocate customers or fix prices, and discussing rates with competitors except for legitimate market research. It has provisos that let the company negotiate prices with customers, share strategy information with investors and make disclosures required by securities law, and it would expire after 20 years (VERIFIED against the analysis, as summarized by the fetch tool). I did not confirm that the order was made final.

The agencies' own guidance has lapsed. On 11 December 2024 the FTC and the Department of Justice withdrew their Antitrust Guidelines for Collaborations Among Competitors of April 2000, saying that the guidelines "no longer provide reliable guidance about how enforcers assess the legality of collaborations involving competitors" and that businesses should review the statutes and case law, and committing to "vigorous antitrust enforcement on a case-by-case basis." The Commission voted 3-2, with two commissioners dissenting. The page does not mention information sharing (VERIFIED against the FTC page). I opened no replacement guidance.

**The EU: Article 101 TFEU.** According to a law-firm overview of the Commission's revised Horizontal Guidelines, adopted in June 2023 and effective on 21 July 2023, Article 101(1) applies where an exchange of commercially sensitive information is likely to affect competitors' commercial strategy, mainly by reducing market uncertainty. The overview says the guidelines contain no safe harbour for information exchange, that an exchange is a restriction by object when the information is commercially sensitive and the exchange "is capable of removing uncertainty between participants," that publicly available information can still be restricted if it is exchanged in a more granular form, more often than it is published or with added analysis, and that a unilateral public announcement does not exempt the company because it can signal future intentions to competitors, with the consumer benefit as the key question and a public signal of future prices or quantities possibly treated as a restriction by object depending on context (STATED; I did not open the guidelines or the Treaty text).

**Securities: forward-looking statements and Regulation FD.** Section 21E of the Exchange Act, 15 U.S.C. § 78u-5, gives a safe harbor to forward-looking statements by an issuer subject to the reporting requirements, by "any officer, director, or employee of such issuer," and by an outside reviewer or underwriter in defined cases. In a private action, a covered person is not liable for a forward-looking statement if it is "identified as a forward-looking statement, and is accompanied by meaningful cautionary statements," or is "immaterial," or if the plaintiff fails to prove that it was made with actual knowledge that it was false or misleading. Subsection (c)(2) lets an oral statement meet the cautionary requirement by an oral warning plus a reference to a readily available written document. The definition in subsection (i)(1) includes projections of revenues, income and earnings, "statements of management's plans and objectives for future operations," statements of future economic performance and statements of the assumptions underlying them. The safe harbor does not apply to statements in connection with an initial public offering, a tender offer, a penny stock offering or a blank check company, and it does not apply to statements in financial statements prepared under GAAP, among other exclusions in subsection (b), and subsection (d) says there is no duty to update (VERIFIED against the Cornell text, as summarized by the fetch tool).

Regulation FD concerns who may hear material nonpublic information first. In its Report of Investigation of 2 April 2013 on Netflix, the SEC described a post on the chief executive's personal Facebook page, which had more than 200,000 subscribers including analysts, shareholders and reporters, stating that the company's monthly viewing had passed one billion hours. No press release, Form 8-K or post on a company channel accompanied it, the company had not told investors it would use the page for company metrics, and the company's stock rose from about $70.45 to $81.72 by the next day's close. The Commission applied its 2008 guidance on company web sites to social media. It said that "issuer communications through social media channels require careful Regulation FD analysis," that the practical solution is to give investors notice in advance of the specific channels a company will use, and that disclosing material nonpublic information on an individual officer's personal site "without advance notice to investors that the site may be used for this purpose" is unlikely to satisfy Regulation FD's broad distribution standard even if the site has many followers. It "has determined not to pursue an enforcement action," and the report states that it adjudicates no fact or issue (VERIFIED against the SEC's page, as summarized). I did not open Regulation FD itself, 17 CFR § 243.100.

**Trade secrets: 18 U.S.C. § 1839.** A "trade secret" is business, financial, technical or other information, including "plans," "methods" and "procedures," if "the owner thereof has taken reasonable measures to keep such information secret" and the information "derives independent economic value, actual or potential, from not being generally known to, and not being readily ascertainable through proper means by, another person who can obtain economic value from the disclosure or use of the information." Misappropriation includes acquiring a trade secret by improper means and disclosing or using it without consent in defined circumstances (VERIFIED against the govinfo text of the 2021 Code, as summarized). I did not open the remedies sections of the Defend Trade Secrets Act or any state statute.

**The workforce: the WARN Act.** The Department of Labor's page on plant closings and layoffs says that the Worker Adjustment and Retraining Notification Act covers employers with 100 or more employees, applies to a plant closing or mass layoff affecting 50 or more employees at a single site of employment, and requires "at least 60 calendar days advance written notice" to affected employees, their representatives, the local chief elected official and the state dislocated worker unit, subject to exceptions for unforeseeable business circumstances, faltering companies and natural disasters. The page states no penalties and says the Department has no role in seeking damages for workers (VERIFIED against the page, as summarized). I did not open the statute, 29 U.S.C. § 2101 and following sections, or any state law.

### 15.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

The rules reach a strategy post when the author speaks for a company that competes with others, has public shareholders or holds secrets, so the first question is who the author is and what the post commits the company to.

An author who writes general commentary on strategy, planning or leadership and does not state a company's plans is outside the rules in Section 15.2 (INFERENCE from the texts I opened). The word counts suggest that most of this topic is of that kind. The three signals that would bring a post within the competition rules are a price change, a statement of capacity or output, and a message addressed to competitors. A phrase about changing a price appears in 3 posts (0.1%), capacity or supply words in 81 (3.8%), and competitor words in 131 (6.2%), and no post uses antitrust vocabulary. The U-Haul facts show that the Commission relied on more than a public statement: private calls to a competitor, an instruction to tell the competitor that rates would fall unless it followed, and a stated intent (VERIFIED against the analysis). The same analysis also treats the chief executive's public statements as capable of encouraging competitors, and it says that less egregious conduct may lead to liability (VERIFIED). Whether a single public post that announces a price or an intended capacity cut, with no private contact, is an invitation to collude is a question that source does not settle, and a post by a company officer that describes a price plan would fall in the range it leaves open (INFERENCE).

An author who is an officer of a public company and writes about the company's future meets the securities rules. A post that states plans or projections is a forward-looking statement in the sense of § 78u-5(i)(1), and the safe harbor needs the statement to be identified as forward-looking and accompanied by meaningful cautionary statements. The oral-statement provision in subsection (c)(2) covers spoken statements, and a post is written, so a post would need its own cautionary language or a written document that it refers to (INFERENCE from the text as summarized). In the data, 92 posts (4.4%) use investor or earnings words and 17 (0.8%) use projection phrases, and 6 (0.3%) contain a disclaimer phrase of any kind, which is too few to suggest that cautionary language is routine. I cannot tell which authors are officers of issuers. The Netflix report concerned a personal Facebook page, and its reasoning would extend to an officer's LinkedIn profile unless the company has told investors that it will use the profile for disclosures (INFERENCE from the SEC's reasoning).

Pod boosting adds three questions. The first is the one in Chapter 10. A business that buys or procures engagement that it knew or should have known to be fake is within § 465.8(b), as Chapter 10 explained, and a company announcement with bought engagement would be an instance. The second concerns Regulation FD. The Commission said that a large following does not make a personal page a recognized channel, which suggests that a follower count, real or inflated, does not decide the matter, and the question is whether investors were told to expect disclosures there (INFERENCE from the report). The third concerns antifraud liability. A statement of strategy that is false, made in connection with the purchase or sale of a security and amplified by purchased engagement, meets Rule 10b-5 as discussed in Chapter 13, and whether engagement is part of a scheme is the open question stated there.

An author who posts a company's confidential plans publishes them to the public, and the published parts cease to meet the "not being generally known" condition of § 1839(3)(B), while the "reasonable measures" condition of (A) is also hard to meet for information the owner itself posted (INFERENCE from the definition). The author who publishes another company's secrets without consent faces the misappropriation provisions, if the author knew or had reason to know that the information came through improper means or a breach of a duty of secrecy (INFERENCE from the summary of § 1839(5)). Twelve posts (0.6%) use confidentiality, proprietary or patent words, and I cannot tell what any of them disclosed.

The engaging member, the tool operator and the platform have no exposure under these rules in the ordinary case. The members do not speak for an issuer or a competitor, the tool operator does not compete with the authors' companies, and the platform's position is the one in Chapter 10 (INFERENCE).

Table 15.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on strategy and planning, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 15.1.** Rules that reach posts on strategy and planning, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy or penalty stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Sherman Act § 1, 15 U.S.C. § 1 | Any person party to a contract, combination or conspiracy in restraint of trade | None opened | Fine up to $100,000,000 (corporation) or $1,000,000 (other person), imprisonment up to 10 years (VERIFIED) |
| FTC Act § 5, invitation to collude | A company that invites a competitor to fix prices or divide markets | U-Haul proposed consent order, 2010 | Proposed order barring such invitations and rate discussions with competitors, 20 years (VERIFIED) |
| Article 101 TFEU and the 2023 Horizontal Guidelines | Competitors that exchange commercially sensitive information | None opened | Not stated in the source (STATED) |
| Exchange Act § 21E, 15 U.S.C. § 78u-5 | Issuers and their officers, directors and employees | None opened | A defense, not a penalty (VERIFIED) |
| Regulation FD, as applied to social media | Public issuers and their officers | SEC Report of Investigation, 2 Apr 2013 | No enforcement action; guidance only (VERIFIED) |
| 18 U.S.C. § 1839 | Owners and misappropriators of trade secrets | None opened | Not stated in the part I read |
| WARN Act | Employers with 100 or more employees | None opened | Not stated on the page I read |

### 15.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

The rules in Section 15.2 protect consumers, investors and employees of existing firms, and they protect people who are not yet in the workforce only indirectly (INFERENCE from the texts I opened).

Competition law addresses harm to the competitive process, and consumers of every age bear that harm (INFERENCE). The agencies say that competitor collaborations "can harm competition and subvert the competitive process" (VERIFIED against the FTC page). The proposed U-Haul order would bar the company from inviting competitors to fix prices or divide markets. The withdrawal of the 2000 guidelines in 2024 removed a document that businesses had used to judge collaborations with competitors, and the agencies tell businesses to review the statutes and case law instead (VERIFIED). I found no replacement guidance.

The securities rules protect investors, among them the young investors in Chapter 13. The Netflix report records a stock price that rose from about $70.45 to $81.72 by the close of the day after one personal post, and it records that the Commission chose guidance and not enforcement (VERIFIED). The report does not say that the post caused the rise. I do not claim that any post in this topic disclosed material nonpublic information.

The workforce posts matter for coming generations. In this topic 236 posts (11.2%) discuss jobs, hiring, talent or employees, 30 (1.4%) pair AI or automation with workforce words, and 11 (0.5%) use entry-level, early-career, new-graduate, intern or junior words. The Federal Reserve Bank of New York's page on the labor market for recent college graduates reports, for the second quarter of 2026, an unemployment rate of about 5.6 percent and an underemployment rate of 42 percent that "edged up." Its frequently asked questions define early-career graduates as aged 22 to 27, and the page does not state the all-worker unemployment rate (VERIFIED against the page, which I read as a summary). Chapter 12 reported a Stanford and ADP paper that found employment of workers aged 22 to 25 in AI-exposed occupations 19% below where it would be had it kept pace with less-exposed peers, a descriptive finding and not a causal one.

The WARN Act addresses a plant closing or a mass layoff of 50 or more employees at a single site and requires 60 days' notice. A decision to hire fewer entry-level workers closes no plant and lays off no one, so by the description I read the Act does not apply to it (INFERENCE). My inference is that the labor-market effect that the two sources above measure, if it is caused by firms' strategies, falls on people for whom no notice rule exists because they were never employed there (INFERENCE; neither source establishes a cause, and I opened no source on antitrust law as it applies to hiring or wages). Only 11 posts use early-career words, so the topic itself says little on the subject.

### 15.5 How this topic bears on mental health

The mental-health evidence that fits strategy posts concerns job loss, and its authors report support for a causal reading, which is rare among the sources I opened.

Job, workforce, hiring, layoff, employee or talent words appear in 236 of 2,106 posts (11.2%), layoff, restructuring, headcount or return-to-office words in 16 (0.8%), and entry-level, early-career, new-graduate, intern or junior words in 11 (0.5%). Paul and Moser (2009, *Journal of Vocational Behavior*) combined 237 cross-sectional and 87 longitudinal studies. Unemployed people showed more distress than employed people (d = 0.51), and 34% of the unemployed had psychological problems, against 16% of the employed. The authors report that longitudinal studies and natural experiments support the view that unemployment causes distress, and that the effect was larger in countries with weaker unemployment protection. I read the abstract record, which gives no participant total.

Insecurity affects people who still have jobs. In the American Psychological Association's 2025 survey, 54% of 2,017 workers said that job insecurity had significantly affected their stress at work, and among workers who were worried about an economic downturn, 42% said that work stress made it hard to sleep.

The rules in Section 15.2 include layoff notice under the WARN Act and what a company may say about its plans. A notice requirement gives workers time to prepare, and a false statement about plans can mislead workers and investors. I found no study of whether either changes mental-health outcomes (INFERENCE as to the route).

### 15.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show that any post in this topic states a price plan, signals to a competitor, projects a company's results, discloses material nonpublic information or reveals a trade secret. The counts are of words. A post that uses "we expect" may describe a personal goal, and a post that uses "confidential" may describe a book on confidentiality. I cannot tell whether an author is an officer of a public company, whether any engagement under a post was bought or prompted, or whether any reader or competitor acted on a post.

My reading of the law has gaps. Several of the primary texts, including 15 U.S.C. § 78u-5, 18 U.S.C. § 1839, the Sherman Act text and the SEC and FTC documents, were returned by the fetch tool as summaries with short quotations, and I quote only the words it placed in quotation marks. I read the Article 101 material through a law-firm overview and not the guidelines. I did not open Regulation FD, the case law on § 1 and on invitations to collude, any replacement for the withdrawn guidelines, the SEC's 2008 guidance on web sites, the Defend Trade Secrets Act's remedy provisions, the state trade-secret statutes, the WARN statute and its state counterparts, or any antitrust guidance on hiring and wages. Two questions remain open after this chapter. One is whether a single public post that announces a price or capacity plan, without private contact, can be an invitation to collude under Section 5. The other is whether an inflated audience on an officer's personal page has any bearing on the Regulation FD question of whether a channel is recognized by investors.

## Chapter 16. Building relationships and community: endorsements, material connections and social connection

### 16.1 The topic in the data

Building relationships and community is the thirteenth-largest topic, with 2,096 posts, which is 3.2% of the 64,970 placed posts. The 65 exports supply the largest share, 928 posts (44.3%), followed by HyperClapper with 570 (27.2%), the podawaa file with 547 (26.1%) and LinkBoost-2025 with 51 (2.4%). The median post is 423 characters and the 90th percentile 1,228. Five or more hashtags appear in 314 posts (15.0%), which is less than half the rate in the motivation topic of Chapter 14 (33.5%), and a link appears in 326 (15.6%).

The vocabulary of connection is common. Relationship, network, connection, bond or rapport words appear in 328 posts (15.6%), and community, tribe, belonging, group, member or peer words in 190 (9.1%). Words of trust, authenticity, empathy, kindness, gratitude or thanks appear in 390 (18.6%), and a word of recognition (thank you, congratulations, shout-out, proud of, celebrate, kudos) in 119 (5.7%). Mentor, sponsor, coach, "give first," generosity or "helping others" words appear in 62 (3.0%), and an introduction or referral word in 23 (1.1%). Words about family, friends, partners or dating appear in 117 (5.6%), and a romance or dating word in 7 (0.3%). Loneliness, isolation, "alone," mental health, well-being, burnout or belonging words appear in 81 (3.9%), and the word human or authentic in 81 (3.9%). In-person, face-to-face, meetup, coffee, event, conference, lunch or dinner words appear in 85 (4.1%), and remote, virtual, online, Zoom, webinar or digital words in 95 (4.5%). Words about students, teens, Gen Z, kids, children, graduates, early-career workers, interns, young professionals, the next generation or the word young appear in 75 (3.6%).

Commercial language is present in a minority. Lead, prospect, client, customer, sales, pipeline or outreach words appear in 202 posts (9.6%), sponsor, partnership, ambassador or "partnered with" words in 31 (1.5%), and influencer, creator, personal-brand, follower, viral or algorithm words in 89 (4.2%). A DM-me call appears in 18 (0.9%) and a call to comment or follow in 31 (1.5%). A networking ask (let's connect, connect with me, follow me, send a request, reach out, DM, message me, comment below, tag someone, share this) appears in 96 (4.6%).

The words for the exchange itself are nearly absent. A plain request to like, comment, share, repost or follow (such as "please like," "support my post" or "comment below"), or the words "engagement pod" or "pod," appears in 52 posts (2.5%), and the word "pod" or "engagement pod" in 2 (0.1%). A reciprocity phrase (reciprocity, return the favor, comment back, like back, follow back, support each other, pay it forward, give back) appears in 4 (0.2%). A phrase about fake accounts, profiles, followers, engagement or likes, bot accounts or impersonation appears in none of the 2,096 posts, and scam, fraud, phishing, impersonation, safety or privacy words in 36 (1.7%).

Automation and AI words are more common. The word AI appears in 274 posts (13.1%), with large differences by source: 22 of 547 in the podawaa file (4.0%), 128 of 570 in HyperClapper (22.5%), 8 of 51 in LinkBoost-2025 (15.7%) and 116 of 928 in the 65 exports (12.5%). Automation, automated, bot or auto-comment words appear in 67 (3.2%), and a post that pairs the word AI with a relationship, connection, community, trust, human or loneliness word appears in 54 (2.6%). I do not know the dates of the podawaa posts and draw no conclusion about time. These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts words and not claims. A post that praises a community is counted with a post that sells access to one.

This topic is where a reader would expect to see the exchange described, because it is about relationships. The text shows relationship talk and almost no talk about exchanging engagement.

### 16.2 The laws that apply

Three sets of rules matter here beyond Chapters 10 and 14: the Endorsement Guides on what counts as an endorsement and what counts as a material connection, the FTC's guidance for people who post, and the EU's rules on concealed commercial intent. The Surgeon General's 2023 advisory on loneliness supplies the factual background for Section 16.4 and is not a legal rule.

**What counts as an endorsement: 16 CFR § 255.0.** The Guides define an endorsement as any advertising, marketing or promotional message for a product that consumers are likely to believe reflects the opinions, experiences or findings of a party other than the sponsoring advertiser, even when those views match the advertiser's. According to the summary I read, the definition lets social media tags, demonstrations and depictions of a person's likeness count. The endorser is the party whose opinions, beliefs, findings or experience the message appears to reflect, and the party can be an individual, a group or an institution. The Guides define a clear and conspicuous disclosure as one that is "difficult to miss" and "easily understandable by ordinary consumers," and in interactive media it "should be unavoidable" (VERIFIED against the Cornell text of § 255.0, as summarized by the fetch tool). The page does not use the phrase "material connection," which § 255.5 does.

**Material connections: 16 CFR § 255.5.** Paragraph (a) says that "material connections can include a business, family, or personal relationship." They can include "monetary payment or the provision of free or discounted products," whether or not the products are related and "regardless of whether the advertiser requires an endorsement in return," and "other benefits to the endorser, such as early access to a product," or the chance of being paid or of winning a prize. A connection must be disclosed when a significant minority of the audience would not understand or expect it, and the disclosure must "clearly communicate the nature of the connection sufficiently for consumers to evaluate its significance." The examples apply this to a tennis player who received discounted surgery and posts on social media (Example 3), a physician (Example 4), reviewers given a free product and a $30 payment (Example 6), a woodworker given a lathe (Example 7), employees who post about their employer (Example 8), a program that gives points redeemable for prizes (Example 9) and affiliate links (Example 11). Example 9 says that points redeemable for prizes are material incentives that "should be clearly and conspicuously disclosed," and that the advertiser should ensure that the disclosures are made (VERIFIED against the Cornell text of § 255.5, as summarized; the page does not mention likes or follows).

**The FTC's guidance for people who post.** The Commission's guide "Disclosures 101 for Social Media Influencers" says to "disclose when you have any financial, employment, personal, or family relationship with a brand," not to "assume your followers already know about your brand relationships," and that "it's your responsibility to make these disclosures." It also says: "Keep in mind that tags, likes, pins, and similar ways of showing you like a brand or product can be endorsements." It does not discuss giveaways, follows or penalties (VERIFIED against the page, as summarized; the URL of the Commission's announcement shows November 2019, and I could not confirm the date of the version I read).

**Fake testimonials and fake indicators.** Chapter 10 set out Part 465. Section 465.1(f) defines a consumer testimonial as a promotional message that consumers are likely to believe reflects the opinions or experiences of a consumer who purchased or used the product, and § 465.2 bars fake or false testimonials. Section 465.1(d) defines a consumer review by reference to a platform dedicated, in whole or in part, to receiving evaluations, so a comment on a general feed is probably not a consumer review (INFERENCE from the reading in Chapter 10). Section 465.1(h) includes within "fake indicators" those metrics that "otherwise do not reflect a real individual's or entity's activities, opinions, findings, or experiences," and Chapter 10 left open whether a like given in return for a like reflects a real individual's opinion (VERIFIED in Chapter 10).

**The EU.** The Unfair Commercial Practices Directive 2005/29/EC treats a practice as a misleading omission under Article 7(2) when a trader "fails to identify the commercial intent of the commercial practice if not already apparent from the context" and the omission is likely to lead the average consumer to a transactional decision they would not otherwise take. Annex I, which lists practices that are unfair in all circumstances, includes in point 11 "using editorial content in the media to promote a product where a trader has paid for the promotion" without making that clear, and in point 22 "falsely claiming or creating the impression that the trader is not acting for purposes relating to his trade, business, craft or profession, or falsely representing oneself as a consumer" (VERIFIED against the Directive as adopted in 2005, read on the UK legislation site, so later amendments are not reflected). Chapter 10 discussed points 23b and 23c, which were added later and concern false reviews and social endorsements, and the open question of whether a prompted like is an endorsement.

### 16.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

The rules reach a post about relationships only when the post promotes something and the person behind it has a connection to the seller that the audience would not expect.

An author who writes about friendship, mentoring, belonging or networking and promotes no product is outside the Endorsement Guides, because the definition in § 255.0 requires a message for a product (INFERENCE from the text). That description fits the large majority of this topic, if the word counts are a guide. Words of trust, authenticity and thanks appear in 390 posts (18.6%), and sales, client or customer words in 202 (9.6%).

An author who thanks a client, praises a partner or promotes a community that the author runs may be making a promotional message about a product. If the author has a business, family, personal or employment relationship with the seller, § 255.5(a) and the FTC guide call for disclosure when a significant minority of the audience would not expect the relationship (INFERENCE as applied). Sponsor, partnership or ambassador words appear in 31 posts (1.5%), and a thank-you or gratitude word within sixty characters of a client, customer, partner or sponsor word in 4 (0.2%). I cannot tell which of these posts are promotional or whether the relationships were disclosed.

The pod adds a question that none of the sources I opened answers: whether reciprocal engagement is a material connection. The FTC guide says that likes can be endorsements. Section 255.5 counts "other benefits to the endorser" and says that a connection can exist "regardless of whether the advertiser requires an endorsement in return," and Example 9 treats points redeemable for prizes as a material incentive. A member who likes or comments on a seller's promotional post, expecting the seller to engage with the member's own posts in return, receives a benefit that comes from the seller, and the benefit is the kind of thing the Guides list (INFERENCE). Several facts are needed for that reading and are not in the data. The liked post must promote a product, the like must be a message that consumers are likely to believe reflects the member's opinion of the product, the exchange must exist, and a significant minority of the audience must not expect it. If a pod tool gives members credits that they spend on engagement, the credit resembles the points in Example 9, but I do not know whether any tool in the data does so, and I did not read their terms (INFERENCE). Where the like is given to a post that does not promote a product, the Guides do not apply on their face.

The author who buys or procures engagement meets § 465.8(b) under the conditions in Chapter 10. The author who commissions praise from people who have not used the product meets § 465.2 if the praise is a consumer testimonial under § 465.1(f), and in the EU may meet Annex I point 22 if the praise is presented as coming from consumers when it comes from the trader's side (INFERENCE; I found no case that applies either provision to a comment prompted by a pod). The tool operator is not an advertiser or endorser in the ordinary case, and Chapter 12 covered the one FTC case against a tool supplier, which the Commission set aside. The platform's position is the one in Chapter 10.

Table 16.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on building relationships and community, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 16.1.** Rules that reach posts on building relationships and community, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy or penalty stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 16 CFR § 255.0 and § 255.5 | Advertisers and endorsers; connections the audience would not expect | None opened | Not stated in the text I read |
| FTC guide, "Disclosures 101 for Social Media Influencers" | People who post about brands | None opened | Not stated on the page I read |
| 16 CFR §§ 465.1(f) and 465.2 | Businesses that create or sell fake testimonials | See Chapter 10 | See Chapter 10 |
| 16 CFR § 465.8 | Sellers and buyers of fake indicators | See Chapter 10 | See Chapter 10 |
| Directive 2005/29/EC, Art 7(2) and Annex I points 11 and 22 | Traders that conceal commercial intent or pose as consumers | None opened | Not stated in the text I read |

### 16.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

The law I opened protects an audience from deception about who is speaking and why. It does not protect the connection between people that the topic is about.

The Surgeon General's advisory of 2023, "Our Epidemic of Loneliness and Isolation," reports that about one in two adults in America reported experiencing loneliness, that loneliness and social isolation raise the risk of premature death by 26% and 29%, and that lacking social connection "can increase the risk for premature death as much as smoking up to 15 cigarettes a day." It reports that for people aged 15 to 24, time spent in person with friends fell by nearly 70% over almost two decades, from roughly 150 minutes a day in 2003 to 40 minutes a day in 2020, and that young adults are almost twice as likely as people over 65 to report feeling lonely. On technology, it says that technology can foster connection by providing opportunities to stay in touch, and it names "technology that displaces in-person engagement, monopolizes our attention" among the harms. It cites a U.S. study in which participants who used social media for more than two hours a day had about double the odds of reporting increased social isolation compared with those who used it for under 30 minutes. Its six pillars include "Reform Digital Environments" and "Cultivate a Culture of Connection" (VERIFIED against the advisory, of which the tool returned part; the figures are the advisory's own, and the social-media finding is from a single study the advisory cites).

Those findings do not mention engagement pods, and I found no source that does. They bear on this study through one question that Chapter 5 left open: whether prompted reciprocal engagement adds to in-person connection or replaces it. In this topic, 85 posts (4.1%) use in-person words, against 95 (4.5%) that use remote or virtual words, and 81 (3.9%) use loneliness or belonging words. The advisory's association between displaced in-person time and loneliness gives a reason to ask the question, and no source I opened measures the effect of a pod on any member's connections or well-being (INFERENCE).

A second consideration involves automation. The word AI appears in 274 posts (13.1%) and automation or bot words in 67 (3.2%), and 54 posts (2.6%) pair AI with a word about relationships, trust, community, humans or loneliness. The FTC opened an inquiry into companion chatbots in September 2025, covered in Chapter 12, and the Pew survey of teens in Chapter 12 reported that 12% use chatbots for emotional support. Those facts concern chatbots that talk with young people, and they do not show that any post in this topic describes one. I draw from them only that the question of what replaces human contact has two parts, automated conversation and automated engagement, and that the rules I opened address neither as a matter of connection (INFERENCE).

A third consideration involves the people entering a profession. Early-career workers, students and young professionals are named in 75 posts (3.6%). A young professional who builds a network on LinkedIn may read likes and comments as signals of whom to trust, and a pod that inflates them could mislead that reader about which relationships matter (INFERENCE). The disclosure rules would protect that reader only where the post promotes a product and a connection was not disclosed. The rule aimed at a misleading signal is the fake-indicators rule, and Chapter 10 found no complaint or order under it.

My conclusion for this chapter is that the protections in the law attach to commercial speech and to counterfeit signals, and that the social effect on the next generation, if there is one, falls in a space no rule I opened addresses (INFERENCE). The advisory's call for policy reform of digital environments is the nearest statement of a public aim, and I opened no statute or rule that adopts it.

### 16.5 How this topic bears on mental health

This topic is where the loneliness evidence applies most directly, and the question that the data cannot answer is whether prompted reciprocal engagement adds to connection or replaces it.

Words of trust, authenticity, empathy, kindness, gratitude or thanks appear in 390 of 2,096 posts (18.6%), and loneliness, isolation, "alone," mental health, well-being, burnout or belonging words in 81 (3.9%). The Surgeon General's 2023 advisory (Section 16.4) says that the odds of developing depression are more than double among people who often feel lonely. It reports that, in a U.S. study, people who used social media for more than two hours a day "had about double the odds" of reporting increased social isolation compared with people who used it under 30 minutes, and it says that the evidence on social media and relationships "has been complex," with both benefits and harms. Naslund and colleagues (2016, *Epidemiology and Psychiatric Sciences*) describe online peer support for people with serious mental illness. They name benefits, such as feeling less alone and less stigma, and risks, such as unreliable advice and dependency, and they conclude that the benefits appear to outweigh the risks while noting that few studies collect objective health outcomes. The article is a commentary and not an empirical study.

A post that thanks a colleague or praises a community is the ordinary material of those sources. A pod comment that a member writes because the pod expects it differs in one respect that the sources do not address: the recipient may take it to reflect the writer's opinion. Whether receiving such a comment feels like support, or feels like less once the reader learns its source, is untested (INFERENCE). The data show 52 posts (2.5%) with a plain request to like, comment, share or follow or with the word "pod," and 2 posts with the word "pod."

### 16.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show that any post in this topic is promotional, that its author has a connection to a seller, that any engagement under it was exchanged, or that any comment was a testimonial. The text of the posts almost never describes the exchange, and the comment threads are not in the data. A count of trust words or AI words is a count of words. I cannot tell which authors are young, which readers are lonely, or whether any reader formed or lost a relationship because of a post.

My reading of the law has gaps. The tool I used returned summaries of §§ 255.0 and 255.5, the FTC guide and the Surgeon General's advisory, and I quote only the words it placed in quotation marks. The advisory text was truncated, and I did not read its recommendations in full. I did not open § 465.4 or § 465.5, any case that applies the Endorsement Guides to likes, the terms of any pod tool, privacy law as it applies to tagging and photographs in community posts (Chapter 8 covers outreach), the amendments to the EU Directive, or any national law. Two questions remain open after this chapter. One is whether reciprocal engagement, given in expectation of engagement in return, is a material connection under § 255.5 when the liked post promotes the poster's own product. The other is the question from Chapter 5, whether prompted reciprocal engagement adds to or replaces in-person connection, which no source I opened measures.

## Chapter 17. Supply chain, logistics and operations: forced labor, customs fraud, tariffs and due diligence

### 17.1 The topic in the data

Supply chain, logistics and operations is the fourteenth-largest topic, with 2,000 posts, which is 3.1% of the 64,970 placed posts. The podawaa file supplies 1,411 posts (70.6%), the 65 exports 439 (22.0%), HyperClapper 142 (7.1%) and LinkBoost-2025 8 (0.4%). The median post is 534 characters and the 90th percentile 1,254. Five or more hashtags appear in 1,223 posts (61.1%), which compares with 33.5% in the motivation topic of Chapter 14 and 15.0% in the relationships topic of Chapter 16. The share differs by source: 1,024 of the 1,411 podawaa posts (72.6%) carry five or more hashtags, against 174 of the 439 posts in the 65 exports (39.6%). A link appears in 425 posts (21.2%).

The vocabulary is the vocabulary of a trade. Logistics, freight, shipping, shipment, warehouse, fulfillment, last-mile, cargo, trucking, carrier or port words appear in 1,232 posts (61.6%), and the phrase "supply chain" in 743 (37.1%). Manufacturing, factory, supplier, sourcing, procurement or inventory words appear in 667 (33.4%). Words about e-commerce, dropshipping, Amazon, Shopify, FBA, online stores or marketplaces appear in 297 (14.8%), of which 261 are in the podawaa file. A disruption, resilience, volatility or shortage word appears in 187 (9.3%), a quality, safety, recall, defect or inspection word in 161 (8.1%), and a food, pharmaceutical, medicine or cold-chain word in 99 (5.0%). Words for a critical material or component (semiconductor, chip, rare earth, lithium, cobalt, battery, steel, aluminum, solar, polysilicon, cotton) appear in 45 (2.2%).

The words that connect the topic to the law are much rarer. A tariff, customs, duty, anti-dumping or de minimis word appears in 37 posts (1.9%), and a broader trade word (adding import, export, trade war, trade policy and sanctions) in 63 (3.1%). The words China, Chinese, Xinjiang or Shenzhen appear in 35 (1.8%). Words about forced labor, modern slavery, child labor, trafficking, the UFLPA, Xinjiang or the Uyghurs appear in 3 posts (0.1%), and words about human rights, labor rights, fair labor or ethical or responsible sourcing in 6 (0.3%). Counterfeit, fraud, theft, smuggling, transshipment, illicit or sanctions words appear in 19 (0.9%). EU-law words (CSDDD, CSRD, CBAM, EUDR, due diligence, GDPR, "EU regulation," European Union or EU) appear in 16 (0.8%). No post in the topic uses a "made in" phrase.

Claims about conduct are more common than claims about law. Words of ethics, responsibility, compliance, certification, sustainability, traceability or transparency appear in 262 posts (13.1%), and environmental words (eco-friendly, carbon neutral, net zero, green, sustainable, recyclable, biodegradable, climate) in 168 (8.4%). A claim of certification, guarantee or a "100%" property (certified, guaranteed, fully compliant, zero waste) appears in 13 (0.7%).

The topic also speaks of automation more than of people. The word AI or the phrase artificial intelligence appears in 180 posts (9.0%), and a broader automation set (adding automate, robot, machine learning, predictive and digital twin) in 463 (23.1%). Worker, workforce, labor, employee or driver words appear in 130 (6.5%), which is about 28% as many as the broad automation set, although the two sets differ in breadth and the comparison is crude. Words about wages, overtime, injuries, quotas, unions or OSHA appear in 17 posts (0.8%). Words about students, teens, kids, graduates, young professionals, interns or the next generation appear in 21 (1.1%).

Commercial offers are present. A contact or call-to-action phrase (contact us, get a quote, book a call or demo, reach out, DM, message us, call us, visit our, learn more or a web address) appears in 450 posts (22.5%), and a phrase of the form "we offer," "our services," "our solutions," "we provide," "we specialize," "we deliver" or "we help" in 82 (4.1%). A "DM me" call appears in 18 (0.9%), 17 of them in the 65 exports. These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts words and not claims or conduct. A post that advertises a freight service and a post that criticizes one are counted alike.

### 17.2 The laws that apply

Four groups of rules meet this topic: the U.S. import ban on goods made with forced labor, the False Claims Act as the government now uses it against customs evasion, the U.S. tariff authorities and the litigation over them, and two EU instruments on due diligence and forced labor. None of them is aimed at social media. They apply to importers, producers and large companies, and they matter to this study because the topic describes the business those parties do.

**The import ban: 19 U.S.C. § 1307.** Section 307 of the Tariff Act prohibits the importation of goods "mined, produced, or manufactured wholly or in part in any foreign country by convict labor or/and forced labor or/and indentured labor under penal sanctions." It defines forced labor as "all work or service which is exacted from any person under the menace of any penalty for its nonperformance," and it includes forced or indentured child labor, which an amendment in 2000 added. A 2016 amendment (Pub. L. 114-125) struck an earlier proviso that allowed such goods in when domestic production could not meet consumer demand (VERIFIED against the Cornell text of § 1307, as summarized by the fetch tool, which placed the quoted words in quotation marks).

**The Uyghur Forced Labor Prevention Act.** The Act applies a rebuttable presumption that goods made wholly or in part in China's Xinjiang Uyghur Autonomous Region, or by entities that the government lists as enabling forced Uyghur labor, are made with forced labor and so fall under § 307. According to a law-firm alert on the guidance that U.S. Customs and Border Protection (CBP) issued on 13 June 2022, the presumption can be rebutted only with "clear and convincing evidence." The Act took effect on 21 June 2022, an importer whose goods are detained has 30 days to present evidence (against 90 days under a withhold release order), and CBP may expect an importer to trace its supply chain "from raw materials to the imported good" (STATED; the alert is a law firm's summary, and I did not open the statute or CBP's own page). The Center for Strategic and International Studies reported in August 2025 that the entity list held 144 names, that CBP had detained 16,755 shipments valued at nearly $3.7 billion, that detentions fell between April and July 2025, and that no entities had been added since January 2025. It also cited a 2023 KPMG survey in which 45% of companies whose shipments were stopped reexported them (STATED, as summarized from the CSIS analysis).

**The False Claims Act and customs evasion.** On 12 May 2026 the Department of Justice announced a settlement of $549.5 million with the Perfectus Aluminum defendants, in a case that alleged evasion of antidumping and countervailing duties on more than 2.2 million aluminum extrusions imported from China between 2011 and 2014. The case began as a qui tam action, in which a private party sues for the government, and the relators receive 17.5% (about $96 million). An earlier criminal conviction in 2021 had produced $1.8 billion in restitution. The law-firm alert on the settlement says that the Department launched a Trade Fraud Task Force in August 2025 and that an executive order created a Task Force to Eliminate Fraud in March 2026 (STATED; a law firm's account of the announcement). I did not open the False Claims Act, 31 U.S.C. § 3729, or any court record in the case.

**The tariff authorities.** In *Learning Resources, Inc. v. Trump*, decided on 20 February 2026 after argument on 5 November 2025, the Supreme Court held by a vote of 6 to 3, in an opinion by Chief Justice Roberts, that "IEEPA does not authorize the President to impose tariffs." The Federal Circuit's judgment was affirmed in No. 25-250, and in No. 24-1287 the district court's judgment was vacated and the case remanded with instructions to dismiss (VERIFIED against the Cornell text of the opinion, of which the tool returned the opening portion; I did not read the refund discussion, if any). A law-firm summary reports that CBP stopped collecting the IEEPA tariffs on 24 February 2026, that the administration imposed a 10% tariff under Section 122 of the Trade Act of 1974 for 150 days from that date, and that about $175 billion in duties might be subject to refund claims (STATED). A second summary dated 15 May 2026 reports that CBP opened the first phase of a refund process on 20 April 2026, that the Court of International Trade ruled on 7 May that the use of Section 122 was unlawful with relief only for the plaintiffs in those cases, and that the government appealed (STATED).

The next step bears directly on this chapter. Law-firm summaries report that the United States Trade Representative opened investigations on 12 March 2026 into the practices of 60 economies and found on 2 June that all 60 were actionable under Section 301(b) of the Trade Act. The finding was that some had imposed but not effectively enforced a prohibition on imports made with forced labor, and that most had none. The Representative imposed tariffs of 10% to 12.5% on those economies with effect from 12:01 a.m. on 24 July 2026, the day the Section 122 tariffs expired, with exemptions that include goods already subject to Section 232 tariffs and goods that qualify under the USMCA (STATED, from two law-firm alerts; one gives 12.5% in its text and 13% in its country table, and I report the range that both alerts support). Importers sued in the Court of International Trade on the first day. A news report on the complaints says that they allege that the Representative skipped the country-by-country finding that Section 301(b) requires, that one complaint alleges the Representative "determined the tariff rates first" and set them "to replicate the invalidated IEEPA rates," and that both argue in the alternative that the delegation is unconstitutional. A three-judge panel heard argument on 30 September 2026, and an update dated 8 October 2026 reported no ruling and no stay of collection (STATED; allegations and secondary reports, not findings). I did not look for events after that date.

**The EU instruments.** Directive (EU) 2024/1760 on corporate sustainability due diligence (CSDDD) was changed by the first "Omnibus" regulation, published in the Official Journal on 26 February 2026 and in force from 18 March 2026. A law-firm summary says that member states must transpose the Directive by 26 July 2028, that companies must apply it from 26 July 2029, and that it covers companies with more than 5,000 employees and more than €1.5 billion in turnover (for non-EU companies, €1.5 billion of turnover in the EU). The amendments removed the obligation to adopt a climate transition plan and the EU-level civil-liability regime, capped penalties at 3% of net worldwide turnover, and set a review by 26 July 2031 (STATED). Regulation (EU) 2024/3015 bans placing on the EU market, or exporting from it, any product made with forced labor, including forced child labor, for all operators regardless of size. It was published on 12 December 2024, entered into force on 13 December 2024 and applies from 14 December 2027. National competent authorities and the Commission investigate on risk-based criteria, and the remedies are a ban, withdrawal and disposal of the product, within 30 working days or 10 for perishables; the summary I read describes no fines (STATED, from the eucrim summary; I did not open either text).

### 17.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

These rules reach the parties who import, produce or sell goods, and they reach a post only when the post carries a claim about those goods or services.

The rules in this chapter can fall on an author, or on a member who engages with the author's post, only in a second role as importer, producer or seller of goods. A freight forwarder, a manufacturer or a seller who posts in this topic is subject to § 307, to the UFLPA presumption and to the tariff and customs rules as an importer or a participant in importing. These laws attach to the import and not to the post, and the data contain no importation. If a seller who uses a pod is the importer of record for goods, the burden of the UFLPA presumption falls on that seller (INFERENCE from the alert's reference to importers; I did not open a definition of importer of record). A small seller whose supplier or its inputs trace to Xinjiang would meet the presumption in the same way as a large importer (INFERENCE), and the text does not show whether any post reflects such a seller. The dropshipping and FBA vocabulary appears in 297 posts (14.8%), and Chapter 14 covers the FTC's actions against the sellers of courses on that business model, including FBA Machine.

A post that advertises a compliance, sourcing or logistics service is a commercial claim, and claims about being compliant, certified or ethically sourced are objective claims that, on the substantiation doctrine in Chapter 4, an advertiser must be able to support (INFERENCE as applied; I found no case that applies the doctrine to a forced-labor-free claim). Such words appear in 262 posts (13.1%) and a claim of certification or guarantee in 13 (0.7%). Chapter 18 takes up environmental claims, which appear in 168 posts (8.4%), together with the Lanham Act. Chapter 10 sets out the rule on buying fake indicators for the case where an author buys likes, comments or followers for such a post.

As the alert describes it, the matter was a False Claims Act case about evaded duties, and the Perfectus settlement concerned duties and not advertising. A post comes within that concern only if it is part of a scheme to evade duties, and no post in the data states such a purpose. Counterfeit, fraud, smuggling, transshipment or sanctions words appear in 19 posts (0.9%), and I cannot tell whether any post describes an evasion, a defense against one or a news item.

The tool operator is outside the rules in this chapter on every reading I can support, and the platform's position is the one in Chapter 10. Regulation 2024/3015 applies to all operators who place products on the EU market, so a small EU seller who is also a pod member would be an operator. The rule would apply because of the seller's goods and not because of the pod (INFERENCE from the eucrim summary).

Table 17.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on supply chain, logistics and operations, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 17.1.** Rules that reach posts on supply chain, logistics and operations, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy or penalty stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 19 U.S.C. § 1307 and the UFLPA | Importers of goods made with forced labor; Xinjiang goods presumed covered | CSIS: 144 listed entities; 16,755 shipments (about $3.7 billion) detained as of August 2025 | Detention and exclusion of goods; fines not stated in the sources I read |
| False Claims Act, customs evasion | Persons alleged to have evaded customs duties | Perfectus Aluminum defendants, announced 12 May 2026 (STATED) | $549.5 million settlement; relators 17.5% |
| *Learning Resources, Inc. v. Trump* | The President's tariff authority under IEEPA | Decided 20 February 2026, 6-3 | No penalty; refund process opened 20 April 2026 (STATED) |

### 17.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

The laws in this chapter aim to protect people who are far from the posts: the workers who make the goods, and among them the children.

The International Labour Organization and UNICEF reported in June 2025 that nearly 138 million children were in child labour in 2024, which is more than 20 million fewer than in 2020. About 54 million of them were in hazardous work, 61% were in agriculture, and about 87 million were in sub-Saharan Africa. The 2025 target for ending child labour was missed, and the two agencies said that ending it within five years would require progress 11 times faster than recent progress (VERIFIED against the UNICEF page, as summarized). The statutes I read use the term forced labor and, in the U.S. statute and the EU Regulation, extend it to forced child labor. Child labour in the ILO's count is a wider category, so the 138 million is not the number of children the statutes reach, and I cannot say what share they reach (INFERENCE). The U.S. ban has been amended since 2000, and the EU ban applies only from December 2027, after the 2025 target year that the two agencies say was missed. I opened no source that measures whether either instrument has changed the number of children in work.

The recent changes in the law make a mixed picture, and I describe it without a verdict. In the United States, the detention figures show a rule that stops goods, with detentions down in the months before the CSIS report and no entities added after January 2025. In 2026 the same concept, a country's forced-labor import prohibition, became the stated ground for a broad tariff on 60 economies, and the plaintiffs who challenge it allege that the aim was to keep a minimum tariff in place of the invalidated IEEPA tariffs (STATED as an allegation). The court has not decided whether that is so, and I have no evidence on how the tariffs affect the protection of workers in the supply chains concerned. In the EU, the Omnibus amendments set the scope of the due-diligence directive at companies with more than 5,000 employees and €1.5 billion in turnover and removed the EU-level civil-liability regime and the climate transition plan duty, while the forced-labor Regulation applies to operators of any size (STATED). I did not open the earlier text of the directive, so I do not say how far its scope changed, and I did not read either text on remedies, so I cannot say which remedies for a harmed worker remain under national law (INFERENCE that removing the EU-level regime narrows them).

The sums are large. The summary I read puts possible IEEPA refund claims at about $175 billion, and the Perfectus settlement at $549.5 million over alleged evasion of duties on imports from 2011 to 2014. Duties are public revenue, and a refund or an evasion changes it. I opened no source on who bears the cost of tariffs, and I draw no conclusion about consumers' prices or about the tax burden of the next generation.

For workers in logistics and operations the data point toward a question that I cannot answer. The topic speaks of automation in 463 posts (23.1%) and of workers in 130 (6.5%), and 17 posts (0.8%) mention wages, injuries, quotas, unions or OSHA. I opened no source on the effect of automation on warehouse, trucking or port employment. The entry-level labor evidence I opened is in Chapters 12 and 15, and I did not read it for logistics occupations. Young-person words appear in 21 posts here (1.1%), so the topic gives little direct evidence on what its subject means for the next generation (INFERENCE from low counts).

Consumers and the future also appear through product quality. Quality, safety, recall, defect or inspection words appear in 161 posts (8.1%), and food or pharmaceutical words in 99 (5.0%). I did not open the Consumer Product Safety Improvement Act or the food and drug laws, so I do not say what protection applies to a child who uses a product that passes through these chains.

My conclusion for this chapter is that the strongest rules reach conduct that the data cannot show, and that the posts in the topic mostly advertise logistics services or discuss the field (INFERENCE). The people the rules aim to protect do not read or write the posts. The nearest link between the two is a seller who imports goods and carries the legal duty as importer, and the data do not show whether any seller in the topic does so.

### 17.5 How this topic bears on mental health

The mental-health harm that this chapter's rules address falls on workers in forced labor, and the research on it covers fewer people than the harm affects.

Words about forced labor, modern slavery, child labor, trafficking, the UFLPA, Xinjiang or the Uyghurs appear in 3 of 2,000 posts (0.1%). The International Labour Organization reported on 19 March 2024 that 27.6 million people were in forced labour on any given day in 2021, which is 3.5 per thousand people worldwide, and that forced labour in the private economy generates US$236 billion in illegal profits a year. Oram and colleagues (2012, *PLoS Medicine*) reviewed 19 papers on 16 studies of trafficked people. In studies that used screening tools, symptom levels were high: anxiety in 48.0% to 97.7% of participants, depression in 54.9% to 100% and post-traumatic stress in 19.5% to 77.0%. Only one study used a validated diagnostic interview, and in it 16.7% had depression and 35.8% post-traumatic stress disorder. The authors state that the samples came mostly from support services and that nearly all the studies concerned women and girls, so evidence on men, children and people trafficked for labor is largely absent.

I therefore cannot give figures for the workers who make or move the goods that these posts discuss. The import and due-diligence rules in Section 17.2 aim to keep goods made with forced labor out of markets. I found no evidence in the sources I opened on whether those rules reduce the harm to workers' mental health.

### 17.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show that any post in this topic describes an importation, a forced-labor supply chain, a customs evasion or a false compliance claim. Three posts use words about forced labor, the UFLPA or Xinjiang, and I worked from aggregate counts and not from individual posts. A count of "supply chain" is a count of a phrase. I cannot tell whether a given author imports goods, whether any seller on a pod is an importer of record, or which posts were boosted by an exchange of engagement.

My reading of the law has gaps. The fetch tool returned summaries of most sources, and I quote only the words it placed in quotation marks. I did not open the text of the Uyghur Forced Labor Prevention Act (Pub. L. 117-78), CBP's own pages on it, the Congressional Research Service reports on it (the site blocks automated access), the False Claims Act, the Federal Register notices for the Section 301 action, the statute for Section 301, the two EU texts, any court record in the Perfectus case, or the opinion's discussion of remedies in *Learning Resources*. I did not read the Section 232 actions at all. I did not open the rules on the de minimis exemption, the Made in USA Labeling Rule (16 CFR Part 323), the Consumer Product Safety Improvement Act, the INFORM Consumers Act or state laws on warehouse workers, so the chapter does not say what applies to a small online seller who ships to consumers. The tariff picture is the most perishable part. My sources run to an update dated 8 October 2026, one alert gives two rates for the same group of countries, and the court's ruling, any appeal and the refund process may have moved since.

One question remains open after this chapter. It is whether a claim of ethical or forced-labor-free sourcing made in a social media post is an objective claim that needs substantiation. I found no case that applies the substantiation doctrine to such a claim, and the sources I opened do not address how purchased or exchanged engagement bears on a court's view of one.

## Chapter 18. Marketing, brands and advertising: false advertising, green claims and disguised promotion

### 18.1 The topic in the data

Marketing, brands and advertising is the fifteenth-largest topic, with 1,862 posts, which is 2.9% of the 64,970 placed posts. The podawaa file supplies 1,009 posts (54.2%), the 65 exports 444 (23.8%), HyperClapper 392 (21.1%) and LinkBoost-2025 17 (0.9%). The posts are short. The median is 279 characters, which is lower than the medians of 327 to 541 in Chapters 14 to 17, and the 90th percentile is 888. Five or more hashtags appear in 422 posts (22.7%), and a link in 216 (11.6%).

The vocabulary is that of the trade. Brand or branding words appear in 1,024 posts (55.0%), marketing or marketer words in 640 (34.4%), and advertising words (ad, advertising, campaign, sponsored, paid media, PPC, CPC, ROAS) in 453 (24.3%). Words about search optimization, algorithms, followers, engagement, impressions, reach or going viral appear in 127 (6.8%). Words about the creator economy (influencer, creator, UGC, ambassador) appear in 60 (3.2%), and a testimonial, review, case study, social proof, five-star or rating word in 39 (2.1%). Words of trust, transparency or honesty appear in 124 (6.7%), and the word authentic in 46 (2.5%).

The posts make measurable claims in a minority.
