A superlative or proof claim (best, #1, number one, world's best, first or largest, award-winning, top-rated, guaranteed, proven) appears in 128 posts (6.9%), a competitor or comparison word (competitor, competition, versus, better than, unlike, outperform) in 77 (4.1%), and a statistic of the form of a percentage, "10x," "doubled" or "tripled" in 74 (4.0%). Words of results (ROI, conversion, lead, sales, revenue, growth) appear in 121 (6.5%). A percentage figure appears in 57 posts (3.1%), a dollar or euro figure in 41 (2.2%), the word "free" in 79 (4.2%) and "guarantee" in 12 (0.6%).

The words that connect the topic to the rules in this chapter are rare. A disclosure word (#ad, #sponsored, #gifted, paid partnership, sponsored, disclose, affiliate link, gifted) appears in 6 posts (0.3%), and a paid-promotion phrase (paid partnership, sponsored, brand deal, brand collaboration, paid promotion, paid ad or post) in 26 (1.4%). Environmental words (eco-friendly, carbon neutral, net zero, green, sustainable, recyclable, biodegradable, climate, plastic-free) appear in 12 posts (0.6%), against 168 (8.4%) in the supply chain topic of Chapter 17. A trademark, copyright, intellectual property, infringement, knock-off or plagiarism word appears in 12 (0.6%), a health-claim word (clinically, scientifically, cure, detox, FDA, supplement, wellness) in 11 (0.6%), and a regulatory or legal word (FTC, disclosure, regulation, compliance, law, legal, GDPR, lawsuit) in 15 (0.8%). Privacy, cookie, tracking, data or consent words appear in 70 (3.8%).

Words about the exchange of engagement are nearly absent. A word about fake followers, fake engagement, fake reviews, fake accounts, fake likes, bots, inflated numbers, vanity metrics, buying followers, engagement pods or engagement farming appears in 7 posts (0.4%), and the word "pod" in 2 (0.1%). A contact or call-to-action phrase appears in 105 (5.6%), a "DM me" call in 10 (0.5%), and a request to comment or follow in 26 (1.4%).

AI appears in 191 posts (10.3%): 66 in the podawaa file, 48 in HyperClapper, 3 in LinkBoost-2025 and 74 in the 65 exports. Words for AI-generated content (AI-generated, generated by AI, AI content, AI image, AI video, deepfake, synthetic media, AI avatar, AI influencer) appear in 13 (0.7%). Words about students, teens, kids, children, graduates, young people, youth, Gen Z, Gen Alpha, minors or the next generation appear in 33 (1.8%), and words about future generations in 3 (0.2%). These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts words and not claims. A post that makes a comparison and a post that reports someone else's comparison are counted alike.

### 18.2 The laws that apply

Five items matter here beyond Chapters 4, 10, 14 and 16: the Lanham Act's rules on false advertising and false association, the Federal Trade Commission's Green Guides, the Commission's policy on advertisements formatted as editorial content, the EU's directive on environmental claims, and the EU's sweep of influencer disclosure.

**The Lanham Act, 15 U.S.C. § 1125(a).** Subparagraph (A) covers a use of a word, name, symbol or false designation that "is likely to cause confusion, or to cause mistake, or to deceive" "as to the affiliation, connection, or association of such person with another person," or "as to the origin, sponsorship, or approval of his or her goods, services, or commercial activities by another person." Subparagraph (B) covers a use that "in commercial advertising or promotion, misrepresents the nature, characteristics, qualities, or geographic origin" "of his or her or another person's goods, services, or commercial activities." The statute allows a "civil action by any person who believes that he or she is or is likely to be damaged by such act" (VERIFIED against the Cornell text, as summarized by the fetch tool, which placed the quoted words in quotation marks). In *Lexmark International, Inc. v. Static Control Components, Inc.*, decided unanimously on 25 March 2014 in an opinion by Justice Scalia, the Supreme Court held that a plaintiff suing for false advertising under § 1125(a) must allege "an injury to a commercial interest in reputation or sales" and must show "economic or reputational injury flowing directly from the deception wrought by the defendant's advertising" (VERIFIED against the FindLaw text of the opinion, as summarized). Section 1125(a) gives a private right of action, and under *Lexmark* the plaintiff is a business that alleges a commercial injury. I did not open the Lanham Act's remedies provision, § 1117, or the circuit decisions on what counts as "commercial advertising or promotion."

**The Green Guides, 16 CFR Part 260.** The Commission's Guides on environmental marketing claims state in § 260.1 that they give the Commission's "current views about environmental claims" and that they "do not confer any rights on any person and do not operate to bind the FTC or the public." The Commission may still act under the FTC Act if a claim conflicts with the Guides, and it must prove that the practice is unfair or deceptive. Deception "will depend on the net impression of the advertisement, label, or other promotional material at issue" (§ 260.1(d)), and § 260.2 asks marketers to ensure that "all reasonable interpretations of their claims are truthful" and "supported by a reasonable basis before they make the claims," which "often requires competent and reliable scientific evidence." Under § 260.4, unqualified general environmental benefit claims "are difficult to interpret and likely convey a wide range of meanings," and marketers "should not make unqualified general environmental benefit claims" (VERIFIED against the Commission's copy of the 2012 Guides, as summarized). A reference site reports that the Commission opened a review of the Guides in 2022 and that the Guides "remain in effect"; I found no final revision, and I cannot date that site's page (STATED).

**Advertisements formatted as editorial content.** On 22 December 2015 the Commission issued, by a vote of 4 to 0, an Enforcement Policy Statement on Deceptively Formatted Advertisements, with a companion guide for business. The release says that advertising should be "identifiable as advertising to consumers," that a format is deceptive if it materially misleads consumers about the commercial nature of the content, including by implying that the content comes from a party other than the sponsoring advertiser, and that people "have a right to know if they're seeing editorial content or an ad" (VERIFIED against the Commission's release, as summarized; I did not open the statement or the guide).

**The Endorsement Guides and Part 465.** Chapters 10, 14 and 16 set out the Endorsement Guides, the Disclosures 101 guide and the rule on fake reviews and indicators. I do not repeat them here.

**The EU directive on environmental claims.** Directive (EU) 2024/825, which amends the Unfair Commercial Practices Directive, was published in the Official Journal on 6 March 2024, entered into force on 26 March 2024 and applies from 27 September 2026. A law-firm summary says it allows generic environmental claims, such as "green" or "eco-friendly," only with "excellent recognised environmental performance" that is relevant to the claim. It requires private sustainability labels to follow a certification scheme with an independent verifier, bans claims that a product has a neutral, reduced or positive greenhouse gas impact based on offsetting, such as "climate neutral," and requires claims about future environmental performance to be backed by "concrete plans, steps and a published monitoring." A second law-firm alert gives the same date of application, says that Italy implemented the Directive by Legislative Decree No. 30/2026, and reports that the Italian competition authority can impose fines of up to €10 million and that in certain cross-border cases fines can reach 4% of annual turnover (STATED, from two law-firm summaries; I did not open the Directive or any national law other than as the Italian law is described, and I did not find the transposition deadline).

**Influencer disclosure in the EU.** The German Environment Agency reports that a sweep by the EU consumer protection network examined 576 influencers, that 97% regularly post commercially backed content, and that "only around 20 per cent of these consistently label this as advertising" (VERIFIED against the agency's page, as summarized; the page does not give the number of authorities or the date, and I did not open the Commission's own report).

### 18.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

The rules reach a post when the post makes a claim about a good or service in order to sell it, and the pod bears on the rules only through the reach and credibility it gives the claim (INFERENCE).

An author who writes a post for a brand or an agency makes statements that fall into three kinds. Some are opinion or boasting, such as "best" or "#1," which the Commission's deception statement treats as puffery in the circumstances that Chapter 4 describes (VERIFIED in Chapter 4). Some are measurable claims, such as a percentage improvement or "doubled conversions," which an advertiser must be able to support under the substantiation doctrine in Chapter 4. Some are comparisons with a named or unnamed competitor. Superlative or proof words appear in 128 posts (6.9%), statistics in 74 (4.0%) and comparison words in 77 (4.1%). I cannot tell from word counts which claims are measurable, which are false and which are puffery.

The Lanham Act reaches a false measurable or comparative claim made "in commercial advertising or promotion" about the author's or another person's goods or services. A competitor that suffers "an injury to a commercial interest in reputation or sales" flowing directly from the deception can sue under *Lexmark*. The statute does not mention engagement, and I found no case that treats a pod's amplification of a false claim as relevant to liability or to damages. If amplification widened the audience who saw the claim, it could bear on the competitor's injury, but that is a point about proof and not a rule I read (INFERENCE).

An author who promotes a brand without saying that the author has a connection to it presents the case that the Endorsement Guides address, as Chapter 16 sets out. Creator-economy words appear in 60 posts (3.2%), a disclosure word in 6 (0.3%) and a paid-promotion phrase in 26 (1.4%). The EU sweep found about one in five influencers consistently labeling advertising, and the data cannot show whether the posts in this topic were labeled in the way the rules call for, because a label may sit outside the text I analyzed (INFERENCE). The Commission's 2015 statement concerns a different question from the disclosure of a connection: whether the format of an advertisement misleads about its commercial nature, including by implying that it comes from a party other than the sponsor. A brand's promotional post written to read as an author's independent commentary is the closest case (INFERENCE).

A brand that buys followers, likes or comments meets § 465.8 as Chapter 10 sets out, and the fake-engagement words in this topic appear in 7 posts (0.4%).

Environmental claims in this topic are few, 12 posts (0.6%), and they meet the Green Guides in the United States and, from 27 September 2026, the national laws that implement Directive 2024/825 in the EU, if the seller addresses EU consumers (INFERENCE as applied). The Chapter 17 topic contains many more such words, 168 posts (8.4%). The Guides do not bind the Commission or the public, so the Commission must still prove deception under the FTC Act, whereas the Directive's bans on generic and offsetting-based claims are rules stated in the Directive's terms and not guidance (INFERENCE from the summaries; I did not read the Directive).

AI-generated content words appear in 13 posts (0.7%). Chapter 4 covers the EU's transparency rule for synthetic content in Article 50 of the AI Act.

The tool operator is not an advertiser in the ordinary case and is outside these rules on every reading I can support. The platform's position is the one in Chapter 10.

Table 18.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on marketing, brands and advertising, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 18.1.** Rules that reach posts on marketing, brands and advertising, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy or penalty stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Lanham Act, 15 U.S.C. § 1125(a)(1)(A) and (B) | Persons who misrepresent goods or services in commercial advertising or promotion; false association or sponsorship | *Lexmark v. Static Control* (2014), on who may sue | Not stated in the text I read (§ 1117 not opened) |
| FTC Green Guides, 16 CFR Part 260 | Marketers of environmental claims | None opened | The Guides "do not confer any rights" and do not bind the Commission or the public; no remedy stated in the text I read |
| FTC Enforcement Policy Statement on Deceptively Formatted Advertisements (22 December 2015) | Advertisers and publishers of ads that resemble editorial content | None opened | Not stated in the release I read |
| Endorsement Guides, Part 465 | See Chapters 10, 14 and 16 | See Chapters 10, 14 and 16 | See Chapters 10, 14 and 16 |
| Directive (EU) 2024/825, applies from 27 September 2026 | Traders making environmental claims to EU consumers | Italy: Legislative Decree No. 30/2026 (STATED) | Italy: fines up to €10 million; cross-border cases up to 4% of annual turnover (STATED) |
| EU consumer network sweep of influencers | Influencers who post commercial content | 576 influencers examined; about 20% consistently labeled advertising | Not stated on the page I read |

### 18.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

Advertising law protects people at the moment they decide what to buy or whom to believe, and the data show a field that talks about trust far more than it talks about the rules.

Trust, transparency and honesty words appear in 124 posts (6.7%), authenticity in 46 (2.5%), and legal or regulatory words in 15 (0.8%). The rules in this chapter protect a reader from a false statement of fact and from advertising that conceals its character. They do not address whether the engagement that makes a post look popular is real, which Chapter 10 treats as a question for the fake-indicators rule. A reader who sees a brand's post with many comments may take them as evidence that others find it credible, and I opened no source that measures that effect (INFERENCE). The EU sweep supplies the one measure I have of how well the disclosure rules work: about 20% of the influencers examined consistently labeled advertising, which is a sample and not a count of all influencers. In that sample, compliance with the labeling rule was low.

Teenagers' exposure to marketing depends on the platforms they use. A secondary report on a Pew Research Center survey, conducted from 25 September to 9 October 2025 among 1,458 U.S. teens aged 13 to 17 and published on 12 December 2025, says that 92% use YouTube, 68% TikTok, 63% Instagram and 55% Snapchat, and that four in ten say they are online almost constantly, slightly fewer than in 2024 (STATED; I could not open Pew's own page). The report as summarized does not give a figure for LinkedIn. The pods in my data serve a professional network, so I draw only the limited conclusion that the marketing practices in this topic are those of adult professionals and that I have no measure of teenagers' exposure to them (INFERENCE). Youth words appear in 33 posts (1.8%) and words about children, teens or Gen Z in 15 (0.8%). I opened no rule on advertising directed at children, and Chapters 7 and 22 treat children's privacy.

The environmental rules bear on the future in a narrow way. The Green Guides and Directive 2024/825 regulate what a seller may say about a product's environmental effect. They do not regulate the effect itself. The Directive's ban on offsetting-based "climate neutral" claims removes a statement from the marketplace, and I opened no source on whether it changes what people buy or how much is emitted (INFERENCE). The Green Guides were last revised in 2012 according to the reference site, and the Commission began a review in 2022 that, as far as I found, it has not finished (STATED). In this topic, environmental words appear in 12 posts and future-generation words in 3 (0.2%), so the data say little about the subject.

A last consideration involves the people who will work in marketing. AI appears in 191 posts (10.3%) and words for AI-generated content in 13 (0.7%). For a new entrant, the practical question is what disclosure obligations attach to content that a machine produced. The EU's Article 50, covered in Chapter 4, applies from 2 August 2026 according to the law-firm alert I read there, and I found no U.S. rule specific to AI-generated advertising content among the sources I opened (INFERENCE from what I opened).

My conclusion for this chapter is that the law of advertising reaches the claims in these posts when they are factual, commercial and false, and that it leaves open the effect of a pod on those claims (INFERENCE). The people who gain from the law are the buyers who rely on a claim and the competitors whose sales fall because of a false one, and the data do not show either.

### 18.5 How this topic bears on mental health

Marketing reaches mental health through three channels in the sources I opened: comparison with idealized images, money lost to deceptive advertising, and anxiety about climate change, which green claims address.

The topic is about brands and advertising. Brand or branding words appear in 1,024 of 1,862 posts (55.0%), words of trust, transparency or honesty in 124 (6.7%), and environmental words in 12 (0.6%). The American Psychological Association's 2023 advisory links appearance-based comparison to poorer body image, disordered eating and depressive symptoms, and advises adolescents to limit social media use for comparison. The posts here are mostly about business and not about appearance, so the evidence bears on the setting more than on the content (INFERENCE).

On money, the FTC reported on 27 April 2026 that shopping was the most frequently reported social media scam type, and that over 40% of the people who lost money to those scams said they ordered something they saw in a social media ad. The loss is financial, and Section 3.8 gives the fraud-victim studies, which report effects that range from little to devastating.

Green claims connect to climate anxiety. Hickman and colleagues (2021, *The Lancet Planetary Health*) surveyed 10,000 people aged 16 to 25 in 10 countries. Of these, 59% were very or extremely worried about climate change and 84% at least moderately worried, and more than 45% said that their feelings about climate change negatively affected their daily life and functioning. The Green Guides and Directive 2024/825 (Section 18.2) govern what a seller may say about a product's environmental effect. The survey did not test how green claims affect its respondents, and I do not claim an effect.

### 18.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show that any post in this topic makes a false claim, that any author has an undisclosed connection to a brand, that any comparison is untrue, or that any engagement under a post was exchanged. A post can be an advertisement, a comment on advertising or a report of someone else's campaign, and the counts treat them alike. I analyzed text only: I did not analyze images, platform labels such as a "promoted" tag, or comment threads.

My reading of the law has gaps. The fetch tool returned summaries of the Lanham Act, *Lexmark*, the Green Guides, the Commission's release and the two law-firm alerts, and I quote only the words it placed in quotation marks. I did not open the remedies provision of the Lanham Act (§ 1117), any decision on what counts as "commercial advertising or promotion," any decision on puffery, Part 260 beyond §§ 260.1, 260.2 and 260.4 (carbon offsets and recyclability claims are in other sections), the Commission's native advertising statement or guide, the text of Directive 2024/825 or of any national law other than as described for Italy, or the sweep's own report. The date of the reference site's account of the Green Guides is unknown to me, and I could not open Pew's page.

One question remains open after this chapter. It is whether a pod's amplification of a post that makes a false comparative or measurable claim bears on liability or damages under § 1125(a)(1)(B), and I found no authority that addresses it.

## Chapter 19. Energy, sleep, food and fitness: health claims, supplements, weight-loss products and professional licensing

### 19.1 The topic in the data

Energy, sleep, food and fitness is the sixteenth-largest topic, with 1,775 posts, which is 2.7% of the 64,970 placed posts. Two-thirds of them come from the 65 exports, 1,188 posts (66.9%), followed by the podawaa file with 307 (17.3%), HyperClapper with 231 (13.0%) and LinkBoost-2025 with 49 (2.8%). These posts are long: the median is 853 characters, against 279 to 541 in Chapters 14 to 18, and the 90th percentile is 2,447. Five or more hashtags appear in 472 posts (26.6%), and a link in 148 (8.3%).

The vocabulary covers the four subjects of the label. Food or nutrition words (food, nutrition, diet, protein, meal, recipe, eat, sugar, vitamin, gut, probiotic, keto, fasting, calorie) appear in 1,123 posts (63.3%). Energy words (energy, fatigue, tired, exhaust, caffeine, coffee, stamina) appear in 1,023 (57.6%), although the word "energy" is also used for mood and for a person's presence, so this count overstates talk about nutrition or stimulants. Fitness words (fitness, workout, exercise, gym, marathon, strength, yoga, steps, walking, running, training) appear in 886 (49.9%), and sleep words (sleep, insomnia, melatonin, circadian, bedtime, nap) in 491 (27.7%). Words about mental health or stress (mental health, anxiety, stress, burnout, well-being, wellness, mindfulness, meditation) appear in 519 (29.2%), and workplace words (work, employee, team, corporate, leader, executive, burnout) in 570 (32.1%).

Product and body language is common. A weight-loss word (weight loss, lose weight, fat loss, GLP-1, Ozempic, semaglutide) appears in 313 posts (17.6%), and the name of a GLP-1 drug (GLP-1, Ozempic, semaglutide, Wegovy, tirzepatide, Mounjaro, Zepbound) in 27 (1.5%). A word about burning fat or calories, metabolism, detox or cleanse appears in 226 (12.7%), and a phrase of the form "lost 20 pounds" in 38 (2.1%). A supplement word (supplement, vitamin, probiotic, collagen, creatine, nootropic, adaptogen, ashwagandha, magnesium, melatonin, gummies) appears in 228 (12.8%), and the word melatonin in 13 (0.7%). Longevity, healthspan, anti-aging or biological-age words appear in 125 (7.0%), and a caffeine, energy-drink, pre-workout or coffee word in 194 (10.9%). A food-label word (organic, non-GMO, gluten-free, vegan, clean eating, superfood, sugar-free, low-carb, high-protein, all-natural, plant-based) appears in 114 (6.4%).

Claims of effect are a minority, and claims of disease treatment are rarer. A health-effect word (cure, treat, prevent, heal, reverse, detox, immune, inflammation, anti-aging, clinically, scientifically, proven) appears in 289 posts (16.3%), and the name of a disease or condition (diabetes, cancer, anxiety, depression, ADHD, Alzheimer's, heart disease, obesity, blood pressure, cholesterol, autoimmune, COVID) in 201 (11.3%). A word of curing, treating, preventing, reversing or healing within sixty characters of the name of a disease or condition, in either order, appears in 11 (0.6%). A safety or proof phrase (no side effects, safe, all natural, clinically proven or tested, scientifically proven, studies show, doctor recommended, no prescription, guaranteed results) appears in 84 (4.7%), and a science or professional word (FDA, doctor, physician, clinical, study, research shows, science-backed, peer-reviewed) in 245 (13.8%). The abbreviation FDA in capitals appears in 7 posts (0.4%), and FTC in none. A disclaimer phrase (not medical advice, consult your doctor, results may vary, individual results, disclaimer, not intended to) appears in 14 (0.8%). Results language (before and after, transformation, my results, results, testimonial) appears in 290 (16.3%), a percentage in 294 (16.6%), and a number with pounds, kilograms, days or weeks in 221 (12.5%).

The selling language is distinctive. A "DM me," "inbox me" or "message me" call appears in 176 posts (9.9%), against 1.19% of all 273,771 posts and 1.37% of the top twenty topics together. By source, it appears in 148 of the 1,188 posts in the 65 exports (12.5%), 18 of 231 in HyperClapper (7.8%), 8 of 49 in LinkBoost-2025 (16.3%) and 2 of 307 in the podawaa file (0.7%). A request to comment or follow appears in 153 (8.6%). The word "free" appears in 245 (13.8%), though the pattern also matches hyphenated uses such as "gluten-free," and a free guide, plan, consultation, call, trial, sample, checklist, template, training or webinar appears in 45 (2.5%). Coach, program, challenge, course or membership words appear in 169 (9.5%). Sales words (shake, distributor, my team, join my, business opportunity, brand ambassador, discount code, link in bio, percent off, order now, shop) appear in 69 (3.9%), and words for multi-level marketing (MLM, network marketing, direct sales, upline, downline, join my team) in none. A sponsorship, partnership, ambassador, discount code or affiliate word appears in 25 (1.4%), and an income word in 15 (0.8%).

Words about the exchange of engagement do not appear: the word "pod" and its related phrases occur in none of the 1,775 posts. Wearable-device words appear in 16 (0.9%) and the word AI in 20 (1.1%). Words about kids, children, teens, teenagers, youth, young people or the next generation appear in 89 (5.0%), and a phrase about future generations in 4 (0.2%). These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts words and not claims. A post that recommends a supplement and a post that warns against one are counted alike.

### 19.2 The laws that apply

Six sets of rules bear on this topic: the FTC Act's rules on false advertising of food, drugs and services; the Commission's guidance on health claims; the FDA's rules on what a supplement may claim; the FDA's actions on GLP-1 marketing; state licensing of dietetics and nutrition practice; and the EU's rules on health claims and unfair practices. Chapter 14 covers the Endorsement Guides on testimonials and Chapter 16 the rule on material connections, and I apply them here without restating them.

**False advertisements for food, drugs and services: 15 U.S.C. § 52.** Section 52(a) makes it unlawful "for any person, partnership, or corporation" to disseminate, or cause to be disseminated, a false advertisement that is meant to induce the purchase of food, drugs, devices, services or cosmetics, and subsection (b) treats that conduct as an unfair or deceptive practice under § 45 (VERIFIED against the Cornell text, as summarized; the page does not define "false advertisement" or state the penalty in § 54, which I did not open).

**The Commission's Health Products Compliance Guidance.** A law-firm summary reports that the Commission issued this guidance on 20 December 2022, replacing its 1998 guide for dietary supplements and extending to all health-related claims. It says that health benefit claims need "competent and reliable scientific evidence," that substantiation will generally need "randomized, controlled human clinical trials," that "the quality of the research is more important than the quantity," and that consumer surveys are anecdotal and inadequate. Disclosures must be "clear and conspicuous," and the labeling disclaimer that the Dietary Supplement Health and Education Act requires "is not required in other forms of advertising or marketing" (STATED, from a law-firm summary; I did not open the guidance).

**What a supplement may claim: the FDA's rules.** The FDA states that a supplement label may carry a structure or function claim, a general well-being claim or a classical nutrient-deficiency disease claim, which it calls "403(r)(6) claims." The claim must be truthful and not misleading, and the company must hold substantiation. The company must notify the FDA "no later than 30 days after first marketing the dietary supplement with the claim." A disclaimer must appear in boldface: "This statement has not been evaluated by the Food and Drug Administration. This product is not intended to diagnose, treat, cure, or prevent any disease." A claim may not "claim to diagnose, mitigate, treat, cure, or prevent a specific disease or class of diseases" (VERIFIED against the FDA's page, as summarized; the page is about labels and notifications, and it did not state what follows when a disease claim is made).

**GLP-1 marketing.** A law-firm summary reports that on 3 March 2026 the FDA announced 30 warning letters to telehealth companies that market compounded GLP-1 products. The agency objected to online claims presenting the compounded products as "effectively the same as the approved drug" and to private-label presentations that could mislead consumers about which entity compounds the product. The summary does not name the provisions the letters rely on and does not mention influencers (STATED; I did not open the letters).

**An enforcement example that joins health claims and influencers.** On 6 March 2020 the Commission announced, by a vote of 5 to 0, a complaint and stipulated order against Teami LLC and its two owners in the Middle District of Florida. The complaint alleged that the defendants claimed "without reliable scientific evidence" that a 30-day detox pack would help consumers lose weight and that other teas would fight cancer, clear clogged arteries, decrease migraines and treat or prevent flu and colds. It alleged that posts by ten Instagram influencers hid paid endorsements, because followers "could not see a disclosure that the endorsements were paid-for unless they clicked the 'more' option," and that the practices continued after a staff warning letter in April 2018. The order enters a judgment of $15.2 million, equal to total sales of the challenged products, suspended on payment of $1 million because the defendants could not pay more. It bars unsupported weight-loss and health claims and requires "clear and conspicuous disclosures of any unexpected material connection" and "endorser monitoring requirements" (VERIFIED against the Commission's release, as summarized; the release states allegations and does not state findings).

**State licensing of dietetics and nutrition practice.** In a case about a Florida health coach, Heather Kokesch Del Castillo, whom the Florida Department of Health cited in 2017 for being paid to give dietary advice without a license as a dietitian or nutritionist, a panel of the Eleventh Circuit upheld Florida's law and rejected her First Amendment claim. The opinion, written by Judge Luck, reasoned that the licensing scheme "regulated professional conduct and only incidentally burdened Del Castillo's speech." (STATED, from a news report that does not give the date of the ruling; the date in the report's web address is 21 February 2022. I did not open the opinion, and I did not find the outcome of any further appeal.)

**The EU.** The Commission's register of nutrition and health claims lists permitted nutrition claims, authorised health claims and non-authorised health claims, and describes itself as "for information only" (VERIFIED against the Commission's page, as summarized). I read Regulation (EC) No 1924/2006 on the UK legislation site, which carries the retained text with UK edits, so its wording may differ from the EU text. Article 10(1) says that health claims are not allowed unless they comply with the Regulation's general requirements and are authorised and listed. Article 12 bars claims "which suggest that health could be affected by not consuming the food," claims "which make reference to the rate or amount of weight loss," and claims that cite recommendations from individual doctors or health professionals, with an exception for national associations of medical, nutrition or dietetic professionals and health charities. Article 14 covers disease risk reduction claims and claims about children's development and health, and requires labeling that notes that the disease has multiple risk factors (VERIFIED against the retained text as summarized). Annex I of the Unfair Commercial Practices Directive lists as unfair in all circumstances, in point 17, "Falsely claiming that a product is able to cure illnesses, dysfunction or malformations," and, in point 20, describing a product as "gratis," "free," "without charge" or similar if the consumer has to pay anything other than the unavoidable cost of responding to the practice (VERIFIED against the Directive as adopted in 2005).

### 19.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

The rules reach a post when it promotes a product or service for sale and makes a claim about health, and they reach a coach also when the coach gives individual advice for pay.

The author is the party on whom nearly all of these rules fall. A coach, seller or brand that posts a claim that a product helps with weight loss, energy or sleep needs the substantiation the guidance describes (INFERENCE as applied; the data cannot show what evidence any author holds). A claim that a product treats or prevents a named disease is a disease claim that the FDA's page says a supplement may not make. Disease-claim patterns appear in 11 posts (0.6%), health-effect words in 289 (16.3%) and results or transformation language in 290 (16.3%). I cannot tell from word counts whether any of those posts promotes a supplement, a program or nothing for sale. A disclaimer phrase of any kind appears in 14 posts (0.8%). The guidance says that the labeling disclaimer is not required in other advertising, so the absence of one in a post is not by itself a violation (INFERENCE from the summary).

Results language bears on the Endorsement Guides, which Chapter 14 sets out. A post that reports a customer's result is a testimonial of the kind Chapter 14 describes, and the *Teami* order shows the Commission requiring disclosure of "unexpected material connection" for influencers who promote health products. Whether a comment under a post, given in return for engagement from the poster, is such a connection is the open question of Chapter 16, and nothing in the *Teami* release addresses it (INFERENCE). A member who comments "this changed my life" under a post that sells a weight-loss program is the case where the question would matter, and the data do not show that any member did.

A coach who gives individual nutrition advice for pay may meet state licensing law. The Florida decision upheld a licensing scheme against a free speech challenge, in a case in which the plaintiff ran a health-coaching business. States differ, and I did not open any state statute other than as the news report describes Florida's (INFERENCE as applied). The topic's coaching language, program words in 169 posts (9.5%), shows that coaching is sold, and the "DM me" calls in 176 posts (9.9%) show that conversations move to private messages. What a coach says in a private message is outside the data, so I cannot tell whether any post leads to individual advice (INFERENCE). Chapter 8 sets out the rules on direct outreach.

GLP-1 drug names appear in 27 posts (1.5%). The FDA's letters went to telehealth companies, and I cannot tell whether the authors of those posts are such companies, patients, coaches or commentators.

A post that offers something "free" falls under point 20 of Annex I in the EU only if the consumer must pay more than the unavoidable cost of responding. A free guide, call or trial appears in 45 posts (2.5%), and I cannot tell whether any of those carries a cost (INFERENCE).

The tool operator is not an advertiser, and the platform's position is the one in Chapter 10.

Table 19.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on energy, sleep, food and fitness, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 19.1.** Rules that reach posts on energy, sleep, food and fitness, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy or penalty stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 15 U.S.C. § 52 | Anyone who disseminates a false advertisement for food, drugs, devices, services or cosmetics | See the *Teami* row | Treated as an unfair or deceptive practice under § 45; penalty not stated on the page I read |
| FTC Health Products Compliance Guidance (20 December 2022) | Advertisers of health-related claims | None opened | Not stated in the summary I read |
| Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act, structure/function claims (FDA page) | Supplement makers and sellers | None opened | Not stated on the page I read |
| FDA warning letters on compounded GLP-1 products (3 March 2026) | Telehealth companies marketing compounded GLP-1 products | 30 letters (STATED) | Not stated in the summary I read |
| FTC and Teami LLC (6 March 2020) | Seller and owners; influencers' undisclosed paid posts | Stipulated order, M.D. Fla., 5-0 vote | $15.2 million judgment suspended on payment of $1 million; ban on unsupported claims; disclosure and monitoring duties |
| State dietetics licensing (Florida) | Unlicensed persons paid for dietary advice | Del Castillo, Eleventh Circuit (STATED) | Not stated in the report I read |
| Regulation (EC) No 1924/2006, Arts 10, 12, 14 | Food and supplement claims in the EU | None opened | Not stated in the text I read |
| Directive 2005/29/EC, Annex I points 17 and 20 | Traders who claim cures or misuse "free" | None opened | Not stated in the text I read |

### 19.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

The law in this chapter protects people from paying for a benefit that a product does not deliver, and from products that harm them. The second protection is the one where the sources I opened show harm to children.

Sleep is the subject where the data in this topic meet the evidence on young people. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention report from the 2025 Youth Risk Behavior Survey that about one in four U.S. high school students got at least 8 hours of sleep on an average school night, 24% in 2025 against 23% in 2023, and that the share declined between 2015 and 2025. The same release reports that persistent sadness or hopelessness fell from 40% to 33% between 2023 and 2025, that daily fruit eating rose from 33% to 39% and daily vegetable eating from 58% to 63%, and that daily physical activity did not change (VERIFIED against the CDC pages, as summarized). Sleep words appear in 491 posts here (27.7%) and sleep-improvement phrases in 100 (5.6%), while kid, child or teen words appear in 89 (5.0%) and a rough pattern pairing teen, child or kid words with sleep in 4 (0.2%). The topic talks about sleep as an adult's problem more than a child's (INFERENCE from the counts).

The melatonin evidence shows a direct harm from a supplement. A CDC report in the *Morbidity and Mortality Weekly Report* found 260,435 pediatric melatonin ingestions reported to U.S. poison control centers from 2012 through 2021, with annual ingestions rising 530%, from 8,337 in 2012 to 52,563 in 2021. Children aged 5 or younger accounted for 83.8%, and 94.3% were unintentional. Of 27,795 patients treated at a health care facility, 14.7% were hospitalized and 1.0% needed intensive care. Five children required mechanical ventilation and two children under 2 died. The report says that melatonin, regulated as a dietary supplement, "is subject to less regulatory oversight" than prescription drugs, cites a "lack of robust manufacturing regulations," and says that "Child-resistant packaging for this supplement should be considered" (VERIFIED against the report, as summarized; the summary noted that the text and a table give different counts for the more serious outcomes, 4,555 and 3,211, and I use neither). Melatonin appears in 13 posts here (0.7%). The report concerns ingestion by children, and the posts concern adults' sleep, so the link between them is the regulatory category and not the content of any post (INFERENCE).

The weight-loss language raises a question about the next generation that I cannot answer from my sources. Weight-loss words appear in 313 posts (17.6%), including the names of GLP-1 drugs in 27. The EU Regulation bars claims about the rate or amount of weight loss in food claims, and the FDA's letters concern misleading claims about compounded drugs. I opened no source on how adolescents encounter weight-loss marketing, on any effect on eating behavior, or on whether the data's weight-loss posts reach young readers, and the pods in the data serve a professional network (INFERENCE). Where the topic is relevant to young people's health in the sources I opened, it is through sleep and supplements.

The licensing case shows two interests in conflict, and I describe both. The plaintiff argued that a rule requiring a license before giving paid dietary advice restricts what a person may say. The Eleventh Circuit held that the rule regulates professional conduct and burdens speech only incidentally. The interests on each side, as I understand them, are protection from unqualified advice and access to guidance from people who are not dietitians (INFERENCE; the report I read states the plaintiff's claim and the court's holding and not the parties' full arguments). I did not open the opinion, and I draw no conclusion on which interest should prevail.

The topic's vocabulary is also that of working life. Workplace words appear in 570 posts (32.1%) and burnout, stress or well-being words in 519 (29.2%). The CDC pages I read concern adolescents, and I opened no source on adult workers' sleep or burnout, so I make no claim about the topic's reach among working adults beyond the word counts.

My conclusion for this chapter is that the rules on health claims set specific requirements for evidence and labeling, that the posts mostly use the vocabulary of wellness while a minority use the vocabulary of treatment or results, and that the strongest evidence of harm to young people in my sources, the melatonin data, bears on how a product is regulated and not on what the posts say (INFERENCE).

### 19.5 How this topic bears on mental health

This topic has the most direct mental-health content of the twenty, because its posts discuss sleep, stress and well-being, and the sources I opened treat those subjects as linked.

Words about mental health or stress appear in 519 of 1,775 posts (29.2%), sleep words in 491 (27.7%), and weight-loss words in 313 (17.6%). The American Psychological Association's 2023 advisory says that adolescents should get at least eight hours of sleep each night, that social media use near bedtime is associated with sleep disruption, and that exposure to eating-disorder content may promote similar behavior in vulnerable youth. It also associates appearance-based comparison with disordered eating. Section 19.4 reports the CDC's survey figures on adolescent sleep and its report of 260,435 pediatric melatonin ingestions to U.S. poison control centers from 2012 through 2021.

Workplace words appear in 570 posts (32.1%). The World Health Organization describes burn-out as an occupational phenomenon that results from chronic workplace stress that has not been successfully managed, and the Surgeon General's workplace framework says that long hours raise the risk of exhaustion, anxiety and depression. Advice in these posts on energy and stress therefore concerns subjects that have an official evidence base. I did not open a source that tests whether the supplements the posts name (228 posts, 12.8%) change sleep or mood, and I make no claim about their effect.

The health-claim rules in Section 19.2 govern what a seller may say. The American Psychological Association's 2025 advisory on AI notes that health misinformation can lead to harmful behaviors and delayed treatment. By analogy, an unsupported claim about sleep or stress could delay a person from seeking care (INFERENCE; the advisory concerns AI output and not posts).

### 19.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show that any post in this topic makes a false or unsubstantiated health claim, promotes a supplement or a drug, offers advice that requires a license, or has a connection that needed disclosure. The counts are of words. A post that tells a reader to see a doctor is counted with a post that advises against it. The posts do not include the private messages that follow a "DM me" call, images, comment threads or the products' labels.

My reading of the law has gaps. The fetch tool returned summaries, and I quote only the words it placed in quotation marks. I did not open the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act, the Dietary Supplement Health and Education Act, the FDA's warning letters, the Commission's Health Products Compliance Guidance itself, the *Teami* complaint or order, the Eleventh Circuit's opinion, any state dietetics statute, the official EU text of Regulation 1924/2006, Commission Regulation (EU) No 432/2012 or the Directive on food supplements. The EU text I read is the retained UK copy. I did not search for data on childhood obesity or for the study of melatonin labels that the CDC report discusses, and the CDC report's own count of serious outcomes is inconsistent between its text and its table.

Two questions remain open after this chapter. One is whether a comment given in return for engagement under a post that sells a health product is a testimonial or an endorsement with an unexpected material connection, in the sense of the *Teami* order. The other is whether private-message coaching that follows a public post, offered to a state's residents by an unlicensed coach, is "professional conduct" in the Eleventh Circuit's sense, and I found no authority that applies the decision to coaching conducted by direct message.

## Chapter 20. Learning, courses and skills: earnings claims, the Business Opportunity Rule, vocational-school guides, state school licensing and AI literacy

### 20.1 The topic in the data

Learning, courses and skills is the seventeenth-largest topic, with 1,690 posts, which is 2.6% of the 64,970 placed posts. Nearly half of them come from HyperClapper, 834 posts (49.3%), followed by the podawaa file with 555 (32.8%), the 65 exports with 288 (17.0%) and LinkBoost-2025 with 13 (0.8%). The median post is 857 characters and the 90th percentile is 2,849. A link appears in 934 posts (55.3%), including 625 of the 834 HyperClapper posts (74.9%) and none in LinkBoost-2025. Five or more hashtags appear in 260 posts (15.4%).

The vocabulary is that of instruction for sale. A course, bootcamp, cohort, workshop, webinar, masterclass, curriculum, class, program, academy, certification or certified word appears in 819 posts (48.5%). A certificate, certification, accredited, diploma, degree, badge or credential word appears in 459 (27.2%), although "degree" has other meanings. The word "certified" appears in 21 posts (1.2%) and a word beginning "accredit" in 4 (0.2%). A coach, mentor or consultant word appears in 194 (11.5%), and an enrollment word (enroll, sign up, join, waitlist, cohort, seats) in 99 (5.9%). Price language is uncommon. A fee word (tuition, pricing, price, cost, fee, discount, coupon, promo code, percent off, offer) appears in 162 posts (9.6%), a dollar figure in 60 (3.6%) and a word of payment (premium, subscription, membership, purchase, buy, "pay for," or "paid" before course, program, training or cohort) in 61 (3.6%). A refund or money-back word appears in 1 post (0.1%), a cancel or withdraw word in 2 (0.1%), and a "terms and conditions" phrase in none. A guarantee, refund, money-back or risk-free word appears in 10 (0.6%).

Artificial intelligence appears in about half of the posts. The word "AI" or the phrase "artificial intelligence" appears in 813 posts (48.1%). A phrase about AI skills (AI skills, AI literacy, AI training, AI course, upskilling in AI, learn AI, prompt engineering, ChatGPT course) appears in 296 (17.5%), and a tool word (prompt, GPT, Copilot, Gemini, Claude, LLM, generative) in 700 (41.4%). A post that contains the capitalized word AI and a course, bootcamp, masterclass, academy or certificate word appears 666 times (39.4%), 565 of them in HyperClapper. The phrase "AI literacy" appears in 5 posts (0.3%), and the pattern "EU AI Act," "AI Act" or "Article 4" adds no further post.

Jobs and earnings are the next subjects. A job, career, hired, resume, interview or salary word appears in 748 posts (44.3%). A narrow pattern for promised job outcomes (get hired, job guarantee, guaranteed job, placement or interview, job placement, career change, placement rate) matches 33 posts (2.0%), 30 of them in HyperClapper, and a wider pattern that lists get hired, land a job, job guarantee, placement, career change and break into matches 75 (4.4%), 72 in HyperClapper. A strict pattern for an earnings claim matches 30 posts (1.8%), and all 30 are in HyperClapper. The pattern is a form of earn, make or generate followed by a currency figure, the phrase "passive income," "six-figure" or "seven-figure," or an amount per month, day, week or year. Of those 30 posts, 29 contain the capitalized word AI, 24 a course, bootcamp, masterclass, academy or program word, 29 a link, 23 the word "free" and 28 a job, career or salary word. None contains a disclaimer phrase (results vary, not typical, no guarantee, individual results, disclaimer, typical results, not a guarantee). Across the whole topic, a disclaimer phrase appears in 3 posts (0.2%). A looser list of income words (income, earn, six-figure, make money) appears in 92 posts (5.4%). Phrases about quitting a job, financial freedom, the 9-to-5, working from home or a laptop lifestyle appear in 2 (0.1%), and a phrase about making money with AI in none.

The posts use proof, authority and urgency in small numbers. A number of students, learners, members or similar, or a phrase such as "trusted by" or "joined by," appears in 128 posts (7.6%), and the narrower form of a number followed by students, learners, graduates or alumni in 5 (0.3%). A word of credential or authority (PhD, professor, certified, accredited, expert, authority, bestselling, MBA) appears in 63 (3.7%). A phrase of scarcity (limited time, spots, seats or places; "only" and a number of spots; spots or seats left; deadline; ends today, tonight or soon) appears in 21 (1.2%), 19 of them in HyperClapper. The word "free" appears in 459 posts (27.2%), although the pattern also matches words such as "risk-free," and a free course, training, masterclass, webinar, workshop, class, trial, guide, ebook, checklist, template, consultation or call appears in 214 (12.7%). Of these, 194 are in HyperClapper, which is 23.3% of that source's posts in the topic.

Young people and education appear in a minority. A word for students, teens, kids, graduates, college, school, Gen Z or interns appears in 185 posts (10.9%), the word "students" in 103 (6.1%), and a word for teens, kids, children, high school, Gen Z or minors in 35 (2.1%). College, university, graduate, student loan, tuition or campus words appear in 62 (3.7%), and teacher, classroom, K-12, curriculum, homework, exam or grade words in 128 (7.6%). A phrase about AI in education (AI tutor, students using AI or ChatGPT, cheating, plagiarism, academic integrity, AI-generated essays) appears in 8 (0.5%), a phrase about the future of work or future skills in 6 (0.4%), and a post with the capitalized word AI and a student, teen, kid, child, graduate or Gen Z word in 56 (3.3%). The name of a learning platform (Coursera, Udemy, LinkedIn Learning, edX, Khan Academy, Skillshare, Pluralsight, DataCamp, Udacity) appears in 93 posts (5.5%), and a corporate-training word (learning and development, corporate training, upskill, reskill, workforce development, onboarding) in 56 (3.3%).

Words about the exchange of engagement do not appear: "pod," "engagement pod" and "engagement group" occur in none of the 1,690 posts, and neither do the words of multi-level marketing. A "DM me," "inbox me" or "message me" call appears in 14 posts (0.8%), against 1.19% of all 273,771 posts, and a request to comment or follow in 137 (8.1%). These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts words and not claims. A post that warns against an expensive course is counted with a post that sells one.

### 20.2 The laws that apply

Seven items bear on this topic: the Business Opportunity Rule and the Commission's proposal to extend it; the Commission's guides for private vocational and distance education schools; the Endorsement Guides on expert endorsements; the Commission's guide on the word "free"; state licensing of private postsecondary schools; the EU's rules on withdrawal and on unfair practices; and the EU's duty of AI literacy. The general standard of deception and substantiation is in Chapter 3, and the Endorsement Guides on testimonials and on material connections are in Chapters 14 and 16.

**The Business Opportunity Rule.** The Rule is at 16 CFR Part 437. In the current text on the eCFR, a business opportunity is a commercial arrangement in which a seller solicits a prospective purchaser to enter a new business, the purchaser makes a required payment, and the seller represents that it or designated persons will provide locations, outlets, accounts, customers or buy-backs of the purchaser's goods or services (§ 437.1(c), paraphrased by the fetch tool). A seller must give the purchaser the material information required by §§ 437.3(a) and 437.4(a) in writing "at least seven calendar days before" the purchaser signs a contract or pays. A seller may not make an earnings claim unless it has a reasonable basis for the claim when made, holds written substantiation and makes it available on request, and furnishes an earnings claim statement, a document that must carry the title "EARNINGS CLAIM STATEMENT REQUIRED BY LAW" and give the number and percentage of purchasers who reached the claimed level (§ 437.4). Section 437.6 bars, among other things, misstating sales or income figures, presenting the opportunity as employment and misrepresenting purchasers' experiences, and § 437.7 requires records, including substantiation for each earnings claim, to be kept for three years (VERIFIED against the eCFR text, as summarized; the summary paraphrased the definition, I did not see §§ 437.3, 437.5 or 437.8, and I did not find the date of the page).

**The proposal to cover coaching.** The Commission's page for its notice of proposed rulemaking, matter R511993, is dated 13 January 2025. The notice would retitle the Rule the "Business and Money-Making Opportunity Rule." It says of coaching and similar programs that they "are often structured in a way that does not obviously trigger coverage under the Rule" (the fetch tool's quotation). The notice would add a category of "money-making opportunity" that covers business coaching and investment opportunities where the purchaser makes a required payment. Sellers in that category would have to meet the substantiation and recordkeeping requirements and most of the misrepresentation prohibitions, and the disclosure document would still be required only of business opportunities. The notice also considers excluding certain Title IV higher-education programs (VERIFIED against the notice, as summarized; the summary showed the comment period as 60 days after publication in the Federal Register and gave no calendar date). A trade article reports that Commissioner Ferguson dissented from the proposals, that Commissioner Holyoak joined him, and that an executive order paused pending rules for review (STATED). The Commission's page for the notice lists it as a proposal and records no final rule and no withdrawal, and the eCFR text I read still states the unamended definition. I found no report of a final rule as of the date of my searches in October 2026, though I cannot exclude one.

**An enforcement example in this subject.** *Federal Trade Commission v. Click Profit, LLC*, No. 1:25-cv-20973-DSL, concerns a Florida company and affiliated companies and four individuals sued as officers, directors or owners. The Commission's case page says that the Commission alleges that the defendants promised large earnings from "passive income" through an AI-powered system for selling products on Amazon, Walmart and TikTok, and that they claimed affiliations with well-known brands to persuade consumers to pay tens of thousands of dollars each. The court issued a temporary restraining order with an asset freeze and a temporary receiver, and preliminary injunctions followed. The page records stipulated orders for permanent injunction and monetary judgment in August 2025 that permanently ban the defendants from the industry and require them to surrender cash, real estate and personal property for consumer redress. The Commission's press-release headline, as the case page reproduces it, put consumers' losses at "At Least $14 Million." A trade article from August 2025 reports the Commission's allegation that most consumers earned "little or nothing" (STATED). The page does not name the court, does not cite the provisions the complaint relies on and still lists the case as "Pending," and it gives conflicting dates for the complaint (3 or 18 March 2025) and for the preliminary injunction, so I give only the month of the final orders (VERIFIED against the case page, as summarized; I did not open the complaint, the orders or the release). Chapter 14 sets out the Commission's other matters about e-commerce coaching and income schemes, and Chapter 12 sets out the *Air AI* matter.

A June 2026 business blog post from the Commission, whose date comes from its web address, tells marketers: "Make sure you can back up your earnings (or lifestyle) claims." It is addressed to multi-level marketing companies and distributors and describes matters of that kind from April and May 2026. It does not mention coaching, courses or AI (VERIFIED against the post, as summarized).

**The Guides for Private Vocational and Distance Education Schools.** The Commission's notice of 10 August 1998, effective 9 October 1998, amended these Guides to add a provision on claims about post-graduation employment and salaries. The notice calls the Guides "administrative interpretations of laws administered by the Commission," says that "The amendment does not create new requirements," and says that "Conduct inconsistent with the Guides may result in corrective action by the Commission" (VERIFIED against the Federal Register notice, as summarized). For the content of the sections I read an unofficial mirror of the Code of Federal Regulations, again as summarized. Section 254.0 applies the Guides to privately owned schools that offer resident or distance courses preparing people for jobs, trades or skilled work, or helping people improve their appearance, social skills or personality, and it excludes primary and secondary schools and colleges offering at least a two-year program that is generally creditable toward a bachelor's degree. The Guides address accreditation, which they define by reference to an accrediting agency recognized by the U.S. Department of Education; the availability of jobs after graduation and graduates' success and salaries; enrollment prerequisites, including claims that a lack of schooling or experience is no obstacle; credentials, which may not misstate course content; and the disclosure, before enrollment, of the total cost of the program and the refund policy. Students financed by loans are to be told that the loans must be repaid whether or not they finish or find a job (STATED, from the mirror).

**Expert endorsements and the word "free."** Under 16 CFR 255.3, if an advertisement says or implies that an endorser is an expert, "the endorser's qualifications must in fact give the endorser the expertise," and the endorsement must rest on "an actual exercise of the expertise" and on examination or testing "at least as extensive as" a person with that expertise would need (VERIFIED against the Cornell text, as summarized). The Commission's Guide Concerning Use of the Word "Free," 16 CFR 251.1, says that such offers "must be made with extreme care so as to avoid any possibility that consumers will be misled or deceived," and that the terms, conditions and obligations for receiving and keeping the free item should be set out "clearly and conspicuously at the outset of the offer." A footnote reached by an asterisk does not count as disclosure at the outset (VERIFIED against the eCFR text, as summarized; the page does not say whether the guide binds, and it dates from 1971).

**State licensing of private schools.** In California the Bureau for Private Postsecondary Education approves and oversees private postsecondary institutions. A 2013 legislative analysis says that the Act "requires all unaccredited colleges in California to be approved by BPPE" and exempts an institution that "offers solely avocational or recreational educational programs" (VERIFIED against the analysis, as summarized, which describes the law as it stood in 2013). A modified citation of 15 May 2019 shows the Bureau applying the Act to a San Francisco company that advertised programs, one of them a bootcamp, priced at $1,999 and $7,995. The Bureau cited Education Code sections 94886 (approval to operate), 94858 (the definition of a private postsecondary educational institution) and 94874 (an exemption it found unmet because the company advertised to the public and charged more than $2,500). It set a fine of $75,000, reduced to $1,000 after an informal conference on 3 May 2019 in which the Bureau cited new substantive facts, and it ordered abatement, including the end of recruiting and enrollment and a roster of enrolled students with the amounts they had paid (VERIFIED against the citation, as summarized; I did not open the Education Code, so the dollar threshold in the exemption may have changed since 2019, and I have not named the company because the citation concerns a single 2019 action).

**The EU.** The Commission's consumer information page says that a consumer has 14 days to withdraw from an online purchase, counted for services from the date of the contract, and that the right does not apply to digital content once the consumer has "expressly agreed to lose your right of withdrawal by starting the performance" or to a fully delivered service when the consumer expressly agreed to start immediately and acknowledged the loss of the right (VERIFIED against the page, as summarized; I did not open Directive 2011/83/EU). Annex I of the Unfair Commercial Practices Directive lists as unfair in all circumstances, in point 7, "Falsely stating that a product will only be available for a very limited time," in order to elicit an immediate decision, and, in point 20, describing a product as "gratis," "free," "without charge" or similar "if the consumer has to pay anything other than the unavoidable cost of responding to the commercial practice" and collecting or paying for delivery (VERIFIED against the Directive as adopted in 2005, on the UK legislation site).

**The EU duty of AI literacy.** Article 4 of the AI Act has applied since 2 February 2025. As first written, it required providers and deployers of AI systems to "take measures to ensure, to their best extent" a sufficient level of AI literacy among their staff and others operating the systems on their behalf (quoted in a law-firm article that does not mention the amendment and adds that Article 4 "doesn't specify penalties for non-compliance"). Regulation (EU) 2026/1744, the Digital Omnibus on AI, replaced the text. One secondary source gives its publication in the Official Journal as 24 July 2026 and its entry into force as 27 July 2026. A second article and a Goodwin alert I read earlier also give 27 July 2026, while the Commission's page, as summarized, says mid-July 2026. The new text requires providers and deployers to "take measures to support" AI literacy. The Commission's page says: "AI literacy remains an obligation for providers and deployers of AI systems," that "no specific – or 'sufficient' - level is mandated," and that "This does not imply that a certain level of AI literacy of any individual is required." It adds that national market surveillance authorities supervise and enforce the duty from 2 August 2026, and that following the practices in the Commission's repository of AI literacy initiatives "does not grant presumption of compliance with Article 4." The same regulation moved the application date for high-risk systems in Annex III, which include education, from 2 August 2026 to 2 December 2027 (the change to Article 4 is CORROBORATED across the Commission's page and two secondary sources, and the Annex III date comes from one secondary source; I did not open the Regulation or the Official Journal, and I did not find how the penalty framework applies to Article 4).

### 20.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

The rules reach a post when it sells instruction or a course for a price, and they reach it more firmly when the post states what the instruction will earn or lead to.

The author is the party on whom nearly all of these rules fall. A seller who posts an earnings figure needs the reasonable basis and substantiation that § 437.4 describes if the seller is selling a business opportunity as the Rule defines it, and the general standard of Chapter 3 applies to the claim in any case (INFERENCE as applied). The strict earnings pattern matches 30 posts (1.8%), all in HyperClapper, and none of the 30 contains a disclaimer phrase. The pattern also matches a poster's description of the poster's own income and advice about passive income, and the data do not show what any of the 30 sells, so the count is of posts that state a figure and not of posts that make a claim about what a buyer will earn.

Whether the Business Opportunity Rule applies depends on what is sold. The *Click Profit* case page describes defendants who allegedly offered a system for selling on retail platforms to consumers who paid tens of thousands of dollars each, which resembles an arrangement in which a seller supplies accounts or outlets. A seller of instruction alone supplies none, and the Commission's own notice says that such programs often do not obviously trigger the Rule (INFERENCE from the notice as summarized). This is the question Chapter 14 deferred to this chapter. The topic's coaching words appear in 194 posts (11.5%) and its course words in 819 (48.5%), and I cannot tell from a post whether the seller also supplies accounts, stores or customers. The answer for a seller of instruction alone, on the sources I read, is that the present Rule does not clearly reach it and that the proposed extension had not become a rule when I searched.

Claims about jobs reach the Vocational School Guides if the seller is a school within § 254.0. The narrow pattern for promised job outcomes matches 33 posts (2.0%), 30 in HyperClapper. Nothing I opened says whether an individual who sells a skills course on a social platform is a "privately owned school" in the Guides' sense, or whether such a seller needs approval under a state's school law. The Guides cover courses that prepare people for jobs, which describes much of the topic's vocabulary, but the Guides are interpretations of the FTC Act and not a statute. The deception standard of Chapter 3 reaches a job-outcome claim whether or not the seller is a school (INFERENCE).

Claims about credentials reach the same Guides and the Endorsement Guides. The word "accredited" is a defined term in the Guides, and it appears in 4 posts (0.2%). The word "certified" appears in 21 (1.2%), and certificate words in 444 (26.3%). A certificate word may refer to a certificate of completion, which carries no accreditation claim, or to a credential that does, and the counts cannot separate the two (INFERENCE). Section 255.3 applies to an endorser presented as an expert, and a seller's statement about the seller's own degree is a claim about the seller, which falls under the general standard (INFERENCE). Credential and authority words appear in 63 posts (3.7%). Where a post states how many students or members a program has, the statement is an objective claim of a kind that the substantiation standard of Chapter 3 covers. The 128 posts (7.6%) with the social-proof pattern include such statements and may include others (INFERENCE as applied).

Offers of something free reach the Free Guide and, in the EU, point 20 of Annex I. A free course, webinar, trial, guide or call appears in 214 posts (12.7%), and 194 of them are in HyperClapper. The Free Guide asks that conditions be stated at the outset. Point 20 asks whether the consumer pays anything beyond the unavoidable cost of responding. Whether giving an email address or a phone number in return for a free webinar is paying anything is a question on which I found no authority (INFERENCE; the posts do not show what a link leads to). Scarcity phrases appear in 21 posts (1.2%), and point 7 concerns only a false statement of limited availability, which the data cannot reveal.

A buyer in the EU has a withdrawal right that almost no post mentions. Terms, refunds and cancellation language is almost absent from the topic (refund words in 1 post, cancel or withdraw words in 2, and "terms and conditions" in none). Posts rarely carry terms, so the absence says little about the landing pages that the 934 links lead to, which are outside the data (INFERENCE).

The AI-literacy duty reaches the employers who buy training and not the posts. A company that deploys AI systems must take measures to support literacy among the people who use them, and a seller of an AI-skills course is subject to Article 4 only if the seller is itself a provider or deployer (INFERENCE from the Commission's wording). AI-skills phrases appear in 296 posts (17.5%), and "AI literacy" in 5 (0.3%). The Commission says that replicating the practices in its repository gives no presumption of compliance, and I infer that a course certificate gives none either: it shows that training took place and does not show that an employer met Article 4 (INFERENCE).

The engaging member is the party to whom the questions of Chapters 14 and 16 apply. A member who comments "this course changed my career" under a course post is giving a statement of the kind that Chapter 14 describes as a consumer testimonial under § 255.2, and the question that Chapters 16 and 19 left open applies: whether a comment given in return for engagement involves a connection that needed disclosure. The topic has 137 requests to comment or follow (8.1%) and 14 "DM me" calls (0.8%), and the data do not show a member who gave such a comment. The tool operator is not the advertiser, and the platform's position is the one in Chapter 10.

Table 20.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on learning, courses and skills, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 20.1.** Rules that reach posts on learning, courses and skills, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy or penalty stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Business Opportunity Rule, 16 CFR Part 437 | Sellers whose offer meets § 437.1(c) | *Click Profit* (case page); see also Chapters 12 and 14 | Stipulated orders (August 2025): industry ban and surrender of cash, real estate and personal property; amounts not stated on the page |
| Proposed Business and Money-Making Opportunity Rule (13 January 2025) | Would add business coaching and investment opportunities with a required payment | None (a proposal) | Not applicable; no final rule found |
| Guides for Private Vocational and Distance Education Schools, 16 CFR Part 254 | Privately owned schools within § 254.0 | None opened | The Guides are voluntary; conduct inconsistent with them "may result in corrective action" |
| Endorsement Guides, 16 CFR 255.3 | Experts who endorse, and advertisers who use them | None opened | Not stated on the page I read |
| Guide on "free," 16 CFR 251.1 | Sellers who advertise something "free" | None opened | Not stated on the page I read |
| California Private Postsecondary Education Act (as cited in a 2019 citation) | Institutions operating without approval | Bureau citation, 15 May 2019 | $75,000 fine reduced to $1,000; order of abatement |
| EU 14-day withdrawal right | Traders who sell to consumers online | None opened | Consumer may withdraw within 14 days; exceptions for digital content and fully delivered services after express agreement |
| Directive 2005/29/EC, Annex I points 7 and 20 | Traders who falsely limit availability or misuse "free" | None opened | Not stated in the text I read |
| AI Act Article 4, as amended by Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 | Providers and deployers of AI systems | None opened | Supervised by national market surveillance authorities from 2 August 2026; penalty for Article 4 not found |

### 20.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

The harm alleged in this chapter's enforcement source is money paid for earnings that did not follow, and the stake for coming generations is that the market for skills is where people will pay to prepare for work that is changing.

The headline of the Commission's press release in the *Click Profit* matter reports consumers' losses of at least $14 million, and the case page's allegations describe payments of tens of thousands of dollars by individual buyers. The August 2025 trade article reports the allegation that most buyers earned "little or nothing." Chapter 14 reports the Commission's data spotlight that about one in three job and business-opportunity losses started on social media. The *Click Profit* statements are allegations, the case page does not say whether the defendants admitted them, and I did not open the orders. The count in this topic that comes closest to such a claim is the 30 posts of the strict earnings pattern, which is 1.8% of the topic, and I make no claim that any of them is false.

Employers expect a large demand for training. The World Economic Forum's *Future of Jobs Report 2025*, which surveyed more than 1,000 employers representing more than 14 million workers in 22 industry clusters and 55 economies, reports that employers expect about 39% of workers' current skill sets to be transformed or to become outdated between 2025 and 2030. It reports that of every 100 workers, 59 would need training by 2030, that employers expect 29 to be upskilled in their present roles and 19 to be upskilled and redeployed, and that 11 are unlikely to get the reskilling they need (VERIFIED against the Forum's digest, as summarized; the figures are employers' expectations). A market that must serve that demand also has a large incentive to promise outcomes, which is a reason for the claims rules in this chapter to matter, though no source I opened measures the amount of overstatement (INFERENCE).

The position of young workers is the subject of the Stanford working paper in Chapter 12, which reports that employment of workers aged 22 to 25 in AI-exposed occupations stands 19% below the level it would have reached had it kept pace with less-exposed peers, and its authors describe the finding as descriptive. Pew's survey of 1,458 U.S. teenagers in autumn 2025, in Chapter 4, reports that 64% use AI chatbots and that 54% report using them for schoolwork (the base for the 54% is not recorded in my notes). UNESCO launched competency frameworks for students and for teachers at Digital Learning Week 2024. The student framework names four competencies: "A human-centred mindset," "Ethics of AI," "AI techniques and applications" and "AI system design," and states the aim of engaging with AI "in a safe, ethical and responsible manner" (STATED, from a UNESCO national commission page, as summarized). This topic's posts say little about children and schools. Words about teens, kids, children, high school, Gen Z or minors appear in 35 posts (2.1%), and a phrase about AI in education in 8 (0.5%). Most of the topic addresses adults who want a career or an income, as the 748 job and career posts (44.3%) suggest (INFERENCE from the counts).

Several protections in this chapter are procedural and bear on those who pay for education with borrowed or scarce money. The Vocational School Guides, as the mirror summarizes them, say that a school should disclose the total cost and the refund policy before enrollment and that borrowers should be told they must repay whether or not they finish or find a job. The California order of abatement requires a roster of students with the amounts they paid, which would let a regulator identify who paid what. The EU withdrawal right gives a buyer 14 days to reconsider an online service, subject to the exceptions in Section 20.2. Each informs or protects a buyer around the time of sale, and none verifies that a course teaches what it says (INFERENCE from the sources). The Guides define accreditation by reference to agencies recognized by the Department of Education, so the value of a credential that a buyer hopes to use in a later job depends on a status that a social-media post can state and that the buyer must check elsewhere (INFERENCE).

The amendment narrowed the duty in Article 4. The first text asked providers and deployers to take measures to ensure, to their best extent, a sufficient level of AI literacy. The amended text asks them to take measures to support literacy, and the Commission's page says that it does not imply that any individual's level of AI literacy is required. I do not judge the change. Its effect on an individual worker is that the law guarantees no level of skill, so the worker has to judge the training offered (INFERENCE). That individual may be a young person entering a workplace in which the employer's training is the only AI instruction available, and I opened no source on what such training contains.

The pods in the data bear on this topic through visibility. A post that sells a course and is boosted by a group of members is shown more widely among people who are choosing training (INFERENCE; the data do not measure the reach of any post). The posts in the topic never mention a pod, and no source I opened records an instance in which a boost changed a buyer's decision.

My conclusion for this chapter is that the Business Opportunity Rule, which the enforcement examples in Chapter 14 invoke, applies on its present text to a seller who supplies locations, outlets, accounts, customers or buy-backs (INFERENCE), that the proposal to cover coaching had not become a rule in the sources I found, and that the posts in this topic which state earnings are few, all in one source and without disclaimers (INFERENCE as to significance).
