For coming generations, the sources show a large expected need for training, an amendment in the EU that narrowed the AI-literacy duty, and no source I opened that tests whether a course delivers what its advertising says.

### 20.5 How this topic bears on mental health

Courses and credentials bear on mental health through money and work, and the evidence I opened covers debt and unemployment.

A course, bootcamp, cohort or certification word appears in 819 of 1,690 posts (48.5%), a fee word in 162 (9.6%), and college, student-loan or tuition words in 62 (3.7%). The debt analysis in Section 9.5 found that people in unsecured debt were more than three times as likely to have a mental health problem (STATED), and it does not say which comes first. A course fee is one of many routes into debt. The meta-analysis by Paul and Moser (Section 15.5) found that unemployed people showed more distress, which matters because many posts in this topic present training as a route to work. In the American Psychological Association's 2025 survey, 51% of workers said that finding a new job would take a significant amount of time if they lost theirs, and 44% said they would need to move to a different field or industry.

The rules in Section 20.2, among them the Business Opportunity Rule and the Guides for Private Vocational and Distance Education Schools, require that earnings and employment claims be supported. A course that promises a job at a stated income and does not deliver it leaves a buyer with the cost and without the income (INFERENCE as to the route). I found no study of buyers of courses whose claims proved false.

AI literacy is the one subject where I found a professional recommendation aimed at students. The association's 2025 advisory says that schools, policymakers and developers should teach what AI is and what its limits are. A phrase pairing AI with a student, teen, kid, child, graduate or Gen Z word appears in 56 posts (3.3%).

### 20.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show that any post in this topic makes a false earnings, job or credential claim, sells a business opportunity, operates a school that needed approval, offers something called free that carries a cost, or involves an exchange of engagement. The counts are of words. A post that quotes an earnings figure to criticize it is counted with a post that sells it. The posts do not include the landing pages behind the 934 links, the courses themselves, enrollment contracts, refund terms, private messages that follow a "DM me" call, comment threads or any buyer's outcome. The counts of the strict earnings pattern and the narrow job-outcome pattern depend on the patterns I chose, and a differently defined pattern would give other numbers.

My reading of the law has gaps. The fetch tool returned summaries, and I quote only the words it placed in quotation marks. I did not open the *Click Profit* complaint, orders or release, §§ 437.3, 437.5 and 437.8, the notice of proposed rulemaking beyond a summary or the Commission's separate proposal on earnings claims, the official text of Part 254 (I read an unofficial mirror), Part 255 beyond § 255.3, any rule on negative-option or free-trial offers, the California Education Code, any other state's school or business-opportunity law, the Department of Education's rules on accreditation and student aid, Directive 2011/83/EU, Regulation (EU) 2026/1744 or the official text of Article 4. The trade article on the Commissioners' positions is a secondary source. The status of the proposed Rule is the most perishable point in the chapter, and my searches ran to October 2026.

Three questions remain open after this chapter. The first is whether a coaching-only seller, which supplies instruction and nothing else, is a business opportunity under § 437.1(c) as it stands, a question on which the notice of proposed rulemaking says only that coverage is not obvious and for which I found no decision. The second is whether the vocational-school guides and a state's private-school law reach an individual who sells a skills course on a social platform, which I found no authority to answer. The third is whether giving contact details in return for a free webinar is a payment for the purposes of point 20 of Annex I of the Unfair Commercial Practices Directive.

## Chapter 21. Luxury travel and hotels: total-price rules, travel-seller registration, package-travel protection and hotel liability for the trafficking of minors

### 21.1 The topic in the data

Luxury travel and hotels is the eighteenth-largest topic, with 1,600 posts, which is 2.5% of the 64,970 placed posts. Almost nine in ten posts come from one source: 1,405 come from the podawaa file (87.8%), 110 from the 65 exports (6.9%), 74 from HyperClapper (4.6%) and 11 from LinkBoost-2025 (0.7%). The median post is 458 characters and the 90th percentile is 902. A link appears in 588 posts (36.8%). Hashtags are the topic's most visible feature: 1,319 posts carry at least one (82.4%), and 905 carry five or more (56.6%), of which 865 are in the podawaa file.

The most common hashtags show what the topic contains. A hashtag for big data appears in 467 posts (29.2%), a hashtag for travel in 407 (25.4%), a hashtag for AI in 358 (22.4%), luxury travel in 321 (20.1%), hospitality in 306 (19.1%) and travel agent in 273 (17.1%). A hashtag for structured data appears in 205 (12.8%), and two further hashtags that are not travel words appear in 14.9% and 14.8% of the posts. I do not name those two, because they may identify a single account. An AI mention in any form (the capitalized word AI, the phrase "artificial intelligence," or an AI hashtag) appears in 537 posts (33.6%). In running text, the capitalized word AI or the phrase "artificial intelligence" appears in 167 (10.4%). An AI hashtag (#AI or #artificialintelligence) appears in 512 (32.0%), and in 370 posts (23.1%) the hashtag is the only mention, which is 68.9% of all posts that mention AI in any form. A post that carries an AI hashtag and a hashtag for big data or structured data appears 458 times (28.6%), and all of these are in the podawaa file. Of the 905 posts with five or more hashtags, 401 carry an AI hashtag. These counts show that a count of "AI" in this topic mostly counts a hashtag block.

The vocabulary of the text is that of hotels and advisors. A hotel, resort, suite, boutique, villa, bed-and-breakfast or hospitality word appears in 1,058 posts (66.1%), and a travel word (travel, trip, vacation, holiday, destination, itinerary, journey, cruise, flight, airline, airport) in 858 (53.6%). A luxury word (luxury, luxe, high-end, exclusive, five-star, premium, bespoke, private jet, island, villa, yacht or chef) appears in 468 (29.2%), and a booking word (book, reserve, availability, check-in) in 368 (23.0%). The name of a hotel group (Marriott, Hilton, Hyatt, Four Seasons, Ritz, Rosewood, Aman, Mandarin Oriental, Belmond, Accor, IHG, Wyndham) appears in 149 posts (9.3%), and the name of a destination such as Dubai, the Maldives, Bali, Abu Dhabi, Monaco, the Seychelles or Bora Bora in 46 (2.9%). A word of wellness, spa or healing appears in 94 (5.9%). A family, kids, children or teens word appears in 65 (4.1%), and in 50 (3.1%) together with a hotel or resort word.

Words about price and fees are rare. A phrase for a fee or a total price (resort fee, destination fee, amenity fee, service fee or charge, hidden fee, cleaning fee, nightly rate, all-in price, total price) appears in none of the 1,600 posts. A per-night price pattern (a number followed by "per night," "/night," "a night" or "nightly"; "from" followed by an amount; or an amount followed by "per person," "pp" or "per night") appears in 3 (0.2%). A dollar figure appears in 41 (2.6%), and in 31 (1.9%) together with a hotel, resort or suite word. A percentage appears in 25 (1.6%). A word for a deal, offer, discount, promotion, sale, saving or early-bird rate appears in 189 posts (11.8%), and in 20 (1.2%) together with a number. A phrase of "book direct," "direct booking," "best rate," "lowest price" or "price match" appears in 24 (1.5%). A scarcity phrase (limited time, spots, availability, rooms or offer; "only" and a number of rooms, spots or seats; "selling out," "sold out," "last minute," "hurry," "don't miss") appears in 34 (2.1%). A guarantee word appears in 9 (0.6%), and the word "free" in 63 (3.9%). A phrase of no cost (no cost, no charge, no fee, no extra cost, free of charge, no booking fee) appears in 6 (0.4%), 5 of them in HyperClapper, where the topic has only 74 posts.

Travel advisors are a visible group. A word for a travel advisor, agent, designer, planner or consultant, or for IATA, a host agency, a seller of travel, a consortium or the name of an advisor network, appears in 167 posts (10.4%), and 166 of them are in the podawaa file. A perks word (complimentary, upgrade, resort or hotel credit, early check-in, late check-out, free breakfast, perks, amenity, VIP) appears in 88 posts (5.5%), and in 35 (2.2%) together with an advisor word. A recruiting phrase (become a travel agent, start your own business or agency, work from home, home-based, join my team, host agency, be your own boss) appears in 2 (0.1%) and an income word in 12 (0.8%). A word of licensing, bonding or protection (ATOL, ABTA, bonded, licensed and insured, travel insurance, financial protection, ASTA, CLIA, IATA, CST, seller of travel) appears in none.

Claims about quality and conduct appear in small numbers. A superlative (best hotel, resort, spa, destination, place or stay; #1; number one; world's best, finest or leading; finest; unrivalled; unmatched; unparalleled) appears in 78 (4.9%), and an award or ranking word (award, Michelin, Forbes Travel Guide, Condé Nast, Travel + Leisure, ranked, voted, recognized as) in 59 (3.7%). A review word (review, TripAdvisor, Google Reviews, Yelp, Booking.com, star rating, guest feedback, testimonial) appears in 15 (0.9%). A sponsorship or gifting word (#ad, #sponsored, sponsored, paid partnership, gifted, complimentary, comped, press trip, fam trip, hosted by, in partnership with, courtesy of, collaboration with, #collab) appears in 35 (2.2%), though "complimentary" is also a word of hotel perks and the count includes it. A sustainability word (sustainab-, eco-friendly, eco-luxury, carbon, net-zero, regenerative, Green Key, LEED) appears in 36 (2.2%), and in 24 (1.5%) together with a hotel or resort word. Words for data, personalization, privacy, GDPR, guest data, cookies or tracking appear in 109 (6.8%), and a phrase for AI-powered, AI-driven, assistant, concierge, agent, trip or itinerary, "agentic" or chatbot appears in 59 (3.7%), though "concierge" also names hotel staff. A safety, security or COVID word appears in 25 (1.6%). A word about trafficking, ECPAT, exploitation, safeguarding, child protection or human rights appears in 2 (0.1%). A phrase about the future of travel, hospitality or tourism, the next generation, Gen Z, millennials or future generations appears in 18 (1.1%).

The word "pod" appears in 1 post (0.1%), and I cannot tell whether it refers to an engagement pod or to a sleeping pod or other lodging. A "DM me" call appears in 4 posts (0.2%), against 1.19% of all 273,771 posts, and a request to comment or follow in 9 (0.6%). These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts words and not claims. A post that criticizes a resort fee is counted with a post that charges one.

### 21.2 The laws that apply

Six items bear on this topic: the Federal Trade Commission's rule on unfair or deceptive fees as it applies to lodging; state attorney general actions on resort fees; European consumer-law action against accommodation platforms; California's law on sellers of travel; the EU Package Travel Directive; and civil liability of hotel operators under the federal law on the trafficking of persons. The rules on reviews are in Chapter 12, on endorsements and material connections in Chapters 14 and 16, on environmental claims in Chapter 18, on offers of something "free" in Chapter 20, and on AI chatbots and AI claims in Chapter 4.

**The total-price rule for lodging.** The FTC's Rule on Unfair or Deceptive Fees, 16 CFR Part 464, took effect on 12 May 2025. The FTC's guidance says that the Rule covers any business that "offers, displays, or advertises" short-term lodging, including third-party platforms, resellers and travel agents, and that covered lodging includes hotel and motel stays, short-term and vacation rentals, home shares listed through platforms and discounted extended stays. It applies to offers, displays and advertisements online, in a mobile app, in physical locations or "through some other means," and the page does not mention social media by name. A business that advertises a price must disclose the total price up front, including all known charges it can calculate in advance, such as mandatory fees, and must display the total price more prominently than other pricing information. The page's example is a resort that adds a mandatory "resort fee" to its nightly rate, and the fee must be included in the total. Fees count as mandatory if they are unavoidable, required regardless of the customer's choices or effectively forced through default billing, pre-checked boxes or opt-out provisions. A business may leave government charges, shipping charges and fees for optional add-ons out of the total, if it discloses them clearly before payment is requested. Descriptions of fees must be truthful, and the page's example is a fee labeled "environmental" that is not used for environmental purposes (VERIFIED against the FTC's guidance, as summarized; I did not open the text of § 464.1 or § 464.2 for this chapter, and the civil penalty that applies to a knowing violation of a rule is in Chapter 3).

**State action on resort fees.** The District of Columbia's Office of the Attorney General sued Marriott International in the Superior Court of the District of Columbia. The complaint alleges "drip pricing," meaning that Marriott advertises a room rate that omits mandatory daily resort fees, which are added later in the booking process, and that Marriott describes the fees ambiguously, sometimes under "Taxes and Fees." It cites the District's Consumer Protection Procedures Act, including the provisions on misrepresentation and on advertising without the intent to sell as advertised, and it seeks an injunction, restitution and civil penalties. It alleges fees of $9 to $95 per day across at least 189 Marriott-affiliated properties, and that Marriott has earned "hundreds of millions of dollars" from them over a decade (VERIFIED against the complaint, as summarized; the text shows no filing date, the web address suggests July 2019, and I did not find the outcome). The ABA Journal reports that the Pennsylvania attorney general announced a settlement with Marriott on 17 November 2021 under which Marriott would "prominently disclose the total price of a hotel stay online, including all mandatory fees," within nine months and sort search results by total price. Marriott did not admit wrongdoing and said that its mandatory fees had long been clearly disclosed (STATED, from the ABA Journal report, which does not state any payment amount).

**Action against accommodation platforms in the EU.** The European Commission's page on a coordinated action by the consumer protection authorities records that Booking.com committed to changes in how it presents "offers, discounts and prices" (a letter and commitments of 18 December 2019), that Expedia Group did the same (18 December 2020), and that Airbnb changed its price presentation after a common position of the authorities (5 July 2018), so that the consumer sees "the total price inclusive of all the applicable mandatory charges and fees." The page does not describe further enforcement and does not mention pressure selling or sustainability claims (VERIFIED against the page, as summarized; I did not open the factsheets).

**California's law on sellers of travel.** The California Attorney General's guidance defines a seller of travel as a person who "sells, provides, furnishes, contracts for, arranges, or advertises" certain transportation, which includes air or sea travel, and land or water vessel travel other than sea carriage where the total charge to the passenger exceeds $300. Air carriers, ocean carriers, certain lodging establishments that arrange transportation but never receive payment for it, government-authorized transportation providers and certain agents of registered sellers are generally exempt. The annual fee is $100 for each business location, and a registered seller with a principal place of business in California that does business with persons in California must participate in the Travel Consumer Restitution Fund. The registration number appears on the seller's certificate, and the page refers readers to another document for how to use it. The page does not describe penalties, and says that disbursing trust funds for purposes other than paying for goods and services, or making refunds, "may constitute a crime" (VERIFIED against the guidance, as summarized; I did not open the statute, Business and Professions Code section 17550 and following, or any other state's law).

**The EU Package Travel Directive.** The European Commission's e-justice page says that a package combines "at least 2 different types of travel service" for the same trip, selected before payment and sold at an inclusive price or advertised as a package, and that a linked travel arrangement involves separate contracts and arises where services are chosen and paid for in one visit to the point of sale or where another service is offered within 24 hours of the first booking. The organiser is "responsible for the performance of the travel services in the contract," must remedy a service that does not conform unless this is impossible or disproportionate, and must give travellers insolvency protection. Price increases are generally capped at 8%, are allowed only on specified grounds and require notice "at least 20 days before the start of the package," and a traveller may terminate with a refund if the organiser makes significant changes or raises the price by more than 8%. A traveller may cancel before departure, with an appropriate fee, but owes none where "unavoidable and extraordinary circumstances" at or near the destination significantly affect the package. Member States had to transpose the Directive by 1 January 2018, and it has applied since 1 July 2018 (VERIFIED against the page, as summarized; the page does not mention any revision). A law-firm article reports that the European Commission proposed an amendment on 29 November 2023, that the Council of the EU adopted it on 30 March 2026, that the Directive "has been amended by Directive (EU) 2026/1024 of 29 April 2026" and that Member States have 28 months to transpose it. The article says that the amendment makes vouchers voluntary for the traveller, requires an accepted voucher to be valid for at least 12 months and covered by insolvency protection, and sets a refund deadline of six months, extendable by nine months in justified cases (STATED; I did not open the amending Directive).

**Hotel liability for the trafficking of minors.** The federal civil remedy for victims of trafficking, 18 U.S.C. § 1595, allows a victim to sue the perpetrator and also anyone who knowingly benefits, financially or by receiving something of value, from participating in a venture that the defendant "knew or should have known" violated the statute. The plaintiff may recover damages and "reasonable attorneys fees," and a suit may be filed up to 10 years after the cause of action arose or, if the victim was a minor when the offense occurred, 10 years after the victim reaches 18 (VERIFIED against the Cornell text, as summarized). On 30 March 2026 the Eleventh Circuit decided *A.G. v. Northbrook Industries, Inc.*, vacating summary judgment for hotel operators. A law-firm article reports that the court reaffirmed that "something more" than an ordinary commercial transaction is required to show participation in a venture, that routine room rentals are not enough alone even with knowledge of trafficking, and that evidence that staff dealt directly with traffickers, admitted minors without verification and ignored warning signs was enough to reach a jury. A news report states that the decision revived three suits by women who say that they were trafficked as minors at two hotels near Atlanta, and that the cases returned to the Northern District of Georgia (STATED, from two secondary reports that agree on the date and the outcome; I did not open the opinion, I make no statement about the facts of the underlying suits, which are allegations, and I name neither the plaintiffs nor the hotels).

### 21.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

The rules on price and fees reach a post that states a price for lodging, and the topic contains very few such posts.

The author is the party on whom the price rules fall. A hotel or an advisor that states a nightly rate in a post is, on the FTC's guidance, a business that "advertises" short-term lodging, and the guidance requires the total price (INFERENCE as applied, since the guidance does not name social media). The per-night price pattern matches 3 posts (0.2%), a dollar figure together with a hotel, resort or suite word matches 31 (1.9%), and no post contains a fee phrase. The data cannot show whether the 31 posts state a rate for a stay or a price for something else, such as a meal, an event or a package, and a post that states no price falls outside the price requirement. The few posts that carry a price are the only ones in this topic where the total-price question would arise, and the data do not show that any omits a mandatory fee.

A travel advisor is the party to whom the California law may apply. The California Attorney General's guidance defines the seller of travel to include a person who "advertises" covered transportation, and travel advisor words appear in 167 posts (10.4%). Some travel advisors may be exempt as agents of registered sellers, and posts addressed to readers outside California, or about trips with no covered transportation, fall outside the law (INFERENCE from the guidance). A word of registration or protection appears in no post, and I treat that as unremarkable: a post is a short statement, and the guidance I read does not say that a post must carry a registration number. Whether a social-media post is an advertisement that requires one is an open question that I found no authority to answer.

Posts about groups and packages reach the Package Travel Directive. A package, all-inclusive, retreat, group trip, guided tour or hosted-trip word appears in 64 posts (4.0%). A person who sells a combination of travel services to travellers in the EU at an inclusive price may be an organiser, with responsibility for the services and a duty of insolvency protection, and a person who only recommends separate services is not (INFERENCE from the page's definitions). The posts do not say who sells what to whom.

Claims of free perks and no cost reach the guide on "free" and, in the EU, the Directive on unfair practices, whose treatment I set out in Chapter 20. A perks word appears in 88 posts (5.5%), and a perks word together with an advisor word in 35 (2.2%). A phrase of no cost appears in 6 (0.4%), a statement that the data cannot test, because whether an advisor charges a fee is outside the posts.

Reviews, sponsorship and awards reach rules set out elsewhere. A review word appears in 15 posts (0.9%), so the rule on reviews in Chapter 12 has little to apply to. A gifting or sponsorship word appears in 35 (2.2%), but the count includes "complimentary," so I cannot say how many mark a disclosed relationship, and the material-connection question of Chapter 16 arises when a hotel gives a stay to a person who then posts about it (INFERENCE). An award or ranking word appears in 59 (3.7%) and a superlative in 78 (4.9%). A statement that a hotel was ranked or voted something is a measurable claim that the substantiation standard of Chapter 3 covers, and the line between such a claim and an opinion is one I did not research for this chapter (INFERENCE).

Sustainability words reach the Directive on empowering consumers for the green transition, which Chapter 18 sets out and which, on the sources I read there, applies from 27 September 2026. Sustainability words appear in 36 posts (2.2%) and in 24 (1.5%) with a hotel or resort word. The FTC's guidance on fees gives the example of a fee labeled "environmental," which links the price rule to the green-claims rules (INFERENCE).

AI claims reach the rules in Chapter 4, and the topic's AI counts need a caution. A hashtag for AI appears in 512 posts (32.0%) and is the only mention in 370 (23.1%). A hashtag attached to a hotel post is not an express statement that the hotel uses AI, and I found no authority on how the rules treat a hashtag alone (INFERENCE). In running text, the AI-system phrases (AI-powered, assistant, concierge, agent, chatbot) appear in 59 posts (3.7%). Where a hotel deploys a chatbot that guests in the EU use, Chapter 4's treatment of the AI Act's transparency duty bears on it.

The liability of hotel operators for trafficking reaches operators and their franchisors and not the posts. Words about trafficking, exploitation, safeguarding or child protection appear in 2 posts (0.1%). The decision concerns what a hotel did, and the topic's posts are about hotels' products and services.

The engaging member, the tool operator and the platform are as in earlier chapters. The topic has 4 "DM me" calls (0.2%) and 9 requests to comment or follow (0.6%), few for a topic whose posts are so heavily tagged. The tool operator is not the advertiser, and the platform's position is the one in Chapter 10.

Table 21.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on luxury travel and hotels, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 21.1.** Rules that reach posts on luxury travel and hotels, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy or penalty stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Rule on Unfair or Deceptive Fees, 16 CFR Part 464 | Businesses that offer, display or advertise short-term lodging, including platforms, resellers and travel agents | None opened under the Rule | Not stated on the page I read (civil penalties: Chapter 3) |
| D.C. Consumer Protection Procedures Act (as alleged) | A hotel company that advertises rates without mandatory resort fees | *District of Columbia v. Marriott International* (complaint); outcome not found | Relief sought: injunction, restitution, civil penalties |
| State settlement (Pennsylvania, 17 November 2021) | Online display of hotel prices | Marriott settlement (STATED) | Total price with mandatory fees within nine months; sorting by total price; no admission |
| EU coordinated action on accommodation booking | Booking.com, Expedia Group, Airbnb | Commitments of December 2019 and December 2020; common position of July 2018 | Changes to the presentation of offers, discounts and prices; total price with mandatory charges |
| California Seller of Travel law | Persons who sell, arrange or advertise covered transportation | None opened | $100 fee per location; restitution fund participation; penalties not described on the page |
| Package Travel Directive (EU) 2015/2302, as amended by (EU) 2026/1024 | Organisers of packages and linked travel arrangements | None opened | Remedy, refund and insolvency protections; 8% cap on price increases |
| 18 U.S.C. § 1595 | Perpetrators and those who knowingly benefit from a venture they knew or should have known violated the law | *A.G. v. Northbrook Industries* (11th Cir., 30 March 2026; STATED) | Damages and reasonable attorneys' fees; suit allowed until 10 years after the victim reaches 18 |

### 21.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

The law in this chapter protects a traveller's money and, in one provision, the safety of children, and I take them in that order.

The price rules protect a person's ability to compare. The District of Columbia's complaint alleges daily fees of $9 to $95 and describes them as added late in the booking process, and the Pennsylvania settlement and the FTC's rule respond by requiring the total price at the start. The figures are allegations, and my sources do not measure how much consumers paid or whether the rules have changed what they pay (INFERENCE). A state attorney general, the EU's consumer authorities and the FTC have each, in the sources I read, required or adopted a display of the total price, which means that the price a person sees first is the price the person pays.

The protections for money paid in advance matter most for people who book far ahead. The Package Travel Directive gives the traveller a remedy against the organiser for a service that the organiser did not supply, a refund on a cancellation caused by extraordinary circumstances and protection if the organiser becomes insolvent, and the 2026 amendment extends that protection to vouchers. California's restitution fund serves the same purpose for the customers of registered sellers. Both mechanisms depend on a traveller knowing which kind of seller the traveller is dealing with, and the topic's posts contain no word of licensing, bonding or protection (INFERENCE; the posts are short, and the landing pages are outside the data). My sources contain no data on who buys luxury travel through advisors or on the age of those buyers.

The child-protection law is a distinct matter. Section 1595 allows a victim who was a minor when the offense occurred to sue until 10 years after turning 18, a period that allows for the years it can take a person to bring a claim (INFERENCE as to the reason). The Eleventh Circuit's decision shows where a court draws the line for hotel operators: a rented room with knowledge of trafficking is not enough alone, and conduct such as dealing directly with traffickers, admitting minors without verification and ignoring warning signs can be. The reports state allegations by women who say they were trafficked as minors, and I do not know how the suits will end. I opened no source on how common trafficking is in hotels, and I make no statement about it. The topic's posts almost never mention the subject (2 posts, 0.1%), and the posts are about hotels and their marketing while the cases I read concern two hotels near Atlanta, so the decision bears on the industry's duties and not on any statement in the data (INFERENCE).

Tourism is also a source of the emissions that coming generations will live with. A study by Lenzen and others in *Nature Climate Change* in 2018, as Carbon Brief reports it, found that tourism accounted for 8% of global greenhouse gas emissions over 2009 to 2013, that its footprint rose from 3.9 to 4.5 billion tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent over those years and that the growth was "largely driven by an increased demand for goods and services" rather than air travel (STATED, from a news report on the study; I did not open the study). Sustainability words appear in 36 posts here (2.2%), and the law on sustainability claims applies from 27 September 2026 on the sources in Chapter 18. The claims rule governs how a hotel may describe its performance and does not cap emissions, so the law protects a traveller's ability to choose on accurate information and leaves the emissions themselves to other law (INFERENCE).

The topic's treatment of the future is thin. A phrase about the future of travel, the next generation, Gen Z or future generations appears in 18 posts (1.1%). The AI count, which looks like the topic's main theme at 33.6%, is mostly a hashtag block, and 68.9% of the posts that mention AI do so only in a hashtag. I draw no conclusion about whether the block was added to widen the audience, and I note that a reader who relied on the count would overstate by a factor of about three the amount of discussion of AI in hotels.

My conclusion for this chapter has three parts. The rules that most fit the topic, those on total prices, apply to few posts (31 with a price and a hotel word). The registration and package rules depend on facts that a post does not show. The evidence of harm in my sources concerns fees and prepayment, which I read as allegations and rules, and the safety of minors in hotels, which I read only through reports of litigation, and the posts discuss neither (INFERENCE).

### 21.5 How this topic bears on mental health

The mental-health evidence that fits hotels concerns survivors of trafficking, and the posts in this topic say almost nothing about it.

The text is about hotels and travel. A hotel, resort or hospitality word appears in 1,058 of 1,600 posts (66.1%), a luxury word in 468 (29.2%), and a wellness, spa or healing word in 94 (5.9%). A word about trafficking, ECPAT, exploitation, safeguarding, child protection or human rights appears in 2 posts. Section 21.2 sets out hotel liability for the trafficking of minors. Oram and colleagues (2012, *PLoS Medicine*) found high symptom levels of depression, anxiety and post-traumatic stress in studies of trafficked people and, in three studies, that distress appeared to decline with time since leaving exploitation. The authors warn that few studies used diagnostic interviews, that nearly all concerned women and girls, and that evidence on children and on people trafficked for labor is largely absent. Section 17.5 gives the figures for forced labor overall. I found no figure for trafficking that involves lodging.

For a traveler, the evidence I opened is slight. The total-price rule and the rules on package travel protect consumers from surprise costs, and I found no study of their mental-health effect. A post that advertises a spa or wellness stay makes a leisure claim, and I found no source on whether such stays change mental health, so I make no claim about it. The 94 posts with wellness words are counted only as words.

### 21.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show that any post in this topic advertises a price without a mandatory fee, operates as an unregistered seller of travel, sells a package, makes a false claim about a perk, an award or sustainability, conceals a gifted stay or involves an exchange of engagement. The counts are of words. The posts do not include the images that carry much of a hotel's marketing, the landing pages behind the 588 links, the booking flows, the advisors' registrations or the identity of the person who wrote a post. Hashtags dominate the topic, and a count of any word that also appears as a hashtag, such as AI, includes it.

My reading of the law has gaps. The fetch tool returned summaries, and I quote only the words it placed in quotation marks. I did not open § 464.1 or § 464.2 of the FTC's rule or any complaint or order under it, the outcome of the District of Columbia case, the Pennsylvania press release, the European Commission's factsheets on the EU action, the California statute or any other state's seller-of-travel law, the text of Directive 2015/2302 or the amending Directive (EU) 2026/1024, the UK's package travel rules, the Department of Transportation's rules for airlines, the study of tourism's emissions, the Eleventh Circuit's opinion, the other provisions of the federal trafficking law, any state law on hotel staff training, or the law on guest data. I did not examine the privacy and data-security law that applies to hotels, though data words appear in 109 posts (6.8%). Several of the sources are law-firm articles, trade reports and news reports. The decision of 30 March 2026 and the 2026 amendment of the Package Travel Directive are recent, and I read them only in those reports.

Two questions remain open after this chapter. The first is whether a travel advisor's social-media post that arranges or promotes covered transportation "advertises" it within California's law and so calls for a display of a registration number. The second is whether a post that states a nightly rate for a hotel with no mechanism for booking is an offer, display or advertisement under the FTC's fee rule, so that the total price must appear in it. I found no authority that answers either.

## Chapter 22. Life, family and personal stories: children's online privacy, child-influencer statutes, likeness rights, health data and the duties of platforms toward minors

### 22.1 The topic in the data

Life, family and personal stories is the nineteenth-largest topic, with 1,314 posts, which is 2.0% of the 64,970 placed posts. The podawaa file supplies 796 of them (60.6%), HyperClapper 267 (20.3%), the 65 exports 237 (18.0%) and LinkBoost-2025 14 (1.1%). The median post is 287 characters and the 90th percentile is 946. A total of 395 posts (30.1%) are 140 characters or shorter, and 112 (8.5%) are 1,000 characters or longer. A link appears in 107 posts (8.1%). A hashtag appears in 547 (41.6%), and five or more hashtags in 221 (16.8%), of which 175 are in the podawaa file.

One word defines the topic. The word "life" appears in 1,270 of the 1,314 posts (96.7%), counting words outside hashtags and links. The topic is therefore the set of posts that talk about life in general, and families are only part of it. The most common other words, counted by posts, are time and work (206 posts each), people (197) and day (172). The most common hashtags are #life (44 posts, 3.3%), #success (29, 2.2%), #worklifebalance (26, 2.0%), #personalgrowth (22, 1.7%), #leadership (20, 1.5%), #happiness (18, 1.4%) and #mindset (15, 1.1%). Four of the 25 most common hashtags are personal or brand names, each in 9 to 14 posts (0.7% to 1.1%), and I do not name them. An advice, lesson, goal, success, mindset, habit or growth word appears in 436 posts (33.2%), a gratitude word in 83 (6.3%), and a storytelling phrase such as "my story," "my journey," "when I was," "growing up" or "I remember" in 101 (7.7%). A word about work-life balance, burnout or hustle appears in 107 (8.1%). First-person words (I, my, me, we, our) appear in 640 posts (48.7%), and 99 posts (7.5%) begin with "I," "I'm," "I've," "My" or "We."

Family words are present in a minority of posts. A word for a relative (family, kids, children, child, son, daughter, mom, mother, dad, father, wife, husband, parent, baby, grandparent, sibling, brother, sister) appears in 142 posts (10.8%), and 91 of them are in the podawaa file. A word for a child or teenager (kids, children, child, toddler, newborn, baby, teen, teenager, minor) appears in 36 posts (2.7%), and a phrase of the form "my son," "my daughter," "my kids" or "my child" in 8 (0.6%). A number followed by "year old," or "aged" and a number, appears in 16 posts (1.2%). Of these, 3 give an age under 13 and 3 an age from 13 to 17 (0.2% each); I did not classify the other 10, which include adult ages. A word about students, youth, teens, Gen Z or the next generation appears in 10 posts (0.8%), a school word in 28 (2.1%), and a word about screens, social media, phones or a named social platform in 15 (1.1%). A photo, picture, selfie or video word appears in 55 posts (4.2%). A phrase about legacy, future generations or grandchildren appears in 10 (0.8%).

Personal hardship appears in small numbers. A word about illness (including cancer), a hospital, a disability, a diagnosis, surgery, therapy, burnout, anxiety, depression, trauma, addiction or pregnancy appears in 64 posts (4.9%). A word about death, a funeral or grief appears in 21 (1.6%), a word about divorce, separation or custody in 7 (0.5%), and a word about debt, bankruptcy, eviction, a layoff or a lost job in 11 (0.8%). An insurance word appears in 17 posts (1.3%), 14 of them in the podawaa file, and the hashtag #lifeinsurance appears in 7. A holiday, birthday, wedding or anniversary word, including the names of holidays, appears in 45 (3.4%). These counts show which words occur and not whose illness, loss or child a post describes.

Promotion and engagement requests are uncommon. A promotional word or phrase (sponsor, paid partnership, affiliate, promo code, link in bio or comments, "my course," "my program," "my book," "my podcast," "my newsletter," "my coaching," sign up, subscribe, "join my" or "join our," buy, purchase) appears in 46 posts (3.5%), and a word about business (business, client, customer, founder, entrepreneur, startup, CEO, brand, marketing, sales, revenue, career, job, employer, boss, manager, team) in 171 (13.0%). A child word, with "my son" and "my daughter" added, appears in 42 posts (3.2%), and 5 of them (0.4%) also contain a promotional word. A relative word appears together with a promotional word in 6 posts and with a narrower set of business words (business, client, customer, founder, entrepreneur, startup, CEO, brand, marketing, sales, revenue) in 11. A "DM me" call appears in 4 posts (0.3%), against 1.19% of all 273,771 posts, and a request to comment or follow in 17 (1.3%). A phrase of reciprocal support (support each other, engage with each other, like and comment, drop a comment, boost) appears in 12 (0.9%), and a giveaway, contest, "tag someone," "share if" or repost phrase in 26 (2.0%), 5 of them (0.4%) with a relative word. The word "pod" appears in none of the 1,314 posts. A relative word appears with five or more hashtags in 29 posts (2.2%). No post contains a word about privacy, data protection, consent, GDPR or COPPA. The capitalized word AI or the phrase "artificial intelligence" appears in 20 posts (1.5%), in the running text of 19; four posts carry an AI hashtag, and in one it is the only mention.

These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts words and not claims. The data contain no images or video, so the photographs and clips that a post may carry are outside the counts.

### 22.2 The laws that apply

Six items bear on this topic: the federal rule on children's online privacy; state statutes on children who appear in monetized online content; state law on the use of a person's likeness in advertising; the GDPR's provisions on children's consent, health data and personal activity; the FTC's Endorsement Guides on advertising addressed to children; and the Digital Services Act's duty on platforms to protect minors. The rules on endorsements and material connections are in Chapters 14 and 16, on health claims in Chapter 19, on AI in Chapter 4, on engagement-pod rules and the platform's own position in Chapter 10, and on offers of something "free" in Chapter 20.

**The children's online privacy rule (COPPA).** The FTC amended its Children's Online Privacy Protection Rule in a final rule published in the Federal Register on 22 April 2025. The Rule applies to operators of websites or online services directed to children under 13 and to general-audience operators that have actual knowledge that they collect personal information from children under 13. The amended Rule took effect on 23 June 2025, and regulated entities have until 22 April 2026 to comply, with three provisions of section 312.11 that apply from the effective date. The summary I read says that the amendments add biometric identifiers (fingerprints, handprints, retina and iris patterns, genetic data, voiceprints, gait patterns and facial templates) and government-issued identifiers to the examples of personal information, add a definition of "mixed audience" sites, allow a mobile phone number to be used only to text a parent for consent, and revise the duties on notices, information security, deletion and retention, methods of parental consent and reports by safe-harbor programs. The FTC deferred its proposals on educational technology and school authorization (VERIFIED against the Federal Register page, as summarized; the summary covered the first 100,000 of 124,761 characters, and I did not open the text of sections 312.2 or 312.5). Enforcement shows what the Rule reaches. In September 2019 the FTC and the New York Attorney General announced a settlement with Google and YouTube of $170 million, of which $136 million went to the FTC and $34 million to New York, over the collection of persistent identifiers from children to serve behavioral advertising. A law-firm article reports that the complaint alleged actual knowledge that some channels were child-directed and treated individual channels as websites or online services, so that companies posting child-directed content there could be treated as operators (STATED, from a law-firm article; I did not open the complaint). Another law-firm article reports that on 2 September 2025 the FTC announced a COPPA settlement with a video publisher, which the article does not name, for labeling child-directed videos "Not Made For Kids," with a $10 million civil penalty, and on 3 September 2025 a settlement with Apitor, a maker of toy robots, over a third-party kit that collected children's location data in its app, with a $500,000 penalty that was suspended because the company could not pay (STATED).

**State statutes on child influencers.** Illinois and Minnesota have laws that give a child a financial claim on content in which the child appears. A law-firm article reports that the Illinois law, which took effect on 1 July 2024, covers minors under 16 who appear in compensated video content. A minor qualifies if, at any time in the previous 12 months, the minor appeared in at least 30% of the creator's compensated content produced in a 30-day period, measured by the share of video time in which the minor appears or is the subject of an oral narrative, and either the video reached the platform's compensation threshold or the creator received at least $0.10 per view. The creator must set aside a share of gross earnings in a trust that the minor can reach at 18 or on emancipation, and the share scales with the minor's presence: at least 50% of gross compensation when the minor appears in all qualifying content, and at least 25% when the minor appears in half of it. The creator must keep records, and a minor may sue the creator, including a parent, for knowing or reckless violations, with actual and punitive damages available (STATED; I did not open the statute). A second law-firm article and a legislative summary report that the Minnesota law (House File 3488, Chapter 103 of 2024) took effect on 1 July 2025, uses the same 30% test, requires a trust until the minor reaches 18, and lets a person who appeared in content as a minor ask for its deletion and sue for damages (CORROBORATED for the effective date, the 30% test, the trust and the deletion right; the right to sue comes only from the legislative summary). The law-firm article and a policy article add that Minnesota bars children under 14 from the work of content creation. The legislative summary words this part in a way that I could not reconcile with the other two, so I rely on them for it. The policy article, published on 25 July 2025, states that California has passed a similar law (Senate Bill 764), that Utah passed House Bill 322 in April 2025 with a trust requirement only for account holders who have earned at least $150,000 from content creation, and that Montana passed a similar law. It gives no effective dates and says that the laws address revenue-generating content and so address financial exploitation (STATED). The article of September 2024 listed bills pending in eight states, among them California, and I did not check what happened to the others.

**Likeness: California Civil Code section 3344.** Section 3344 makes liable "Any person who knowingly uses another's name, voice, signature, photograph, or likeness" for purposes of advertising or selling, or soliciting purchases of, products, merchandise, goods or services without the person's prior consent, and for a minor the consent must come from a parent or guardian. The injured party may recover the greater of $750 or actual damages, profits from the use that are not already counted in the damages, punitive damages and attorney's fees. Uses in connection with a news, public affairs or sports broadcast or account, or a political campaign, do not need consent under subdivision (a) (VERIFIED against the page, as summarized; I opened only California's statute and no case under it, and other states protect likeness by statute or by common law that I did not read).

**The GDPR: children's consent, health data and personal activity.** Article 8 provides that, where consent is the lawful basis for an information society service offered directly to a child, processing is lawful if the child is at least 16, that below 16 it is lawful only if the holder of parental responsibility gives or authorizes consent, that Member States may lower the age but "not below 13 years," and that the controller must make reasonable efforts to verify parental consent "taking into consideration available technology" (VERIFIED against the text, as summarized). Article 9 prohibits processing of special categories of data, which include data concerning health, and Article 9(2)(e) lifts the prohibition when the processing "relates to personal data which are manifestly made public by the data subject" (VERIFIED; the summary truncated the text of Article 9(1)). Article 2(2)(c) excludes processing "by a natural person in the course of a purely personal or household activity," and Recital 18 says that the Regulation does not apply to such processing "with no connection to a professional or commercial activity," that social networking and online activity within that context can be personal or household activity, and that the Regulation applies to "controllers or processors which provide the means" for such processing (VERIFIED against the text, as summarized). The Court of Justice has read the exemption narrowly. In *Lindqvist* (C-101/01, 6 November 2003), under the earlier Directive 95/46/EC, the Court held that naming people on a web page is processing of personal data, that a reference to a person's state of health is data concerning health, and that the exemption for purely personal or household activities did not apply to the pages (VERIFIED against the Court's press release, as summarized; I did not open the judgment). In *Ryneš* (C-212/13, 11 December 2014), the Court held that the exemption covers only processing in a "purely personal or household" setting and that a camera covering part of a public space is directed outward from the home and falls outside it (STATED, from a commentary on the judgment; I did not open it).

**Endorsements directed to children.** Section 255.6 of the Endorsement Guides, titled "Endorsements directed to children," provides in full: "Endorsements in advertisements addressed to children may be of special concern because of the character of the audience. Practices that would not ordinarily be questioned in advertisements addressed to adults might be questioned in such cases." (VERIFIED against the Cornell text.) The section applies to advertisements addressed to children. The material-connection rule in section 255.5, which names a family relationship as a connection that may need disclosure, is set out in Chapter 16.

**The platform's duty toward minors under the Digital Services Act.** The European Commission published guidelines on 14 July 2025 under Article 28(1) of the Digital Services Act. A law-firm summary reports that they apply to online platforms accessible to minors, defined as persons under 18, and exclude small and micro enterprises, and that they call for private accounts by default, accurate and non-intrusive age assurance, adjusted recommender systems and the switching off by default of features such as streaks, autoplay and push notifications. It states that "Self-declaration does not constitute appropriate age assurance" and that the guidelines are "not legally binding," although the Commission will use them to assess compliance with Article 28(1) (STATED; I did not open the Article or the guidelines). An Australian regulator's guide to LinkedIn states a minimum age of "16+ minimum age according to LinkedIn," and says that from 10 December 2025 certain social media platforms cannot let Australian children under 16 create or keep an account, without saying whether LinkedIn is among them (STATED; I could not read LinkedIn's own terms, as Chapter 10 reports).

### 22.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

Most of these rules reach a post only if it names, shows or collects data about a child, and the topic has few such posts.

The children's privacy rule reaches operators and not authors. It applies to an operator that collects personal information from children under 13, either through a service directed to them or with actual knowledge. A parent who writes about a child in a post on a business network provides information about the child and does not collect it from the child, so the Rule's definitions do not seem to reach the parent (INFERENCE; I did not open the definitions). On the Australian guide's account the platform sets a minimum age of 16, and the posts read as addressed to adult readers, so the Rule would reach this topic only through a platform's knowledge that a user is under 13, which the posts do not show (INFERENCE). The topic has 36 child-word posts (2.7%), and only 2 (0.2%) of the 42 posts that contain a child word, "my son" or "my daughter" also contain a word about social media, the internet, apps, phones or data. The data cannot show that any post is directed to a child.

The child-influencer statutes reach an adult who earns money from content in which a child appears. The summaries I read describe the Illinois law in terms of "vloggers" and compensated video content, with a threshold based on the share of video time, so a text post on a business network seems to fall outside the thresholds (INFERENCE from the summary; whether a text or image post counts as content under either statute is an open question that I found no authority to answer). The topic contains 5 posts (0.4%) with a child word and a promotional word and 1 with a child word and a business word. The data count posts, so they cannot show the share of an account's content in which a child appears, and they contain no figures on compensation. I cannot say that any post is covered by these statutes.

The likeness statute reaches an author who uses another person's photograph or name to promote something. A photo, picture, selfie or video word appears in 55 posts (4.2%), and the data do not include the images, so I cannot tell who is shown. A parent who posts a picture of the parent's own child consents for the child, in the statute's terms, and the statute's concern is with use by someone else (INFERENCE). A person who tells a story about a named client, colleague or relative in a post that promotes the person's services is the case where the statute and the rules of privacy and defamation could apply, and I did not research the last two. Phrases about a boss, manager, employer, ex-spouse, coworker or colleague appear in 4 posts (0.3%).

The GDPR reaches posts that contain data about identifiable people to whom the Regulation applies, and its territorial scope is outside this chapter's research. A health word appears in 64 posts (4.9%) and a death or grief word in 21 (1.6%). An author who writes about the author's own illness is covered by the exception for data manifestly made public by the data subject (INFERENCE as applied). An author who writes about another person's illness or death does not have that exception, because that person has not made the data public, though other bases for processing may exist (INFERENCE). The household exemption turns on the absence of any "professional or commercial activity." A post on a professional network, by a person who markets services, may have that connection, and *Lindqvist* held that the exemption did not apply to pages on the internet that named people (INFERENCE as applied). Business words appear in 171 posts (13.0%) and promotional words in 46 (3.5%), but the data do not show whether an author sells anything. Article 8 binds a service that offers itself directly to a child on the basis of consent, which means the platform and not the author.

Family praise and the endorsement rules meet in a narrow place. A relative word appears with a promotional word in 6 posts, and a post that praises a relative's business without saying so is the situation that section 255.5 describes, with the family relationship as the connection (INFERENCE as applied; Chapter 16 sets out the standard). Section 255.6 applies to advertisements addressed to children, and the posts in this topic address adult readers. The teens, students, youth and next-generation words that could indicate a younger audience appear in 10 posts (0.8%).

Engagement devices reach rules that I set out elsewhere. A giveaway, contest, tag or share request appears in 26 posts (2.0%), and the law of sweepstakes and contests is outside this chapter's research. A request to comment or follow appears in 17 (1.3%), a phrase of reciprocal support in 12 (0.9%) and a "DM me" call in 4 (0.3%), the last against 1.19% of all posts. The data cannot show that any engagement with a personal story was exchanged or prompted.

The tool operator, the engaging member and the platform are as in earlier chapters, with one addition. The Digital Services Act's duty on platforms accessible to minors falls on the platform, and Chapter 10 reports LinkedIn's designation as a very large online platform. The guidelines say that self-declaration of age is not appropriate age assurance, which suggests that a stated minimum age of 16 does not by itself meet them (INFERENCE; the guidelines are not binding, and I did not open them).

Table 22.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on life, family and personal stories, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 22.1.** Rules that reach posts on life, family and personal stories, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy or penalty stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| COPPA Rule, 16 CFR Part 312, as amended (effective 23 June 2025; compliance by 22 April 2026) | Operators of services directed to children under 13, and general-audience operators with actual knowledge | FTC and New York Attorney General v. Google and YouTube (September 2019); a video publisher (2 September 2025); Apitor (3 September 2025) (all STATED) | $170 million ($136 million to the FTC, $34 million to New York); $10 million civil penalty; $500,000 penalty, suspended |
| Illinois child-influencer statute (effective 1 July 2024) | Adults who feature a minor under 16 in at least 30% of compensated video content in a 30-day period | None opened | Trust set-aside (at least 50% of gross at full presence, 25% at half); suit by the minor; actual and punitive damages (STATED) |
| Minnesota House File 3488, Chapter 103 (effective 1 July 2025) | Creators who feature minors in compensated content; bar on content-creation work by children under 14 | None opened | Trust until 18; deletion on request; suit for damages (CORROBORATED) |
| California Civil Code section 3344 | Use of a person's name, photograph or likeness in advertising or selling without consent, with a parent's consent for a minor | None opened | Greater of $750 or actual damages; profits; punitive damages; attorney's fees |
| GDPR Articles 8 and 9, Article 2(2)(c) and Recital 18 | Services offered to children on consent; processing of health data; personal activity with no professional or commercial connection | *Lindqvist* (6 November 2003); *Ryneš* (11 December 2014) | Not stated in the sources I read |
| 16 CFR 255.6 | Endorsements in advertisements addressed to children | None opened | Not stated in the section; the route to enforcement is in Chapter 3 |
| Digital Services Act Article 28(1) and the Commission's guidelines of 14 July 2025 | Online platforms accessible to minors, other than small and micro enterprises | None opened | Guidelines "not legally binding"; used to assess compliance (STATED) |

### 22.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

The law in this chapter protects two groups of children: those who use platforms, and those who appear in other people's posts without having chosen to. For the second group, the UN Committee on the Rights of the Child, in its General Comment No. 25 on children's rights in the digital environment (distributed on 2 March 2021), states that "Privacy is vital to children's agency, dignity and safety and for the exercise of their rights," that threats to it can come from family, peers or strangers, "for example, by parents sharing photographs online or a stranger sharing information about a child," and that "The digital environment presents particular problems for parents and caregivers in respecting children's right to privacy" (VERIFIED against the text, as summarized). A general comment is a treaty body's interpretation and does not by itself impose a duty on a parent, and I did not research its standing in United States law (INFERENCE as to its effect).

Parents' own views point the same way, in a dated source. A Michigan Public report on a University of Michigan poll, dated March 2015 in its web address, says that 68% worried about their child's privacy, 67% worried that someone would re-share the child's photographs and 52% worried that the child would be embarrassed when older, and that 74% knew a parent who shares too much (STATED; the text I read does not give the base for these percentages, the sample size or the dates of the fieldwork, and the poll is more than ten years old).

The statutes respond to one part of the problem. Illinois and Minnesota give a child who appears in compensated content a claim on the money, and Minnesota also lets an adult who appeared as a child ask for the content to be deleted. The policy article that I read says that the laws address revenue-generating content and so only financial exploitation, and it notes that the deletion right could remove material that law enforcement needs as evidence (STATED). The privacy interest of a child whose parent posts without earning anything is outside the thresholds that the summaries describe (INFERENCE). In my data the question arises seldom: 36 posts use a child word and 8 use the form "my son," "my daughter" or "my child," and I cannot tell what, if anything, they reveal about a child.

Health and loss are subjects on which a personal story can reveal information about other people. *Lindqvist* treated a reference to a person's health as data concerning health, and the GDPR lets a person publish their own health data and gives no such permission for someone else's (INFERENCE). Posts with a health word number 64 (4.9%) and posts with a death or grief word 21 (1.6%). The posts may describe the author's own experience, and I cannot tell. A story about a parent's illness or a child's diagnosis, once posted, is available to anyone who can see the post, including employers and future acquaintances of the person it describes, and I opened no source on how often that causes harm (INFERENCE as to the risk). Amplification by an engagement pod widens the audience for a post, and the data cannot show whether any post in this topic was boosted for that purpose.

Teenagers are heavy users of social media. The Surgeon General's advisory page, which I also used in Chapter 10, states that up to 95% of young people aged 13 to 17 report using social media, that nearly two thirds do so daily, that about a third use it "almost constantly," and it says "We cannot conclude that social media is sufficiently safe for children and adolescents" (VERIFIED against the page). The sources I read in this chapter place the age-related duties on platforms: the amended COPPA Rule, Article 28 of the Digital Services Act and its guidelines, and the Australian rule from 10 December 2025. They place the money-related duties on adults who feature children: the Illinois and Minnesota statutes. I found no source that places a duty on a person who boosts a post that shows a child (INFERENCE from the absence of such a source in my searches).

The topic's treatment of the future is thin. A phrase about legacy, future generations or grandchildren appears in 10 posts (0.8%), and a word about teens, students, youth or the next generation in 10 (0.8%); the two sets overlap by an amount I did not count. The topic's main content is adults writing about work, balance, gratitude and lessons, and the people most directly affected by the law are those adults and the relatives they mention. The AI count is small (20 posts, 1.5%), and the AI hashtag is the only mention in one.

My conclusion for this chapter has three parts. The privacy rules for children bind platforms and operators and reach this topic through who uses the platform, which the posts do not show. The statutes on child influencers depend on compensation and on how much of a creator's content features a child, and the data contain neither. The rules on likeness and health data apply to what a post says about other people, and the data contain a count of words and not the identity of the people described (INFERENCE).

### 22.5 How this topic bears on mental health

Personal stories are the topic whose posts most often name mental-health subjects, and the sources I opened are mixed on whether sharing them helps.

A word about illness, a hospital, a disability, a diagnosis, surgery, therapy, burnout, anxiety, depression, trauma, addiction or pregnancy appears in 64 of 1,314 posts (4.9%), a word about death, a funeral or grief in 21 (1.6%), and a word about debt, bankruptcy, eviction, a layoff or a lost job in 11 (0.8%). These counts show which words occur and not whose illness or loss a post describes. Naslund and colleagues (2016) describe benefits of sharing illness experience among peers, such as feeling less alone and challenging stigma, and risks, such as unverified advice, dependency and harmful content. They also say that most studies use surveys or content analysis and that few collect objective health outcomes. Public posts are not the peer groups that the article discusses, so I use it as an analogy only (INFERENCE).

For children, the American Psychological Association's 2025 advisory says that platforms should restrict the use of minors' images and voices and limit data uses such as targeted advertising, and its 2023 advisory says that effects on adolescents depend on their own characteristics. I found no study of the mental-health effects on children who appear in other people's posts or on child influencers, and I opened no evidence of effect for the child-influencer statutes in Section 22.2. The data show 36 posts (2.7%) with a word for a child or teenager and 8 (0.6%) with "my son," "my daughter," "my kids" or "my child."

### 22.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show that any post in this topic identifies a child, uses a photograph of a person without consent, discloses another person's health, is compensated content in which a child appears, is directed to children, or involves an exchange of engagement. The counts are of words, and the topic is defined by the word "life," so most posts in it have no family content. The data contain no images, no video, no account information and no age of any user.

My reading of the law has gaps. The fetch tool returned summaries, and I quote only the words it placed in quotation marks. I did not open the text of 16 CFR 312.2 or 312.5, the COPPA statute, the last part of the Federal Register notice, the Illinois or Minnesota statutes, the California, Utah and Montana child-influencer laws, any case under section 3344 or any other state's law on likeness or on privacy torts, the judgments in *Lindqvist* and *Ryneš* (I read a press release and a commentary), the GDPR's recitals on children, guidance from the European Data Protection Board, Article 28 of the Digital Services Act or the Commission's guidelines (I read a law firm's summary), state laws on age-appropriate design and on minors' use of social media, the litigation over those laws, the Kids Online Safety Act, the United Kingdom's Children's Code, the law on contests and giveaways, or the platform's terms. Several of the enforcement examples come from law-firm articles. The COPPA amendments and the Digital Services Act guidelines are recent, and I did not check the status of the state bills listed in 2024.

Two questions remain open after this chapter. The first is whether a text post by a parent about the parent's child, on a business network, is "content" within the child-influencer statutes, which the summaries I read describe in terms of video. The second is whether the household exemption in Article 2(2)(c) of the GDPR can cover a post on a professional network by a person who also markets a service, given Recital 18's reference to a "professional or commercial activity." I found no authority that answers either.

## Chapter 23. Crypto and blockchain: the securities tests, touting, commodity-fraud rules, consumer-protection data, stablecoin and market-structure legislation, and EU marketing rules

### 23.1 The topic in the data

Crypto and blockchain is the twentieth-largest topic and the smallest of the twenty, with 1,180 posts, which is 1.8% of the 64,970 placed posts. The podawaa file supplies 1,091 of them (92.5%), HyperClapper 45 (3.8%), the 65 exports 42 (3.6%) and LinkBoost-2025 2 (0.2%). The median post is 408 characters and the 90th percentile is 1,134. A link appears in 349 posts (29.6%), and 335 of them are in the podawaa file.

Hashtags make up much of the topic. Five or more hashtags appear in 669 posts (56.7%), 658 of them in the podawaa file. The median post has 5 hashtags, the 90th percentile 16 and the maximum 75; 286 posts (24.2%) have 10 or more and 159 (13.5%) have 15 or more. Once hashtags and links are removed, the median post has 48 words and the 90th percentile 164. A total of 34 posts (2.9%) have no other text, 102 (8.6%) have 10 words or fewer and 222 (18.8%) have 20 or fewer. A crypto word appears in the running text of 963 posts (81.6%), and 217 (18.4%) mention the subject only in a hashtag or a link, so whether a post counts as being about crypto depends on whether hashtags are counted. The most common hashtags, as a share of the 1,180 posts, are #blockchain (515 posts, 43.6%), #crypto (415, 35.2%), #web3 (259, 21.9%), #bitcoin (236, 20.0%), #cryptocurrency (219, 18.6%), #nft (212, 18.0%), #metaverse (159, 13.5%), #defi (145, 12.3%), #btc (141, 11.9%) and #ethereum (123, 10.4%). Counting #nft and #nfts together gives 280 posts (23.7%), all in the podawaa file. One project-style tag appears in 32 posts (2.7%), and I do not name it.

The running text is mostly about investing, trading, learning and events. A word about investing or returns appears in 215 posts (18.2%) and a word about trading, buying, selling or price in 190 (16.1%). An explainer or education word appears in 137 (11.6%), an event, conference or summit word in 115 (9.7%), a word about the future, decentralization or financial freedom in 108 (9.2%), and a word about institutions or ETFs in 60 (5.1%). A word about stablecoins, central bank digital currencies, tokenization or real-world assets appears in 59 (5.0%); the word "stablecoin" alone in the running text of 16 (1.4%). A word about enterprise uses such as supply chains, identity or smart contracts appears in 48 (4.1%), a word about mining, staking or yield farming in 38 (3.2%), and a word about hiring in 13 (1.1%). The word NFT appears in the running text of 210 posts (17.8%), and a word about selling an NFT in 51 (4.3%). The names of exchanges and platforms appear in 124 posts (10.5%), and I do not name them.

Words that bear on an offer of tokens are present in a minority of posts. A token-sale word (ICO, IEO, IDO, token sale, presale, airdrop, whitelist, launchpad, tokenomics, white paper, roadmap) appears in the running text of 33 posts (2.8%), and in 98 (8.3%) when hashtags and links are counted. A hashtag for an ICO, IEO, IDO, token sale, presale or airdrop appears in 67 (5.7%), all in the podawaa file. A cashtag appears in 57 (4.8%), also all in the podawaa file. A dollar figure appears in 140 (11.9%) and a percentage figure in 84 (7.1%). A claim of the form "N% APY," "N% return" or "N% ROI" appears in 2 posts (0.2%), a multiplier such as "10x" or "double your" in 12 (1.0%), and a word for a guarantee or "risk-free" in 9 (0.8%). A word of urgency, early access or presale appears in 43 (3.6%), an "invest now" phrase in 9 (0.8%), and an audit, team or backing claim in 33 (2.8%). A claim about price or market data appears in 61 (5.2%), a claim about legal status (not a security, utility token, compliant, regulated, licensed, KYC) in 13 (1.1%), and a word about referrals, airdrops or free tokens in 14 (1.2%). A word for a meme coin appears in 21 posts (1.8%), counting hashtags.

Disclosure and legal words are less common still. A word about risk, or a phrase such as "not financial advice," NFA or DYOR, appears in 40 posts (3.4%), and the last three alone in 3 (0.3%) of the running text. A word that discloses a position or a payment (sponsored, paid, #ad, "I hold," referral link) appears in 2 (0.2%). A word for a regulator or the law appears in 93 (7.9%): a form of "regulate" in 54 (4.6%), the capitalized word SEC in 17 (1.4%), CFTC, FINRA or MiCA in 7 (0.6%), "securities" or "security token" in 13 (1.1%), and law, legal, lawsuit or court in 28 (2.4%). The word "MiCA" appears in 8 posts (0.7%) when hashtags and links are counted. A word for a bank appears in 52 (4.4%), and a central-bank or CBDC word in 5 (0.4%). A scam, fraud, Ponzi or rug-pull word appears in 16 posts (1.4%). A word about getting rich or financial freedom appears in 16 (1.4%), and a word about students, youth or Gen Z in 12 (1.0%).

Engagement requests are modest. A request to comment, like or share appears in 80 posts (6.8%) and a request to comment or follow in 17 (1.4%). A phrase of reciprocal support (support each other, engage with each other, like and comment, drop a comment, boost) appears in 3 (0.3%). A "DM me" call appears in 2 posts (0.2%), against 1.19% of all 273,771 posts. The word "pod" appears in none of the 1,180 posts. A mention of AI, as a word or a hashtag, appears in 92 posts (7.8%): in the running text of 56 (4.7%) and in a hashtag of 67 (5.7%), and in 36 (3.1%) the hashtag is the only mention.

The corpus file I used for the counts holds no dates. The topic is 92.5% podawaa, which I cannot date in that file, so I cannot place the counts before or after the laws in this chapter, most of which changed in 2025 and 2026. The 65 exports supply 42 of the posts (3.6%), and every export record carries a creation date, but I did not date those 42, and Section 2.10 describes what the export dates can and cannot show. These counts are VERIFIED against the data, and each counts words and not claims. The data contain no images, so the charts, logos and videos that a crypto post may carry are outside the counts.

### 23.2 The laws that apply

Seven items bear on this topic: the investment-contract test and the 2026 classification of crypto assets; the SEC's proposed rules for offerings; the securities law on touting; the CFTC's rule on fraud in commodity markets; the stablecoin and market-structure legislation; the FTC's consumer-protection data; and the European Union's rules on marketing crypto-assets. The securities law on offers (sections 5, 12(a)(2) and 17(a) of the Securities Act, and Rule 506) is in Chapter 11, Rule 10b-5, section 17(b) and the *Kardashian* case in Chapter 13, the FTC's data on social-media scams in Chapter 8, and the platform's status under the Digital Services Act in Chapter 10.

**The investment-contract test and the 2026 classification.** *SEC v. W.J. Howey Co.*, decided on 27 May 1946, says that "an investment contract for purposes of the Securities Act means a contract, transaction or scheme" (VERIFIED against the Cornell page for the opening words; the rest of the definition, an investment of money in a common enterprise with profits expected from the efforts of others, is the page's summary). The SEC's statement of 1 November 2017, issued by its Enforcement Division and its examinations office, says that "virtual tokens or coins sold in ICOs may be securities," citing the SEC's DAO Report, which I did not open. On 17 March 2026 the SEC and the CFTC jointly issued an interpretation of how the federal securities laws apply to crypto assets (SEC Press Release 2026-30). It sorts crypto assets into five categories: digital commodities, digital collectibles, digital tools, stablecoins and digital securities. It addresses how a crypto asset that is not a security may become subject to an investment contract and may cease to be, and it addresses airdrops, protocol mining, protocol staking and wrapping. The release quotes Paul Atkins as saying that "investment contracts can come to an end" and that "most crypto assets are not themselves securities," and it calls the interpretation a bridge while Congress works on legislation (VERIFIED against the release). A law firm's summary says that the interpretation replaces the SEC staff's 2019 framework, that digital commodities, collectibles and tools are not securities, and that certain airdrops may not involve an "investment of money" (STATED). Neither source says anything on meme coins, NFTs or promoters.

**The proposed Regulation Crypto Assets.** Two law-firm summaries report that the SEC proposed on 18 August 2026 a set of rules for offerings of crypto assets. There would be a startup exemption for up to $5 million over four years (Form NOR) and a two-tier exemption, $20 million for Tier 1 and $75 million for Tier 2 in 12 months (Form 1-CRYPTO), with a cap of 30% on secondary sales in the first offering. Issuers would disclose on ten crypto-specific topics. A proposed Rule 400 would give a safe harbor (Form TR) for showing that an investment contract has ended. Issuers would remain subject to the antifraud and antimanipulation provisions, "testing the waters" communications would be permitted, and proposed Rule 103 would require offering disclosures to be consistent with promotional materials. The proposal relies on section 18(b)(3) of the Securities Act for the preemption of state law (STATED). Comments were due on 20 October 2026, the day before I wrote this chapter, and I did not check whether the date was extended or what was filed. The summaries do not state the Commission's vote and do not discuss promoters or social media.

**Touting.** The 2017 statement says that promoters "must disclose the nature, scope, and amount of compensation received in exchange for the promotion," refers to the "anti-touting provisions," and says that promoters "may also be liable" under the antifraud provisions (VERIFIED against the statement, as summarized). Chapter 13 reports the *Kardashian* case, in which the SEC's order of 2022 (Press Release 2022-183) concerned EMAX tokens, a $250,000 payment and a promotion that was not disclosed as paid, and ended in $1.26 million in total payments and a three-year ban on promoting crypto asset securities.
