**The CFTC's rule on fraud.** 17 CFR 180.1 applies "in connection with any swap, or contract of sale of any commodity in interstate commerce," and reaches a person who acts "intentionally or recklessly." Paragraph (a)(1) forbids using or attempting to use "any manipulative device, scheme, or artifice to defraud." Paragraph (a)(2) forbids untrue or misleading statements of material fact and omissions that make statements misleading, and paragraph (a)(3) forbids any "act, practice, or course of business" that "operates or would operate as a fraud or deceit upon any person." Paragraph (b) says that there is no duty to volunteer nonpublic information but that this is no excuse for a materially misleading statement (VERIFIED against the Cornell text). In *CFTC v. McDonnell* (E.D.N.Y., 6 March 2018) Senior Judge Weinstein granted a preliminary injunction and wrote that "A 'commodity' encompasses virtual currency both in economic function and in the language of the statute" and that the "CFTC has standing to exercise its enforcement power over fraud related to virtual currencies sold in interstate commerce." The CFTC alleged that paid "memberships" were sold with a promise of returns of up to 300% a week, after which the sellers stopped communicating (VERIFIED against the opinion, as summarized).

**The legislation.** The GENIUS Act (Public Law 119-27, signed on 18 July 2025) says, in the Congressional Research Service's overview, that payment stablecoins are not securities or commodities. It requires reserves of 1 to 1, limits issuers to banks, credit unions and certain nonbanks, bars names that imply issue or a guarantee by the United States government, and says that stablecoins are not federally insured (VERIFIED against the overview; the excerpt gave no effective date). The CLARITY Act (H.R. 3633) passed the House on 17 July 2025 by 294 to 134 (STATED, Orrick) and cleared the Senate Banking Committee in May 2026 (STATED, DLA Piper). A vote on cloture on the motion to proceed failed on 15 September 2026; Orrick reports 49 to 50 against the 60 needed, and DLA Piper gives no tally. The disputes were about ethics provisions on officials' crypto holdings, stablecoin yield and the preemption of state law, to which 18 state attorneys general objected, and passage in this Congress is described as unlikely. The CFTC sent a prerule to the Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs on 17 September 2026 and updated its staff FAQs on 24 September. One of the articles adds that state law on licensing, securities registration and consumer protection still applies (STATED).

**Consumer-protection data.** The FTC's data spotlight of June 2022 reports that from January 2021 through March 2022 more than 46,000 people reported losing over $1 billion in crypto to scams, with a median loss of $2,600. Of those who reported, 49% said the scam began on social media. Bitcoin was the payment in 70%, Tether in 10% and Ether in 9%. Investment scams accounted for $575 million and romance scams for $185 million, with a median romance loss of $10,000 (VERIFIED against the PDF, as summarized). A news report of 28 April 2026 says that the FTC reports at least $2.1 billion lost in 2025 to scams that began on social media and $1.1 billion to investment scams that began there (STATED, as in Chapter 8). These are reports of losses and not rules, and I did not open an FTC rule specific to crypto.

**The European Union's rules (MiCA).** I read two secondary sources, a law firm's article and a page from an influencer-marketing firm, and not the Regulation, (EU) 2023/1114. The sources say that an offeror's marketing must be fair, clear and not misleading and consistent with the white paper. The marketing firm's page adds that the Regulation applies from 30 December 2024 to ordinary crypto-assets and that the transitional period for service providers ended on 1 July 2026. It says that marketing must be identifiable as marketing, may not appear before the white paper is published, and must state that the white paper has not been reviewed or approved by any competent authority, and that the duty rests on the issuer or offeror, who stays liable for promoters. The sources add that marketing communications go to the national authority on request, that a draft white paper goes in at least 20 days before the offer, and that the European Securities and Markets Authority reads "solicitation" broadly enough to include social media advertisements (STATED; the law firm cites Article 6 for the white paper and the marketing page Article 7 for marketing).

### 23.3 How the rules reach pod-boosted posts in this topic

Most of these rules turn on an offer, a sale or a paid promotion of a crypto asset, and the data show words about such things and not the things themselves.

Offers of tokens are the first case. A token-sale word appears in the running text of 33 posts (2.8%), a hashtag for a token sale in 67 (5.7%) and a word of urgency or early access in 43 (3.6%). If a token is offered as an investment contract, the registration and antifraud rules of Chapter 11 apply to the offer, and whether it is one depends on facts that a post may not contain (INFERENCE). A claim of legal status appears in 13 posts (1.1%), and the data cannot show whether any is accurate. Under proposed Rule 103, an issuer's posts could count as promotional materials that must be consistent with the offering disclosure, though the summaries I read do not say that social media posts are covered (INFERENCE; not addressed in the sources).

Promoters are the second. A word that discloses a position or a payment appears in 2 posts (0.2%). The 2017 statement requires a promoter of a security to disclose the nature, scope and amount of compensation, and the *Kardashian* case shows enforcement where a payment was not disclosed. The data show nothing about whether any author was paid. The sources I read on the 2026 interpretation say nothing on promoters, so I cannot say how section 17(b), which applies to the promotion of a security, treats the promoter of a "digital collectible" or a "digital commodity" (open question).

Return claims, guarantees and risk words are the third. A claim of a percentage return appears in 2 posts (0.2%), a multiplier in 12 (1.0%) and a guarantee or "risk-free" word in 9 (0.8%). Rule 180.1(a)(2) reaches an untrue or misleading statement of material fact if it is made intentionally or recklessly and "in connection with" a commodity sale, and *McDonnell* involved a promised return of up to 300% a week. The counts show words, and "10x" may describe a past price or a hope, so I cannot say that any post states a promise (INFERENCE). A risk word or "not financial advice" phrase appears in 40 posts (3.4%), and the phrase alone in 3 (0.3%). Paragraph (b) says that the absence of a duty to volunteer nonpublic information is no excuse for a materially misleading statement. By analogy, a missing disclaimer is not by itself the wrong, and a disclaimer would not excuse a misleading statement (INFERENCE, by analogy; paragraph (b) speaks of nonpublic information).

NFTs and stablecoins are the fourth. The NFT hashtag appears in 280 posts (23.7%) and a word about selling an NFT in 51 (4.3%). The 2026 taxonomy has a category of digital collectibles, which a law firm says are not securities, but the sources do not say how it treats a collection sold with promises about its price, and I did not open any NFT enforcement order (INFERENCE; open question). A stablecoin, CBDC, tokenization or real-world-asset word appears in 59 posts (5.0%). The GENIUS Act's bar on names that imply a government issue or guarantee reaches issuers, and the data show no issuer.

The regulation words are the fifth. A form of "regulate" appears in 54 posts (4.6%), and CFTC, FINRA or MiCA in 7 (0.6%). The failure of the CLARITY Act means that, on the sources I read, the rules for a post written now are existing law and the 2026 interpretation, with the proposal still pending (INFERENCE). A European offer would bring MiCA's marketing rules, which on the sources I read rest on the offeror, who stays liable for promoters; whether they reach a third party who writes about the offer is not stated (STATED; open question).

Engagement devices are the sixth. A request to comment, like or share appears in 80 posts (6.8%), a request to comment or follow in 17 (1.4%), a phrase of reciprocal support in 3 (0.3%) and a "DM me" call in 2 (0.2%), against 1.19% of all posts. Chapter 13 left open whether engagement given in return for engagement is consideration under section 17(b), and I found no source in this chapter that answers it. The same gap exists for rule 180.1: a pod-boosted post could be one step in a scheme under paragraph (a)(1) or (a)(3), but I found no case that treats reciprocal engagement that way (INFERENCE).

The four parties are as in earlier chapters. The author may be an issuer, a promoter or neither. The tool operator is reached by none of the sources in this chapter, which concern issuers and promoters. The engaging member, who comments on a post about a token, is not described as a promoter in any source that I read. The platform's duties are in Chapter 10, and under MiCA the duty rests on the offeror and not on the platform (STATED).

Table 23.1 sets out the rules that reach posts on crypto and blockchain, the parties they reach, an enforcement example and the remedy that the source states.

**Table 23.1.** Rules that reach posts on crypto and blockchain, with the parties reached, an enforcement example and the remedy stated in the source

| Rule | Reaches | Enforcement example | Remedy or penalty stated in the source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Securities Act and the *Howey* test (Chapter 11) | An offer or sale of a token that is an investment contract | *Howey* (27 May 1946) | Not stated in the sources I read for this chapter |
| Section 17(b) and the SEC statement of 1 November 2017 | Promoters of securities who do not disclose compensation | *Kardashian*, EMAX tokens (SEC Press Release 2022-183, Chapter 13) | $250,000 payment; $1.26 million in total; three-year ban on promoting crypto asset securities |
| 17 CFR 180.1 | Fraud and misleading statements in connection with a swap or a commodity sale in interstate commerce, made intentionally or recklessly | *CFTC v. McDonnell* (6 March 2018) | Preliminary injunction (as summarized) |
| SEC and CFTC interpretation of 17 March 2026 | Classification of crypto assets in five categories and when an investment contract starts and ends | None | An interpretation, with no penalty stated |
| Proposed Regulation Crypto Assets (18 August 2026) | Offerings by issuers under the exemptions; offering disclosure consistent with promotional materials | None | Antifraud and antimanipulation provisions continue to apply (STATED) |
| GENIUS Act, Public Law 119-27 (18 July 2025) | Issuers of payment stablecoins | None opened | Not stated in the overview I read |
| CLARITY Act, H.R. 3633 | Market structure for digital assets | None; cloture failed on 15 September 2026 | Not law |
| MiCA marketing rules, as described in two secondary sources | Offerors of ordinary crypto-assets in the EU, who remain liable for promoters | None opened | Not stated in the sources I read |

### 23.4 What this means for people and for coming generations

The laws in this chapter protect people who buy crypto assets, and the evidence that I opened about those people is thin and dated. The Pew Research Center reports from a survey of 10,133 adults on 7 to 11 February 2024 that 17% of Americans had ever invested in, traded or used cryptocurrency, the same share as in 2021. Among men aged 18 to 29 the share was 42%, against 17% of women, and among men aged 30 to 49 it was 36%, against 15% of women. A total of 63% of adults had little or no confidence in the safety and reliability of cryptocurrency, including 71% of those aged 50 and over and 55% of younger adults, and 5% were extremely or very confident (VERIFIED against the 2024 page; the 2025 version of the page did not load).

The harm shows up in reports of fraud. The FTC's 2022 spotlight says that people aged 20 to 49 reported crypto-scam losses at more than three times the rate of older groups, and that people in their 30s were hardest hit, with 35% of their fraud losses in crypto (VERIFIED against the PDF, as summarized). The age bands in the Pew and FTC figures overlap, but one source measures holding and the other reported losses, and neither shows that the people who held crypto were the people who lost money (INFERENCE as to the overlap). The reported losses are a floor and not a measure of all losses (INFERENCE). That 49% of reporters said the scam began on social media does not say what share began on a professional network, and the 2025 figure that I read for social media as a whole is not broken down by platform.

Chapter 13 reports the FINRA Foundation's data on Gen Z, which I do not repeat. In my data, a word about students, youth or Gen Z appears in 12 posts (1.0%), a word about getting rich or financial freedom in 16 (1.4%), and a word about investing or returns in 215 (18.2%). The topic is not mostly about offers, though. Posts with an explainer or education word number 137 (11.6%) and posts with an event word 115 (9.7%), and 18.4% of the posts mention crypto only in a hashtag or link. The people most exposed to the law here are the authors who sell tokens or promote them for pay, and I cannot tell from the counts which authors those are.

The law governing the future is unsettled. In the 15 months before this chapter, a payment-stablecoin statute was signed (July 2025), the SEC and the CFTC issued a joint taxonomy (March 2026), the SEC proposed offering rules (August 2026), and the Senate failed to advance market-structure legislation (September 2026). A person who writes a post about a token today does so under rules that are changing, and the one rule on promoters that my sources identify, section 17(b), is older than all of them. If the proposed rules are adopted, offering disclosures would have to be consistent with promotional materials (proposed Rule 103), and the effect on posts by people other than the issuer is not addressed in the summaries I read. The posts in the data are undated, so they may have been written under any of these regimes.

My conclusion for this chapter has three parts. The rules on offers, touting and fraud turn on who is selling and who is paid, and the data contain counts of words and not any of that. The classification rules were rewritten in 2025 and 2026, and I could open only summaries of most of them. The consumer data say that people aged 20 to 49 reported crypto losses at higher rates and that social media was a common starting point, and they say nothing about the posts in this topic.

### 23.5 How this topic bears on mental health

Crypto is the topic with the clearest measured financial harm among the sources I opened, and the mental-health evidence about it is indirect.

A word about investing or returns appears in 215 of 1,180 posts (18.2%), a word about trading, buying, selling or price in 190 (16.1%), and a scam, fraud, Ponzi or rug-pull word in 16 (1.4%). The FBI's 2025 Internet Crime Report, released on 6 April 2026, reports 181,565 cryptocurrency-related complaints with losses of "more than $11 billion," and about $7.7 billion in losses reported by people over 60, up 37% from 2024. The FTC reported on 27 April 2026 that investment scams were $1.1 billion of the $2.1 billion lost in 2025 to scams that began on social media.

The evidence on mental health comes from other fields. Mills and Nower (2019) found more symptoms of depression and anxiety among regular gamblers who traded both cryptocurrency and high-risk stocks (STATED; Section 13.5). The debt evidence in Section 9.5 links unsecured debt to mental-health problems without establishing direction. Button and colleagues (Section 3.8) found that the effects of fraud on victims range from little to devastating. None of these studies concerns people who lost crypto to a deceptive promotion, and I found none. The securities, commodities and consumer-protection rules in Section 23.2 apply to the promotion. I found no study of whether enforcement lessens the harm to victims (INFERENCE as to the route).

### 23.6 What the data cannot show

The data cannot show that any post in this topic offers a token, is paid for, promises a return, misstates a legal status, omits a risk, comes from an issuer or involves an exchange of engagement. The counts are of words, the topic has few such words, and 18.4% of its posts mention crypto only in a hashtag or link. The data contain no images, no account information, no dates and no price or sales data. The AI counts include hashtags, and in 36 posts the hashtag is the only mention.

My reading of the law has gaps. The fetch tool returned summaries, and I quote only the words it placed in quotation marks. I did not open the text of *Howey* beyond its opening phrase, the 17 March 2026 interpretation (I read the SEC's press release and one law firm's summary), the text of Regulation Crypto Assets (I read two law-firm summaries, which do not state the vote), the CLARITY or GENIUS texts (I read an overview and law-firm articles), MiCA (I read two secondary sources and not Articles 6 or 7), the SEC's DAO Report, the SEC's *Munchee* order, the *Ripple* litigation, any NFT enforcement order, CFTC cases other than *McDonnell*, state securities laws, the United Kingdom's rules on financial promotions, or the platform's terms. The Regulation Crypto Assets comment period was due to close on the day before I wrote, and the CLARITY Act could return through a motion to reconsider, so this chapter is the most likely to be out of date.

Four questions remain open after this chapter. The first is whether engagement given in return for engagement is consideration under section 17(b), which Chapter 13 left open. The second is whether a pod-boosted post about a token can be part of a scheme under Rule 10b-5 or 17 CFR 180.1(a)(1) or (a)(3). The third is whether MiCA's marketing duty reaches a post by an author outside the European Union about an offer in it, given that the sources place the duty on the offeror. The fourth is where the promoter of a "digital collectible" sits under the 2026 taxonomy. I found no authority that answers any of them.

## Chapter 24. Comparing the twenty topics: which signals differ, which laws recur and what boosting changes

### 24.1 How I compared the topics

I compared the topics with ten counts that I defined once and applied to every topic in the same way, so that a difference between two topics is a difference in the posts and not in how I counted. Seven appear in Table 24.1: a link (an address beginning "http," or a lnkd.in or bit.ly link); five or more hashtags; the word "free"; a currency figure (a dollar, euro or pound sign followed by a digit); a percent figure; a "DM me," "inbox me" or "message me" call; and a request of the form "comment below," "comment here," "follow me" or "follow for," which the table calls Ask. The other three are a word beginning "guarantee," the word "pod," and the set of reciprocal-support phrases that I used in Chapters 22 and 23 (support each other, engage with each other, like and comment, drop a comment, boost). The chapters count wider sets of words chosen for each subject, so a figure here can differ from the figure for the same topic in its chapter, and the chapter's figure is the one that goes with that chapter's argument.

The topics are built from different mixes of the four files, and the files differ. Among the 54,870 posts in the twenty topics, the podawaa file supplies 31,006 (56.5%), HyperClapper 9,190 (16.7%), LinkBoost-2025 567 (1.0%) and the 65 exports 14,107 (25.7%). A podawaa post has five or more hashtags in 41.8% of cases, against 7.5% for HyperClapper, 27.0% for LinkBoost-2025 and 25.8% for the exports. A "DM me" call appears in 0.59% of podawaa posts, against 1.24%, 2.12% and 3.12%. The podawaa share of a topic runs from 4.6% for AI agents to 92.5% for crypto. Across the twenty topics, the rank correlation between the podawaa share and the share of posts with five or more hashtags is 0.68, with "DM me" calls it is -0.66, and with requests to comment or follow it is -0.63 (VERIFIED; Spearman, 20 topics). A difference between topics in these counts therefore cannot be separated from a difference between files, and I do not claim that a topic causes any of the differences (INFERENCE as to the limit).

### 24.2 What the counts show across topics

Sale-funnel words are concentrated in two topics and numbers in three, and only links and hashtags reach a majority of posts anywhere. Table 24.1 gives each count as a percentage of the topic's posts, and the Podawaa column gives the share of the topic's posts that come from that file.

The corpus does not announce itself. The word "pod" appears in 43 of the 54,870 posts (0.08%), and 14 of the 43 are in the LinkedIn topic. The reciprocal-support phrases appear in 972 posts (1.8%): most often in energy, sleep, food and fitness (8.8%), supply chain (4.8%) and learning (4.0%). The set includes "boost," which has ordinary business uses, so these figures are upper bounds on reciprocal support (INFERENCE). The posts read as ordinary professional posts, which is the description in Chapter 1.

Direct-selling language is most common in the energy topic and the learning topic. A "DM me" call appears in 750 posts (1.37%) and is most common in energy, sleep, food and fitness (9.9%), sales (3.3%) and AI agents (2.8%). It is least common in technology, luxury travel and crypto (0.2% each). A request to comment or follow appears in 1,366 posts (2.5%), most often in energy (8.6%), learning (8.1%), AI agents (4.2%) and LinkedIn (4.1%). The word "free" appears in 3,614 posts (6.6%), and in 27.2% of the learning topic, 13.8% of energy and 10.2% of sales. A link appears in 12,601 posts (23.0%), and in 55.3% of the learning topic, against 8.1% of life and family, 8.3% of energy and 9.1% of motivation. These are the topics in which the posts most resemble an invitation to buy, and the rules that turn on a sale are in Chapters 14 and 20 (the Business Opportunity Rule), 19 (health claims) and 5 and 20 (the Guide on "free"). The motivation topic, which Chapter 14 treats under the Business Opportunity Rule, has low counts here (0.5% "DM me," 9.1% links), and its chapter relies on wider word lists.

Numbers are most common in three topics. A currency figure appears in 12.3% of investing and finance posts, 11.9% of crypto and 10.9% of AI agents, and in 0.2% of life and family and 0.7% of leadership. A percent figure appears in 17.5% of investing, 16.7% of AI agents and 16.6% of energy, sleep, food and fitness. The rules that turn on a figure are the earnings-claim rules (Chapters 14 and 20), the securities antifraud rules (Chapters 11, 13 and 23) and the rules on substantiation (Chapters 4 and 19). A figure may be a claim, a price or a statistic about something else, so the counts show where the rules could apply and not that they do.

A guarantee word appears in 304 posts (0.55%), and no topic exceeds 1.1% (money and prices), followed by strategy and planning at 1.0%. Five or more hashtags appear in 17,423 posts (31.8%): in 61.1% of the supply-chain topic, 56.7% of crypto and 56.6% of luxury travel, against 15.0% of relationships, 15.4% of learning and 16.8% of life and family. Those three high values are in topics with podawaa shares of 70.5%, 92.5% and 87.8%, and Section 24.1 explains why that matters.

Apart from links in the learning topic and hashtags in three topics, no count reaches a majority in any topic. The largest figure among the other counts is the word "free" in 27.2% of the learning topic. The counts are of words, and a post can state a claim without any of these words, so I do not read the small shares as evidence that most posts are free of claims (INFERENCE as to the limit).

### 24.3 Which laws recur

Each topic draws on a different set of rules, a few rules recur, and in every chapter a rule reaches a post through what the post says, sells or discloses. Table 24.2 lists, for each topic, the principal rules that its chapter applied and what makes each rule reach a post. The column on the right is my reading of each chapter's section on how the rules reach the posts (INFERENCE).

| Chapter and topic | Principal rules applied | What makes a rule reach a post |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 4 Technology and innovation | FTC substantiation under Section 5; SEC rules on advisers' and issuers' disclosure; federal wire fraud; EU AI Act | A claim about what AI or a product does; a statement to investors |
| 5 Events and networking | FTC rule on unfair or deceptive fees (Part 464); BOTS Act; Guide on "free" (Part 251); Endorsement Guides | A stated ticket price, a "free" offer, a scarcity claim, an endorsement by a speaker or sponsor |

| 21 Luxury travel and hotels | Total-price rule for lodging; state resort-fee action; EU action on accommodation platforms; California seller-of-travel law; EU Package Travel Directive; hotel liability for trafficking of minors | A post that states a price for lodging |

A few sets of rules recur. Section 5 of the FTC Act is named in the laws section of 11 chapters (4 to 7, 9, 10, 12, 14, 15, 18 and 19). The Endorsement Guides, Part 255, are named in 8 (5, 6, 14, 16, 18, 19, 20 and 22), and the rule on fake reviews and fake indicators, Part 465, in 7 (5, 6, 7, 10, 14, 16 and 18). The securities laws are named in 6 (4, 6, 11, 13, 15 and 23). The European Union's instruments are named in 15 (4, 5, 8, 10, 12 and 14 to 23), and the law of individual states in 13 (4, 6, 7, 9, 10, 12, 14, 15 and 19 to 23). These counts show where a chapter names a rule in its laws section, and a mention is not an application, because some chapters name a rule to say that it does not fit (VERIFIED as counts of mentions).

Two items are aimed at the engagement itself. Section 465.8 reaches the sale or purchase of fake indicators of social media influence, and the platform's contract reaches automation that produces them (Chapters 3 and 10). Every other rule in Table 24.2 attaches to a claim, a price, an offer, a product, a connection or a person, and the engagement matters to it only through its effect on how many people see the post and how credible it looks (INFERENCE from the chapters' sections on how the rules reach the posts). Chapter 10 found no complaint or decision that applies section 465.8 to pod engagement. The only enforcement document it found is a warning-letter template dated 22 December 2025. The rule took effect on 21 October 2024 by my count, which is two years to the day before the date on this dissertation.

### 24.4 Who each rule reaches

The four parties named in Chapter 3 are reached by different rules, and most chapters add a fifth party that those four do not cover.

The author is reached by most of the rules in Table 24.2, because most attach to what the author states. Under section 465.8(b) an author is reached only if the author bought or procured indicators that the author knew or should have known to be fake and that materially misrepresented influence for a commercial purpose, and Chapter 10 found each of those elements open for pod engagement. Under the other rules the author is reached when the post states a price, makes a claim, offers securities, promises earnings or conceals a connection.

The tool operator is reached by section 465.8(a), which asks only whether the indicators "can be used" to misrepresent influence; by the platform's contract, which LinkedIn has enforced against sellers of fake engagement (Chapter 3); and by data-security and computer-access law where the tool holds members' credentials (Chapters 3 and 8). A theory that a supplier of a review-writing tool is liable was the basis of the FTC's *Rytr* order, which was set aside on 22 December 2025, so no order now supports that route (Chapter 12).

The engaging member is the party for whom the chapters found the least authority. Chapter 10 found none for reading the member's like as either supplying or procuring an indicator. The Endorsement Guides could reach a comment that praises a product (Chapters 3 and 16), and Chapter 6 considers employees who engage at an employer's direction. The member who is a consumer is likely outside the European rule on false endorsements, which is addressed to traders (Chapter 10, INFERENCE).

The platform is reached by the rules on platforms and not by the rules on content. Section 230(c)(1) protects a platform from treatment as the publisher of its members' posts, and Chapter 10 found no decision on whether a like count is information that the platform provided or that others provided. *FTC v. LeadClick Media* denied immunity to a party responsible in part for developing the content (STATED, Chapter 10). In the European Union, LinkedIn's designation as a very large online platform brings Articles 34 and 35 of the Digital Services Act (Chapter 10), and Article 28 and the Commission's guidelines place a duty toward minors on platforms accessible to them (Chapter 22).

The fifth party differs by chapter: the employer in Chapter 6, the issuer who pays a promoter in Chapters 11 and 13, the seller of a business opportunity in Chapters 14 and 20, the importer in Chapter 17, the hotel in Chapter 21, the adult who features a child in Chapter 22, and the offeror of a crypto asset in Chapter 23. A competitor who loses sales to a boosted rival can sue under section 43(a) of the Lanham Act (Chapters 3 and 18), and a buyer's remedy depends on state law (Chapter 3).

**A responsibility matrix.** Table 24.3 sets each party against the rule that could reach it, what the rule would require a claimant to show, what the sources in this dissertation contain on each requirement, and what is missing. It says which facts would have to be shown and which of them I have. It does not say that any party is responsible, and no chapter finds that any author, tool operator, member or platform broke any law.

**Table 24.3.** Responsibility matrix: parties, rules, what a claimant would have to show, what the sources contain, and what is missing

| Party | Rule that could reach it | What a claimant would have to show | What my sources contain | What is missing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Author who posts and receives pod engagement | 16 CFR § 465.8(b); the platform contract; point 23c of Annex I to the Unfair Commercial Practices Directive if the author is a trader | That the author bought or procured indicators that the author knew or should have known to be fake, that the indicators could be used to misrepresent influence materially, and a commercial purpose | Posts were submitted to pod tools to be boosted (Chapter 2). In Chapter 10, 4.7% of posts use personal-brand or influencer language (INFERENCE that influence has commercial value). LinkedIn's help page says that excessive comment creation or an automation tool may limit the visibility of comments (STATED) | Whether any engagement was fake under § 465.1(h); what the author knew; whether a personal brand is a commercial purpose. I found a warning-letter template of 22 December 2025 and no complaint |
| Pod tool operator or seller of pod engagement | § 465.8(a); the platform contract; computer-access and data-security law where the tool holds members' credentials (Chapters 3 and 8) | That the operator sold or distributed indicators that it knew or should have known to be fake. Paragraph (a) asks only whether the indicators can be used to misrepresent influence, not whether they were | A vendor page of August 2025 says that other members can automatically like and comment on a member's posts (STATED; the vendor's own description). In the 65 exports, 74.6% of records have type normal and 36.2% carry the timing value 20 (Table 10.1). The book describes timing settings (STATED). LinkedIn has sued sellers of fake engagement under its contract, and those defendants used fake accounts (Chapter 3) | Whether the engagement is fake; what engagementType and engageTiming mean; any enforcement action against a pod tool (none found). The Commission's *Rytr* order, which rested on a supplier theory, was set aside on 22 December 2025 (Chapter 12) |
| Engaging member | The platform contract; the Endorsement Guides where a comment praises a product; § 465.8(a) or (b) on readings for which I found no authority | That the member supplied or procured an indicator and knew or should have known that it was fake; under the Guides, a comment that is an endorsement with a material connection | The exports give a count of engaged users for each post (median 278) and nothing about who they were. The book says that a member in an automated pod may have no idea what the account is doing (p. 50), which is a description of members in general | Any authority for either reading of § 465.8; any data on what members knew or read |
| Platform | Section 230(c)(1) and (c)(2) as defenses; *FTC v. LeadClick Media* if the platform is responsible in part for developing content; Articles 34 and 35 of the Digital Services Act for a very large platform; the contract that it sets and enforces | In the United States, a statute that imposes a duty, or conduct that develops the content; I found no statute that requires detection of pods. In the European Union, a failure to assess and mitigate systemic risk, which Article 34(2) says includes inauthentic use or automated exploitation (INFERENCE that pods fall within it) | The two case threads in Chapter 10 show that LinkedIn answered on the day each case was opened, said that it had resolved a view-count issue and had put actions in place to mitigate the LEMPOD report, and wrote in March 2024 that automation tooling is prohibited (STATED). In 2025 it told a trade publication that it reduces the reach of detected pod activity and is aware of pod services (STATED) | Whether LinkedIn counts or removes engagement from pod tools; any LinkedIn action against LEMPOD or its users; its risk assessments, which I did not open; a decision on whether a like count is information that the platform provided |
| Competitor or buyer who relies on a count | Section 43(a)(1)(B) of the Lanham Act for a competitor; state consumer-protection law for a buyer | For a competitor, an injury to a commercial interest in reputation or sales that the deception proximately caused (*Lexmark*, Section 3.4). Consumers generally cannot sue under § 43(a) | No data on who read the posts or relied on the counts. Question 21 in Section 25.4 asks whether a pod's amplification bears on liability or damages | Reliance, injury, and any link between a boosted count and a sale |

Read across the rows, the table shows two patterns. The rows for the author, the tool operator and the engaging member depend on the same missing fact, which is whether the engagement was fake under section 465.1(h). That turns on who engaged and whether they acted on their own opinion. The 65 exports record how many users a tool engaged and which settings it used, and they do not record who those users were or what they read, so I cannot complete any of the three rows (INFERENCE). The platform's row depends on different facts. Section 465.8 is written for those who sell, distribute, buy or procure fake indicators, and I found no source that applies it to a platform that displays counts (INFERENCE). A platform's notice therefore matters under the Digital Services Act, where Articles 34 and 35 ask what a very large platform assessed and did, and under its own contract (INFERENCE). The case threads in Chapter 10 document notice and a response. They do not show what LinkedIn counted or removed, and the question of the platform's responsibility turns on that fact.

### 24.5 What boosting changes in the law, and what it does not

Boosting changes three things in the legal analysis and leaves the substantive tests where they were. Chapter 4 states the second part for technology claims, "boosting changes none of the substantive tests," and the later chapters found the same.

The first change is that boosting is the subject of a rule. Section 465.8 and the platform contract treat the manufactured signal as the wrong, and that is why Chapter 10 says that they apply to the LinkedIn topic more directly than to any other.

The second change is that boosting raises reach and credibility, which are inputs to other rules. The deception standard looks at the net impression on the general public and at materiality (Chapter 3). A founder's offer that is promoted to a wider audience raises the question of general solicitation, which Rule 506(b) bars and Rule 506(c) permits on conditions (Chapter 11). A competitor's claim for damages may depend on how far a boosted claim spread (Chapter 18, open question). In each case the rule's test is unchanged and boosting affects the facts that the test examines (INFERENCE).

The third change is that boosting often runs through a tool that holds members' accounts, which brings in rules on data security and unauthorized access that have no link to what a post says (Chapters 3 and 8).

Boosting does not change whether a claim is true, whether a price is complete, whether an offering needs registration, whether a health claim is substantiated or whether a connection must be disclosed. A post that satisfies those rules is not made unlawful by a boost, and a post that breaks them is not excused because the boost was real (INFERENCE).

The same few questions recur across the chapters. Whether prompted engagement "reflects" a real person's opinion under section 465.1(h) arises in Chapters 3, 10 and 12. Whether reciprocal engagement is a material connection, a testimonial or consideration arises in Chapters 3, 13, 16, 19 and 23. Chapter 25 lists the open questions in full.

### 24.6 Rules that moved while I was writing

The rules in this dissertation changed during the work, so each chapter carries the date of its sources. The Eighth Circuit vacated the FTC's click-to-cancel rule on 8 July 2025, and on 30 January 2026 the Commission announced that it had sent a draft advance notice of proposed rulemaking to the Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs, with no proposed rule yet (Chapter 7). The Commission's rule on unfair or deceptive fees took effect on 12 May 2025 (Chapter 5). The amended children's privacy rule took effect on 23 June 2025 with compliance due on 22 April 2026 (Chapters 7 and 22). The Rytr order was set aside on 22 December 2025 (Chapter 12). The EU AI Act's transparency duties in Article 50 became applicable on 2 August 2026 (Chapter 4). The SEC and the CFTC issued a joint classification of crypto assets on 17 March 2026, the SEC proposed Regulation Crypto Assets on 18 August 2026, and cloture on the CLARITY Act failed on 15 September 2026 (Chapter 23). The Commission's notice of proposed rulemaking to extend the Business Opportunity Rule to coaching, which Chapter 20 discusses, is dated 13 January 2025, and I did not check its later status. A reader who needs the current position on any of these should check it at the source.

### 24.7 What this means for coming generations, taken together

Young people appear in four roles across the chapters, and the rules name them most specifically where the role is that of a child.

Young people are users of the platforms and tools (Chapters 4, 7, 10, 12 and 22). The Surgeon General's advisory reports that up to 95% of young people aged 13 to 17 use social media and that nearly two thirds of teenagers do so daily (Chapters 3 and 22). The Pew Research Center reports, from a survey of 1,458 teenagers in late 2025, that 64% use chatbots and about three in ten do so daily (Chapters 4 and 12). The rules aimed at them are the children's privacy rule, the Digital Services Act's duty toward minors and the state laws on companion chatbots (Chapters 7, 12 and 22).

Young people are buyers, investors and learners (Chapters 8, 9, 13, 14, 20 and 23). The FTC's April 2026 data say that 40% of loss reports from people aged 18 to 29 began on social media, against 32% for ages 30 to 59 and 14% for ages 80 and over (Chapter 8, VERIFIED against the FTC page). In Chapter 23, the Pew Research Center reports that 42% of men aged 18 to 29 had invested in, traded or used crypto in early 2024. The rules that protect these buyers are general: Section 5, the earnings-claim rules and the securities antifraud rules, enforced after the loss.

Young people are future workers (Chapters 6, 12, 15 and 20). The chapters' evidence here is about engagement at work, the effect of AI on people entering the workforce and the market for skills, and, as Chapters 12 and 15 say, the rules I opened reach people who are not yet in the workforce only indirectly.

Young people are also affected at a distance: as children shown in other people's posts (Chapter 22), as workers in supply chains (Chapter 17) and as children in hotels (Chapter 21). The rules for these are specific statutes on child influencers, on forced labor and on hotel liability for trafficking.

The pattern is that where the law names children, it is specific, and it falls on platforms, operators and adults who profit from a child's presence. For young adults the rules are general and run through laws that apply to everyone. The argument that a manufactured signal teaches a young reader the wrong lesson is mine from Chapter 1, and I have no data on how teenagers read engagement counts or on whether any reader of these posts was a teenager (INFERENCE).

### 24.8 Mental health across the twenty topics

Across the twenty topics the mental-health evidence falls into eight channels, and in none of them did I find a study of engagement pods or of manufactured counts.

Table 24.4 sets each channel against the chapters that use it, the strongest sources I opened, and what they show with their main limit.

**Table 24.4.** Channels in the mental-health evidence, with the chapters that use each, the strongest sources opened, and what they show with their main limit

| Channel | Chapters | Strongest sources opened | What they show, and the main limit |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Fraud and financial loss | 3, 8, 11, 13, 18, 23 | Button, Lewis and Tapley (2014); FTC (27 April 2026); FBI (6 April 2026); Identity Theft Resource Center (2025) | Effects on victims range from little to devastating, and reported losses are large. None concerns engagement fraud, and the Center's survey had about a 4% response |
| Debt and money stress | 9, 13, 14, 20, 23 | Richardson, Elliott and Roberts (2013), through a university release (STATED) | People in unsecured debt were more than three times as likely to have a mental health problem. The direction of cause is unknown |
| Work, job insecurity and burnout | 4, 6, 14, 15, 19, 20 | Surgeon General's workplace framework (2022); WHO (2019); APA Work in America surveys (2023, 2025); Paul and Moser (2009) | Unemployment is linked to distress (d = 0.51), and 54% of workers said job insecurity affected their stress. The surveys are correlational, and the meta-analysis is the exception |
| Social media features, comparison and counts | 3, 7, 10, 16, 18, 22 | APA advisory (2023); Surgeon General's advisory; Orben and Przybylski (2019); Sherman and others (2016) | Findings are mixed and the average association is small. No study concerns manufactured counts, and Sherman measured no mental-health outcome |
| Loneliness and connection | 5, 16, 22 | Surgeon General's advisory (2023); Naslund and others (2016); Przybylski and others (2013) | Depression odds are more than double among adults who are often lonely, and fear of missing out is linked to lower mood. The studies are correlational, and the peer-support article is a commentary |
| AI companions and young people | 4, 12, 20 | APA advisory on AI (2025); Common Sense Media (2025); Pew Research Center (2026) | 72% of teens had used AI companions, and the advisory says the research is early. The survey figure comes from a press release without a sample size |
| Forced labor and trafficking | 17, 21 | ILO (2024); Oram and others (2012) | 27.6 million people were in forced labour in 2021, and symptom levels were high among trafficked people. The evidence is mostly about women and girls |
| Climate anxiety | 18 | Hickman and others (2021) | 59% of 10,000 young people were very or extremely worried about climate change. The survey did not test green claims |

Three patterns stand out. The evidence with the strongest design, the meta-analysis on unemployment, is the farthest from pods. The evidence nearest to pods, on likes and comparison, is mixed and small on average. The sources on harm after a loss describe a range of effects, and they do not support a single claim about what a particular deceptive post does to a reader.

Each chapter's section ends on the same gaps: no data on who read the posts, no study of pods or manufactured counts, and no study of whether the rules change the outcome. The first is a gap in the corpus. The other two are gaps in the literature that I opened, and I did not search every database (INFERENCE as to the completeness of the literature).

### 24.9 What the comparison cannot show

The comparison covers 54,870 posts, which is 20.0% of the 273,771 distinct posts, and each count is of words. A topic's counts are tied to the files that supply it, so a difference between topics may be a difference between files. The legal sections rest on primary texts in some places and on summaries in others, and each chapter lists what I did not open. The map in Table 24.2 says which rules a regulator, a competitor or a court would examine first for a topic, and it does not say that any post broke any rule.

## Chapter 25. Conclusion, limitations, ethics and open questions

### 25.1 What I found

The law I examined protects a reader from a false claim more reliably than from a false crowd. I asked four questions in Chapter 1, and this section answers them in order.

**Which laws would be examined first for each topic.** The twenty topics map onto different rules, and Table 24.2 sets out the map. My reading in Chapter 1 was that the topics about money, health, children and product safety would meet specific rules. The chapters bear this out for money, health and children, and I did not examine the rules on product safety (Chapter 17 lists what I did not open). Money brought the securities rules (Chapters 11, 13 and 23), the earnings-claim rules (Chapters 14 and 20) and the pricing rules (Chapters 5, 9 and 21). Health brought the rules on health claims and licensing (Chapter 19). Children brought the children's privacy rule, the child-influencer statutes and the duties of platforms toward minors (Chapters 7, 12 and 22). Trade brought the import bans and customs fraud law (Chapter 17). The topics about leadership, relationships, motivation and marketing met general rules, mainly Section 5 of the FTC Act, the Endorsement Guides and the rule on fake reviews. In every chapter the rule reached a post through what the post says, sells or discloses: a price, a claim about performance, an offer of securities, a promise of earnings, a connection to the seller, or the collection of data about a child (INFERENCE from the chapters; Section 24.3).

**Which actor each rule reaches.** The author is reached by most rules. The tool operator is reached by section 465.8(a), the platform's contract and the law on data security. The engaging member is the party for whom I found the least authority. The platform is reached by the rules on platforms. Most chapters add a party that none of those four covers: the employer, the issuer, the seller of a business opportunity, the importer, the hotel, the adult who features a child and the offeror of a crypto asset (Section 24.4).

**What the rules mean for the people who rely on the posts, and for young people.** The rules that protect a buyer or an investor work mainly after the loss, through enforcement by the FTC, the SEC and the CFTC and through the remedies that statutes give to buyers. A competitor can sue under the Lanham Act, and a consumer's remedy depends on state law (Chapters 3, 11 and 18). The evidence that I opened says that 40% of loss reports from people aged 18 to 29 said the loss began on social media, against 14% for ages 80 and over (Chapter 8), that 42% of men aged 18 to 29 had invested in, traded or used crypto in early 2024 (Chapter 23), and that up to 95% of teenagers aged 13 to 17 use social media (Chapters 3 and 22). Where the law names children, its rules are specific and fall on platforms, operators and adults who profit from a child's presence. Where it concerns young adults, the rules are general (Section 24.7). I have no data on how any young person reads an engagement count (INFERENCE).

**Which questions have no answer yet.** I list 25 in Section 25.4. The first three concern the one rule aimed at the engagement itself, section 465.8 of the Commission's rule on fake reviews and indicators. It took effect on 21 October 2024 by my count, and in the sources I opened I found no complaint or decision that applies it to pod engagement. The only enforcement document that Chapter 10 found is a warning-letter template dated 22 December 2025. A boosted post that tells the truth about its product meets few rules, and a boosted post that lies meets the same rules as one that was never boosted (INFERENCE).

The data add four findings. Pods rarely announce themselves: the word "pod" appears in 43 of the 54,870 posts in the twenty topics (0.08%). The words that bear on a rule are present in a minority of posts in every topic, and only links and hashtags reach a majority anywhere (Section 24.2). The counts differ between topics partly because they differ between the source files (Section 24.1). The 65 exports add a date: 55,925 of their 56,363 distinct records (99.2%) were created on or after 21 October 2024, the date on which 16 CFR 465.8 took effect by my count. A date shows when a record was made and does not show that anyone broke a rule, and the counts by month are not a trend because of the 5,000-record limit (Section 2.10).

### 25.2 Limitations

The limits on the data come first. The posts are those that people submitted to pod and boost tools, and they are not a random sample of LinkedIn or of any other platform (Chapter 2). The records show what was submitted and, in the 65 exports, how a tool handled it, and they do not show who engaged, who paid, what a post earned or what any viewer did next. The topic model places 64,970 of the 273,771 distinct posts (23.7%), so the topic shares describe the placed posts and not the whole corpus. I merged 80 components into 33 named topics by hand, and a different corpus, seed or grouping would give a different list of twenty. I set aside posts that were written mostly in other languages (Chapter 2), so the analysis covers posts written mostly in English. The counts are of words from lists that I chose, and a word in a post shows that the word occurs and does not show that the post makes a claim or does an act. The corpus file I used for the counts holds no dates, so I cannot place a post before or after any rule change (Chapter 23). The 65 export files carry a creation date on every record, but 22 of them stop at exactly 5,000 records, so older records are probably missing and the counts by month are not a trend (Section 2.10). The four files differ in what they contain, and Section 24.1 shows that a topic's counts are tied to the files that supply it.

The limits on the law come next. I took United States federal law as the base, with the law of some states and of the European Union where a chapter needed it. I did not cover the United Kingdom, Canada, Australia, India or any other country, and the European Union material rests largely on law-firm and secondary sources. Many of the primary texts came back from the fetch tool as summaries, and each chapter says which texts I opened and which I did not, with the tier of each claim. Some enforcement examples come from law-firm articles and news reports, and an allegation in a complaint is an allegation and not a finding. The examples have no denominator, so they show what a rule reaches and not how often it is enforced. I could not open LinkedIn's User Agreement, because the site's robots file disallowed my fetch tool, and I did not fetch the platform's risk assessments under the Digital Services Act, so the analysis of the platform's contract rests on court decisions and a help page (Chapters 3 and 10). Chapter 5 did not determine whether Part 464 reaches conference registrations, and Chapter 8 did not determine whether a LinkedIn message is an electronic mail message under the CAN-SPAM Act.

The law moved while I wrote, and the parts most likely to be out of date are the tariff picture (Chapter 17), the status of the proposed extension of the Business Opportunity Rule to coaching (Chapter 20), the amendment of the Package Travel Directive (Chapter 21), the EU AI Act's transparency duties (Chapter 4) and its duty of AI literacy (Chapter 20), and the 2026 developments in crypto regulation (Chapter 23). Section 24.6 lists the dated items. I am not a lawyer, and this dissertation is not legal advice. Whether a rule applies to a particular post depends on facts that the data do not contain, such as who wrote the post, what the author was paid and what the author knew. No chapter says that any author, tool operator, member or platform broke any law.

The evidence on young people is the thinnest part. I have no data on how teenagers or young adults read engagement counts, and the posts say nothing about who read them. The evidence I opened concerns how many young people use the platforms and what they lose to scams that begin there, and I connect it to pods by reasoning that I label INFERENCE. Chapter 10 reports evidence that counts change judgments in some conditions and not in others, and that evidence does not test pod boosting, so the connection is plausible and not established.

### 25.3 Ethics and my position

I analyzed the posts in aggregate and named no account, author or individual post, because the authors wrote for public audiences and did not agree to be studied, and the study does not need their identities (Chapter 2). The enforcement examples name the parties to public orders, complaints and decisions, with the status of each matter, because those records are public. I have no data about any child, and no chapter identifies one.

I have an interest in the subject. I have worked with engagement pods since 2019, I coauthored *Top 100 List of Lies on Social Media*, I wrote the teen guide *DIGITAL BOUGHTulism: POONAMI*, and I am the CEO of Dream IT Software, which publishes the SPOTAPOD Analysis Repository (Hall, 2026; Sections 1.5 and 2.1). The evidence tiers and the rule that a claim marked VERIFIED rests on a text that I opened are my safeguards against that interest, and a reader should weigh my position in addition. I used an AI assistant to help retrieve and read sources, run the counts and draft text, and I chose the questions and the claims and am responsible for the result (Chapter 1).

I, Daniel Hall, am the sole author of this dissertation and the only researcher named in it, and it names no co-author, supervisor or institution. The statement that the subject is uncharted rests on the sources I opened, and an earlier study that I missed would qualify it (Section 1.4). The claim to primary research has the limits that Section 1.4 states: I ran no interviews or surveys, the field demonstrations in my earlier book are described and not analyzed (Section 2.9), and the mental-health sections review published studies.

The terms of the site that served the 65 export files prohibit scraping outside approved functionalities and restrict disclosure of non-public information (Section 2.8). I report the terms and do not decide whether my capture complied with them, and a reader should treat that question as open. Reporting only aggregate results limits what the study discloses about any author, and it does not answer the question.

My earlier book reports two demonstrations in which I released posts into pods and other members' automated accounts engaged with them. One post said in its own text that it was fake news, and in the other I supplied the comments that the accounts posted (Section 2.9). The book describes those members as unaware of what their accounts did. It mentions no consent procedure, and I do not claim that anyone's consent was sought. The demonstrations are not part of this analysis. I record them here because a reader who judges my position should know that I have acted inside pods and not only observed them.

The case correspondence in Chapter 10 bears on my conduct in two ways. In the 2023 thread I reported that I had seen view counts for posts by creators other than myself, and the thread records permission from one creator only. In the 2024 thread I wrote that I used a second account for LEMPOD, a pod tool, and that I had told LinkedIn so, and I reported a flaw in that third party's system to LinkedIn. I record both facts because a reader who judges my position should know them. I make no claim about whether either action complied with LinkedIn's terms or with the law, and I do not offer the reports as evidence that I acted properly.

A map from topics to laws could be read two ways. A regulator, a competitor or a court can use it to see where to look first, and a pod operator or an author can use it to see which claims to avoid. I wrote it for the first use and for readers who want to know what the rules say. The map does not tell anyone how to evade a rule, because the rules attach to claims and sales and not to the boost, and Section 24.5 says that a boost does not make a lawful post unlawful or an unlawful post lawful.

### 25.4 Questions that no court or agency has answered

I found no authority that answers any of the 25 questions in Table 25.1, and the last column gives the chapters that raise each. The questions are mine, and they are drawn from the chapters' own lists.

| No. | Subject | Question | Chapters |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |

| 2 | Fake indicators and the platform | Who "sells or distributes" the indicator under section 465.8(a): the tool operator, the engaging member or both, and does a member who engages in exchange for engagement "purchase or procure" it under paragraph (b)? | 3 and 10 |

| 12 | Securities and crypto | Does MiCA's marketing duty reach a post by an author outside the European Union about an offer in it? | 23 |
| 13 | Securities and crypto | Where does the promoter of a "digital collectible" sit under the 2026 SEC and CFTC classification? | 23 |
| 14 | Selling, earnings and education | Is a seller of coaching alone, with no promise of locations, outlets, accounts or buyback, a seller of a business opportunity under section 437.1(c)? | 14 and 20 |
| 15 | Selling, earnings and education | Do the vocational-school guides and a state's private-school law reach an individual who sells a skills course on a social platform? | 20 |
| 16 | Selling, earnings and education | Is giving contact details in return for a free webinar a payment under point 20 of Annex I of the Unfair Commercial Practices Directive? | 20 |
| 17 | Prices and travel | Is a post that states a nightly rate for a hotel, with no way to book, an offer, display or advertisement under the FTC's fee rule, so that the total price must appear in it? | 21 |
| 18 | Prices and travel | Does a travel advisor's social post "advertise" covered transportation under California's seller-of-travel law, so that it must show a registration number? | 21 |
| 19 | Competition, advertising and claims | Can a single public post that announces a price or capacity plan, without private contact, be an invitation to collude under Section 5? | 15 |
| 20 | Competition, advertising and claims | Is a claim of ethical or forced-labor-free sourcing, made in a post, an objective claim that needs substantiation? | 17 |
| 21 | Competition, advertising and claims | Does a pod's amplification of a post that makes a false comparative or measurable claim bear on liability or damages under 15 U.S.C. 1125(a)(1)(B)? | 18 |
| 22 | Health, children and privacy | Is private-message coaching that follows a public post, offered to a state's residents by an unlicensed coach, "professional conduct" in the Eleventh Circuit's sense? | 19 |
| 23 | Health, children and privacy | Is a text post by a parent about the parent's child, on a business network, "content" within the child-influencer statutes, which the summaries I read describe in terms of video? | 22 |
| 24 | Health, children and privacy | Can the household exemption in Article 2(2)(c) of the GDPR cover a post on a professional network by a person who also markets a service? | 22 |
| 25 | Connection between people | Does prompted reciprocal engagement add to or replace in-person connection? No source I opened measures it, and it is an empirical question and not a legal one. | 5 and 16 |

### 25.5 What would settle them

Four kinds of evidence would answer most of what the data cannot show. The first is a decision. A complaint or an order that applies section 465.8 to pod engagement would settle questions 1 to 3, and a ruling on section 17(b) and reciprocal engagement would settle question 9. Commission guidance that says whether a prompted like is a fake indicator would answer question 1 without litigation (INFERENCE). The second is data on who engaged: accounts, timing and whether each member read the post. Those data would show whether any indicator was fake in the rule's sense, and the 65 exports carry fields for timing and engagement type whose meaning I do not know (Chapter 10, Table 10.1). The third is data on the audience, including the ages of the people who read the posts and what they did next. Without it, the argument about future generations rests on usage figures and on reasoning. The fourth is dated posts, which would let a reader place each post under the rules in force when it was written.

**A question for future research.** The matrix in Section 24.4 leaves one question that no source I have answers. If a platform has notice that automated engagement from third-party tools reaches its members' posts, and its systems count that engagement in the signals they show, how should responsibility for the resulting false signals be divided among the platform, the tool operator, the engaging member and the author? Both halves of the condition are untested. The case threads in Chapter 10 document notice to LinkedIn and a response, and a vendor's page and the 65 exports document automated engagement, but I found no source on whether LinkedIn counts that engagement (INFERENCE). A study would need three kinds of record that the data lack: which interactions were automated, what the platform counted and removed, and the dates of its notices and actions. It could then compare the United States, where I found no statute that requires a platform to detect pods, with the European Union, where Articles 34 and 35 of the Digital Services Act require a very large platform to assess and mitigate systemic risks, including "intentional manipulation of their service, including by inauthentic use or automated exploitation of the service."

### 25.6 What the sources support about mental health

The sources I opened support three statements about mental health, and they do not support a statement about engagement pods.

First, some of the harms that the rules in this dissertation address have documented mental-health associations. The clearest are fraud and debt (Chapters 3, 9 and 23), unemployment and job insecurity (Chapters 4, 6 and 15), loneliness (Chapters 5 and 16) and forced labor (Chapter 17). Second, for social media features and counts the findings are mixed. The American Psychological Association says that social media is not inherently beneficial or harmful, the Surgeon General cannot conclude that it is sufficiently safe for children and adolescents, and one large analysis found an association with at most 0.4% of the variation in adolescent well-being (Chapters 7 and 10). Third, professional bodies say that the research on AI and young people is early (Chapter 12).

I found no study of engagement pods, of manufactured counts, or of the effect on mental health of any rule in this dissertation, and the corpus has no information about its readers. The mental-health sections therefore connect topics to evidence by inference, and each says so.

Four questions are open. Does a manufactured like count affect a reader's comparison with others differently from a real count? Does a comment that a member wrote because a pod expected it change how supported the recipient feels once the recipient knows its source? Do enforcement actions against deceptive promotion reduce the harm that victims report? What happens to the mental health of people who buy a course or an investment product on a false claim? An experiment that varies the count a reader sees and measures well-being, a survey of fraud victims that records how the fraud reached them, and audience data that include ages would each answer part of these questions.

### 25.7 Conclusion

The question was which laws apply to the twenty most common topics in 273,771 engagement-pod posts, and what those laws mean for the people who read the posts and for the generations that will grow up on the platforms that carry them. The answer in these chapters is that the law attaches to the claim and not to the crowd. A false price, a false health claim, a false promise of earnings, an unregistered offer of securities or the use of a child's data is reached by rules that apply whether or not the post was boosted. The crowd itself is reached by one rule and by the platform's contract, and I found no complaint that applies that rule to pod engagement.

The people who will live longest with the result are the youngest. Teenagers use the platforms heavily, and young adults report a higher share of losses that began on social media than older adults do. The law names children specifically, and what it asks of platforms, operators and adults who profit from a child's presence is detailed. The law that reaches young adults is general, and it works after the loss has happened. The questions in Section 25.4 are the places where a court, an agency or a researcher could change that, and the data in this dissertation cannot answer any of them.

## References

This list holds every source that I opened for this dissertation, grouped by kind and followed by the chapter or chapters that rely on it. It leaves out every source that I only saw in a search result and every page that I could not open. Pages were read in October 2026 and can change.

I read pages through a fetch tool that returns a summary of long pages rather than the raw text. A tag in square brackets marks the access level where it matters: \[V\] means that I read the primary legal text or official page and checked the quoted wording against it (VERIFIED in the chapters); \[T\] means that the source is a secondary account, such as a law-firm alert, a trade report, a news item or a mirror of an official text, and the chapters treat it as STATED. An entry without a tag is an official or primary page that I read through the fetch tool, and the chapter that relies on it says how far I read it. The statutes and rules listed in section A and section B are cited from the pages named here, not from a printed code.

### I. Sources that I could not open

I could not read LinkedIn's User Agreement, because the site disallows automated readers, so I do not quote its terms on automation or engagement. I did not obtain LinkedIn's Digital Services Act risk assessments. I did not open the following: the consolidated text of the Unfair Commercial Practices Directive on EUR-Lex (the page returned no text); the official EU text of Regulation 1924/2006; the text of Regulation (EU) 2023/1114 (MiCA), Directive (EU) 2024/825, Regulation (EU) 2024/3015 and the Corporate Sustainability Due Diligence Directive; Pew's own page on teens and social media (the fetch tool returned an error) and Pew's 2025 page on cryptocurrency (it did not load); the text of the SEC and CFTC interpretation of 17 March 2026, of proposed Regulation Crypto Assets, and of the CLARITY and GENIUS Acts; the complaints and orders in the Click Profit, Ecommerce Empire Builders and FBA Machine matters; the docket in the TopSocial24 case; the outcome of the District of Columbia suit against Marriott; and the definitions in 16 CFR 464.1. Each chapter's closing section on what the data cannot show names further items that I did not open.

For the mental-health section in each chapter, I opened some sources only in part. I read Button, Lewis and Tapley (2014), Freeman and others (2019), Hickman and others (2021), Orben and Przybylski (2019) and Paul and Moser (2009) as abstract records. I read Richardson, Elliott and Roberts (2013), Wood, Perunovic and Lee (2009) and Mills and Nower (2019) only through a university release or a news summary, and I did not open the journal pages. I did not open the full 2025 Internet Crime Report of the FBI, the full Consumer Impact Report of the Identity Theft Resource Center or the full Common Sense Media report.

I also saw one source only in search results and did not open it: the 2022 paper in the *Journal of Marketing Management* titled "With a little help from my friends: exploring mutual engagement and authenticity within foodie influencers' communities of practice" (doi:10.1080/0267257X.2022.2041705, <https://doi.org/10.1080/0267257X.2022.2041705>). The publisher's page did not load in my fetch tool, and I do not describe the paper's findings (Chapter 1).

I did not open the two Harvard Dataverse records that the analysis repository names: doi:10.7910/DVN/WD9AUR ("LinkedIn posts using fake socials") and doi:10.7910/DVN/NT6YM2 ("Replication Data for: SPOTAPOD Analysis Repository"). For the first, I report the checksum match between the record's file and the podawaa file as the repository's provenance notes record it (STATED). For the second, I give the title as the repository's citation file gives it and make no claim about what the record holds (Section 2.1).

### J. My own earlier work

- Hall, D., Adam, M. and Berg, S., 2025, *Top 100 List of Lies on Social Media*. Nine chapters; Chapters 7 and 8 are signed by Berg and Chapter 9 by Adam. Copyright 2025 by the three authors; the PDF names no publisher and no ISBN. Ch. 1, 2, 10, 25. A trade book that is not peer reviewed, and my own work, so it cannot corroborate a claim (Section 2.9). I read a 111-page PDF through optical character recognition and checked each quotation against the page images; page numbers in the text are the printed page numbers.
- Hall, D., 2026, *SPOTAPOD Analysis Repository: aggregate-only companion analysis of engagement-pod datasets*. Software and documents, GitHub, published by Dream IT Software. <https://github.com/DreamITSoftware/SPOTAPOD> Code under the MIT licence and documents under CC BY 4.0. I read the repository as it stood at commit 2c8d3f8, dated 22 October 2026. Ch. 1, 2, 25. My own work, published by a company that I lead, so it cannot corroborate a claim and is cited for what it contains (Section 1.5). Its citation file states that it is independent of the project at github.com/PolymathWizard/SPOTAPOD. It holds no raw data (Section 2.1).
